The Life of Benvenuto Cellini

Written by himself in Florence, from the Italian: goldsmith, sculptor, soldier, duellist, prisoner of the Pope, and the least reliable narrator of the Renaissance

by Benvenuto Cellini

1558-1567 2026

This is an AI modernization of The Life of Benvenuto Cellini into contemporary English. The original is available from Wikisource. Also available as an epub.

Proem

	I am writing this troubled Life of mine
	to give thanks to the God of nature
	who gave me my soul and has looked after it since;
	I have done high and various things, and I am alive.
	That cruel Destiny of mine has failed to rob me
	of life, of gold, of glory, of more skill than my share,
	of grace, of courage, of looks, of the kind of figure
	that passes many men, and catches the ones who pass me.
	Only one thing grieves me badly, now that I know it:
	how much of that dear time I lost in vanity.
	The wind carries our fragile thoughts away.
	Since regretting is no use, I shall be content,
	climbing back the way I came down — Benvenuto,
	Welcome, in the flower of this worthy Tuscan ground.

I had begun to write this Life of mine with my own hand, as you can see from certain pages stuck together; but seeing that I was losing too much time over it, and that it looked to me like enormous vanity, there came into my hands a son of Michele di Goro from the Pieve a Groppine, a boy of about thirteen and rather sickly. I set him to writing, and while I worked I dictated my Life to him; and because I got some pleasure out of it, I worked much harder and got a great deal more done. So I left him that job, which I hope to keep up as far forward as my memory will carry me.

Book One

Book One, Chapters 1 to 8

Chapter 1

All men of every sort who have done anything worth doing — or at any rate anything that resembles it — ought, if they are truthful and honest, to write down their own lives with their own hand. But nobody should begin so fine an undertaking before he is past the age of forty. That thought has come to me now, when I am walking on past my fifty-eighth year, finished, and am in Florence, my home town; and remembering the many contrary things that happen to anyone who lives, and finding myself with fewer of them on me than I have ever had up to this age — indeed it seems to me I have greater contentment of mind and health of body than I have ever had before; and remembering some pleasant good things and some evils beyond all reckoning, which, when I turn round and look back, frighten me with astonishment that I have got as far as this age of fifty-eight, walking so happily forward, by the grace of God.

Chapter 2

Even though the men who have worked with some smell of skill about them have made themselves known to the world, and that alone ought to be enough for them — having been seen to be a man, and recognised as one — still, a man has to live the way he finds other people living, and so a little worldly swagger comes into it, and that has several heads. The first is to let other people know that your line comes down from men of quality, and of great antiquity.

I am called Benvenuto Cellini, son of Master Giovanni, son of Andrea, son of Cristofano Cellini; my mother was Madonna Elisabetta, daughter of Stefano Granacci; both of them citizens of Florence. We find it written in the chronicles made by our very old Florentines, men to be trusted — and Giovanni Villani writes it too — that the city of Florence was plainly built in imitation of the beautiful city of Rome; and you can still see some traces of the Colosseum and of the Baths. These are near Santa Croce. The Capitol was where the Old Market is now. The Rotonda is standing whole, which was made for the temple of Mars and is now our San Giovanni. That this was so can be seen perfectly well and cannot be denied; but the buildings are much smaller than the ones in Rome. The man who had them built, they say, was Julius Caesar, along with some Roman gentlemen who, when Fiesole had been stormed and taken, put up a city in this place, and each of them took on one of these notable buildings.

Julius Caesar had a first captain of his, a very brave man, called Fiorino of Cellino, which is a castle about two miles from Montefiascone. This Fiorino had made his quarters under Fiesole, where Florence is now, so as to be near the river Arno for the convenience of the army; and all those soldiers, and everyone else who had business with the captain, used to say: "Let's go to Fiorenze" — partly because the captain's name was Fiorino, and partly because the place where he had his quarters was, by the nature of it, extraordinarily rich in flowers. So, at the founding of the city, since this struck Julius Caesar as a very beautiful name, and one that had arrived by chance, and since flowers themselves bring a good omen, he gave the city the name of Fiorenze; and he did it to pay his brave captain that compliment as well, and he was all the fonder of him for having pulled him out of a very humble place, and because so able a man was a creature of his own making. As for the name these learned imaginers and investigators of the descent of names give — that it is because the city is flowing at the Arno — that does not seem to me to hold, because Rome is flowing at the Tiber, Ferrara is flowing at the Po, Lyons is flowing at the Saône, Paris is flowing at the Seine, and yet they all have different names, which came to them by other roads.

That is what we find, and that is what we believe: that we come down from a man of quality. Then we find that there are Cellinis of ours in Ravenna, the most ancient city in Italy, and there are great gentlemen there; and there are some in Pisa too, and I have found them in many parts of Christendom; and in this state as well some of the family are left, still given to arms — for it is not many years ago now that a young man called Luca Cellini, a beardless boy, fought with an experienced soldier and a very brave man, called Francesco da Vicorati, who had fought in the lists more than once before. This Luca, by his own courage, with his sword in his hand, beat him and killed him, with so much valour and skill that he astonished the world, which was expecting exactly the opposite; so that I take glory in tracing my descent from men of quality. As for the small honours I have won for my house, such as they are, under the way we live today and for the reasons everybody knows, and by my art, which is no great matter — I shall speak of those in their proper place; taking far more pride in having been born humble, and having laid some honourable beginning for my house, than if I had been born of great lineage and had stained it or blotted it out with rotten qualities. So I shall make a start by saying how it pleased God that I should be born.

Chapter 3

My people lived in the Val d'Ambra, and they had a great deal of property there, and lived like little lords, having pulled back there because of the factions; they were all men given to arms, and very brave. In that time one of their sons, the youngest, who was called Cristofano, got into a great quarrel with certain neighbours and friends of theirs; and because the heads of the house on both sides had put their hands into it, and because they saw the fire was burning so dangerously that the two families looked likely to be wiped out altogether, the older men, considering this, agreed together: mine took Cristofano away, and the other side took away the other young man who was the origin of the quarrel. They sent theirs to Siena; ours sent Cristofano to Florence, and there they bought him a little house in the Via Chiara, by the convent of Sant'Orsola, and at the Ponte a Rifredi they bought him some very good land. Cristofano took a wife in Florence and had sons and daughters, and when all his daughters had been settled, the rest was divided among the sons after their father's death. The house in the Via Chiara, with a few other small things, went to one of those sons, whose name was Andrea. He too took a wife, and had four male children. The first was called Girolamo, the second Bartolomeo, the third Giovanni, who was afterwards my father, and the fourth Francesco. This Andrea Cellini understood a good deal about the architecture of those days, and lived by it as his trade; Giovanni, who was my father, worked at it harder than any of the others. And because, as Vitruvius says among other things, anyone who wants to do that art properly needs to have some music and good draughtsmanship, Giovanni, having made himself a good draughtsman, began to work at music, and along with it learned to play the viol and the flute very well; and being a very studious person, he seldom went out of the house.

They had a next-door neighbour called Stefano Granacci, who had several daughters, all of them very beautiful. As it pleased God, Giovanni saw one of these girls, whose name was Elisabetta; and he liked her so much that he asked for her as a wife. The two fathers knew each other perfectly well from living so close, and the match was easy to make; and each of them felt he had arranged his affairs very well. First the two good old men settled the match, then they began to talk about the dowry, and there was a little affectionate arguing between them, because Andrea said to Stefano: "My son Giovanni is the ablest young man in Florence and in Italy, and if I had wanted to give him a wife before now I should have had the biggest dowries that are given in Florence to people of our sort." And Stefano said: "You have a thousand reasons on your side; but I find myself with five girls, and so many other children, that when I do my sums this is as far as I can stretch." Giovanni had been listening for a while, hidden from them, and came out on them suddenly and said: "Oh, my father — I have wanted that girl and loved her, and not their money. Bad luck to the men who want to make themselves up out of their wife's dowry. Well then: since you have boasted that I am so clever, shall I not be able to keep my wife, and satisfy her needs, on rather less money than you want? Now I tell you plainly: the woman is mine, and I want the dowry to be yours." At this Andrea Cellini was rather put out, being a bit of an odd character; and a few days later Giovanni brought his wife home, and never asked for any dowry at all.

They enjoyed their youth and their holy love for eighteen years, wanting children very badly all that time; then, in those eighteen years, his wife miscarried twice of male children, through the small understanding of the doctors; then she was pregnant again and gave birth to a girl, and they called her Cosa, after my father's mother. Two years later she was pregnant again; and because the cravings pregnant women have are watched very closely, hers were exactly the same as at the birth before, so that they were sure she was going to have a girl like the first one, and had agreed to call her Reparata, to make up my mother's mother. It happened that she gave birth on the night of All Saints, when All Saints' Day had just finished, at half past four, in exactly the year fifteen hundred. The midwife, who knew they were expecting a girl, when she had cleaned the child up and wrapped it in beautiful white cloths, came very quietly to Giovanni my father and said: "I am bringing you a fine present, which you were not expecting." My father, who was a true philosopher, was pacing up and down, and said: "What God gives me is always dear to me"; and uncovering the cloths, he saw with his own eye the unexpected male child. Putting his old palms together, he lifted his eyes to God with them and said: "Lord, I thank you with all my heart; this is very dear to me, and let him be the Welcome one." All the people who were there asked him cheerfully what name he was going to give me, and Giovanni never answered them anything but: "Let him be the Welcome one." And they settled it, and holy Baptism gave me that name, Benvenuto, and that is how I go on living, by the grace of God.

Chapter 4

Andrea Cellini my grandfather was still alive when I was about three years old, and he was past a hundred. One day they had changed a certain pipe on a sink, and a great scorpion had come out of it, which they had not seen, and it had got down from the sink to the floor and gone under a bench. I saw it, and ran to it, and put my hands on it. It was so big that, when I had it in my little hand, its tail stuck out on one side and both its claws stuck out on the other. They say that I ran to my grandfather in great delight, saying: "Look, grandfather, my lovely little crab!" When he saw that it was a scorpion, he nearly dropped dead of fright and of fear for me; and he asked me for it, coaxing me; and the more he coaxed the tighter I squeezed it, crying, because I would not give it to anybody. My father, who was still in the house, ran in at all this shouting, and stood there stupefied, not knowing what remedy to find that would keep the poisonous creature from killing me. Then he happened to catch sight of a pair of small scissors; and so, flattering me, he cut off its tail and its claws. Afterwards, when he was sure the great harm was past, he took it for a good omen.

At about the age of five, my father was in a little room of ours where they had been doing the washing, and a good fire of oak logs had been left; and Giovanni, with a viol in his arms, was playing and singing to himself by the fire. It was very cold. Looking into the fire, he happened to see, in the middle of the strongest flames, a little creature like a lizard, which was enjoying itself in the most vigorous part of the fire. Realising at once what it was, he had my sister and me called, and showed it to us children; and he gave me a great box on the ear, at which I began to cry my heart out. He quieted me pleasantly and said: "My dear little son, I am not giving you that for any wrong you have done, but only so that you will remember that the lizard you see in the fire is a salamander, which has never been seen by anybody else that we have any true report of." And then he kissed me and gave me some small coins.

Chapter 5

My father began to teach me to play the flute and to sing music; and although I was of the tenderest age, when little children usually take pleasure in a whistle and toys of that sort, I disliked it beyond all reckoning, and only played and sang to obey him. In those days my father was making marvellous organs with wooden pipes, and heavy harpsichords, the best and the most beautiful that were to be seen then, and viols, lutes, and harps of the most beautiful and excellent kind. He was an engineer, and for making instruments — such as ways of throwing bridges, ways of building fulling mills, and other machines — he worked wonders; he was the first man to work ivory well. But because he had fallen in love with the woman who became my mother, and he my father — perhaps on account of that little flute of his, which he was playing much more than he ought to have done — he was asked by the Signory's Pipers to play along with them. He kept it up for a while for his own pleasure, and they worked on him so much that they made him one of their fellow pipers. Lorenzo de' Medici and his son Piero, who were very fond of him, saw afterwards that he was giving himself over entirely to the pipe and leaving his fine talent and his fine art behind, and they had him taken out of that post. My father took it very badly, and thought they had done him a great injury. He went straight back to his art, and made a mirror about an arm's length across, of bone and ivory, with figures and foliage, very finely finished and finely designed. The mirror was made in the shape of a wheel: in the middle was the glass; round it were seven roundels, in which the seven Virtues were carved and inlaid in ivory and black bone; and the whole mirror, and the Virtues with it, was on a balance, so that when you turned the wheel all the Virtues moved; and they had a counterweight at their feet that kept them upright. And because he had some knowledge of Latin, he put a Latin verse round the mirror, which said: "Whichever way Fortune's wheel turns, Virtue stays on her feet":

	Rota sum; semper, quoquo me verto
	stat virtus

Not long afterwards his place among the pipers was given back to him. Some of these things happened before I was born, but since I remember them I have not wanted to leave them out. In those days those players were all highly respected craftsmen, and some of them worked in the greater guilds of silk and wool; which is why my father did not think it beneath him to take up that profession. The greatest wish he had in the world, so far as my affairs went, was that I should become a great player; and the greatest displeasure I could have in the world was when he talked to me about it, telling me that if I wanted to, he could see I was so fit for it that I should be the first man in the world.

Chapter 6

As I have said, my father was a great servant of the house of Medici, and a great friend of theirs, and when Piero was driven out he trusted my father with a great many very important things. Afterwards, when the magnificent Piero Soderini came in, and my father was at his post as a player, Soderini heard about my father's marvellous talent and began to use him for very important work as an engineer; and as long as Soderini stayed in Florence he was as fond of my father as anyone in the world could imagine. And in this time my father used to have me carried on somebody's shoulder, being so young, and made me play the flute; and I took the treble part along with the palace musicians in front of the Signory, and I played from the book, and one of the ushers held me on his shoulder. Afterwards the Gonfalonier — that is, Soderini — took great pleasure in making me chatter, and used to give me sweets and say to my father: "Master Giovanni, teach him those other very beautiful arts of yours as well as the playing." To which my father answered: "I do not want him to do any art but playing and composing; because in that profession I hope to make him the greatest man in the world, if God gives him life." At these words one of the old Signori answered, saying to Master Giovanni: "Do what the Gonfalonier tells you; because would he ever be anything but a good player?"

So the time went by until the Medici came back. As soon as the Medici were back, the cardinal, who was afterwards Pope Leo, made a great deal of my father. While they had been away, the balls had been taken off the arms that were on the Medici palace, and a great red cross had been painted there instead, which were the arms and device of the Commune; so that as soon as they came back the red cross was scraped off, and the red balls were set into the shield, and the field made gold, and the whole thing very beautifully done. My father, who had a little natural vein of poetry in him, quite plain, with something prophetic in it as well — and that in him was certainly divine — as soon as the arms were uncovered, made these four lines underneath them, which went like this:

	These arms, which have been buried so long
	under the meek and holy cross,
	now show their face glorious and glad,
	waiting for the sacred mantle of Peter.

This epigram was read all over Florence. A few days later Pope Julius the Second died. The cardinal de' Medici went to Rome, and against everything anybody in the world believed, he was made Pope — Pope Leo the Tenth, generous and great-souled. My father sent him his four prophetic lines. The Pope sent word that he should come there, and it would be good for him. He would not go; and instead of a reward, his place at the palace was taken away from him by Jacopo Salviati, as soon as he was made Gonfalonier. That is what put me into the goldsmith's trade; and part of the time I was learning that art and part of the time playing, very much against my will.

Chapter 7

When he talked to me like that, I used to beg him to let me draw for so many hours of the day, and I would spend all the rest of it playing, only to keep him happy. And to this he would say: "Then you take no pleasure in playing?" And I told him no, because it seemed to me too low an art for what I had in mind. My good father, in despair over it, put me into the shop of the father of the Cavaliere Bandinello, whose name was Michelagnolo, a goldsmith from Pinzi di Monte, and very able at that art; he had no light of family behind him, but was a charcoal burner's son. That is nothing to blame Bandinello for, since he has made a beginning for his own house — if he had come by it in a good way. Whatever it is, I have no call to say anything about him. After I had been there a few days, my father took me away from Michelagnolo, being a man who could not live without seeing me all the time. So I stayed on, unhappily, at my playing, until I was fifteen. If I wanted to describe the great things that happened to me up to that age, and the great dangers to my own life, I should astonish anyone who read them; but so as not to run on too long, and because I have plenty to say, I shall leave them out.

When I got to fifteen, against my father's wish I put myself into a goldsmith's shop, with a man called Antonio di Sandro, a goldsmith, nicknamed Marcone the goldsmith. He was a very good practical hand, and a very decent man, proud and free in everything he did. My father would not let him give me any wages, as they do with other apprentices, so that, since I was taking up the art of my own free will, I could satisfy my appetite for drawing as much as I liked. And I did it very willingly, and that good master of mine took a marvellous pleasure in it. He had a natural son of his own, an only son, whom he often gave the orders to, so as to spare me. My eagerness was so great — or my natural bent, or both of them together — that in a few months I caught up with the good young men in the trade, indeed with the best of them, and began to draw some profit from my work. For all that, I did not fail now and then to please my good father, playing sometimes on the flute and sometimes on the cornet; and every time he heard me I always made the tears fall from him, with great sighs; and very often, out of pity, I would satisfy him, pretending that I was getting a good deal of pleasure out of it myself.

Chapter 8

At this time I had a brother of my own blood, two years younger than me, very bold and very fierce, who afterwards became one of the great soldiers of the school of that marvellous man Signor Giovannino de' Medici, the father of Duke Cosimo. This boy was about fourteen and I was two years older. It was a Sunday, about two hours before dark, between the Porta a San Gallo and the Porta a Pinti, and there he had challenged a fellow of about twenty, with swords in their hands; and he pressed him so bravely that, having wounded him badly, he was going on further. There were a great many people watching, and among them a good number of the other man's relations; and seeing the thing going the wrong way, they got out a lot of slings, and one of the stones caught my poor young brother on the head. He fell to the ground at once, senseless, like a dead man. I happened to be there, without friends and without weapons, and I was shouting at my brother as loudly as I could to pull back, because what he had done to him was enough — and just then it happened that he fell like that, as good as dead. I ran up at once and took his sword, and stood in front of him; and against several swords and a great many stones I never moved away from my brother, until some brave soldiers came from the Porta a San Gallo and got me out of that great fury, marvelling a good deal that so much courage should be in anyone so young. So I carried my brother home like a dead man, and when we got home he came round again with great difficulty.

When he was better, the Eight, who had already condemned our adversaries and banished them for some years, banished us too for six months, outside a radius of ten miles. I said to my brother: "Come with me"; and so we left our poor father, and instead of giving us any sum of money, because he had none, he gave us his blessing. I went off to Siena to find a certain gallant gentleman called Master Francesco Castoro; and because on another occasion, when I had run away from my father, I had gone to this decent man and stayed with him some days, still working at the goldsmith's trade — when I came to him, Francesco recognised me at once and set me to work. Having put me to work, Francesco gave me a house for as long as I stayed in Siena; and I settled my brother and myself in it, and worked away for several months. My brother had made a beginning in Latin letters, but he was so young that he had not yet tasted the flavour of anything worth doing, and was wasting his time.

Book One, Chapters 9 to 14

Chapter 9

About this time the Cardinal de' Medici, who was afterwards Pope Clement, had us called back to Florence at my father's request. A certain pupil of my father's, moved by his own spite, told the Cardinal that he ought to send me to Bologna to learn to play properly under a master who was there, called Antonio, a genuinely able man in that profession of playing. The Cardinal told my father that if he sent me there he would write me letters of recommendation and support. My father, who was dying of eagerness for it, sent me; and I, being keen to see the world, went willingly.

When I got to Bologna I set to work with a man called Master Ercole del Piffero, and began to earn; and meanwhile I went every day for my playing lesson, and in a few weeks I made a great deal of progress in this cursed playing. But I made far more progress in the goldsmith's art, because, having had no help at all from the Cardinal, I went to live in the house of a Bolognese miniature painter called Scipione Cavalletti, in the street of Our Lady of the Baraccan; and there I applied myself to drawing, and to working for a man called Graziadio, a Jew, with whom I earned very well.

At the end of six months I came back to Florence, where that Pierino the piper, who had once been my father's pupil, took it very badly; and I, to please my father, went to see him at his house and played the cornet and the flute with a brother of his called Girolamo, several years younger than Piero, and a very decent, good young man, exactly the opposite of his brother. One day among others my father came to this Piero's house to hear us play; and taking the greatest pleasure in my playing, he said: "All the same I shall make a marvellous player of him, against the wish of the man who tried to stop me." To this Piero answered, and he spoke the truth: "Your Benvenuto will get much more profit and honour if he sticks to the goldsmith's art than out of this piping." My father was so angry at those words, seeing that I was of Piero's opinion too, that he said to him in a great rage: "I knew perfectly well that it was you who blocked this end I so much wanted, and it was you who had me moved out of my place at the Palace, paying me back with the great ingratitude people use as a reward for great favours. I got it given to you, and you had it taken away from me; I taught you to play, with all the arts you know, and you stop my son doing what I want. But keep these prophetic words in mind: it will not be years, I do not say, or months, but a few weeks, before you sink through the floor for this dishonest ingratitude of yours."

To these words Pierino answered and said: "Master Giovanni, most men, when they grow old, go mad along with the old age, as you have done; and I do not wonder at it, because you have given away every scrap of your property with the greatest generosity, without considering that your children were going to need it. Whereas I mean to do the exact opposite: to leave my children so much that they will be able to help yours." To this my father answered: "No bad tree ever made good fruit, and the other way round; and I tell you more: you are a bad man, and your children will be mad and poor, and will come begging from my able and wealthy children." So he left the house, the two of them grumbling wild words at each other.

I, who had taken my good father's side, came out of that house with him and told him I wanted to take revenge for the insults that scoundrel had done him — "on condition that you let me stick to the art of drawing." My father said: "Oh, my dear son, I too have been a good draughtsman; and as a refreshment after such marvellous labours, and for the love of me, who am your father and got you and brought you up and gave you the beginning of so many honourable skills, will you not promise me that, when they are resting, you will take up that flute now and then, and that most wanton cornet, and play it for your own pleasure and delight?" I said yes, and very willingly, for the love of him. Then my good father said that skills of that sort would be the greatest revenge I could take for the injuries received from his enemies.

Not a full month had gone by after these words when that Pierino, who was having a vault put in at a house of his in the Via dello Studio, was one day in his ground-floor room, over the vault they were building, with a good many companions; and it came up in conversation, and he began talking about his master, who had been my father, and repeating the words my father had said to him about sinking through the floor. He had hardly got them out when the room he was in — because the vault had been badly thrown, or else by the true power of God, who does not pay on Saturdays — sank; and the stones of the vault and the bricks came down with him and broke both his legs. The men who were with him, being left on the rim of the vault, came to no harm, but were left stunned and astonished, especially at what he had been saying so scornfully a moment before.

When my father heard of it, he armed himself and went to see him, and in the presence of his father, whose name was Niccolaio da Volterra, trumpeter to the Signory, he said: "Oh, Piero, my dear pupil, I am very sorry for your trouble; but if you remember rightly, it is not long since I warned you of it; and what I told you will happen between your children and mine will happen just the same."

A short while afterwards the ungrateful Piero died of that injury. He left his shameless wife with a son, who some years later came to me in Rome to beg. I gave to him, partly because it is my nature to give alms; and afterwards I remembered with tears the happy state Pierino had been in when my father said those words to him — that Pierino's children would one day go begging from his own able children. And enough has been said about that; and let nobody ever make fun of the forecasts of a decent man when he has been unjustly wronged, because it is not he who is speaking, but the voice of God itself.

Chapter 10

I kept on at the goldsmith's art, and with it I helped my good father. His other son, my brother, called Cecchino — as I said above, he had had him started on Latin letters, because he wanted to make me, the elder, a great player and musician, and him, the younger, a great scholar of law. But nobody can force what nature inclines us to, which made me apply myself to the art of drawing, and my brother, who was of a fine build and full of grace, entirely to arms. And being still very young, he had left a first choice of the school of that most marvellous man Signor Giovannino de' Medici; and coming home, when I was not there, being rather badly supplied with clothes, he found his sisters and mine, who, hidden from my father, gave him a beautiful new cloak and doublet of mine. Beyond the help I was giving my father and my good, honest sisters, I had made myself those respectable clothes out of my own surplus labour. Finding myself cheated and my clothes taken, and not finding my brother, whom I wanted to take them back from, I asked my father why he let so great a wrong be done to me, seeing how willingly I wore myself out to help him. To this he answered that I was his good son, and that he had got back what he had thought lost; and that it was necessary — indeed a commandment of God himself — that whoever had good things should give to whoever had none; and that for love of him I should put up with this injury, and God would increase every good thing to me.

I, being a young man with no experience, answered my poor afflicted father back; and taking a wretched remnant of my clothes and my money, I set off towards one of the city gates. Not knowing which gate would put me on the road to Rome, I found myself at Lucca, and from Lucca at Pisa. When I got to Pisa — I was about sixteen — I stopped near the middle bridge, at the place they call the Fish Stone, at a goldsmith's shop, watching attentively what the master there was doing; and the master asked me who I was and what my trade was. I told him I did a little of the same art he was doing. This decent man told me to come into his shop, and immediately put work in front of me, and said these words: "Your good face makes me believe you are honest and good." So he gave me gold and silver and jewels; and when the first day was over he took me home in the evening to his house, where he lived respectably with a beautiful wife and children.

Remembering the grief my good father might be feeling about me, I wrote to him that I was in the house of a very good and decent man, called Master Ulivieri della Chiostra, and that I was working with him on a great many fine, large pieces; and that he should be easy in his mind, because I was applying myself to learning, and that I hoped with those skills soon to bring him back profit and honour. My good father answered the letter at once, saying this: "My son, the love I bear you is so great that if it were not for the great honour of the family, which I keep above everything, I should have set out at once to come for you; because it truly seems to me that I am without the light of my eyes when I do not see you every day, as I used to. I shall attend to bringing my house through to an honourable name, and you attend to learning your skills; and I only want you to remember these four plain words. Keep them, and never forget them:

	In whatever house you mean to stay,
	live honestly, and do not steal.

Chapter 11

This letter came into the hands of my master Ulivieri, and he read it without my knowing; then he told me he had read it, and said these words: "Well, my Benvenuto, your good face did not deceive me, as a letter from your father which has come into my hands assures me; he must be a very good and decent man. So consider yourself to be in your own house and with your own father."

While I was in Pisa I went to see the Campo Santo, and there I found many beautiful antiquities — marble sarcophagi, that is — and in many other places in Pisa I saw a great many other ancient things, and I worked away at them steadily on all the days I had left over from my work in the shop. And because my master used to come very affectionately to see me in the little room he had given me, and saw that I spent all my hours on things worth doing, he came to love me as if he had been my father. I made great progress in the year I stayed there, and worked in gold and in silver on important and beautiful pieces, which gave me the greatest heart to go further forward.

Meanwhile my father wrote to me very pitifully that I ought to come back to him, and in every letter reminded me that I must not lose that playing which he had taught me with so much labour. At that, the wish to go back to him went out of me at once, I hated this cursed playing so much; and it truly seemed to me that I was in paradise for the whole year I was in Pisa, where I never played once.

At the end of the year my master Ulivieri had occasion to come to Florence to sell certain gold and silver sweepings he had; and because a touch of fever had jumped on me in that filthy air, I came back to Florence with it and with my master. My father made a great fuss of that master of mine, begging him affectionately, without my knowing, to be so kind as not to take me back to Pisa. I stayed ill about two months, and my father very lovingly had me doctored and got me well, telling me continually that a thousand years seemed to have gone by while he waited for me to be well enough to hear me play a little. And while he was talking to me about this playing, holding my fingers on the pulse — because he had some knowledge of medicine and of Latin letters — he felt in that pulse, the moment he began to speak about playing, such a great disturbance that many times he went away from me dismayed and in tears. So that, seeing this great unhappiness of his, I told one of my sisters to bring me a flute; and although I still had the fever continually, since the instrument takes very little effort, playing gave me no disturbance. I played with such a fine command of hand and tongue that when my father came in unexpectedly he blessed me a thousand times, telling me that in the time I had been away from him it seemed to him I had made a great advance; and he begged me to go on, and not to lose so fine a skill.

Chapter 12

When I was well, I went back to my Marcone, the decent man, the goldsmith, who gave me my living; and with what I earned I helped my father and my household.

At this time there came to Florence a sculptor called Piero Torrigiani, who was coming from England, where he had been for many years; and because he was a great friend of that master of mine, he came to him every day. Seeing my drawings and my work, he said: "I have come to Florence to carry off as many young men as I can; because, having a great work to do for my King, I want some of my Florentines to help me. And since your way of working and your drawings are more a sculptor's than a goldsmith's, and I have great works in bronze to do, I shall make you able and rich at the same time." This man was of the most beautiful build, extremely bold, with more the air of a great soldier than of a sculptor, especially in his astonishing gestures and his ringing voice, and a way of knitting his eyebrows fit to frighten any man off anything; and every day he was talking about his exploits with those beasts of Englishmen.

In this connection he happened to fall into talking about Michelangelo Buonarroti, the occasion of it being a drawing of mine copied from a cartoon by the most divine Michelangelo. That cartoon was the first beautiful work in which Michelangelo showed his marvellous powers, and he made it in competition with another man who was making one — with Leonardo da Vinci; and they were to serve for the Council Hall in the Palace of the Signory. They represented the taking of Pisa by the Florentines; and the wonderful Leonardo da Vinci had chosen to show a battle of horsemen with the capture of some standards, done as divinely as anyone could imagine. Michelangelo Buonarroti, in his, showed a quantity of foot soldiers who, it being summer, had gone to bathe in the Arno; and at that instant he shows the alarm being given, and those naked foot soldiers running to arms, with so many fine gestures that no work was ever seen, either of the ancients or of any of the moderns, that reached so high a mark; and, as I have said, the great Leonardo's was most beautiful and wonderful. These two cartoons stood, one in the Medici palace and one in the Pope's hall. As long as they stood there they were the school of the world. Even though the divine Michelangelo made the great chapel of Pope Julius afterwards, he never reached half so high a mark; his powers never afterwards came up to the force of those first studies.

Chapter 13

Now let us come back to Piero Torrigiani, who, with that drawing of mine in his hand, said this: "This Michelangelo Buonarroti and I used to go as boys to learn in the Carmine church, in the chapel of Masaccio; and because Buonarroti had a habit of making fun of everybody who was drawing, one day among others, when he was annoying me, I got much angrier than usual, and clenched my hand and gave him such a punch on the nose that I felt the bone and the gristle of his nose crumble under my fist like a wafer; and marked by me like that he will stay as long as he lives." These words made such a hatred grow in me — because I was constantly seeing the works of the divine Michelangelo — that far from wanting to go off to England with him, I could not bear the sight of him.

I kept on steadily in Florence, learning under Michelangelo's beautiful manner, and I have never come away from it. At this time I formed a very close acquaintance and friendship with a well-bred young man of my own age, who was also with a goldsmith. His name was Francesco, son of Filippo, son of Fra Filippo, that most excellent painter. Going about together bred such love in us that we were never apart, night or day; and since his house was full of the beautiful studies his able father had made — several books of drawings by his own hand, copied from the beautiful antiquities of Rome — seeing them, I fell in love with them. We kept company for about two years.

In this time I made a piece of silver in low relief, about as big as a small child's hand. This piece served as a clasp for a man's belt, which they wore that size in those days. Carved on it was a knot of foliage done in the antique manner, with a number of little boys and other very beautiful masks. I made this piece in the shop of a man called Francesco Salimbene. When it had been seen by the goldsmiths' guild, I was given the credit of being the best young man in that art.

Now a certain Giovanbattista, called Il Tasso, a wood carver, a young man exactly my own age, began to tell me that if I wanted to go to Rome he would willingly come with me — this conversation of ours happened just after dinner — and because I had been angry with my father again over the same business of the playing, I said to Tasso: "You are a man for words and not for deeds." Tasso said: "I have quarrelled with my mother too, and if I had money enough to get me to Rome I would not go back to shut up that little bit of a shop I keep." To this I added that if that was what was stopping him, I had money by me that was enough to carry us both to Rome. Talking together like this as we walked, we found ourselves without noticing it at the Porta San Piero Gattolini. I said to him: "My dear Tasso, it is God's doing that we have come to this gate, when neither you nor I noticed it. Now that I am here, it seems to me I have done half the journey." And so, agreeing together as we went on our way, we said: "Oh, what will our old people say tonight?" Saying which, we made a pact together not to mention them again until we had got to Rome. So we tied our aprons behind us and went almost in silence as far as Siena.

When we got to Siena, Tasso said he had hurt his feet and did not want to come any further, and asked me to lend him money to get back with. I told him: "I should have nothing left to go on with; so you ought to have thought about it before you moved out of Florence. And if it is your feet that stop you coming, we will find a return horse for Rome, and then you will have no excuse for not coming." So I took the horse; and seeing that he did not answer me, I set off on the road towards Rome. When he saw I was set on it, he did not stop grumbling, but came limping along behind, a good way back and slowly, doing the best he could. When I got to the gate, feeling sorry for my little companion, I waited for him and took him up behind me, saying: "What on earth would our friends say about us, if, having set out for Rome, we had not had the courage to get past Siena?" Then the good Tasso said that I was right; and being a cheerful person, he began to laugh and sing; and so, singing and laughing all the way, we came to Rome. I was just nineteen, and so was the century.

As soon as we got to Rome I put myself into a shop with a master called Firenzuola. His name was Giovanni, and he came from Firenzuola in Lombardy, and he was an extremely able man at making vessels and large pieces. When I had shown him something of the model of that belt clasp I had made in Florence with Salimbene, he liked it marvellously, and said these words, turning to a workman he kept, a Florentine called Giannotto Giannotti, who had been with him several years: "This is one of those Florentines who know, and you are one of those who do not." Then I, recognising Giannotto, wanted to speak to him, because before he went to Rome we had often gone drawing together, and we had been on very familiar terms. He took what his master had said to him so badly that he said he did not know me and had no idea who I might be. At which, angry at such words, I said to him: "Oh, Giannotto, once my familiar friend — we have been together in this place and that, drawing, and eating and drinking and sleeping at your house in the country. I do not care whether you bear witness about me to this decent man, your master, because I hope my hands are such that, without your help, they will say what I am."

Chapter 14

When I had finished these words, Firenzuola, who was an extremely bold and spirited person, turned to Giannotto and said to him: "Oh, you low scoundrel, are you not ashamed to use words and manners like that to a man who has been so close a companion to you?" And with the same spirit he turned to me and said: "Come into the shop, and do as you have said, and let your hands say what you are." And he gave me a most beautiful piece of silver to make for a cardinal. It was a small casket copied from the porphyry one that stands in front of the door of the Rotonda. Besides what I copied, I enriched it out of my own head with so many beautiful little masks that my master went about boasting and showing it round the trade, that so well made a piece had come out of his shop. It was about half an arm's length in size, and it was arranged so that it served as a saltcellar to stand on the table.

This was the first money I earned in Rome; and part of that money I sent to help my good father, and the other part I kept for my own living; and with it I went about studying the ancient things, until my money ran out and I had to go back to the shop to work. That Battista del Tasso, my companion, did not stay long in Rome, but went back to Florence.

Having taken on new work, I got the wish, when I had finished it, to change masters, being egged on by a certain Milanese called Master Pagolo Arsago. My first master, Firenzuola, had a great quarrel with this Arsago, saying some insulting words to him in my presence; at which I took up the words in defence of my new master. I said that I had been born free, and free I meant to live; and that he had nothing to complain of in him, still less in me, since he was left owing me a few scudi by our agreement; and that as a free workman I meant to go where I pleased, knowing that I was doing nobody any wrong. My new master also used a good many words, saying that he had not called me, and that I would do him a favour by going back to Firenzuola. To this I added that, not seeing that I was doing him any wrong in any way, and having finished the work I had begun, I meant to belong to myself and not to anyone else; and that whoever wanted me should ask me for myself.

At this Firenzuola said: "I do not want to ask you for yourself any more, and do not you come in front of me again for anything." I reminded him of my money; he jeered at me; to which I said that just as well as I could handle the tools for the pieces he had seen, I could handle a sword no worse to recover what I had worked for. At these words an old man happened to stop, whose name was Master Antonio da San Marino. He was the first and most excellent goldsmith in Rome, and he had been Firenzuola's master. Having heard my case, which I put in a way that could be perfectly well understood, he took my part at once and told Firenzuola to pay me. The disputes were great, because this Firenzuola was a marvellous handler of weapons, a good deal better at that than at the goldsmith's art; but right won its place, and I helped it with the same courage, so that I was paid. And in the course of time Firenzuola and I became friends, and I stood godfather to a son of his, at his own request.

Book One, Chapters 15 to 22

Chapter 15

Going on working with this Master Pagolo Arsago, I earned a great deal, always sending the greater part to my good father. At the end of two years, at my good father's entreaty, I went back to Florence, and set to work again with Francesco Salimbene, with whom I earned very well and wore myself out learning. Taking up again with that Francesco di Filippo, although I was very much given to certain pleasures, on account of that cursed playing, I never let go of certain hours of the day or the night which I gave to my studies.

In this time I made a silver clasp of the kind they used to call a heart-key. It was a belt three fingers wide, of the sort that used to be made for newly married women, and it was worked in half relief with some little figures in the round among the rest. It was made for a man called Raffaello Lapaccini. Although I was very badly paid for it, the honour I drew out of it was worth far more than the price I could justly have got.

Having worked at this time with a good many different people in Florence, where I had come to know some decent men among the goldsmiths — like that Marcone, my first master — others who had the name of being very good men, having been undermined by them in my work as far as they could manage, robbed me grossly. Seeing this, I broke away from them and considered them scoundrels and thieves. One goldsmith among the rest, called Giovanbattista Sogliani, obligingly let me have part of his shop, which was at the corner of the Mercato Nuovo, next to what had been the Landi bank. There I made a number of beautiful little pieces and earned well: I was able to help my household very well indeed. This woke the envy of those bad masters I had had before, who were called Salvadore and Michele Guasconti. They had three big shops in the goldsmiths' trade and did a great deal of business; so that, seeing that they were injuring me, I complained to a decent man about it, saying that the thefts they had used on me under the cloak of their pretended goodness ought to have been enough for them. This came back to their ears, and they boasted that they would make me repent of such words; at which I, not knowing what colour fear was, thought nothing or little of them.

Chapter 16

One day it happened that I was leaning against the shop of one of these men, who called me over and partly scolded me and partly bullied me; to which I answered that if they had done right by me I should have said of them what one says of good and decent men; and that since they had done the opposite, they should complain of themselves and not of me. While I was talking, one of them, called Gherardo Guasconti, their cousin — put up to it, perhaps, by the rest of them together — waited for a load to pass. It was a load of bricks. When the load was opposite me, this Gherardo shoved it into me so hard that it hurt me badly. Turning round at once and seeing him laughing, I fetched him such a punch on the temple that he fell senseless like a dead man; then, turning to his cousins, I said: "That is how one treats thieving cowards of your sort." And when they wanted to make some demonstration, since there were a good many of them, I, being on fire, put my hand to a small knife I had, saying: "Whichever of you comes out of his shop, let the other run for the confessor, because the doctor will have nothing to do here." These words frightened them so much that not one of them moved to help his cousin.

As soon as I had gone, the fathers and the sons ran to the Eight, and there they said that I had attacked them in their own shops with weapons in my hand — a thing that had never been done in Florence before. The Eight had me summoned; and I appeared; and they gave me a great scolding and shouted at me, partly because they saw me in a cloak and them in gown and hood like respectable citizens; and also because my adversaries had been to speak to those gentlemen at home, each one separately, while I, not knowing how these things are done, had spoken to none of them, trusting in the great right I had on my side. And I said that, at the great injury and insult Gherardo had done me, moved by the greatest anger, and having given him nothing but a slap, it did not seem to me I deserved so stiff a reprimand.

Prinzivalle della Stufa, who was one of the Eight, hardly let me finish saying "slap" before he said: "You gave him a punch and not a slap." The little bell was rung and we were all sent outside, and in my defence Prinzivalle said to his colleagues: "Consider, gentlemen, the simplicity of this poor young man, who accuses himself of having given a slap, thinking it is a smaller offence than giving a punch; because for a slap in the Mercato Nuovo the penalty is twenty-five scudi, and for a punch little or nothing. This is a very able young man, and he keeps his poor household by his own labours, and keeps it in plenty; and would to God our city had abundance of that sort, seeing how short of them it is."

Chapter 17

There were among them certain scraggy little hoods who, moved by my adversaries' pleading and their false information, being of the party of Fra Girolamo, would have liked to put me in prison and sentence me by the shovelful; but the good Prinzivalle put a stop to all of it. So he had me fined a small fine of four bushels of flour, to be given as alms to the convent of the Murate. As soon as we were called back in, he ordered me not to speak a word, on pain of their displeasure, and to obey what I had been sentenced to. So, giving me a stiff shouting-at, they sent us to the chancellor; and I, still muttering "It was a slap and not a punch," left the Eight laughing.

The chancellor ordered us, on the magistrate's behalf, to stand surety for each other; and they sentenced me alone to those four bushels of flour. It seemed to me I had been robbed on the highway; and so I sent for a cousin of mine called Master Annibale, a surgeon, the father of Messer Librodoro Librodori, wanting him to give the promise for me. He would not come; at which I, in a rage, blew up like an asp and made a desperate decision. Here you can see how much the stars not only incline us but force us. Knowing what a great obligation this Annibale was under to my house, my anger grew so much that, being carried right over to the bad and being somewhat choleric by nature, I stayed and waited until the said office of the Eight had gone to dinner. Being left there alone, and seeing that none of the Eight's staff was watching me any more, on fire with anger I went out of the Palace and ran to my shop, where I found a good-sized dagger, and jumped into the house of my adversaries, who kept house and shop together.

I found them at table; and that young Gherardo, who had been the head of the quarrel, threw himself on me; at which I struck him a dagger blow in the chest that went through his doublet and his jerkin right through to the shirt, without having touched his flesh or done him any harm in the world. It seemed to me, from the way my hand went in and the noise of the clothes, that I had done him tremendous harm; and he fell to the ground with fright, and I said: "Oh, you traitors, today is the day I kill the lot of you." The father, the mother and the sisters, believing it was Judgement Day, threw themselves on their knees on the ground at once and called out for mercy at the top of their voices; and seeing that they made no defence against me at all, and him stretched on the ground like a dead man, it seemed to me too low a thing to touch them; but I ran furiously down the stairs.

When I got to the street I found the whole rest of the family, more than twelve of them; one of them had an iron shovel, some a great iron bar, others hammers and anvils, others sticks. Getting in among them like a poisoned bull, I threw four or five of them to the ground and fell down with them, always striking with the dagger, now at this one and now at that. Those who were left standing worked as hard as they could, hitting me two-handed with hammers, sticks and anvils; and because God sometimes mercifully steps in, he brought it about that neither they to me nor I to them did any harm in the world. All that was left there was my cap, which my adversaries, having pulled themselves together after running some way off from it, each of them struck with his weapon; and afterwards, looking round among themselves for the wounded and the dead, there was not one of them who had any hurt.

Chapter 18

I went off towards Santa Maria Novella, and ran straight into Fra Alesso Strozzi, whom I did not know; and I commended myself to this good friar for the love of God, to save my life, because I had done a great wrong. The good friar told me not to be afraid of anything, because whatever evils in the world I had done, in that little cell of his I was perfectly safe.

An hour later the Eight, meeting outside their usual order, had one of the most terrifying proclamations issued against me that anyone ever heard, with the heaviest penalties for whoever had me or knew where I was, without regard to the place or the quality of the person keeping me. My afflicted, poor, good father went in to the Eight and threw himself on his knees on the ground, asking mercy for his poor young son; at which one of those hot-headed men, shaking the crest of his scraggy hood and standing up, said to my poor father with some insulting words: "Get up from there, and get out at once, because tomorrow morning we shall send him out to your farm with the lances." My poor father, still bold, answered them, saying: "You will do what God has ordained, and no more than that." To which the same man answered that God had certainly ordained it. And my father said to him: "I take comfort in the fact that you certainly do not know it."

Leaving them, he came to find me, along with a young man of my own age called Piero di Giovanni Landi; we loved each other more than if we had been brothers. This young man had a marvellous sword under his cloak and a beautiful coat of mail; and when they came to me my spirited father told me what had happened, and what the lords of the Eight had said to him. Then he kissed me on the forehead and on both eyes; he blessed me from his heart, saying: "May the power of God be what helps you"; and handing me the sword and the armour, he helped me put them on with his own hands. Then he said: "Oh, my good son, with these in your hand, either you live or you die." Pier Landi, who was standing there, never stopped weeping; and when he had handed me ten gold scudi, I asked him to take off certain little hairs of my beard, which were the first down.

Fra Alesso dressed me as a friar and gave me a lay brother for company. Going out of the convent and out by the Prato gate, I went along the walls as far as the Piazza San Gallo; and climbing the slope of Montui, in one of the first houses I found a man called Il Grassuccio, own brother to Messer Benedetto da Montevarchi. I unfriared myself at once, and having turned back into a man, we mounted two horses that were there for us and went off that night to Siena. I sent Grassuccio back to Florence, and he greeted my father and told him I had got safely away.

My father was overjoyed, and thought a thousand years had gone by before he could find that man of the Eight who had insulted him; and when he found him he said: "You see, Antonio, that it was God who knew what was going to happen to my son, and not you?" To which he answered: "Let him come back here another time." My father to him: "I shall attend to thanking God, who has saved him from this."

Chapter 19

Being in Siena, I waited for the Rome courier and joined up with him. When we had passed the Paglia we met the messenger who was carrying the news of the new Pope, who was Pope Clement.

When I got to Rome I set to work in the shop of Master Santi the goldsmith. He was dead, but a son of his kept the shop. This man did not work himself, but had all the business of the shop done by a young man called Lucagnolo da Iesi. He was a countryman, and had come as a small boy to work with Master Santi. He was short, but very well built. This young man worked better than any man I had seen up to that time, with the greatest ease and with a great deal of design; he worked only in large pieces, that is, very beautiful vases and basins and things of that sort.

Having set myself to work in that shop, I undertook to make certain candlesticks for the Spanish bishop of Salamanca. These candlesticks were richly worked, as far as such a piece allows. A pupil of Raphael of Urbino, called Gianfrancesco, nicknamed Il Fattore, was a very able painter; and because he was a friend of the bishop, he put me very much in his favour, so that I had a great many pieces to do for this bishop, and earned very well.

At this time I went sometimes to draw in Michelangelo Buonarroti's chapel, and sometimes to the house of Agostino Chigi of Siena, in which house there were many beautiful paintings by the hand of that most excellent man Raphael of Urbino; and this was on holidays, because Messer Gismondo Chigi, brother of Messer Agostino, lived in that house. They were very proud when they saw young men like me coming to learn inside their houses. Messer Gismondo's wife, having seen me often in this house of hers — this lady was as well bred as could be and beautiful beyond measure — came up to me one day, looked at my drawings, and asked me whether I was a sculptor or a painter. I told the lady I was a goldsmith. She said I drew far too well for a goldsmith; and having a lily of the most beautiful diamonds set in gold brought to her by one of her waiting women, she showed it to me and wanted me to value it. I valued it at eight hundred scudi. Then she said I had valued it very well.

Then she asked me whether I had the confidence to set them well. I said very willingly; and in front of her I made a small drawing of it, and I made it all the better because I was enjoying spending time with this most beautiful and pleasant gentlewoman. When the drawing was finished, another very beautiful Roman lady came up, who had been upstairs and came down and asked Madonna Porzia what she was doing there; and she said, smiling: "I am amusing myself watching this good young man draw, who is both good and handsome." I, having got a little bold, but with a little honest shame mixed in, went red and said: "Whatever I am, madam, I shall always be entirely at your service." The gentlewoman, also blushing a little, said: "You know very well that I want you to serve me"; and handing me the lily, she said I should take it away with me. And more than that, she gave me twenty gold scudi that she had in her pocket, and said: "Set it for me in the way you have drawn it for me, and save me this old gold it is set in now." The Roman lady then said: "If I were that young man, I should be off with a good conscience." Madonna Porzia added that skill is rarely found with vice, and that if I did such a thing I should badly deceive that good look of an honest man that I showed. And turning, taking the Roman lady by the hand, she said to me with the pleasantest laugh: "Goodbye, Benvenuto."

I stayed a while longer over the drawing I was making, copying a certain figure of Jove by the hand of Raphael of Urbino. When I had finished it I left, and set myself to make a small wax model, showing by it how the finished piece would come out; and when I took it to show to Madonna Porzia, that Roman lady I spoke of being present, both of them were greatly satisfied with my work and did me so much honour that, moved by a little boldness, I promised them the piece would be half as good again as the model. So I set to work, and in twelve days I finished the jewel in the form of a lily, as I said above, ornamented with little masks, children and animals, and beautifully enamelled; so that the diamonds the lily was made of were improved more than half.

Chapter 20

While I was working on this piece, that able man Lucagnolo, whom I spoke of above, showed that he took it very badly, telling me more than once that I should make far more profit and more honour by helping him work large silver vases, as I had begun to do. To which I said that I would be able, whenever I wanted, to work on large silver vases; but that pieces like the one I was making did not come along every day; and that in such pieces there was no less honour than in large silver vases, and a great deal more profit.

This Lucagnolo laughed at me, saying: "You will see, Benvenuto; because by the time you have finished that piece of yours, I shall have hurried up and finished this vase, which I began when you began the jewel; and experience will show you what profit I shall get out of my vase and what you will get out of your jewel." To which I answered that I was very glad to make such a trial with so able a man as he was, because at the end of the pieces it would be seen which of us was deceived. And so both of us, with a little scornful laugh, bent our heads fiercely, each eager to finish what he had begun; so that in about ten days each of us had finished his piece with great polish and art.

Lucagnolo's was a very large vase which was used on Pope Clement's table, where he threw the little bones of meat and the peel of various fruits while he was at his meal; made more for show than for necessity. This vase was ornamented with two beautiful handles, with many little and large masks, and with much beautiful foliage, of as much beauty and grace of design as anyone could imagine; to which I said that it was the most beautiful vase I had ever seen. At this Lucagnolo, thinking he had convinced me, said: "Your piece seems no less beautiful to me, but we shall soon see the difference between the one and the other."

So he took his vase and carried it to the Pope, who was very well satisfied, and had it paid for at once at the usual rate of the trade for such large work. Meanwhile I carried my piece to the gentlewoman, Madonna Porzia, who told me with great astonishment that I had gone far beyond the promise I had made her; and then she added that I should ask whatever I liked for my labour, because it seemed to her that I deserved so much that if she gave me a castle she would hardly feel she had satisfied me; but since she could not do that, she said, laughing, that I should ask for what she could do. To which I said that the greatest reward I had wanted for my labour was to have satisfied her ladyship. And so, laughing as well, and having made her a bow, I left, saying that I wanted no other reward than that.

Then Madonna Porzia turned to that Roman lady and said: "Do you see that the qualities we judged were in him are these, and not the vices?" Both of them wondered at it, and Madonna Porzia said: "My Benvenuto, have you ever heard it said that when the poor man gives to the rich, the devil laughs at it?" To which I said: "And so, out of all his troubles, this time I want to see him laugh." And as I went off she said that this time she did not want to do him that favour.

When I got back to my shop, Lucagnolo had the money he had had for his vase in a paper twist; and when I came in he said: "Come and compare the price of your jewel here alongside the price of my vase." To which I said that he should keep it that way until the next day, because I hoped that, just as my piece in its own kind had been no less beautiful than his, so I expected to let him see the price of it.

Chapter 21

The next day came, and Madonna Porzia sent her steward to my shop, called me outside, and handed me a paper twist full of money on that lady's behalf, saying that she did not want the devil laughing at it altogether; giving me to understand that what she was sending me was not the full payment my labour deserved, with a great many other courteous words worthy of such a lady.

Lucagnolo, who thought a thousand years had gone by before he could put his twist next to mine, as soon as I came into the shop, in front of twelve workmen and other neighbours who had come up wanting to see the end of the contest, Lucagnolo took his twist with a scornful laugh, saying "Ho! Ho!" three or four times, and poured his money onto the counter with a great noise. There were twenty-five scudi in giulios, and he thought mine would be four or five scudi in small change. As for me, deafened by his shouting and by the looks and the laughter of the people standing round, after glancing a little way into my own twist and seeing that it was all gold, I stood at one end of the counter with my eyes down, and without a sound in the world lifted my twist high up in both hands and let it pour out like a mill hopper. My money was half as much again as his; so that all those eyes, which had been fixed on me with some scorn, turned at once to him and said: "Lucagnolo, Benvenuto's money, being gold, and being half as much again, makes a much finer show than yours."

I quite believed that out of envy, together with the shame Lucagnolo felt, he would drop dead on the spot; and although a third of that money of mine came to him, since I was the workman — for that is the custom: two thirds go to the workman and the other third to the masters of the shop — reckless envy had more power in him than greed, which ought to have worked entirely the other way, this Lucagnolo being the son of a peasant from Iesi. He cursed his art and the men who had taught it to him, saying that from then on he did not want to do that heavy work any more; he only wanted to attend to making those little bits of tomfoolery, since they were paid so well.

No less angry, I said that every bird sang its own song; that he was talking according to the caves he had come out of; but that I told him plainly, I should manage his rubbish perfectly well, and he would never manage that sort of tomfoolery. And going off in a rage, I told him I would soon let him see it. The people who were there told him out loud that he was in the wrong, holding him for the boor he was, and me for a man, as I had shown myself.

Chapter 22

The next day I went to thank Madonna Porzia, and told her that her ladyship had done the opposite of what she had said: that when I wanted to make the devil laugh, she had made him deny God again. We both laughed pleasantly, and she gave me other beautiful and good pieces to do.

In the meantime I arranged, through a pupil of Raphael of Urbino the painter, for the bishop of Salamanca to give me a large water vessel to make, called an ewer, of the kind that are kept on sideboards for ornament. And since the bishop wanted two of them made of equal size, he gave one to Lucagnolo to make and I had the other; and that painter Gianfrancesco gave us the design for the mouldings of the vessels.

So I set my hand to my vessel with a marvellous appetite, and was accommodated with a small part of a shop by a Milanese called Master Giovanpiero della Tacca. Having got myself in order, I reckoned up the money I might need for my own affairs, and sent all the rest to help my poor good father. While it was being paid to him in Florence, he happened to run into one of those hot-headed men who were of the Eight at the time I made that little bit of disorder, and who, insulting him, had told him that he would send me out to the farm with lances whatever happened. And because that hot-head had certain thoroughly bad sons, my father said, to the point: "Misfortunes can happen to anybody, and above all to hot-tempered men when they are in the right, as happened to my son; but let us look at the rest of his life, and see how well I have known how to bring him up. Please God, for your own sake, that your sons do you neither worse nor better than mine do to me; because just as God has made me able to bring them up, so, where my own powers could not reach, he himself has saved them for me, against your belief, out of your violent hands."

And going off, he wrote me all of this, begging me for the love of God to play sometimes, so as not to lose that fine skill which he had taught me with so much labour. The letter was full of the most loving fatherly words anyone could ever hear; so that they moved me to tears of pity, and I wanted to give him a good measure of contentment about the playing before he died — as God grants us all the lawful favours we faithfully ask him for.

Book One, Chapters 23 to 27

Chapter 23

While I was pressing on with the beautiful Salamanca vessel, I had only one boy to help me, whom I had taken on as an errand boy, half against my will, at the pressing request of friends. This boy was about fourteen; his name was Paulino and he was the son of a Roman citizen who lived on his own income. This Paulino was the best mannered, the most honest and the handsomest boy I ever saw in my life; and for his decent ways and manners, and for his endless beauty, and for the great love he bore me, it came about that I set as much love on him as can be shut up in a man's breast.

This passionate love was the reason why, so as to see that marvellous face clear itself more often — for by its nature it looked serious and melancholy — as soon as I took up my cornet, it broke at once into a smile so honest and so beautiful that I do not wonder in the least at the nonsense the Greeks write about the gods of heaven. If he had been alive in those days, he would perhaps have taken them further off their hinges still.

This Paulino had a sister called Faustina, and I do not believe the Faustina the ancient books make so much noise about was ever so beautiful. Having taken me sometimes to his vineyard, and as far as I could judge, it seemed to me that this decent man, Paulino's father, would have liked to make me his son-in-law. This made me play a great deal more than I had done before.

It happened at this time that a certain Gianiacomo, a piper from Cesena who was with the Pope, a marvellous player, let me know, through Lorenzo the trombonist of Lucca, who is today in the service of our Duke, that they wanted to know whether I would help them at the Pope's Feast of the Assumption, and play the treble on my cornet that day in several motets they had chosen, very beautiful ones. Although I had the greatest wish to finish that beautiful vessel of mine that I had begun, since music is a marvellous thing in itself, and in order partly to satisfy my old father, I was content to keep them company. And for eight days before the Feast we practised together two hours a day, so that on the day of the Assumption we went up to the Belvedere, and while Pope Clement was at dinner we played those well-drilled motets in such a way that the Pope said he had never heard music played more sweetly or better together.

He called Gianiacomo to him and asked him where and how he had come by so good a cornet for the treble, and asked him particularly who I was. Gianiacomo told him my name exactly. At this the Pope said: "Then this is Master Giovanni's son?" He said I was. The Pope said he wanted me in his service among the other musicians. Gianiacomo answered: "Most Blessed Father, I do not undertake that you will get him, because the profession he attends to continually is the goldsmith's art, and he works marvellously in it, and draws far better earnings from it than he would from playing." At this the Pope said: "I want him all the more, having this skill in him besides, which I had not expected. Have the same salary made out for him as for the rest of you; and tell him from me to serve me, and that as time goes on I shall give him plenty to do in the other profession as well." And putting out his hand, he gave him a hundred gold scudi of the Chamber in a handkerchief, and said: "Divide them so that he gets his share."

Gianiacomo, having got away from the Pope, came to us and told us exactly everything the Pope had said to him; and having divided the money among the eight companions that we were, he gave me my share and said: "Now I am going to have you entered on the roll of our company." To which I said: "Let today pass, and I shall answer you tomorrow."

When I had left them, I went along thinking whether I ought to accept such a thing, considering how much it would harm me by turning me aside from the fine studies of my art. The following night my father appeared to me in a dream, and with the most loving tears begged me, for the love of God and of himself, to be content to take up that undertaking; and I thought I answered him that I would not do it in any way. Immediately it seemed to me that he frightened me in a horrible shape and said: "If you do not do it, you will have your father's curse, and if you do it, may you be blessed by me for ever." Waking up, I ran out of fear to have myself entered; then I wrote it to my old father, who out of too much joy had a seizure that brought him close to death; and he wrote at once to say that he too had dreamed almost the same thing that I had.

Chapter 24

It seemed to me, having satisfied my good father's honest wish, that everything ought to come out for me to an honourable and glorious end. So I set to with the greatest urgency to finish the vessel I had begun for Salamanca. This bishop was a very remarkable man, extremely rich, but hard to satisfy: he sent every day to see what I was doing; and any time his messenger did not find me there, Salamanca flew into the greatest fury, saying he meant to have the piece taken away from me and given to somebody else to finish. This was caused by my serving that cursed playing.

Still, with the greatest urgency I had set myself to it night and day, so that when I had brought it to a point where it could be shown to the bishop, I let him see it; at which such a desire grew in him to see it finished that I repented of having shown it to him. In three months I had finished the piece, with as many beautiful little animals, leaves and masks as anyone could imagine.

I sent it at once by my boy Paulino to show to that able man Lucagnolo, mentioned above; and Paulino, with that endless grace and beauty of his, said this: "Messer Lucagnolo, Benvenuto says he sends you his promises and your rubbish, and waits to see your tomfoolery from you." When the words had been said, Lucagnolo took the vessel in his hand and looked at it a long while; then he said to Paulino: "Fine child, tell your master that he is a very able man, and that I beg him to have me for a friend, and let us not go into anything else." That honest and admirable young man brought me the message with the greatest delight.

The vessel was carried to Salamanca, who wanted it valued. This Lucagnolo took part in the valuation, and valued it and praised it so honourably, far beyond what I had expected. Salamanca, taking the vessel, said in his Spanish way: "I swear to God that I shall take as long to pay for it as he has taken to make it." Hearing this, I was left thoroughly unhappy, cursing the whole of Spain and everyone who wished it well.

Among the other beautiful ornaments this vessel had a handle all in one piece, most delicately worked, which by means of a certain spring stood upright over the mouth of the vessel.
