One day, when monsignor was showing this vessel of mine to certain Spanish gentlemen of his out of vanity, it happened that one of these gentlemen, after monsignor had gone, handling the beautiful handle far too roughly, and the delicate spring not being able to resist his boorish strength, broke it in his hand; and thinking he had done very badly, he begged the butler who had charge of it to take it quickly to the master who had made it, who should mend it at once, and to promise him whatever price he asked, provided it was mended quickly.

So the vessel came into my hands, and I promised to mend it very quickly, and I did. It was brought to me before dinner; at two hours before dark the man who had brought it came back, all in a sweat, because he had run the whole way, seeing that monsignor had asked for it again to show it to certain other lords. So this butler would not let me get a word out, saying: "Quick, quick, bring the vessel." At which I, wanting to take my time and not give it to him, said that I did not want to be quick. The servant flew into such a fury that, making as if to put one hand to his sword, with the other he made a show of forcing his way into the shop; which I immediately prevented with my weapons, together with a good many bold words, saying: "I will not give it to you; and go and tell monsignor your master that I want the money for my labour before it leaves this shop."

Seeing that he had not been able to get it by bullying, he set to begging me as one begs the Cross, telling me that if I gave it to him he would do so much for me that I should be paid. These words did not move me from my purpose at all, and I always told him the same thing. In the end, in despair of the business, he swore he would come back with so many Spaniards that they would cut me to pieces; and he went off at a run. Meanwhile I, believing some part of these murderous threats of theirs, promised myself to defend myself boldly; and having got ready a marvellous gun of mine that I used for shooting, I said to myself: "If a man takes my property and my labour with it, may he still be granted his life?" In the middle of this argument with myself, a number of Spaniards appeared together with their steward, who in their reckless way told the crowd of them to go in and take the vessel and give me a beating.

At these words I showed them the mouth of the gun, ready with its fire, and shouted out loud: "Traitors, renegades, is this how you murder houses and shops in a Rome like this? As many of you thieves as come near this door, so many will I bring down dead with this piece of mine." And turning the mouth of the gun on their steward, and making as if to fire, I said: "And you, thief, who let them do it — I mean you to be the first to die." He immediately put his heel into a jennet he was on and made off at full gallop.

At this great noise all the neighbours had come out; and some Roman gentlemen who were passing said as well: "Kill the renegades, and we will help you." These words had so much force that they went off badly frightened by me; so that, driven by the case, they were forced to tell the whole story to monsignor, who was extremely proud, and who shouted at all his servants and officers, both because they had come to commit such an outrage, and because, having begun it, they had not finished it.

Just then that painter who had been mixed up in the business came by, and monsignor told him to come and tell me on his behalf that if I did not bring him the vessel at once, the biggest piece left of me would be my ears; and that if I did bring it he would pay me for it on the spot. This did not frighten me in the least, and I let him know that I would go and tell the Pope about it straight away. In the meantime his temper cooled and so did my fear; and on the word of certain great Roman gentlemen that he would not hurt me, and with a good guarantee of payment for my labour, I got ready with a big dagger and my good coat of mail and went to monsignor's house, where he had got his whole household drawn up. When I went in I had my Paulino behind me with the silver vessel. It was neither more nor less than going through the middle of the Zodiac: one of them was acting the lion, another the scorpion, another the crab; until at last we came before this ugly priest, who let fly the most priestly, most thoroughly Spanish words that anyone could imagine. And I never lifted my head to look at him, nor answered him a word.

At this his temper seemed to grow; and having writing materials handed to me, he told me to write in my own hand that I was well satisfied and paid by him. At this I lifted my head and told him I would do it very willingly if I had my money first. The bishop's rage grew, and the bullying and the arguments were great. In the end I had the money first, and afterwards I wrote, and went off happy and content.

Chapter 25

Afterwards Pope Clement heard of it — he had seen the vessel before, but had not been told it was by my hand — and he took the greatest pleasure in it, and praised me a great deal, and said in public that he had a very great liking for me. So that monsignor Salamanca greatly repented of having bullied me like that; and to make it up with me he sent word by the same painter that he wanted to give me a great many large pieces to do; to which I said I would do them very willingly, but that I wanted payment for them before I began.

These words too came to the ears of Pope Clement, and they moved him greatly to laughter. Cardinal Cibo was there, and the Pope told him the whole quarrel I had had with this bishop; then he turned to one of his officers and ordered him to keep giving me work for the palace continually. Cardinal Cibo sent for me, and after a good deal of pleasant talk gave me a large vessel to make, bigger than Salamanca's; and so did Cardinal Cornaro, and many others of those cardinals, above all Ridolfi and Salviati. I had work from all of them, so that I earned very well.

Madonna Porzia, mentioned above, told me I ought to open a shop that was entirely my own; and I did so, and never stopped working for that good, well-bred lady, who gave me a very great deal of earnings, and it was almost by her doing that I showed myself to the world as a man worth something.

I formed a great friendship with Signor Gabbriello Ceserino, who was gonfalonier of Rome; I made a great many pieces for this gentleman. One of them, among the rest, was notable: a large gold medal to be worn in a hat, and carved on that medal was Leda with her swan. Being very well satisfied with my work, he said he wanted to have it valued so as to pay me the right price. And because the medal was made with great discipline, the valuers of the trade valued it a great deal higher than he had imagined; so, keeping the medal in his own hands, I got nothing for my labour. The same thing happened with this medal as with Salamanca's vessel. And because these matters must not take up the room where I have things of greater importance to say, I shall pass over them briefly.

Chapter 26

Although I am going somewhat outside my profession, since I mean to describe my life some of these things force themselves on me — not to be described in detail, but at least to be touched on briefly.

One morning of our Saint John's day, I was dining together with a good many of our nation, of various professions, painters, sculptors, goldsmiths; among the notable men there were one called Il Rosso, a painter, and Gianfrancesco, a pupil of Raphael of Urbino, and many others. And because I had brought them there freely, they were all laughing and joking, as a number of men together will, enjoying so marvellous a feast day. A brainless young man happened to pass by, a swaggerer, a soldier of Signor Rienzo da Ceri, and hearing this noise he jeered and said a great many insulting things about the Florentine nation. I, being the host of all these able and decent men, and feeling that I was the one insulted, quietly, without anybody seeing me, came up on this fellow, who was together with a whore of his, and who, to make her laugh, was still going on with that jeering. When I got to him, I asked him whether he was the bold one who was speaking ill of the Florentines. He said at once: "I am the man." At these words I raised my hand, hitting him in the face, and said: "And I am this man." At once we both put our hands boldly to our weapons; but no sooner had the brawl begun than a good many people came in between, taking my side rather than otherwise, having heard and seen that I was in the right.

The next day a written challenge was brought me to fight him, which I accepted very cheerfully, saying that this seemed to me a business I could get through a great deal faster than the ones in that other art of mine; and I went at once to speak to an old man called Il Bevilacqua, who had the name of having been the first sword in Italy, because he had been shut into the lists more than twenty times and had always come out of it with honour. This decent man was a great friend of mine, and knew me through my skill in my art, and had also been mixed up in certain terrible quarrels between me and other people. So he cheerfully said to me at once: "My Benvenuto, if you had Mars himself to deal with, I am sure you would come out of it with honour, because in all the years I have known you I have never seen you take up any quarrel wrongly." So he took my business on, and having brought us to the ground with our weapons in our hands, without any blood being shed, my adversary giving way, I came out of the affair with much honour. I shall not tell the other particulars, because although they would be very fine to hear in that line, I want to keep these words for talking about my art, which is what has moved me to write this; and in that I shall have far too much to say.

Even though I was moved by an honest envy, wanting to make some other work that would come up to and even go past those of that able man Lucagnolo, I never for that moved away from my own beautiful art of jewel setting; so that between the one and the other I brought myself great profit and greater honour, and in both of them I was continually making things unlike other people's.

At this time there was in Rome an extremely able man from Perugia named Lautizio, who worked in one profession only, and in that he was unique in the world. It happens that at Rome every cardinal keeps a seal, on which his title is stamped; these seals are made as big as the whole hand of a small boy of about twelve; and, as I have said above, the cardinal's title is carved on it, with a great many figures. One of these seals, well made, is paid a hundred scudi and more. I felt an honest envy of this able man too, although this art is very much apart from the other arts that belong to the goldsmith's trade; because this Lautizio, making this art of seals, could do nothing else. I set myself to study that art too, and although I found it extremely difficult, I never got tired of the labour it gave me, and kept on continually earning and learning.

There was also in Rome another most excellent and able man, a Milanese called Messer Caradosso. This man worked only in little chased medals made of plate, and many other things; he made some Peaces worked in half relief, and certain Christs a palm high, made of the thinnest sheets of gold, so well worked that I judged him to be the greatest master of such things I had ever seen, and I envied him more than anybody else.

There were also other masters who worked at medals cut in steel, which are the mother and the true guide of anyone who wants to know how to make coins really well. I set myself to learn all these different professions with the greatest study.

There is also the most beautiful art of enamelling, which I never saw anybody do well except a Florentine of ours called Amerigo, whom I did not know, though I knew his most marvellous works well; and I never saw anywhere in the world, or by any man, anything that came within a long way of that divine quality. To this exercise too — most difficult of all, because of the fire, which comes in at the very end of the finished, laborious work, and often ruins it and sends it to wreck — to this different profession too I set myself with all my power; and although I found it very difficult, the pleasure I took in it was so great that those great difficulties seemed to me a rest. And this came from an express gift lent me by the God of nature: a constitution so good and so well proportioned that I freely promised myself I could make it do whatever came into my mind to do. These professions I have named are numerous and very different from one another; so that a man who does one of them well, if he wants to do the others, hardly ever succeeds in them as he does in the one he does well; whereas I have used all my power to work at all these professions equally, and I shall show in its place that I have done it, as I say.

Chapter 27

At this time, when I was still a young man of about twenty-three, there broke out a plague so beyond all reckoning that in Rome many thousands were dying of it every day. Being somewhat frightened by this, I began to take certain pleasures, as my mind dictated, and for another reason too, which I shall tell.

On feast days I liked going to the ancient ruins, copying them, now in wax and now in drawing; and because these ruins are all broken down, and a great many pigeons nest among them, I got the wish to use my gun on them: so that, to keep away from company, being frightened of the plague, I would put a gun on my Paulino's shoulder and the two of us alone would go off to the ruins. Which meant that very often I came back loaded with the fattest pigeons.

I never liked to put anything into my gun but a single ball, and so, by the true skill of that art, I made great bags. I kept a gun that was straight, made by my own hand; and inside and out there was never a mirror like it. I also made with my own hand the finest powder for shooting, in which I discovered the best secrets that anybody has found from that day to this; and of this, so as not to go on at length, I shall give one sign to astonish anyone who is expert in the profession. It is this: with a weight of my powder that was a fifth of the weight of the ball, the ball would carry two hundred paces at point blank.

Although the great pleasure I got out of this gun looked as if it were leading me away from my art and my studies — and though that was the truth — in another way it gave me back much more than it had taken from me; the reason being that every time I went out on this sport of mine my health greatly improved, because the air agreed with me strongly. Being melancholy by nature, as soon as I got into these pleasures my heart lifted at once, and I worked better and with far more power than when I stayed continually at my studies and my exercises; so that in the end the gun stood more to my profit than to my loss.

And through this pleasure of mine I made friends with certain searchers who kept watch on some Lombard peasants who came to Rome in their season to dig the vineyards. These men, digging the ground, were always finding ancient medals, agates, prases, cornelians, cameos; and they also found gems, such as emeralds, sapphires, diamonds and rubies. These searchers sometimes got such things from the peasants for very little money; and I, coming on the searchers sometimes — and very often — would give them as many gold scudi for what they had barely bought for as many giulios. This, quite apart from the great profit I got out of it, which was tenfold or more, also made me welcome to almost all the cardinals in Rome.

I shall only tell of one or two of the notable and rarer of these things. Among many others, there came into my hands the head of a dolphin about as big as a good-sized broad bean. Besides the fact that this head was extremely beautiful, nature here went far beyond the art; because this emerald was of so good a colour that the man who bought it from me for tens of scudi had it made up as an ordinary stone to wear in a ring, and set like that he sold it for hundreds.

Another kind of stone: this was a head in the most beautiful topaz that was ever seen in the world, and in this the art came up to nature. It was as big as a large hazelnut, and the head was as well made as anyone could imagine: it was a Minerva.

Then another stone, different from these: this was a cameo, with a Hercules cut on it binding the three-throated Cerberus. This was so beautiful and made with such skill that our great Michelangelo said he had never seen anything so marvellous.

Among many bronze medals, one came into my hands with the head of Jove on it. This medal was bigger than any I had ever seen: the head was so well made that no such medal was ever seen. It had a beautiful reverse with some little figures on it, made as well as the head.

I could say a great many great things about this, but I do not want to go on at length.

Book One, Chapters 28 to 31

Chapter 28

As I said above, the plague had begun in Rome. Although I want to go back a little way, I shall not for that go outside my subject.

There came to Rome a very great surgeon called Master Jacomo da Carpi. This able man, among his other treatments, undertook certain desperate cures of the French disease. And because these diseases in Rome are great friends of the priests, and above all of the richest of them, this able man, having made himself known, gave out that by means of certain fumigations he cured such complaints marvellously; but he wanted the bargain made before he began the cure, and the bargains were in hundreds and not in tens.

This able man had a good deal of understanding of design. Passing my shop by chance one day, he happened to see certain drawings I had in front of me, among them a number of odd little vases I had designed for my own pleasure. These vases were very different and unlike any that had ever been seen up to that time. Master Jacomo wanted me to make them for him in silver; which I did with the greatest willingness, since they were according to my own fancy. Although this able man paid me very well for them, the honour they brought me was a hundred times greater; because in the trade of those able goldsmiths they said they had never seen anything more beautiful or better carried out.

I had hardly finished them when this man showed them to the Pope; and the day after he went off with God. He was very learned, and spoke marvellously about medicine. The Pope wanted him to stay in his service; and this man said he did not want to be in anybody's service in the world, and that whoever needed him should go after him. He was a very cunning person, and he did wisely to get out of Rome; because not many months later all the people he had treated became so much worse that they were a hundred times worse than before: he would have been killed if he had stayed.

He showed my little vases to many lords; among them to the most excellent Duke of Ferrara; and he said he had got them from a great lord in Rome, telling that lord that if he wanted to be cured of his complaint, he wanted those two vases; and that the lord had told him they were antiques, and begged him to ask for anything else, which he would not find it hard to give him, as long as he left him those; and he said he had made a show of not wanting to treat him, and so he got them. Messer Alberto Bendedio told me this in Ferrara, and with a great air of importance showed me some clay copies of them; at which I laughed; and when I said nothing else, Messer Alberto Bendedio, who was a proud man, said to me in a rage: "So you laugh at them, eh? And I tell you that in a thousand years there has not been born a man who could so much as copy them." And I, so as not to take that reputation away from them, kept quiet and admired them in astonishment.

Many gentlemen in Rome spoke to me about this work, saying it seemed miraculous and antique to them; some of them were friends of mine; and I, being bold about the business, confessed that I had made them. When they would not believe it, and I wanted to stay truthful with them, I had to give proof of it by making new drawings; because the old ones were not enough, since Master Jacomo had cunningly carried them off with him. In this small piece of work I gained a great deal.

Chapter 29

The plague going on for many months afterwards, I had come through the skirmish, because a good many of my companions had died and I had been left healthy and free.

One evening among others a confederate companion of mine brought home to supper a Bolognese woman of the town called Faustina. This woman was very beautiful, but she was about thirty, and she had with her a little servant girl of thirteen or fourteen. Since Faustina belonged to my friend, I would not have touched her for all the gold in the world. And although she said she was strongly in love with me, I kept faith constantly with my friend; but once they had gone to bed I stole that little servant, who was quite new to it — and woe to her if her mistress had known. So I passed that night pleasantly, and with far more satisfaction to me than I should have had with the mistress Faustina.

Dinner time was coming on, and I, being tired, having walked a good many miles, wanted to eat; and a great pain took me in the head, with a number of swellings in my left arm, and a carbuncle showing itself in the knuckle of my left hand, on the outside. Everybody in the house was terrified, and my friend, the big cow and the little one, all ran off; so that I was left alone with a poor errand boy of mine, who would never leave me. I felt my heart choking and knew for certain that I was a dead man.

At this, the father of this boy of mine passed along the street. He was doctor to Cardinal Iacoacci and was on his salary; and the boy said to his father: "Come, father, and see Benvenuto, who is in bed with a little indisposition." Without considering what the indisposition might be, he came straight to me, and when he had touched my pulse he saw and felt what he would not have wished. Turning at once to his son, he said: "Oh, you traitor of a son, you have ruined me: how can I go in front of the cardinal now?" To which the son said: "This master of mine, father, is worth far more than all the cardinals in Rome."

Then the doctor turned to me and said: "Since I am here, I mean to treat you. Of one thing only I warn you: if you have had intercourse, you are a dead man." To which I said: "I had it this night." At this the doctor said: "With what creature, and how much?" And I told him: "Last night, and with the very youngest little girl." Then, realising the foolish words he had used, he said to me at once: "Both because they are young, in that way, and do not stink yet, and because the remedy is in good time, do not be so frightened, because I hope to cure you by every means."

Having treated me and gone away, there came at once a very dear friend of mine called Giovanni Rigogli, who, being sorry both for my great illness and for my being left so alone by my companion, said: "Do not be afraid, my Benvenuto, because I shall never leave you until I see you well." I told this friend not to come near me, because I was done for. I only begged him to be so kind as to take a certain good sum of scudi which was in a little box there beside my bed, and, after God had taken me out of the world, to send them as a gift to my poor father, writing to him kindly, as I too had done, according to the custom that season of madness required. My dear friend told me he would not leave me in any way, and that whatever happened afterwards, one way or the other, he knew perfectly well what a friend ought to do.

And so we went forward with God's help; and with the marvellous remedies I began to improve very greatly, and I soon came safely out of that very great illness. Still keeping the sore open, with the plug in it and a plaster over it, I went off on a little wild horse of mine that I had. He had hair more than four fingers long; he was exactly as big as a big bear cub, and truly he looked like a bear. On him I went to find Il Rosso the painter, who was outside Rome towards Civitavecchia, at a place belonging to the Count dell'Anguillara called Cervetera; and I found my Rosso, who was overjoyed; and I said to him: "I have come to do to you what you did to me so many months ago." Bursting out laughing, and embracing and kissing me, he told me afterwards to keep quiet about it for the Count's sake. So, happy and cheerful, with good wines and excellent food, and made much of by the Count, I stayed there about a month; and every day I went off alone along the seashore, and got down there and loaded myself with all sorts of little stones, small shells and rare and beautiful conches.

On the last day, after which I did not go there again, I was set upon by a number of men who had disguised themselves and had landed from a Moorish galley; and thinking they had me shut in at a certain narrow place where it seemed impossible to get out of their hands, I got up at once on my little horse, and, having made up my mind at that dangerous place to be either roasted or boiled there, since I saw little hope of escaping one of the two ways, as God willed it the little horse — which was as I described above — made a jump it is impossible to believe; so that, having saved myself, I thanked God. I told the Count: he called to arms: we saw the galleys out at sea. The next day, sound and cheerful, I went back to Rome.

Chapter 30

By now the plague had almost stopped, so that those who found themselves alive were making much of each other very cheerfully. Out of this there was born a company of painters, sculptors and goldsmiths, the best there were in Rome; and the founder of this company was a sculptor called Michelagnolo. This Michelagnolo was of Siena, and was a very able man, such that he could stand up among any others of the profession; but above all he was the most pleasant and the most fleshly man there ever was in the world. Of this company he was the oldest, but the youngest in the strength of his body.

We used to meet often; at the least twice a week. I will not keep quiet about the fact that in this company of ours there were Giulio Romano the painter and Gianfrancesco, marvellous pupils of the great Raphael of Urbino. Having met a good many times, it seemed good to that good leader of ours that on the following Sunday we should meet for supper at his house, and that each of us should be obliged to bring his crow — for that was the name Michelagnolo had given them — and that whoever did not bring one should be obliged to pay for a supper for the whole company. Those of us who had no acquaintance with such women of the town had to provide themselves with one, at no small expense and trouble, so as not to be shamed at that gathering of talent.

I, who thought I was well provided for by a very beautiful young woman called Pantasilea, who was greatly in love with me, was forced to give her up to a very dear friend of mine called Il Bachiacca, who had been and still was greatly in love with her. In this case there stirred a little lovers' resentment, because, seeing that at the first word I gave her up to Bachiacca, it seemed to this woman that I took very little account of the great love she bore me; from which, in the course of time, a very great matter was born, when she wanted to take revenge for the injury she had had from me — which I shall tell in its place.

Well, the hour was beginning to press when each of us was to present himself to that gathering of talent with his crow, and I found myself without one; and it seemed to me too bad a mistake to fall short in such a mad affair; and what held me back most was that I did not want to bring under my own light, among such talent, some plucked little crow. So I thought of a pleasantry to add to the merriment and make the laughing greater.

Having made up my mind, I called a boy of sixteen who lived next door to me: he was the son of a Spanish brass founder. This young man was studying Latin letters and was very studious. His name was Diego. He was beautiful of body, and marvellous in the colour of his skin; the carving of his head was far more beautiful than the ancient one of Antinous, and I had drawn him many times, and had had much honour out of it in my work. He kept company with nobody, so that he was not known; he dressed very badly and anyhow: he was in love only with his marvellous studies.

Having called him into my house, I begged him to let me dress him in the women's clothes that were laid out there. He was easy about it and dressed quickly; and with the most beautiful ways of arranging his hair I soon added great beauty to his beautiful face. I put two little rings in his ears with two large, beautiful pearls in them — the rings were broken, and only pinched the ears, which looked as if they had been pierced. Then I put beautiful gold chains and rich jewels round his neck, and dressed his beautiful hands with rings. Then, taking him pleasantly by an ear, I drew him in front of a large mirror of mine. The young man, seeing himself, said with such boldness: "Good heavens — is that Diego?" Then I said: "That is Diego, from whom I never asked any favour of any kind; and now I only beg that Diego to do me one honest favour: and that is, that in these very clothes" — I wanted him to come to supper with that gathering of talent I had spoken to him about more than once. The young man, honest, able and wise, put off that boldness of his, turned his eyes to the ground, and stood a while like that without saying anything; then all at once he lifted his face and said: "With Benvenuto I will come; now let us go."

Having put on his head a large towel of the kind they call in Rome a summer cloth, when we got to the place everybody had already arrived, and they all came to meet me. Michelagnolo was standing between Giulio and Gianfrancesco. Having taken the towel off the head of that beautiful figure of mine, that Michelagnolo — as I have said before, the drollest and pleasantest man anyone could imagine — took hold with both his hands, one on Giulio and one on Gianfrancesco, and pulled them down as far as he could, and himself, on his knees on the ground, shouted for mercy and called out to all the people, saying: "Look, look how the Angels of Paradise are made! Because, although they are called Angels, look — there are Angelesses among them too." And shouting, he said:

	O Angel beautiful, O Angel true,
	save me, and make me the sign of the cross.

At these words the pleasant creature raised his right hand, laughing, and gave him a papal blessing with many pleasant words. Then Michelagnolo got up and said that one kisses the Pope's feet and Angels' cheeks; and when he had done it, the young man blushed deeply, which increased his beauty enormously.

So we went in, and the room was full of sonnets that each of us had made and sent to Michelagnolo. This young man began to read them, and read them all; which added so much to his endless beauties that it would be impossible to say it. After that there was a good deal of talk and wondering, which I do not want to go into, since I am not here for that; only one remark I must mention, because it was made by that marvellous painter Giulio, who, having run his eye skilfully over everyone who was there, but studying the women more than the rest, turned to Michelagnolo and said: "My dear Michelagnolo, that name of crows of yours suits these women very well today, although they are somewhat less beautiful than crows beside one of the most beautiful peacocks anyone could imagine."

The food being ready and set out, and it being time to sit down at table, Giulio begged as a favour that he should be the one to place us. This being entirely granted him, he took the women by the hand and put them all on the inner side, with mine in the middle; then he put all the men on the outside, and me in the middle, saying that I deserved every great honour. Behind the women there was a screen of natural jasmine, most beautiful, which made such a fine background for those women, and above all for mine, that it would be impossible to say it in words.

So we all went through that rich supper with the greatest goodwill, and it was marvellously abundant. After we had supped, there came a little marvellous music of voices along with instruments; and because they were singing and playing with the books in front of them, my beautiful figure asked to sing her part; and because he did that part of the music almost better than the rest, it caused so much wonder that the talk Giulio and Michelagnolo were making was no longer pleasant in the way it had been before, but was all in grave, solid words full of amazement.

After the music, a certain Aurelio Ascolano, who spoke marvellously without preparation, began to praise the women in divine and beautiful words; and while he was singing, those two women who had my figure between them never stopped chattering: one of them was telling how she had come to grief, and the other was asking my figure how she had done it, and who her friends were, and how long it was since she had come to Rome, and a great many things of that sort. It is true that if I were writing only to describe such pleasantries, I should tell of a good many accidents that happened there, set going by that Pantasilea, who was strongly in love with me; but since it is not to my purpose, I pass them over briefly.

Now this talk of those stupid women became a nuisance to my figure, whom we had named Pomona; and Pomona, wanting to get away from their silly talk, kept twisting about, now to one side and now to the other. She was asked by the woman Giulio had brought whether she felt any discomfort. She said yes, and that she thought she was some months gone, and felt a pain in her womb. At once the two women, who had her between them, moved to pity for Pomona, put their hands on her belly and found that she was male. Snatching their hands back at once, with the insulting words people use to handsome young men, they got up from the table; and the shouting spread at once, with great laughter and great astonishment, and the fierce Michelagnolo asked leave of everybody to give me a penance in his own way. Having got their yes, with the greatest shouting he lifted me off my feet, saying: "Long live the lord! Long live the lord!" and said that that was the sentence I deserved for having played so fine a trick. So ended that most pleasant supper and that day; and each of us went back to his own house.

Chapter 31

If I wanted to describe exactly what and how many were the many pieces I made for different sorts of men, my telling would be far too long. I need say nothing else for the present, except that I attended with every care and diligence to making myself skilled in that variety and difference of arts I have spoken of above. So I worked continually at all of them; and because no occasion has yet come into my mind to describe any notable work of mine, I shall wait to put them in their place, and they will come soon.

Michelagnolo, the Sienese sculptor, was at this time making the tomb of the dead Pope Adrian. Giulio Romano the painter went off to serve the Marquis of Mantua. The other companions withdrew, this one here and that one there, about their business; so that our able company nearly all broke up.

At this time there came into my hands certain little Turkish daggers; and the handle was of iron, like the blade of the dagger, and the sheath was of iron in the same way. These things were carved, by means of tools, with a great many beautiful leaf patterns in the Turkish manner, and most delicately inlaid with gold; which greatly roused in me the wish to try myself in that profession too, so different from the others. And seeing that it came out very well for me, I made several pieces. These pieces were far more beautiful and far more lasting than the Turkish ones, for several reasons. One is that in my steel I cut very deeply and undercut, which is not done in Turkish work. The other is that Turkish leafwork is nothing but arum leaves with a few little sunflower blossoms; and although they have some grace, they do not go on giving pleasure the way our leafwork does.

Though in Italy we have different ways of making leafwork; because the Lombards make very beautiful leaves, copying ivy and traveller's joy with beautiful turnings, which are very pleasant to see; the Tuscans and the Romans have made a much better choice in this line, because they copy acanthus leaves, called bear's-claw, with their stalks and flowers, turning in different ways; and among the leaves it is very well arranged to put a few little birds and different animals, as anyone with good taste can see. Some of it is found naturally in wild flowers, such as the ones called snapdragons, which can be made out in certain blossoms, accompanied by other beautiful inventions of those able craftsmen; which things are called by people who do not know, grotesques.

These grotesques got that name from the moderns, because they were found by students in certain caverns in the earth at Rome, which caverns were anciently rooms, baths, studies, halls and other things of that sort. These students, finding them in those cavernous places — because the ground has been raised since ancient times and they were left down below, and because in Rome the word for those low places is grottoes — from this they got the name of grotesques. Which is not their true name; because just as the ancients enjoyed making up monsters, coupling goats and cows and mares, and when these mixtures were born they called them monsters, so those craftsmen made this sort of monster with their leafwork: and monsters is their true name, and not grotesques.

Making this sort of leafwork inlaid in the way described above, they were much more beautiful to look at than the Turkish. At this time it happened that in certain vessels which were ancient little urns full of ashes, there were found among those ashes certain iron rings inlaid with gold by the ancients themselves, and in each of those rings a little shell was set. When the learned looked into it, they said that these rings were worn by people who wanted to keep steady in their minds through some strange thing that had happened to them, for good or for ill. This moved me, at the request of certain gentlemen who were great friends of mine, to make some of these little rings; but I made them of well-purged steel, and afterwards, when they had been well cut and inlaid with gold, they made a very beautiful show; and it happened sometimes that for one of these little rings, for my labour alone, I had more than forty scudi.

At this time there were in use certain little gold medals, and every lord and gentleman liked to have some fancy or device of his carved on them, and they wore them in their caps. I made a great many of these pieces, and they were very difficult to make. And because that very able man I spoke of, called Caradosso, made some of them, for which, when they had more than one figure on them, he would take nothing less than a hundred gold scudi each — for that reason, not so much because of the price as because of his slowness, I was put forward by certain gentlemen, for whom, among other things, I made a medal in competition with this great able man, and on that medal there were four figures, over which I had laboured hard. It happened that these gentlemen and lords, setting it beside the marvellous Caradosso's, said that mine was much better made and more beautiful, and that I should ask what I liked for my labour, since, having satisfied them so well, they wanted to satisfy me as much. To which I said that the greatest reward for my labour, and the thing I most wanted, was to come up alongside the works of so able a man; and that, if their lordships thought so too, I called myself fully paid. And when I had gone off at once, they sent after me so generous a present that I was content, and it gave me such heart to do well that it was the cause of what will be heard of hereafter.

Book One, Chapters 32 to 35

Chapter 32

I shall go somewhat away from my profession here, since I want to tell of certain troublesome things that happened in this laborious life of mine. I have already told about that company of talent, and about the pleasantries that came about on account of that woman I spoke of, Pantasilea, who bore me that false and troublesome love; and she, being extremely angry with me over that pleasantry in which the Spaniard Diego took part at that supper, and having sworn to revenge herself on me, an occasion arose which I shall describe, in which my life ran the greatest risk.

It was this. There came to Rome a young man called Luigi Pulci, the son of one of the Pulci who had his head cut off for having lain with his own daughter. This young man had a most marvellous poetic talent and a knowledge of good Latin letters; he wrote well; he was beyond measure handsome in his grace and his figure. He had left the service of some bishop or other, and he was full of the French disease.

When this young man was in Florence, on summer nights in certain parts of the city they used to hold gatherings in the streets themselves, and this young man was among the best of those who sang there without preparation; his singing was so beautiful to hear that the divine Michelangelo Buonarroti, that most excellent sculptor and painter, whenever he knew where he was, went to hear him with the greatest eagerness and pleasure; and a certain man called Il Piloto, a very able goldsmith, and I, went with him. That was how the acquaintance came about between Luigi Pulci and me.

Now, many years later, he showed himself to me in Rome in that bad state, begging me to help him for the love of God. Moved to pity by his great talents, for the love of our country, and because it is my own nature, I took him into my house and had him treated in such a way that, being young as he was, he was soon brought back to health. While he was seeing to that recovery, he studied continually, and I had helped him to a great many books as far as I could manage; so that this Luigi, recognising the great kindness he had had from me, thanked me many times with words and with tears, telling me that if God ever put any good fortune in front of him, he would pay me back for the kindness I had done him. To which I said that I had not done for him what I should have liked, but at least what I could, and that it was the duty of human creatures to help one another; only I reminded him to pay this kindness I had done him to some other man who needed him, as he had needed me; and to be my friend, and consider me his.

This young man began to frequent the Court of Rome, where he soon found a place, and settled with a bishop, a man of eighty, called Bishop Gurgensis. This bishop had a nephew called Messer Giovanni: he was a Venetian gentleman. This Messer Giovanni made a great show of being in love with Luigi Pulci's talents, and under the name of those talents he had made him as familiar with him as if he had been himself. Luigi, having talked to this Messer Giovanni about me and about the great obligation he was under to me, caused Messer Giovanni to want to know me.

Now it happened that one evening among others I had given a little feast for that Pantasilea I have spoken of, and to that supper I had invited a good many able friends of mine; and just as we were going to table Messer Giovanni came up with Luigi Pulci, and after some ceremony they stayed to supper with us. That shameless whore, seeing the handsome young man, immediately set her designs on him; and for that reason, when the pleasant supper was over, I called Luigi Pulci aside and told him that, by all the obligation he had boasted of being under to me, he should not in any way seek out that whore's acquaintance. At which words he said to me: "Good heavens, my Benvenuto, do you take me for a senseless man?" To which I said: "Not senseless, but young"; and I swore to him by God that I had not a thought in the world about her, but that it would hurt me if he broke his neck over her. At which words he swore that he prayed God, if he ever spoke to her, that he might break his neck at once. This poor young man must have made that oath to God with his whole heart, because he did break his neck, as will be told here below.

Messer Giovanni showed himself towards him in a filthy love and not a virtuous one; because the young man was seen every day changing his clothes of velvet and silk, and it was known that he had given himself over entirely to wickedness and had banished his beautiful and admirable talents; and he made a show of not seeing me and not knowing me, because I had rebuked him, telling him that he had given himself as a prey to foul vices which would make him break his neck, as he had said.

Chapter 33

That Messer Giovanni of his had bought him a most beautiful black horse, on which he had spent a hundred and fifty scudi. This horse handled marvellously well, so that Luigi went every day to prance about on this horse round that whore Pantasilea. I, becoming aware of it, took no notice at all, saying that everything acted according to its nature; and I attended to my studies.

It happened one Sunday evening that we were invited to supper by that Sienese sculptor Michelagnolo; and it was summer. At this supper Bachiacca was there, and he had brought with him that Pantasilea, his first acquaintance. So, being at table at supper, she was sitting between me and Bachiacca; and at the very best of the supper she got up from the table, saying she wanted to go about certain conveniences of hers, because she felt a pain in her stomach, and that she would come back at once. While we were talking and supping most pleasantly, she stayed away rather longer than she should have. It happened that, keeping my ears open, I thought I heard a very low sniggering in the street. I had a knife in my hand which I was using at table. The window was so close to the table that, rising a little, I saw in the street that Luigi Pulci along with Pantasilea, and I heard Luigi say: "Oh, if that devil Benvenuto were to see us, woe to us!" And she said: "Do not be afraid; listen to the noise they are making: they are thinking of anything but us."

At these words I, having recognised them, threw myself down from the window to the ground and took Luigi by the cloak, and with the knife I had in my hand I would certainly have killed him; but because he was on a little white horse, which he set spurs to, he left me the cloak in my hand to save his life. Pantasilea ran off into a church close by. The people at table got up at once and all came over to me, begging me not to trouble either myself or them on account of a whore; to which I said that I would not have stirred for her, but I would for that wicked young man, who showed that he thought so little of me. And so I would not be bent by any of the words of those able and decent men; instead I took my sword and went off by myself alone to the Prati, because the house where we were supping was near the Castle gate that leads to the Prati.

Going along towards the Prati, I was not long before, after the sun had gone down, I came back into Rome at a slow pace. It was already night and dark, and the gates of Rome were not shut. When it was getting on for two hours after dark I went past Pantasilea's house, meaning, if Luigi Pulci was there, to do harm to both of them. Seeing and hearing that nobody was in the house but an ugly servant girl called Canida, I went to put down my cloak and the scabbard of my sword, and so came back to the house, which was behind the Banchi on the river Tiber. Opposite this house there was an innkeeper's garden, belonging to a man called Romolo: this garden was shut in by a thick hedge of thorn, in which I hid myself, standing upright, waiting for the woman to come home along with Luigi.

After I had been there a while, that friend of mine called Bachiacca turned up, having either guessed it or been told. He called me in a low voice "gossip" (for that was what we called each other for a joke); and he begged me for the love of God, saying these words almost weeping: "My gossip, I beg you not to do any harm to that poor girl, because she is not at fault in the world." To which I said: "If at this first word you do not get out of my way, I shall give you this sword on the head." My poor gossip, frightened, was taken with a loosening of the bowels at once, and could go so short a distance that he had to obey me.

It was a starry night which gave a very great light. All at once I hear a noise of several horses, and they were coming forward on one side and the other: it was Luigi and Pantasilea accompanied by a certain Messer Benvegnato of Perugia, chamberlain to Pope Clement, and they had with them four very brave Perugian captains, with other very bold young soldiers: there were more than twelve swords among them all. When I saw this, considering that I did not know which way to run, I tried to push myself into that hedge; and because those sharp thorns hurt me, and I was working myself up like a bull, almost resolved to make a jump and run, at this Luigi had his arm round Pantasilea's neck, saying: "I will kiss you once at least, in scorn of that traitor Benvenuto."

At this, being tormented by the thorns and driven on by the young man's words, I sprang out, raised my sword, and said in a great voice: "You are all dead men." At this the blow of the sword fell on Luigi's shoulder; and because those satyrs had put the poor young man all in mail and other things of that sort, the blow was a very great one; and the sword, turning, struck Pantasilea on the nose and on the mouth. Both of them having fallen to the ground, Bachiacca, with his hose half down his legs, was shouting and running away.

Turning boldly on the others with my sword, those brave men, hearing a great noise which had started up in the inn, and thinking there was an army of a hundred men there, although they had put their hands bravely to their swords, two of the little horses among them got frightened and threw them into such disorder that, having knocked two of the best of them head over heels, the rest took to flight. And I, seeing that it was coming out well, got away from that business at a very fast run and with honour, not wanting to try fortune more than I ought.

In that immeasurable disorder some of those soldiers and captains had been wounded by their own swords, and Messer Benvegnato, the Pope's chamberlain, had been knocked over and trampled by a mule of his; and a servant of his, having put his hand to his sword, fell down together with him and wounded him badly in one hand. This injury caused Messer Benvegnato, more than all the others, to swear in that Perugian way of theirs, saying: "By God's — , I mean Benvegnato to teach Benvenuto how to live"; and he gave the commission to one of his captains, perhaps bolder than the others, but being young he had less judgement.

This man came to find me where I had withdrawn, in the house of a great Neapolitan gentleman who, having heard and seen some things of my profession, and besides that the disposition of my mind and body fitted for soldiering, which was what the gentleman was inclined to, so that, seeing myself made much of, and finding myself in my own line as well, I made such an answer to that captain that I believe he greatly repented of having come in front of me.

A few days later, when the wounds of Luigi and the whore and the others had dried up a little, this great Neapolitan gentleman was approached by Messer Benvegnato, whose fury had gone off, to make me make peace with the young man Luigi, and to say that those brave soldiers, who had nothing to do with me at all, only wanted to know me. The gentleman told them all that he would bring me wherever they liked, and would willingly make peace; but on this condition, that neither side should throw words back at each other, because that would be too much against their honour; it was enough to make a show of drinking and kissing, and that he would do the talking, by which he would willingly save them.

And so it was done. On a Thursday evening the gentleman took me to Messer Benvegnato's house, where all those soldiers were who had been at that rout, and they were still at table. With my gentleman there were more than thirty brave men, all well armed; a thing Messer Benvegnato had not expected. When we got into the hall, first the gentleman and then I, he said these words: "God save you, gentlemen. We have come to you, Benvenuto and I, whom I love like a brother of my own blood; and we are here willingly to do whatever you have a mind to do." Messer Benvegnato, seeing the room filling with so many people, said: "We are asking you for peace and nothing else." So Messer Benvegnato promised that the governor of Rome's court would give me no trouble. We made peace; and I went back at once to my shop, not able to be an hour without that Neapolitan gentleman, who either came to find me or sent for me.

In the meantime Luigi Pulci was well again, and every day he was on that black horse of his that handled so well. One day among others, it having been drizzling, and he putting the horse through its paces exactly in front of Pantasilea's door, it slipped and fell, and the horse on top of him; and having broken his right leg clean off, he died a few days later in Pantasilea's house, and fulfilled the oath he had made to God from his heart. So you can see that God keeps account of the good and the bad, and gives each one his deserts.

Chapter 34

By now the whole world was in arms. Pope Clement had sent to ask Signor Giovanni de' Medici for certain bands of soldiers, and when they came they behaved so outrageously in Rome that it was bad to keep an open shop. That was what made me withdraw into a good little house behind the Banchi; and there I worked for all the friends I had won.

My work at this time was nothing of much importance, so I need not talk about it. I took a great deal of pleasure at this time in music and in pleasures of that sort.

Pope Clement, on the advice of Messer Jacopo Salviati, having dismissed those five bands that Signor Giovanni had sent him — Signor Giovanni being already dead in Lombardy — Bourbon, knowing that there were no soldiers in Rome, pushed his army towards Rome with the greatest urgency. On this occasion the whole of Rome took to arms. Now I was a great friend of Alessandro, son of Piero del Bene, and because at the time when the Colonnesi came into Rome he had asked me to guard his house for him, so on this greater occasion he begged me to raise fifty companions to guard the house, and to be their leader, as I had been at the time of the Colonnesi; so I raised fifty very brave young men, and we moved into his house, well paid and well treated.

Bourbon's army had already appeared at the walls of Rome, and Alessandro del Bene begged me to go with him and keep him company; so one of the best of our companions and I went; and on the way a young man called Cecchino della Casa joined us. We got to the walls of the Campo Santo, and there we saw that marvellous army, which was already making every effort to get in. At the place on the walls where we came up there were a number of young men lying dead, killed by those outside: there was fighting there with all their might; there was a fog as thick as anyone can imagine.

I turned to Alessandro and said: "Let us get back home as fast as we can, because there is no remedy in the world here; you see, those are climbing up and these are running away." Alessandro, frightened, said: "Would to God we had never come!" And so, turning about in the greatest hurry to go, I took him up on it, saying: "Since you have brought me here, we must do something like men." And turning my arquebus to where I saw the thickest and closest knot of fighting, I aimed exactly at the middle, at one man I saw raised above the others; on account of which the fog did not let me make out whether he was on horseback or on foot. Turning at once to Alessandro and Cecchino, I told them to fire their arquebuses, and showed them how to do it so as not to get an arquebus shot from those outside. So, having done it twice each, I went up carefully to the wall, and, seeing an extraordinary uproar among them, it turned out that by these shots of ours Bourbon had been killed; and he was that first man I had seen raised above the others, as was understood afterwards.

Leaving there, we went off through the Campo Santo, and came in by Saint Peter's; and coming out behind by the church of Sant'Angelo, we got to the great gate of the Castle with the greatest difficulty, because Signor Renzo da Ceri and Signor Orazio Baglioni were wounding and killing everyone who came away from the fighting at the walls. When we got to the gate, part of the enemy had already entered Rome, and we had them at our backs. The Castle wanting to drop the portcullis of the gate, a little space was made, so that the four of us got inside.

As soon as I was in, Captain Pallone de' Medici took hold of me, because, being one of the Castle's household, he forced me to leave Alessandro; which I did very much against my will. So I went up to the keep, and at the same moment Pope Clement had come into the Castle by the corridors, because he had not wanted to leave the palace of Saint Peter earlier, not being able to believe that they would get in.

After I found myself inside in that way, I went up to certain pieces of artillery which were in the charge of a gunner called Giuliano, a Florentine. This Giuliano, leaning out at the battlement of the Castle, could see his own poor house being sacked and his wife and children abused; so that, for fear of hitting his own, he did not dare fire his guns; and, having thrown the match for firing on the ground, he was tearing his face with great weeping; and certain other gunners were doing the same. So I took one of those matches, getting some who were there to help me who had no such feelings; and I turned certain sakers and falconets where I saw the need, and with them killed a great many of the enemy; and if this had not been done, that party which had entered Rome that morning would have come straight to the Castle, and it is possible that it might easily have got in, because the artillery was giving them no trouble. I went on firing; on account of which some cardinals and lords blessed me and gave me the greatest heart. At which, growing bold, I forced myself to do what I could not; it is enough that I was the cause of saving the Castle that morning, and that those other gunners went back to doing their duty. I went on all that day.

When evening came, while the army was entering Rome by the Trastevere side, Pope Clement having made a great Roman gentleman called Messer Antonio Santacroce head of all the gunners, this great gentleman came to me first of all, making much of me; and he put me with five marvellous pieces of artillery in the highest place in the Castle, the one called the Angel: this place goes right round the Castle and looks towards the Prati and towards Rome. So he gave me so many men under me that I could give orders to, to help me turn my guns; and having had a payment made me in advance, he gave me bread and a little wine, and then begged me to go on as I had begun. And I, who am sometimes more inclined to this profession than to the one I hold as my own, did it so willingly that it came out better for me than the other.

When night came, and the enemy had come into Rome, we who were in the Castle — and above all I, who have always taken pleasure in seeing new things — stood considering this immeasurable strangeness and burning; a thing which those who were anywhere but in the Castle could neither see nor imagine. Therefore I shall not set myself to describe such a thing; I shall only go on describing this life of mine that I have begun, and the things that belong to it exactly.

Chapter 35

Going on working my guns continually, in a whole month that we stayed besieged in the Castle, a great many very great things happened to me, all worth telling; but so as not to be too long, and not to show myself too far outside my profession, I shall leave out most of them, telling only those that force me to it, which will be the fewest and the most notable.

And this is the first. Messer Antonio Santacroce had made me come down off the Angel, so as to fire at certain houses near the Castle where some of the enemy from outside had been seen going in; and while I was firing, a cannon shot came at me which hit the corner of a battlement and carried away so much of it that it was the reason I was not hurt; because the whole greater quantity struck my chest all at once; and, my breath being stopped, I lay stretched on the ground like a dead man, and heard everything the bystanders were saying. Among them Messer Antonio Santacroce was greatly grieved, saying: "Alas, we have lost the best help we had."

Coming up at this noise, a certain companion of mine called Gianfrancesco, a piper — this man was more inclined to medicine than to piping — ran off weeping at once for a little flask of very good Greek wine; and having made a tile red hot, on which he put a good handful of wormwood, he then sprinkled it with that good Greek wine; and when the wormwood was well soaked, he immediately laid it on my chest, where the blow could plainly be seen. There was so much power in that wormwood that it gave me back my lost faculties at once. When I wanted to begin to speak I could not, because some foolish little soldiers had filled my mouth with earth, thinking that with it they were giving me the communion, whereas they had rather excommunicated me, because I could not come round again, this earth giving me a good deal more trouble than the blow. Having escaped this too, I went back to those furies of the artillery, keeping at them with all the skill and urgency I could imagine.

And because Pope Clement had sent to ask help of the Duke of Urbino, who was with the Venetian army, telling the ambassador to tell his Excellency that as long as the Castle held out it would make three fires every evening on top of the Castle, accompanied by three triple shots of artillery, and that as long as this signal lasted it showed that the Castle would not surrender — I had this duty of making these fires and firing these guns. Now, in the daytime I always pointed them at places where they could do some great harm; on account of which the Pope liked me a great deal better, because he saw that I did the work with the care such things call for. The Duke's help never came; and I, who am not here for that, shall describe no more of it.

Book One, Chapters 36 to 40

Chapter 36

While I was up there at that devilish exercise of mine, some of the cardinals who were in the Castle used to come and see me, but most often Cardinal Ravenna and Cardinal de' Gaddi; and I told them several times not to come in front of me, because those little red caps of theirs could be seen a long way off, and from the neighbouring palaces, like the Torre de' Bini, both they and I ran the greatest danger; so that in the end I had them shut out, and I got a good deal of enmity from them for it.

Signor Orazio Baglioni also came about me often, who was very fond of me. One day among others, when he was talking with me, he saw something going on at a certain inn which was outside the Castle gate, at a place called Baccanello. This inn had for its sign a sun painted between two windows, in red. The windows being shut, Signor Orazio judged that opposite that sun, inside, between the two windows, there was a table of soldiers making merry; and so he said to me: "Benvenuto, if you have the heart to hit an arm's length from that sun with this half-cannon of yours, I think you would do a good piece of work, because there is a great noise to be heard over there, where there must be men of much importance." To which I said: "I can well enough hit the middle of that sun"; but there was a barrel full of stones close to the mouth of the cannon, and the fury of the fire and of the wind the cannon made would have sent it to the ground. To which the gentleman answered: "Do not waste time, Benvenuto: in the first place it is not possible, standing as it does, that the wind of the cannon should make it fall; but even if it did fall and the Pope were underneath it, it would be less harm than you think, so fire, fire."

I, without thinking any further about it, hit the middle of the sun exactly, as I had promised. The barrel fell, as I said, and it fell exactly between Cardinal Farnese and Messer Jacopo Salviati, and would have squashed them both flat; and the reason was that Cardinal Farnese had just then reproached Messer Jacopo with being the cause of the sack of Rome; and, as they were insulting each other, they had moved apart to give room to the insulting words, and that was why my barrel did not squash them both.

Hearing the great noise going on in that lower court, the good Signor Orazio went down in a great hurry; and I, leaning out where the barrel had fallen, heard some of them saying: "It would be a good thing to kill that gunner." At which I turned two falconets on the stairs that came up, with my mind made up that the first man who came up should have one of the falconets fired at him. Some of Cardinal Farnese's servants must have had orders from the cardinal to come and make trouble for me; so I came forward with the fire in my hand. Recognising some of them, I said: "Oh, you cut-throats, if you do not get away from there, and if there is anyone who dares to come inside these stairs, I have two falconets here ready with which I will make powder of you; and go and tell the cardinal that I have done what I was ordered to by my superiors, and that these things are being done and have been done in defence of you priests, and not to hurt you."

When they had gone, up came Signor Orazio Baglioni at a run, and I told him to stay back or I would kill him, because I knew perfectly well who he was. This gentleman stopped a moment, not without fear, and said to me: "Benvenuto, I am your friend." To which I said: "Sir, come up alone, and then come in any way you like." This gentleman, who was extremely proud, stopped a moment and said to me angrily: "I have a mind not to come up any further, and to do the exact opposite of what I had meant to do for you." To this I answered that, just as I had been put in that post to defend other people, so I was fit to defend myself too. He told me he was coming alone; and when he had come up, his face being altered more than it should have been, that was the reason I kept my hand on my sword and looked at him like a dog. At this he began to laugh, and the colour coming back into his face, he said to me most pleasantly: "My Benvenuto, I wish you all the good I have, and when it pleases God the time comes, I shall show it to you. Would to God you had killed those two scoundrels, because one of them is the cause of so much evil, and the other may yet be the cause of worse." And he told me that if I were asked, I should not say he was there with me when I fired that gun; and that as for the rest I should not worry.

The uproar was very great, and the business went on a long time. I do not want to go on any longer about it here: it is enough that I nearly took revenge for my father on Messer Jacopo Salviati, who had done him a thousand pieces of treachery (according to my father's own complaints). At any rate, without meaning to, I gave him a great fright. Of Farnese I will say nothing, because it will be heard in its place how well it would have been if I had killed him.

Chapter 37

I attended to firing my guns, and with them I did something very notable every day; so that I had won a credit and a favour with the Pope beyond all reckoning. Not a day ever passed in which I did not kill some of the enemy outside.

One day among others the Pope was walking about the round keep, and could see in the Prati a Spanish colonel whom he recognised by certain marks, having heard that he had once been in his service; and while he was watching him he talked about him. I, who was up on the Angel and knew nothing of this, but saw a man there having trenches put in order, with a little javelin in his hand, dressed all in rose colour, considering what I could do against him, took a gerfalcon of mine that I had there — this piece is bigger and longer than a saker, almost like a half-culverin. I emptied this piece and then loaded it with a good quantity of fine powder mixed with the coarse; then I aimed it very carefully at this man in red, giving it a marvellous elevation, because he was so far off that the art did not promise a shot at that distance with artillery of that sort. I gave it fire and hit that man in red exactly in the middle, who out of self-importance had put his sword in front of him in a certain Spanish way of his: so that when my cannon ball arrived and struck that sword, the man was seen to be divided in two pieces.

The Pope, who was not expecting such a thing, took great pleasure and wonder in it, both because it seemed impossible to him that a gun could carry so far in aim, and because he could not settle how the man could be in two pieces or how such a thing could be. And he sent for me and questioned me. So I told him all the care I had used in the way of firing; but as for the man being in two pieces, neither he nor I knew the cause. Kneeling down, I begged him to bless me again for the homicide, and for the others I had committed in that Castle in the service of the Church. At which the Pope, raising his hands and making a wide cross over my figure, told me that he blessed me and forgave me all the homicides I had ever committed and all those I ever should commit in the service of the apostolic Church.

Having left him, I went up, and pressing on I never stopped firing, and hardly a shot ever went for nothing. My drawing and my fine studies and my beauty of playing music were all in the playing of those guns; and if I had to tell in detail the fine things I did in that cruel infernal business, I should astonish the world; but so as not to be too long I pass over them. I shall tell only one or two of the more notable ones, which are necessary to me.

And this is one: thinking day and night what I could do for my part in defence of the Church, and having considered that the enemy changed their guards and passed by the gate of Santo Spirito, which was a reasonable shot but came at me across, so that I did not do the great harm I wanted to do — still, a good many were killed every day. So that, seeing that this passage was blocked, the enemy put more than thirty barrels one night on the top of a roof, which cut off that view of mine. And I, who thought about this case a little better than I had before, turned all five of my pieces of artillery, aiming them at those barrels; and I waited for the twenty-second hour, just at the fine moment for changing the guards. And because they, thinking they were safe, came more slowly and much more closely packed than usual, when I gave fire to my blowers I not only threw down those barrels which were blocking me, but with that one blast killed more than thirty men.

For which reason, going on to do it twice more afterwards, the soldiers were thrown into such disorder that — being full of the plunder of the great sack, and some of them wanting to enjoy the fruits of their labours — they several times wanted to mutiny and go off. Still, being held back by their brave captain, called Gian di Urbino, they were forced, at very great inconvenience to themselves, to take another way for the changing of their guards; which inconvenience meant more than three miles, where the first was not half of one.

Having done this piece of work, all those lords who were in the Castle showed me marvellous favours. This particular case, having turned out of so much importance, I have wanted to tell in order to make an end of this subject, because I am not in the profession that moves me to write; for if I wanted to make my life fine with things of that kind, I should have far too many left to tell. There is only one more, which I shall tell in its place.

Chapter 38

Jumping forward a good way, I shall tell how Pope Clement, in order to save the tiaras with all the quantity of the great jewels of the apostolic Chamber, had me called, and shut himself up in a room alone with the Cavalierino and me. This Cavalierino had once been a servant in Filippo Strozzi's stable: he was French, a person of the lowest birth; and because he was a great servant, Pope Clement had made him extremely rich, and trusted him like himself. So the Pope and the Cavaliere and I, shut in that room, they put those tiaras in front of me with all that great quantity of jewels from the apostolic Chamber, and he ordered me to strip them all out of the gold they were set in. And I did so; then we wrapped each of them in a little paper and sewed them into certain pads on the Pope's body and the Cavalierino's. Then they gave me all the gold, which was about two hundred pounds, and told me to melt it down as secretly as I could.

I went off to the Angel, where my room was, which I could shut so that nobody troubled me; and having made myself a little draught furnace of bricks there, and fitted into the bottom of the furnace a good-sized ash tray in the shape of a dish, I threw the gold on top onto the coals, and little by little it fell into that dish. While this furnace was working, I was constantly on the watch for how I could harm our enemies; and because we had the enemy's trenches below us at less than a stone's throw, I did them damage in those trenches with certain old-fashioned quarrels, several stacks of which were among the Castle's old munitions. Having taken a saker and a falconet, both of which were a little broken at the muzzle, I filled these with those quarrels; and when I gave fire to the guns they flew off wildly, doing a great many unexpected injuries in the trenches; so that, keeping these constantly ready while I was melting the gold, a little before vespers I saw come along the edge of the trench a man on horseback, on a mule. The mule was going very fast, and this man was talking to the men in the trenches. I took care to give fire to my gun before he came opposite me; and so, having fired with good judgement, when he came up I caught him with one of those quarrels exactly in the face: the rest of them hit the mule, which fell dead. In the trench a very great uproar was heard; I gave fire to the other piece, not without great harm to them.

This was the Prince of Orange, who was carried along inside the trenches to a certain inn nearby, where in a short time all the nobility of the army hurried.

When Pope Clement heard what I had done, he sent for me at once; and when he had asked me about it, I told him everything, and told him besides that the man must have been of very great importance, because in that inn where they had carried him all the leaders of that army had immediately gathered, as far as one could judge. The Pope, with very good sense, sent for Messer Antonio Santacroce, the gentleman who was head and guide of all the gunners, as I have said, and told him to order all of us gunners to aim all our artillery at that house — and there was an endless number of guns — and that at an arquebus shot every man should give fire; so that, if those leaders were killed, that army, which was almost on props, would all break up; and that perhaps God would have heard their prayers, which they were making so often, and by that road would have freed them from those impious scoundrels.

We had got our artillery in order, according to Santacroce's commission, and were waiting for the signal, when Cardinal Orsini heard of it and began to shout at the Pope, saying that on no account should such a thing be done, because they were on the point of concluding the agreement; and that if those men were killed, the camp without a leader would force its way into the Castle and would finish ruining them altogether: therefore they did not want such a thing done. The poor Pope, in despair, seeing himself murdered inside and out, said he left the matter to them.

So, our orders having been taken off us, I, who could not keep still, when I heard that they were coming to give me the order not to fire, gave fire to a half-cannon I had, which struck a pillar in the courtyard of that house, where I could see a great many people leaning. This shot did the enemy such great harm that it nearly made them abandon the house. Cardinal Orsini wanted me either hanged or killed at all costs; at which the Pope defended me boldly. The great words that passed between them, although I know them, since I do not profess to write histories, I need not tell; I shall only attend to my own affair.

Chapter 39

When I had melted the gold, I took it to the Pope, who thanked me a great deal for what I had done, and ordered the Cavalierino to give me twenty-five scudi, excusing himself to me that he had no more to give. A few days later the agreement was made.

I went off with Signor Orazio Baglioni, along with three hundred companions, towards Perugia; and there Signor Orazio wanted to hand me over the company, which for the time being I did not want, saying that I wanted to go and see my father first, and to buy off the sentence of banishment I had from Florence. This gentleman told me that he had been made captain of the Florentines; and there was Ser Pier Maria di Lotto, sent by the Florentines, to whom Signor Orazio strongly recommended me as his own man.

So I came to Florence with several other companions. The plague was very great, beyond all reckoning. When I got to Florence, I found my good father, who thought either that I was dead in that Sack or that I would come back to him with nothing. Which turned out entirely the other way: I was alive, and with a good deal of money, with a servant, and well mounted. When I came to my old man, the joy I saw in him was so great that I certainly thought, while he was embracing and kissing me, that he would die of it on the spot. I told him all those devilries of the Sack, and put a good quantity of scudi into his hand, which I had earned in a soldierly way; and after we had made much of each other, the good father and I, he went off at once to the Eight to buy off my sentence; and it happened that one of the Eight was one of the men who had given it me, and it was the one who had so tactlessly told my father that time that he meant to send me out to the farm with lances; on account of which my father used some sharp words by way of revenge, brought on by the favours Signor Orazio Baglioni had shown me.

As things stood, I told my father how Signor Orazio had chosen me for a captain, and that I ought to start thinking about raising the company. At these words my poor father was immediately upset, and begged me for the love of God not to take up such a business, although he knew I should be fit for it and for more; telling me besides that he had his other son, my brother, who was so extremely brave at war, and that I ought to attend to that marvellous art in which I had laboured so many years and with such great study.
