Although I promised to obey him, being a wise man he thought that if Signor Orazio came, both because I had promised him and for other reasons, I should never be able to help going on with the business of the war; so he thought of a good way to get me out of Florence, saying: "Oh, my dear son, the plague here is very great, beyond all reckoning, and I keep thinking I see you come home with it. I remember, when I was a young man, that I went off to Mantua, where they made a great deal of me, and I stayed there several years. I beg you and I command you, for love of me, to get away from here and go there — today rather than tomorrow."

Chapter 40

Since it has always given me pleasure to see the world, and having never been to Mantua, I went willingly, taking the money I had brought; and the greater part of it I left with my good father, promising him to help him always, wherever I might be, and leaving my elder sister to look after my poor father. Her name was Cosa, and, never having wanted a husband, she had been accepted as a nun at Santa Orsola, and so she stayed on to help and manage my old father and to look after my other, younger sister, who was married to a certain sculptor called Bartolomeo. So I set off with my father's blessing, took my good horse, and went off on it to Mantua.

I should have far too many great things to tell if I wanted to write this little journey in detail. The world being darkened with plague and war, it was with the greatest difficulty that I got at last to Mantua; and when I got there I looked for work, and was set to work by a certain Master Nicolò of Milan, who was goldsmith to the Duke of Mantua. When I had been set to work, two days later I went off to visit Messer Giulio Romano, that most excellent painter I have spoken of, a great friend of mine, who made much of me beyond all reckoning and was very put out that I had not gone to dismount at his house. He lived like a lord, and was doing a work for the Duke outside the gate of Mantua, at a place called the Te. This work was great and marvellous, as perhaps can still be seen.

Messer Giulio at once spoke to the Duke about me with many honourable words; and the Duke ordered me to make him a model for holding the relic of Christ's blood which they have there, and which they say was brought there by Longinus. Then he turned to Messer Giulio, telling him to make me a drawing for the reliquary. At this Messer Giulio said: "My lord, Benvenuto is a man who has no need of other people's drawings, and your Excellency will judge that very well when you see his model."

Setting my hand to this model, I made a design for the reliquary in which the phial could be very well placed; then I made a little wax model for the top. This was a Christ seated, who in his raised left hand held his great Cross, with the action of leaning on it; and with his right hand he made a sign with his fingers as if to open the wound in his breast. When this model was finished, the Duke liked it so much that his favours were beyond all reckoning, and he let me know that he would keep me in his service on such terms that I could live richly there.

In the meantime, having paid my respects to his brother the Cardinal, the Cardinal begged the Duke to be so good as to let me make the pontifical seal for his most reverend lordship; which I began. While I was working on this piece, the quartan fever came on me; and when this fever took me it drove me out of my senses, so that I cursed Mantua and whoever was master of it and whoever liked being there. These words were repeated to the Duke by that Milanese goldsmith of his, who saw perfectly well that the Duke wanted to make use of me. When the Duke heard those sick words of mine, he was badly angered with me; so that, since I was angry with Mantua, we were equal in our tempers.

When my seal was finished, which took four months, with several other little pieces made for the Duke in the Cardinal's name, I was well paid by the Cardinal; and he begged me to go back to Rome, that marvellous city where we had come to know each other.

Having left Mantua with a good sum of scudi, I got to Governo, the place where that most brave man Signor Giovanni was killed. There a short bout of fever took me, which did not hinder my journey at all, and it stayed behind at that place, and I never had it again.

Then, arriving in Florence, and thinking to find my dear father, I knocked at the door, and a certain furious hunchbacked woman came to the window and drove me off with a good deal of rudeness, telling me I made her sick. To which hunchback I said: "Oh, tell me, you crooked hunchback, is there any other face in this house but yours?" "No, and bad luck to you." At which I said loudly: "May this not last you two hours." At this argument a neighbour came out, who told me that my father and everybody in my house were dead of the plague; and since I had partly guessed it, that was why the grief was less. Then she told me that only my younger sister was left alive, whose name was Liperata, and that she had been taken in by a holy woman called Monna Andrea de' Bellacci.

I left there to go to the inn. By chance I ran into a very great friend of mine, called Giovanni Rigogli. Having dismounted at his house, we went off to the square, where I had news that my brother was alive; and I went to find him at the house of a friend of his called Bertino Aldobrandi. When I found my brother, and we had made endless fuss over each other — the reason it was so extraordinary being that he had been given news of my death and I of his — then, after a great burst of laughter, he took me by the hand in astonishment and said: "Come, brother, and I will take you to a place you would never imagine: I have married off our sister Liperata again, and she is quite certain you are dead."

While we were going there we told each other a great many fine things that had happened to us; and when we got to the house where my sister was, she was so overcome by the unexpected strangeness that she fell into my arms in a faint; and if my brother had not been there, the thing was such, and without a word said, that at first her husband did not think I was her brother. Cecchino my brother, speaking and helping the fainting woman, soon brought her round; and having wept a little for our father, our sister, her husband and a little son of hers, orders were given for supper; and at that pleasant wedding party nobody talked any more of the dead all evening, but only of wedding matters. So we finished supper cheerfully and with great pleasure.

Book One, Chapters 41 to 45

Chapter 41

Forced by the entreaties of my brother and my sister, I stayed in Florence, because my own wish was to go back to Rome. And that dear friend of mine as well — the one I spoke of before, who helped me so much in certain of my troubles, that is, Piero di Giovanni Landi — this Piero too told me that I ought to stay in Florence a while; because the Medici had been driven out of Florence, that is Signor Ippolito and Signor Alessandro, who were afterwards one a Cardinal and the other Duke of Florence, and Piero told me I ought to stay a little and see what happened.

So I began to work in the Mercato Nuovo, and set a great quantity of jewels, and earned well. At this time there came to Florence a Sienese called Girolamo Marretti: this Sienese had been a long time in Turkey and was a person of lively wit. He came to my shop and gave me a gold medal to make, to be worn in a hat; he wanted me to make on this medal a Hercules forcing open the lion's mouth. So I set to work at it; and while I was working on it Michelangelo Buonarroti came several times to see it; and because I had laboured hard over it, the action of the figure and the fierceness of the animal being very different from anything anyone had made in that line up to then — and also because that way of working was entirely unknown to the divine Michelangelo — he praised this work of mine so much that my spirit for doing well rose beyond all reckoning.

But because I had nothing else to do except set jewels, which, although it was the greatest earning I could get, did not satisfy me, since I wanted to make works of some other skill than setting jewels — at this point there came along a certain Federigo Ginori, a young man of very lofty spirit. This young man had been at Naples many years, and because he was very handsome in body and presence, he had fallen in love at Naples with a princess; and so, wanting to make a medal in which there should be an Atlas with the world on his back, he asked the great Michelangelo to make him a little drawing of it. Who said to Federigo: "Go and find a certain young goldsmith called Benvenuto; he will serve you very well, and certainly he does not need a drawing from me. But so that you may not think I want to run away from the labour of so small a thing, I shall very willingly make you a little drawing; meanwhile speak to this Benvenuto and let him make a little model of it too; and afterwards the better of the two can be put into work."

Federigo Ginori came to find me and told me what he wanted, and afterwards how much that marvellous Michelangelo had praised me; and that I should make a little wax model of it too, while that admirable man had promised him a drawing. Those words of that great man gave me such heart that I set myself at once with the greatest urgency to make the model; and when I had finished it, a certain painter, a great friend of Michelangelo's, called Giuliano Bugiardini, brought me the drawing of the Atlas. At the same moment I showed Giuliano my wax model, which was very different from Michelangelo's drawing; so that Federigo and Bugiardini too concluded that I should make it according to my own model.

So I began it, and the most excellent Michelangelo saw it and praised it so much that it was beyond all reckoning. It was a figure, as I have said, chased out of plate; it had the heavens on its back, made into a ball of crystal, with the zodiac cut into it, on a ground of lapis lazuli; and together with the figure it made so fine a show that it was beyond all reckoning. Underneath was a motto in letters that said "Summa tulisse juvat" — it is a joy to have borne the highest. Federigo, being satisfied, paid me most generously.

Since Messer Luigi Alamanni was in Florence at this time, and was a friend of Federigo Ginori's, he brought him several times to my shop, and by his kindness he became a very familiar friend of mine.

Chapter 42

When Pope Clement made war on the city of Florence, and the city prepared to defend itself, and had the militia of the people arranged by every quarter, I too was called out for my part. I got myself richly equipped; I kept company with the greatest nobility of Florence, who were seen to be very much of one mind about wanting to serve in that defence; and those speeches were made in every quarter that are well known. Besides that, the young men came together more than usual, and nothing was ever talked about but this.

One day about noon, when there was a crowd of the leading men and young men of the city in my shop, a letter was brought to me from Rome, which came from a certain man known in Rome as Master Jacopino della Barca. His name was Jacopo dello Sciorina, but della Barca in Rome, because he kept a boat that crossed the Tiber between the Ponte Sisto and the Ponte Sant'Angelo. This Master Jacopo was a very ingenious person, and had pleasant and very fine conversation; he had once been in Florence a designer of patterns for the silk weavers. This man was a great friend of Pope Clement's, who took great pleasure in hearing him talk.

Being one day in such talk, they came round to the subject of the Sack and of the action at the Castle; on which account the Pope, remembering me, said as much good of me as anyone can imagine; and he added that if he knew where I was, he would be glad to have me back. Master Jacopo said that I was in Florence; so the Pope ordered him to write to me to come back to him. This letter said that I should come back to Clement's service, and that it would be good for me. The young men who were there wanted to know what the letter contained; so I hid it as well as I could, and afterwards wrote to Master Jacopo begging him on no account whatever to write to me again, for good or ill.

He, his appetite growing greater, wrote me another letter, which went so far beyond all bounds that if it had been seen I should have come off badly. It said that, on the Pope's behalf, I should come at once, since he wanted to use me for things of the greatest importance; and that if I wanted to do well I should leave everything at once, and not stay to set myself against a pope, along with those raving madmen.

When I saw the letter it put me in such fear that I went to find that dear friend of mine called Pier Landi; who, seeing me, at once asked me what news I had, since I looked so troubled. I told my friend that what I had that gave me that great trouble I could not tell him in any way; I only begged him to take the keys I was giving him, and to give back the jewels and the gold to this man and that, whom he would find written in a little book of mine; then to take the goods in my house and look after them a little with his usual kindness, and that in a few days he would know where I was. This wise young man, having perhaps roughly guessed the thing, said: "My brother, go away quickly, then write, and do not give a thought to your own affairs." So I did. He was the most faithful friend, the wisest, the most decent, the most discreet, the most loving I have ever known. Having left Florence, I went to Rome, and wrote from there.

Chapter 43

As soon as I got to Rome, having found some of my friends, by whom I was very well received and made much of, I set at once to work on pieces that were all for earning and not for a name worth describing.

There was a certain old goldsmith called Raffaello del Moro. He was a man of much reputation in the trade, and in other respects a very decent man. He begged me to be so kind as to come and work in his shop, because he had some pieces of importance to do which would earn very well: so I went willingly.

More than ten days had passed without my letting myself be seen by that Master Jacopino della Barca; who, seeing me by chance, gave me the greatest welcome, and having asked me how long it was since I had arrived, I told him it was about a fortnight. This man took it very badly, and told me that I took very little account of a pope, who had already had him write three times for me with great urgency. And I, who had taken it much more badly than he had, never answered him anything, but swallowed my temper. This man, who was extremely full of words, got onto a beaten track and said so much that at last, when I saw he was tired, I said nothing to him except that he should take me to the Pope at his convenience; to which he answered that there was always time; at which I said: "And I am always ready."

He began to make his way towards the palace with me (this was Maundy Thursday), and when we got to the Pope's rooms, he being known and I being expected, we were let in at once. The Pope was in bed, a little unwell, and with him were Messer Jacopo Salviati and the Archbishop of Capua. When the Pope saw me, he was extraordinarily glad; and I, having kissed his feet, went up to him as modestly as I could, showing that I wanted to tell him some things of importance. He immediately made a sign with his hand, and Messer Jacopo and the Archbishop withdrew a good way from us.

I began at once, saying: "Most Blessed Father, since the Sack I have not been able to confess or take communion, because they will not absolve me. The case is this: when I melted the gold and did that work of getting those jewels out, your Holiness gave orders to the Cavalierino that he should give me some little reward for my labour, which I had none of — indeed he gave me rudeness instead. When I went upstairs, where I had melted the gold, and lifted the ashes, I found about a pound and a half of gold in little grains like millet; and because I had not enough money to get myself home respectably, I thought I would make use of it, and give it back afterwards when I had the chance. Now I am here at the feet of your Holiness, who are the true confessor: be so good as to give me leave so that I can confess and take communion, and by the grace of your Holiness get back the grace of my Lord God."

Then the Pope, with a little sigh of modesty, perhaps remembering his own troubles, said these words: "Benvenuto, I am quite certain of what you say, and I can absolve you of any impropriety you may have committed, and more than that, I mean to; so say everything with a completely free and good mind, because if you had had the value of one of those whole tiaras, I am fully disposed to pardon you." Then I said: "I had nothing else, most Blessed Father, than what I have said; and this did not come to the value of a hundred and forty ducats, which is what I had for it from the mint at Perugia, and with it I went to comfort my poor old father."

The Pope said: "Your father was as able, good and decent a man as was ever born, and you do not fall short of him at all: I am very sorry the money was so little; so that as for this which you say there was, I make you a present of it, and I forgive you everything; testify of this to your confessor, if there is nothing else that concerns me; then, when you have confessed and taken communion, let yourself be seen again, and it will be good for you."

When I had left the Pope, and Messer Jacopo and the Archbishop had come up, the Pope said as much good of me as could be said of any man in the world; and he said that he had confessed and absolved me. Then he added, saying to the Archbishop of Capua, that he should send for me and ask me whether anything else was needed about that case, that he should absolve me of everything, that he gave him full authority; and besides that, he should make as much of me as he could.

While I was going off with Master Jacopino, he asked me most inquisitively what that long, close conversation was that I had had with the Pope; and when he had asked me more than twice, I told him I did not want to tell him, because they were not things that concerned him; therefore he should not ask me again. I went and did everything I had agreed with the Pope; then, when the two feast days had passed, I went to visit him; and he, making more of me than before, said: "If you had come to Rome a little sooner, I should have had you remake those two tiaras of mine that we spoiled in the Castle. But because they are things of little skill apart from the jewels on the outside, I shall use you for a work of the greatest importance, where you will be able to show what you can do. And this is the clasp for my cope" — which is made round in the shape of a trencher, and as big as a small trencher, a third of an arm across — "and on this I want a God the Father made in half relief, and in the middle of it I want to fit this beautiful great point of a diamond with a great many other jewels of the greatest importance. Caradosso began one and never finished it; I want this one finished quickly, because I want to enjoy it a little myself. So go and make a fine little model." And he had all the jewels shown to me; at which I went off at once with my heart in my mouth.

Chapter 44

While the siege was round Florence, that Federigo Ginori for whom I had made the medal of the Atlas died of consumption, and the medal came into the hands of Messer Luigi Alamanni, who a short time later took it himself to give to King Francis, King of France, with some very beautiful writings of his own. The King liking this medal beyond measure, that most accomplished man Messer Luigi Alamanni said a few words to his Majesty about my qualities, over and above the art, with such favour that the King showed he wanted to know me.

Pressing on with all the urgency I could with that little model, which I was making exactly the size the work was to be, a good many people in the goldsmiths' trade were roused, who thought themselves fit to do such a thing. And because there had come to Rome a certain Micheletto, a very able man at cutting cornelians, and also a most knowledgeable jeweller — he was an old man and of much reputation, and he had had a hand in looking after the Pope's two tiaras — while I was making this model he wondered a great deal that I had not applied to him, being a knowledgeable man and much in the Pope's credit. At last, seeing that I did not come to him, he came to me, asking what I was doing. "What the Pope has ordered me," I answered. Then he said: "The Pope has ordered me to see all these things that are made for his Holiness." To which I said that I would ask the Pope first, and then I should know what I had to answer him. He told me I would repent of it; and having gone off from me in a rage, he got together with all those of the trade, and, talking about this business, they all gave the charge of it to Michele; who, with that good wit of his, had more than thirty drawings made by certain able draughtsmen, all different from each other, for this piece of work.

And because he had the Pope's ear at his convenience, having come to an understanding with another jeweller called Pompeo, a Milanese (who was much favoured by the Pope, and was a relation of Messer Traiano, the Pope's first chamberlain), these two, that is Michele and Pompeo, began to tell the Pope that they had seen my model, and that it seemed to them I was not an instrument fit for so wonderful a piece of work. At this the Pope said that he had to see it too; and then, if I was not fit, a man would be looked for who was. They both said they had several wonderful drawings on the subject; at which the Pope said he was very glad, but that he did not want to see them before I had finished my model; then he would see everything together.

Within a few days I had finished my model, and taking it up one morning to the Pope, Messer Traiano made me wait, and meanwhile sent in a hurry for Micheletto and Pompeo, telling them to bring their drawings. When they had come, we were let in; on which Michele and Pompeo at once began to spread out their drawings, and the Pope to look at them. And because draughtsmen outside the jeweller's trade do not know the placing of jewels, nor had those who were jewellers taught them — for a jeweller, when figures come in among his jewels, must be able to draw, or nothing good comes of it — so all those drawings had stuck that marvellous diamond in the middle of God the Father's chest.

The Pope, who was of very good wit, seeing such a thing, did not like it at all; and when he had seen ten of them, he threw the rest on the ground and said to me, who was standing at one side: "Show me your model here a moment, Benvenuto, so that I can see whether you are in the same error as these men." I came forward and opened a little round box, and it seemed as if a brightness struck the Pope right in the eyes; and he said in a great voice: "If you had been inside my body you could not have made it otherwise than I see it: these men could not have found any other way of disgracing themselves."

Many great lords came up, and the Pope showed them the difference between my model and their drawings. When he had praised it a good deal, and they stood there frightened and looking foolish, he turned to me and said: "I see one trouble here that is of very great importance. My Benvenuto, wax is easy to work; the whole thing is to do it in gold." To these words I answered boldly: "Most Blessed Father, if I do not do it ten times better than this model of mine, let it be agreed that you do not pay me for it." At these words a great uproar rose among those lords, saying that I was promising too much. There was one of these lords, a very great philosopher, who said in my favour: "From the fine physiognomy and symmetry of body that I see in this young man, I promise myself everything he says, and more." The Pope said: "And so do I believe it too."

Calling that chamberlain of his, Messer Traiano, he told him to bring five hundred gold ducats of the Chamber there. While the money was being waited for, the Pope again considered more slowly the fine way in which I had fitted the diamond with the God the Father. This diamond I had put exactly in the middle of the work, and above the diamond I had fitted God the Father sitting, in a certain fine turned attitude which gave the most beautiful harmony, and did not crowd the stone at all: raising his right hand he was giving the blessing. Under the diamond I had fitted three little boys, who with their arms raised held the diamond up. One of these boys, the middle one, was in full relief; the other two were in half. Round about there were a good number of different boys, fitted in with the other beautiful jewels. The rest of God the Father had a mantle that floated out, out of which came a great many boys, with many other fine ornaments, which made a most beautiful show. This work was made of a white stucco on a black stone.

When the money came, the Pope gave it me with his own hand, and begged me with the greatest pleasantness to manage so that he should have it in his own lifetime, and that it would be good for me.

Chapter 45

Having carried the money and the model away, it seemed to me a thousand years before I could set my hands to it. Having begun at once to work with great urgency, at the end of a week the Pope sent word to me by a chamberlain of his, a very great Bolognese gentleman, that I should come to him and bring what I had worked on. While I was going, this chamberlain, who was the most well-bred person in that Court, told me that the Pope did not only want to see that work, but wanted to give me another of very great importance; and this was the dies for the coins of the mint of Rome; and that I should arm myself to be able to answer his Holiness — that was why he had warned me of it.

I came to the Pope, and spread out before him that plate of gold on which God the Father alone had already been carved, which, sketched out like that, showed more skill than that little wax model; so that the Pope, astonished, said: "From now on I mean to believe everything you say." And having done me a great many extravagant favours, he said: "I want to give you another piece of work, which would be as dear to me as this and dearer, if you have the heart to do it." And having told me that he would be glad to have the dies of his coins made, and asking me whether I had made any before, and whether I had the heart to make them, I said that I very well had the heart for it, and that I had seen how they were made, but that I had never made any.

There being present a certain Messer Tommaso da Prato, who was his Holiness's datary, being a great friend of my enemies, he said: "Most Blessed Father, the favours your Holiness does this young man, and his being extremely bold by nature, are the reason he would promise you a new world; because, having given him a great piece of work, and now adding a greater one to it, they will be the cause of one getting in the way of the other." The Pope turned on him angrily and said that he should attend to his own office; and he ordered me to make a model of a broad gold doubloon on which he wanted a naked Christ with his hands bound, with letters saying "Ecce Homo"; and on the reverse a pope and an emperor together propping up a cross that looked as if it were falling, with letters saying "Unus spiritus et una fides erat in eis" — there was one spirit and one faith in them.

The Pope having ordered me this fine coin, Bandinello the sculptor came up, who had not yet been made a knight, and with his usual presumption dressed up as ignorance said: "These goldsmiths have to be given drawings for such fine things." I turned to him at once and said that I had no need of his drawings for my art; but that I hoped in time to give his art trouble with my drawings. The Pope showed that he was as pleased with these words as anyone can imagine, and turning to me, said: "Go, my Benvenuto, and attend to serving me with a good spirit, and do not lend an ear to the words of these madmen."

So I went off, and very quickly made two dies; and having struck a coin in gold, I took the coin and the dies to the Pope one Sunday after dinner, and when he saw it he was left astonished and pleased — not so much at the fine work, which pleased him beyond measure, but he was made to marvel still more by the speed I had used. And to increase the Pope's satisfaction and wonder, I had brought with me all the old coins that had been made before by those able men who had served Pope Julius and Pope Leo; and seeing that mine gave far more satisfaction, I pulled out of my breast a petition asking for that office of master of the dies of the mint, which office gave six gold scudi a month in salary, quite apart from the dies being paid for by the mint master, who gave three of them to the ducat.

The Pope took my petition and, turning, put it into the datary's hand, telling him to dispatch it for me at once. The datary took the petition, and, wanting to put it in his pocket, said: "Most Blessed Father, your Holiness should not run at it so furiously; these are things that deserve some consideration." Then the Pope said: "I have understood you; give that petition here." And having taken it, he signed it at once with his own hand; then, giving it back to him, he said: "Now there is no more to be said; dispatch it for him now, because that is what I want; and Benvenuto's shoes are worth more than the eyes of all these other blockheads." And so, having thanked his Holiness, I went off to work overjoyed.

Book One, Chapters 46 to 52

Chapter 46

I was still working in the shop of that Raffaello del Moro I mentioned. This decent man had a beautiful young daughter, and he had designs on me for her; and I, having partly noticed it, wanted such a thing myself. But while I had this wish I never showed it in the world at all; on the contrary I behaved so properly that I made them wonder.

It happened that this poor girl got a disease in her right hand which had rotted those two little bones next to the little finger and the one beside it. And because the poor girl was being treated, through her father's carelessness, by an ignorant quack, who said that the poor girl would be left crippled in the whole of her right arm if nothing worse happened to her — seeing the poor father so dismayed, I told him not to believe everything that ignorant doctor said. At which he told me he had no acquaintance with any doctors or surgeons, and begged me, if I knew any, to put him in the way of one.

I at once sent for a certain Master Jacomo of Perugia, a most excellent man in surgery; and when he had seen this poor girl, who was dismayed because she must have got wind of what that ignorant doctor had said, this knowledgeable man said she would have nothing wrong with her and would use her right hand perfectly well, although those two last fingers might be a little weaker than the others; that would give her no trouble in the world.

And having set his hand to treating her, a few days later, when he wanted to eat away a little of the rot on those little bones, the father called me to come and see something too of the operation that had to be done on this girl. So, Master Jacomo having taken certain thick tools, and seeing that with them he was doing very little good and very great pain to the girl, I told the master to stop and to wait for me an eighth of an hour. Running to the shop, I made a little tool of the finest steel, curved, and it scraped. Coming back to the master, he began to work so gently that she felt no pain at all, and in a short time he had finished. For this, among other things, this decent man came to love me more than his two sons; and so he set about curing his beautiful daughter.

I had a very great friendship with a certain Messer Giovanni Gaddi, who was a clerk of the Chamber. This Messer Giovanni took a great delight in the arts, although he had none of them in himself. There lived with him a certain Messer Giovanni, a Greek, a very great scholar; a Messer Lodovico da Fano, a scholar like him; Messer Antonio Allegretti; and at that time Messer Annibal Caro, a young man. From outside there were Messer Bastiano the Venetian, that most excellent painter, and I; and almost every day we saw each other once at Messer Giovanni's. On account of this friendship that decent man Raffaello the goldsmith said to Messer Giovanni: "My dear Messer Giovanni, you know me; and since I should like to give that little daughter of mine to Benvenuto, and can find no better go-between than your lordship, I beg you to help me in it, and yourself to settle out of my means whatever dowry you please."

This scatterbrained man hardly let the poor decent fellow finish before he said to him, without any sense in the world: "Say no more about it, Raffaello, because you are further from it than January is from blackberries." The poor man, much cast down, quickly looked for someone else to marry her to; and the girl's mother and all of them went about sulking at me, and I did not know why. And feeling that they were paying me in bad money for the many kindnesses I had done them, I looked about to open a shop near them. Messer Giovanni said nothing until the girl was married, which happened several months later.

I attended with great urgency to finishing my work and to serving the mint, for the Pope commissioned me again with a coin worth two carlins, on which was a portrait of the head of his Holiness, and on the reverse a Christ on the sea, holding out his hand to Saint Peter, with letters round it saying "Quare dubitasti?" — why did you doubt? This coin pleased so far beyond measure that a certain secretary of the Pope, a man of very great ability, called Il Sanga, said: "Your Holiness may take glory in having a kind of coinage which is not to be seen among the ancients, with all their pomp." To this the Pope answered: "And Benvenuto may take glory in serving an emperor like me, who knows him."

Going on with the great work in gold, I showed it often to the Pope, who kept urging me to see it, and every day he wondered at it more.

Chapter 47

I had a brother in Rome in the service of Duke Alessandro, for whom at this time the Pope had procured the dukedom of Penna. In this Duke's service there were a great many soldiers, decent, brave men from the school of that very great man Signor Giovanni de' Medici, and my brother among them, and the Duke thought as much of him as of any of the bravest of the others.

One day after dinner this brother of mine was in the Banchi, in the shop of a certain Baccino della Croce, where all those bravoes used to take shelter; he had got himself into a chair and was asleep. Just then the sheriff's guard passed by, taking a certain Captain Cisti, a Lombard, to prison — he was also from the school of that great Signor Giovannino, but he was not in the Duke's service. Captain Cattivanza degli Strozzi was in the shop of Baccino della Croce. Captain Cisti, seeing Captain Cattivanza degli Strozzi, said to him: "I was bringing you those few scudi I owed you; if you want them, come and get them before they go to prison with me."

This captain was willing to put other people up to the mark, without caring to try himself; so, finding present there certain very bold young men, more willing than strong for so great an undertaking, he told them to go up to Captain Cisti and get that money of his handed over, and that if the guard resisted they should use force on them, if they had the nerve for it. These young men were only four, all four of them beardless; and the first was called Bertino Aldobrandi, the next Anguillotto of Lucca; of the others I do not remember the names. This Bertino had been brought up as a true pupil of my brother, and my brother loved him as immeasurably as anyone can imagine.

So the four bold young men went up to the sheriff's guard, who were more than fifty constables, with pikes, arquebuses and two-handed swords. In a few words hands were put to weapons, and those four young men pressed the guard so marvellously that if Captain Cattivanza had only shown himself a little, without putting his hand to a weapon, those young men would have put the guard to flight; but they stood too long, and Bertino got certain serious wounds which knocked him to the ground; at the same moment Anguillotto also got a wound in the right arm, so that, not being able to hold his sword any longer, he pulled back as well as he could; the others did the same; Bertino Aldobrandi was picked up off the ground badly wounded.

Chapter 48

While these things were going on, we were all at table, because that morning we had dined more than an hour later than usual. Hearing this noise, one of the sons, the elder, got up from the table to go and see the fight. His name was Giovanni, and I said to him: "Please do not go, because in things like this one always sees a certain loss with nothing to gain." His father said the same to him: "Ah, my son, do not go."

This young man, without listening to anybody, ran down the stairs. When he got to the Banchi, where the great fight was, and saw Bertino picked up off the ground, he ran back; and coming back he met Cecchino my brother, who asked him what it was. Giovanni, having been signalled by some of them not to tell Cecchino, said in his stupidity how it was that Bertino Aldobrandi had been killed by the guard. My poor brother let out such a bellow that it could have been heard ten miles off; then he said to Giovanni: "Alas, could you tell me which of them killed him for me?" Giovanni said yes, and that it was one of them who had a two-handed sword, with a blue feather in his cap.

My poor brother going forward, and having recognised the killer by that mark, threw himself with that marvellous quickness and boldness of his into the middle of the whole guard, and, before anything at all could be done about it, put a thrust into his belly and ran him through to the other side, and with the hilt of his sword pushed him to the ground; then he turned on the others with such skill and daring that he alone put them all to flight. Except that, turning to strike an arquebusier, that man, out of sheer necessity, fired his arquebus and caught the brave, unlucky young man above the knee of his right leg. When he was down on the ground, the guard, half in flight, hurried to get away, so that another like him should not come up.

Hearing the uproar going on, I too got up from the table, and put my sword at my side, which everybody carried in those days; and when I got to the bridge of Sant'Angelo I saw a knot of many men; so I went forward, and being known by some of them, room was made for me, and they showed me what I would least have wanted to see, though I showed great eagerness to see it. At first I did not know him, because he was dressed in clothes different from the ones I had seen him in a little while before; so that, having known me before I knew him, he said: "Dearest brother, do not let my great injury upset you, because my trade promised me such a thing; have me carried away from here quickly, because there are only a few hours of life left."

Being told the case while he was speaking to me, with the shortness such accidents allow, I answered him: "Brother, this is the greatest grief and the greatest sorrow that could come to me in all my life; but be of good heart, because before you lose your sight you shall see your revenge taken by my hands on the man who has hurt you." Our words to each other were of this substance, but very short.

Chapter 49

The guard was fifty paces away from us, because Maffio, their sheriff, had made part of them come back to carry off the corporal my brother had killed; so that, having walked those few paces very fast, wrapped and shut up in my cloak, I had got right beside Maffio, and I should certainly have killed him, because there were plenty of people about and I had got in among them. Already, with as much quickness as anyone can imagine, having half drawn my sword, Berlinghier Berlinghieri, a very brave young man and a great friend of mine, threw himself on me from behind, on my arms; and with him were four other young men like him, who said to Maffio: "Get away, because this man alone was going to kill you." Maffio asked, "Who is this?" And they said: "This is the brother of the man you see there, his own brother." Not wanting to hear any more, he withdrew hastily to the Torre di Nona; and they said to me: "Benvenuto, this hindrance we have put on you against your will has been done for the best; now let us go and help the man who has not long to live."

So, having turned about, we went to my brother, whom I had carried into a house. A council of doctors was called at once and they treated him, not deciding to take the leg right off, which might have saved him.

As soon as he had been treated, Duke Alessandro appeared there and made much of him; and my brother, being still in his senses, said to Duke Alessandro: "My lord, I am sorry for nothing except that your Excellency is losing a servant, than whom you might perhaps find abler men in this profession, but not men who would serve you with as much love and faith as I did." The Duke told him to try to live; as for the rest, he knew him perfectly well for a decent and brave man. Then he turned to certain of his people, telling them that nothing should be wanting to that brave young man.

When the Duke had gone, the abundance of blood, which could not be stopped, was the cause of driving him out of his mind; so that the whole of the following night he raved, except that when they wanted to give him communion he said: "You did well to confess me before; now it is not possible for me to receive this divine sacrament in this instrument that is already spoiled; only be content that I taste it with the divinity of my eyes, by which it will be received by my immortal soul, and that alone asks him for mercy and pardon." When these words were finished and the Sacrament had been taken away, he went straight back to the same madness as before, which was made up of the greatest furies and the most horrible words we could ever imagine; and it never stopped all night until day.

When the sun was out of our horizon, he turned to me and said: "Brother, I do not want to stay here any longer, because these men would make me do something great, and they would repent of having troubled me." And kicking out with both legs, which we had put into a very heavy box, he shifted it as if to get on horseback; and turning his face to me, he said three times: "Goodbye, goodbye" — and with the last word that most valiant soul went away.

At the proper hour, which was late, two hours before dark, I had him buried with the greatest honour in the church of the Florentines; and afterwards I had a very beautiful marble slab made for him, with certain trophies and banners carved on it.

I do not want to leave out that when one of his friends asked him who had given him that arquebus shot, and whether he would recognise him, he said yes, and gave him the marks; and although my brother had been on his guard against my hearing such a thing, I had heard it perfectly well; and what followed will be told in its place.

Chapter 50

To come back to the slab: certain marvellous scholars who knew my brother gave me an epigram, telling me that it was what that admirable young man deserved. It said this: "Francisco Cellino Fiorentino, qui quod in teneris annis ad Ioannem Medicem ducem plures victorias retulit et signifer fuit, facile documentum dedit quantae fortitudinis et consilii vir futurus erat, ni crudelis fati archibuso transfossus quinto aetatis lustro jaceret, Benvenutus frater posuit. Obiit die XXVII Maii MDXXIX." Which is to say: to Francesco Cellini of Florence, who, because in his tender years he won several victories under Giovanni de' Medici his commander, and was his standard-bearer, easily gave proof of what a man of courage and counsel he would have been, had he not been shot through by the arquebus of a cruel fate and lain here in his twenty-fifth year: Benvenuto his brother set this up. He died on the twenty-seventh day of May, 1529.

He was twenty-five; and because he was called among the soldiers Cecchino del Piffero, the piper's boy, when his own name was Giovanfrancesco Cellini, I wanted to put that name he was known by under our arms. I had this name cut in the most beautiful ancient letters; and I had them all made broken, except the first and the last letter. Being asked about those broken letters by the scholars who had made me that fine epigram, why I had done it so, I told them that the letters were broken because that marvellous instrument of his body was spoiled and dead; and that those two whole letters, the first and the last, were: the first, in memory of that great gift God gives us of this soul of ours kindled by his divinity, which is never broken; the other, the last whole one, was for the glorious fame of his brave qualities. This pleased them a great deal, and since then somebody else has made use of this device.

Next I had our Cellini arms carved on the slab, which I altered from what they properly are; because at Ravenna, which is a most ancient city, our Cellini are to be seen as much honoured gentlemen, who have for arms a rampant lion, gold on a blue field, with a red lily set in the right paw, and above it a bar with three little gold lilies. That is the true arms of the Cellini. My father showed me ours, which was the paw alone, with all the rest of those things; but I should prefer that the arms of the Cellini of Ravenna were kept to.

To come back to the one I made on my brother's tomb: it was the lion's paw, and instead of the lily I made it hold an axe, with the field of the arms divided into four quarters; and I made that axe only so that I should not forget to take his revenge.

Chapter 51

I attended with the greatest urgency to finishing that gold work for Pope Clement, which the Pope greatly wanted, and he had me called two and three times a week, wanting to see the work, and it pleased him more every time. And several times he rebuked me, almost shouting at me, for the great sadness I carried about over this brother of mine; and once among others, seeing me more cast down and haggard than I should have been, he said: "Benvenuto, well now — I did not know you were mad. Have you not known before this that there is no remedy for death? You are trying to go after him."

Leaving the Pope, I went on with the work and with the dies for the mint; and for my sweetheart I had taken up the courting of that arquebusier who had shot my brother. This man had once been a light horseman, and afterwards had put himself among the corporals of arquebusiers with the sheriff; and what made my temper rise the most was that he had boasted in this way, saying: "If it had not been for me, who killed that brave young man, at the least delay he alone would have put us all to flight, to our great loss."

Knowing that this passion of seeing him so often was taking away my sleep and my food and was leading me down a bad road, not caring that it was so low an undertaking and not much to be praised, one evening I set myself to get out of so much trouble.

This man lived in a house near a place called Torre Sanguigna, next to a house where there lodged one of the most favoured courtesans of Rome, called Signora Antea. The twenty-fourth hour had just struck, and this arquebusier was standing at his door with his sword in his hand, and had had supper. I came up to him very deftly with a great Pistoja dagger, and swinging a backhand at him, meaning to take his head off clean, he turned very fast as well, and the blow landed on the point of his left shoulder; and having broken the whole bone, he got up, dropped his sword, lost his wits from the great pain and set off running. So, following him, in four paces I caught him, and raising the dagger above his head, he ducking his head hard, the dagger caught exactly the bone of his neck and the middle of the nape, and went into it on both sides so deep that although I made a great effort to get it back I could not; because out of Antea's house four soldiers jumped with their swords drawn in their hands, so that I was forced to put my hand to my sword to defend myself against them.

Leaving the dagger, I got away from there, and for fear of being recognised I went to the house of Duke Alessandro, which was between the Piazza Navona and the Rotonda. When I got there I had word taken to the Duke, who let me know that if I had been alone I should keep quiet and not worry about anything, and that I should go and work on the Pope's piece, which he wanted so much, and work indoors for a week — especially since those soldiers had come up who had hindered me, and had that dagger in their hands, and were telling the thing as it had happened, and the great trouble they had had getting that dagger out of the neck bone and the head of the man, who they did not know who he was. Giovanni Bandini, coming up at this, said to them: "That dagger is mine, and I had lent it to Benvenuto, who wanted to take revenge for his brother." The soldiers had a great deal to say, being sorry they had hindered me, although the revenge had been done by the shovelful.

More than a week went by; the Pope did not send for me as he usually did. Afterwards, having sent for me by that Bolognese gentleman, his chamberlain, whom I have already spoken of, this man with great modesty gave me to understand that the Pope knew everything, and that his Holiness had a very great liking for me, and that I should attend to my work and keep quiet. When I got to the Pope, he looked at me with the eye of a pig, and with his looks alone gave me a frightening scolding; then, having turned his attention to the work, his face began to clear, and he praised me beyond measure, telling me I had done a great deal of work in so short a time; then, looking me in the face, he said: "Now that you are cured, Benvenuto, attend to living." And I, understanding him, said that I would do so.

I opened a very beautiful shop at once in the Banchi, opposite that Raffaello, and there I finished the work a few months afterwards.

Chapter 52

The Pope having sent me all the jewels except the diamond, which for certain needs of his he had pawned to certain Genoese bankers, I kept all the others, and of the diamond I only had the shape.

I kept five very good workmen, and outside this work I did a great deal of business; so that the shop was loaded with a great value of work and of jewels, of gold and of silver. I kept in the house a large, beautiful shaggy dog, which Duke Alessandro had given me; and although this dog was good for hunting, because he brought me every sort of bird and other animal I had killed with the arquebus, he was also most marvellous for guarding a house.

It happened at this time — the season of life I was in promising it, being twenty-nine years old — that I had taken as my servant a young woman of a most beautiful form and grace; and I used her for drawing from, to the purpose of my art, and she also gratified my youth in the delight of the flesh. On that account my room was well away from those of my workmen, and a long way from the shop, and joined by a little closet to a small room of this young servant's; and I very often enjoyed her. And although I have had the lightest sleep that any man in the world ever had, on such occasions of the works of the flesh it sometimes becomes very heavy and deep.

So it happened that one night among others, having been spied on by a thief who, under cover of saying he was a goldsmith, had had his eye on those jewels and planned to steal them from me, he broke into my shop and found a good many little pieces of gold and silver; and while he stood there prising open some boxes to find the jewels he had seen, that dog jumped on him, and he defended himself against him badly with a sword; so that several times the dog ran through the house and into the rooms of my workmen, which were open, it being summer. When they would not hear that great barking of his, he pulled the covers off them; and still not being heard, he took first one and then another by the arm and woke them by force, and barking in that horrible way of his, showed them the way, going on in front of them. And when he saw that they would not follow him, and became a nuisance to these traitors, who threw stones and sticks at the dog — and they could do it, because it was by my orders that they kept the lamp lit all night — at last, having shut their doors tight, the dog, having lost hope of any help from these scoundrels, took the business on all by himself; and running down, and not finding the thief in the shop, he caught up with him; and fighting with him, he had already torn his cloak off him and taken it; and if it had not been that he called for help from some tailors, telling them for the love of God to help him defend himself from a mad dog, and they, believing it was true, jumped out and drove the dog off with great difficulty, it would have gone hard with him.

When day came, my men went down to the shop and saw it broken into and open, and all the boxes broken. They began to shout at the top of their voices: "Alas, alas!" At which, waking up, frightened by that noise, I came out. Coming up to me, they said: "Oh, unlucky us — we have been robbed by a man who has broken open and taken everything!"

These words had such power that they did not let me go to my chest to see whether the Pope's jewels were in it; but out of that anxiety I almost entirely lost the light of my eyes, and told them to open the chest themselves and see how many of the Pope's jewels were missing. These young men were all in their shirts; and when they had opened the chest and saw all the jewels and the gold work along with them, they said to me joyfully: "There is no harm done, since the work and the jewels are all here; although this thief has left us all in our shirts, because last night, with the great heat, we all undressed in the shop and left our clothes there."

My powers coming back at once to their place, and having thanked God, I said: "Go and all of you get dressed again, and I will pay for everything, when I have heard at more leisure how the thing happened."

What grieved me most, and what made me so bewildered and frightened, so far out of my nature, was that the world might have thought that I had made up that pretence of a thief only in order to steal the jewels myself; because Pope Clement had been told by one of his most trusted men and by others — who were Francesco del Nero, Zana de' Biliotti his accountant, the Bishop of Vaison and a good many others of that sort — "How can you trust so great a value of jewels, most Blessed Father, to a young man who is all fire, and is more sunk in weapons than in his art, and is not yet thirty?" To which the Pope answered, did any of them know that I had ever done anything to give them such a suspicion? Francesco del Nero, his treasurer, answered quickly, saying: "No, most Blessed Father, because he has never had such an opportunity." At this the Pope answered: "I hold him for an entirely decent man, and if I saw an evil in him I would not believe it." This was what gave me the greatest trouble, and it came back into my memory at once.

When I had given orders to my young men to get dressed again, I took the work along with the jewels, fitting them into their places as well as I could, and with them I went at once to the Pope, who had been told by Francesco del Nero part of that noise that had been heard in my shop, and had immediately put suspicion into the Pope. The Pope, imagining harm rather than anything else, gave me a terrible look and said in a haughty voice: "What have you come to do here? What is it?" "Here are all your jewels and the gold, and nothing is missing." Then the Pope, his face clearing, said: "So be you welcome."

I showed him the work, and while he was looking at it I told him all the incidents of the thief and of my distress, and what had given me the greatest sorrow. At which words he several times turned to look me hard in the face; and Francesco del Nero was present, on account of which he seemed to be half sorry that he had not been right. At last the Pope, bursting out laughing at all the things I had told him, said to me: "Go, and attend to being a decent man, as I knew you were."

Book One, Chapters 53 to 60

Chapter 53

Pressing on with that work of mine and working continually for the mint, there began to be seen about Rome certain false coins struck with my own dies. They were taken at once to the Pope; and, suspicion having been put on me, the Pope said to Jacopo Balducci, the master of the mint: "Use the greatest diligence to find the criminal, because we know that Benvenuto is a decent man." This treacherous mint master, being an enemy of mine, said: "God grant, most Blessed Father, that it turns out as you say, because we have one or two things that point elsewhere." At this the Pope turned to the governor of Rome and told him to use a little diligence in finding this criminal.

In those days the Pope sent for me; then, with a neat conversation, he came round to the coins, and very much to the point said to me: "Benvenuto, would you have the heart to make false coins?" To which I answered that I believed I could make them better than any of the men who attended to such a low business; because those who attend to such laziness are not men who know how to earn, nor are they men of great talent; and if I with my little talent earned so much that I had a surplus — for when I put dies into the mint, every morning before I dined it fell to me to earn three scudi at the least (for that had always been the custom of paying for the coining dies, and that fool of a mint master hated me because he would have liked to have them cheaper) — what I earned by the grace of God and of the world was quite enough for me; and I should not have earned so much by making false coins.

The Pope took the words in perfectly; and, whereas he had given orders that they should carefully take care that I did not leave Rome, he told them to search diligently and not to bother about me, because he would not have liked to anger me, which might have been the cause of losing me. The men he warmly commissioned were certain clerks of the Chamber, who, having made the proper searches, since it fell to them, found him at once. It was a die-stamper of the mint itself, called by name Cesare Macheroni, a Roman citizen; and along with him a moulder of the mint was taken.

Chapter 54

On this same day, as I was going through the Piazza Navona with that beautiful poodle of mine, when I came in front of the sheriff's door my dog with the greatest violence, barking loudly, threw himself inside the door of the sheriff's house onto a young man who had been held there a while at the instance of a certain Donnino, a goldsmith of Parma, once a pupil of Caradosso, on suspicion of having robbed him. This dog of mine made such an effort to tear this young man to pieces that the constables were moved to pity — above all as the young man boldly defended his own case, and Donnino did not say enough to be convincing, all the more so as one of the corporals of the constables was a Genoese who knew the young man's father; so that, between the dog and these other circumstances, they were behaving as if they meant to let the young man go at all costs.

When I had come up, the dog, knowing no fear of sword or stick, threw himself on the young man again; and they told me that if I did not do something about my dog they would kill him for me. Having got hold of the dog as well as I could, as the young man pulled his cloak round him, certain little papers fell out of the hood; by which Donnino recognised them as his own things. I also recognised a little ring; so I said at once: "This is the thief who broke into and robbed my shop; that is why my dog knows him." And letting the dog go, he threw himself on him again; at which the thief begged my mercy, telling me he would give me back what he had of mine. Having got hold of the dog again, the man gave me back in gold and silver and little rings what he had of mine, and twenty-five scudi over and above; and then he begged my mercy. At which words I told him to beg God's mercy, because I would do him neither good nor harm.

And going back to my business, a few days later that Cesare Macheroni of the false coins was hanged in the Banchi in front of the mint door; his companion was sent to the galleys; the Genoese thief was hanged in the Campo di Fiore; and I was left with a greater name for being a decent man than I had before.

Chapter 55

Having my work almost finished, there came that very great flood which overflowed the whole of Rome with water. I was standing watching what it was doing; the day was already spent, the twenty-second hour had struck, and the waters were rising beyond measure. And because the front of my house and shop was in the Banchi and the back rose several arms' lengths, since it came out towards Monte Giordano, so that, thinking first of the safety of my life and then of my honour, I put all those jewels on me and left the gold work with my workmen to guard; and so, barefoot, I got down out of my back windows, and as well as I could I went through those waters until I got to Monte Cavallo, where I found Messer Giovanni Gaddi, clerk of the Chamber, and Bastiano the Venetian, the painter. Going up to Messer Giovanni, I gave him all those jewels to keep for me; and he looked after me as if I had been his brother.

Then, a few days later, when the fury of the water had passed, I came back to my shop and finished the work with such good fortune, by the grace of God and my own great labours, that it was held to be the most beautiful work that had ever been seen in Rome; so that when I took it to the Pope he could not have enough of praising it, and said: "If I were a rich emperor, I would give my Benvenuto as much land as his eye could travel over; but since these days we are poor bankrupt emperors, all the same we shall give him enough bread to satisfy his small appetites."

When I had let the Pope finish that fit of words, I asked him for a mace-bearer's place that had fallen vacant. At which words the Pope said he wanted to give me something of much greater importance. I answered his Holiness that he should give me that little one meanwhile, as an earnest. Bursting out laughing, he said he was content, but that he did not want me to serve, and that I should come to an agreement with my fellow mace-bearers not to serve, by giving them some favour they had already asked the Pope for, which was to be able to collect their income with authority. That was done. This mace-bearer's place brought me in a little less than two hundred scudi a year of income.

Chapter 56

Going on afterwards to serve the Pope, now with one small piece and now with another, he ordered me to make him a design for a very rich chalice; which I made, the design and the model. This model was of wood and wax; instead of the knop of the chalice I had made three little figures of a good size, in the round, which were Faith, Hope and Charity; then on the foot I had made three matching scenes in three roundels in low relief: in one was the Nativity of Christ, in the other the Resurrection of Christ, in the third Saint Peter crucified head downward; for so I had been ordered to make it.

While I was carrying this work forward, the Pope very often wanted to see it; so that, noticing that his Holiness had never remembered afterwards to give me anything, when a friar of the Piombo's place fell vacant I asked him for it one evening. The good Pope, no longer remembering the fit he had had at the end of that other work, said to me: "The office of the Piombo brings in more than eight hundred scudi, so that if I gave it to you, you would attend to scratching your belly, and that fine art you have in your hands would be lost, and I should have the blame for it."

I answered at once that cats of a good sort catch better when they are fat than when they are hungry: "And so that sort of decent men who are inclined to skill put it into practice much better when they have abundant means to live on; so that those princes who keep such men in abundance, your Holiness should know, are watering their skills; and the other way about, skills are born starved and mangy. And your Holiness should know that I did not ask for it meaning to get it. I was lucky enough to get that poor mace-bearer's place! I imagined as much about this. Your Holiness will do well, not having wanted to give it me, to give it to some able man who deserves it, and not to some great ignoramus who will attend to scratching his belly, as your Holiness said. Take an example from the good memory of Pope Julius, who gave such an office to Bramante, that most excellent architect." And having at once made him a bow, I went off in a fury.

Bastiano the Venetian, the painter, came forward and said: "Most Blessed Father, may your Holiness be pleased to give it to someone who works hard at skilful things; and because, as your Holiness knows, I too willingly work hard at them, I beg you to make me worthy of it." The Pope answered: "This devil Benvenuto will not listen to a reproof. I was disposed to give it to him, but it is not right to be so proud with a Pope; so I do not know what I shall do."

The Bishop of Vaison at once came forward and pleaded for Bastiano, saying: "Most Blessed Father, Benvenuto is young, and a sword suits him much better at his side than a friar's habit; may your Holiness be pleased to give it to this able man Bastiano; and to Benvenuto you can give something good another time, which will perhaps suit him better than this."

Then the Pope, turning to Messer Bartolomeo Valori, said to him: "When you meet Benvenuto, tell him from me that he himself has got the Piombo for Bastiano the painter; and that he should take notice that the first better thing that falls vacant shall be his; and that meanwhile he should attend to doing well, and finish my works."

The next evening, at two hours of the night, I met Messer Bartolomeo Valori on the corner of the mint: he had two torches in front of him and was going in a hurry, having been sent for by the Pope; and when I had bowed to him he stopped and called me, and told me with the greatest affection everything the Pope had told him to say to me. To which words I answered that I would finish my work with greater diligence and study than any of the others; but with no hope at all of ever getting anything from the Pope. Messer Bartolomeo rebuked me, telling me that one should not answer a Pope's offers like that. To whom I said that, putting my hope in such words when I knew I should not get anything anyway, I should be mad to answer otherwise; and, leaving him, I went off to attend to my business.

Messer Bartolomeo must have repeated my bold words to the Pope, and perhaps more than I said, so that the Pope let more than two months go by without calling me, and I in all that time would not go up to the palace for anything. The Pope, who was eating his heart out over that work, ordered Messer Roberto Pucci to keep an eye on what I was doing. This good big man came to see me every day, and always said some affectionate word to me, and I to him.

The Pope being about to leave for Bologna, at last, seeing that I would not go there of my own accord, he let me know through Messer Roberto that I should bring the work up, because he wanted to see how far I had got. So I brought it, showing that the important part of it was all done, and I begged him to let me have five hundred scudi, partly on account and partly because I was a good deal short of the gold to finish the work with. The Pope said to me: "Attend, attend to finishing it." I answered as I left that I would finish it if he let me have money. And so I went off.

Chapter 57

The Pope, having gone off to Bologna, left Cardinal Salviati as legate of Rome, and left him orders to press me about this work, and said to him: "Benvenuto is a person who thinks little of his own skill, and less of Us; so see that you press him, so that I find it finished."

This beast of a cardinal sent for me at the end of a week, telling me to bring the work up; and I went to him without the work. When I got there, this Cardinal said to me at once: "Where is this onion stew of yours? Have you finished it?" To which I answered: "Most reverend monsignor, I have not finished my onion stew, and I shall not finish it unless you give me some onions to finish it with."

At these words the Cardinal, who had more the face of an ass than of a man, became half as ugly again; and coming at me at once with half a sword, he said: "I shall put you in a galley, and then it will be a favour to you to finish the work." I too became a beast with this beast, and said to him: "Monsignor, when I commit sins that deserve the galleys, then you shall put me there; but for these sins I am not afraid of your galleys; and I tell you more, that on account of your lordship I do not ever mean to finish it; and do not ever send for me again, because I shall never come in front of you again, unless you have me brought by the constables."

The good Cardinal tried several times, affectionately, to let me know that I ought to work and ought to bring it to show him; so that to such messengers I said: "Tell monsignor to send me some onions, if he wants me to finish the onion stew." And I never answered them any other words; so that he gave up that hopeless charge.

Chapter 58

The Pope came back from Bologna and asked for me at once, because that Cardinal had already written him the worst he could about my affairs. The Pope being in the greatest fury anyone can imagine, he let me know that I should come with the work. So I did.

During the time the Pope was at Bologna, a rheum had come on me with so much trouble in my eyes that I could hardly live for the pain; so that this was the first reason why I did not carry the work forward. And the trouble was so great that I certainly thought I should be left blind; so that I had made my reckoning of what would be enough to live on as a blind man.

While I was going to the Pope, I was thinking of the way I ought to take to make my excuse for not having been able to carry the work forward. I thought that while the Pope was looking at it and considering it I could tell him how things were; which did not come about, because when I got to him he said at once, in coarse words: "Give me that work here; is it finished?" I uncovered it; at once, in a greater fury, he said: "In God's truth I tell you, who make a profession of taking account of nobody, that if it were not for the honour of the world I would have you thrown out of those windows together with that work."

At which, seeing the Pope turned into such a very bad beast, I made haste to get out of his sight. While he was going on with his bullying, having put the work under my cloak, I muttered: "The whole world could not make a blind man work at such things." Raising his voice louder, the Pope said: "Come here; what are you saying?" I stood in two minds about setting off running down those stairs; then I made up my mind, and threw myself on my knees, shouting loudly, because he did not stop shouting, and said: "And if I have gone blind through an illness, am I obliged to work?" At this he said: "You saw well enough to come here, and I do not believe any of these things you say are true." To which I said, hearing him lower his voice somewhat: "Let your Holiness ask his own doctor, and he will find the truth." He said: "We shall hear at more leisure whether it is as you say."

Then, seeing that I was being given a hearing, I said: "I do not believe that this great illness of mine was caused by anyone but Cardinal Salviati, because he sent for me as soon as your Holiness had gone; and when I came to him he called my work an onion stew, and told me he would have me finish it in a galley; and there was so much power in those indecent words that out of the extreme passion I felt my face take fire at once, and there came into my eyes so immeasurable a heat that I could not find my way home; and a few days later two cataracts fell over my eyes, so that I could not see any light at all, and since your Holiness left I have never been able to work at anything."

Getting up off my knees, I went off with God; and it was reported to me that the Pope said: "If offices are given, discretion cannot be given with them. I did not tell the Cardinal to lay on so hard: if it is true that he has trouble with his eyes, which I shall hear from my doctor, one ought to have some compassion for him."

There was present a great gentleman, a great friend of the Pope's and a very able man. The Pope having asked him what person I was, he said: "Most Blessed Father, I ask you, because it has seemed to me that you have come in one and the same moment into the greatest anger I ever saw and the greatest compassion; so that for this reason I ask your Holiness who he is; because if he is a person who deserves to be helped, I would teach him a secret to cure him of that illness." At these words the Pope said: "That is the greatest man who was ever born in his profession; and one day when we are together I will show you some of his marvellous works, and him with them; and I shall be glad to see whether he can be done any good."

Three days later the Pope sent for me one day after dinner, and this gentleman was there. As soon as I had arrived, the Pope had that clasp of mine for the cope brought. In the meantime I had taken out that chalice of mine; on account of which that gentleman said he had never seen so marvellous a work. When the clasp came up, his wonder grew much greater; and looking me in the face he said: "He is young indeed to know so much, and very fit to gain more." Then he asked me my name. To which I said: "Benvenuto is my name." He answered: "I shall be a Benvenuto — a welcome one — to you this time. Take cornflowers with the stalk, the flower and the roots all together, then have them distilled over a gentle fire, and with that water bathe your eyes several times a day, and you will certainly be cured of that illness; but have yourself purged first, and then go on with the water."

The Pope used some affectionate words to me: and so I went off half content.

Chapter 59

It was true that I had the illness, but I believe I had earned it by means of that beautiful young servant I kept at the time I was robbed. The French disease waited more than four whole months before showing itself to me, and then it covered me all over, all at once: it was not in the way of the other kind you see, but it seemed as if I were covered with certain little blisters, as big as farthings, and red. The doctors would never baptise it a French disease for me; and I kept telling them the causes I believed it came from. I went on treating myself in their way, and nothing did me any good; until at last, having made up my mind to take the wood against the wishes of those first doctors of Rome, I took this wood with all the discipline and abstinence anyone can imagine, and in a few days I felt the greatest improvement; so that at the end of fifty days I was cured and as sound as a fish.

Afterwards, to give myself some refreshment after the great labour I had gone through, winter coming on, I took to shooting for my pleasure, which made me go out in the wet and the wind and stand about in the marshes; so that in a few days there came back on me a trouble a hundred times greater than the one I had had before. Having put myself back in the doctors' hands, and being continually treated, I always got worse. When the fever jumped on me, I decided to take the wood again: the doctors would not have it, telling me that if I went into it with a fever I should die in a week. I decided to go against their wishes; and keeping the same regime I had kept the other time, when I had drunk four days of this holy water of the wood the fever went away entirely.

I began to take a very great improvement; and while I was taking the wood I always carried forward the models for that work; and in that abstinence I made the most beautiful things and the rarest inventions I ever made in my life. At the end of fifty days I was thoroughly cured, and afterwards I attended with the greatest diligence to making my health secure. When I had come out of that great fasting, I found myself as clean of my illnesses as if I had been born again. Although I took pleasure in making that health of mine that I so wanted secure, I did not stop working either; so that in that work and in the mint I certainly gave each of them its proper share.

Chapter 60

It happened that that Cardinal Salviati, who had that great hatred of me I have mentioned, was made legate of Parma. In Parma a certain Milanese goldsmith was taken who was a forger of coins, whose name was Tobbia. Having been sentenced to the gallows and the fire, the Legate was spoken to about it, and he was put in front of him as a very able man. The Cardinal had the execution of the sentence held over, and wrote to Pope Clement, telling him that there had come into his hands one of the greatest men in the world in the profession of the goldsmith's art, and that he had already been condemned to the gallows and the fire for being a forger of coins; but that this man was simple and good, because he said he had asked the opinion of a confessor of his, who, he said, had given him leave to make them. And more than that he said: "If you have this great man come to Rome, your Holiness will be the cause of taking down that great haughtiness of your Benvenuto's; and I am quite certain that this Tobbia's works will please you far more than Benvenuto's."

So that the Pope had him come to Rome at once. And when he had come, he called us both and had each of us make a design for a unicorn's horn, the most beautiful that was ever seen: it had been sold for seventeen thousand ducats of the Chamber. The Pope wanting to give it to King Francis, he wanted it richly mounted in gold first, and ordered both of us to make the designs.

When we had made them, each of us took his to the Pope. Tobbia's design was in the shape of a candlestick, where, like a candle, that beautiful horn was fitted in; and for the foot of this candlestick he made four little unicorn heads, with the simplest invention: so that when I saw such a thing I could not help sniggering a little in a neat way. The Pope noticed it and said at once: "Show your design here." Mine was a single unicorn's head, matching the horn. I had made the finest sort of head that could be seen, and the reason was that I had taken part of the make of a horse's head and part of a stag's, enriched with the finest sort of mane and other elegances; so that as soon as mine was seen everybody gave it the palm.
