But because there were present at this dispute certain Milanese of very great authority, they said: "Most Blessed Father, your Holiness is sending this great present into France; you should know that the French are coarse men and will not recognise the excellence of this work of Benvenuto's; but this ciborium sort of thing will please them well enough, and it will also be made sooner; and Benvenuto will attend to finishing your chalice, and you will get two works done at the same time; and this poor man whom you have had come here will get employed as well."

The Pope, wanting his chalice, very willingly caught at the advice of those Milanese; so the next day he assigned the work on that unicorn's horn to Tobbia, and let me know by his wardrobe keeper that I should finish his chalice for him. To which words I answered that I wanted nothing else in the world but to finish that beautiful work of mine; but that if it had been of any other material than gold, I could very easily have finished it myself; but since it was of gold as it was, his Holiness would have to give me some, if he wanted me to be able to finish it. At these words this vulgar courtier said: "Good heavens, do not ask the Pope for gold, because you will put him in such a rage that it will go badly, badly with you." To which I said: "Oh, sir, will your lordship teach me a little how one can make bread without flour? Just so, without gold that work will never be finished." This wardrobe keeper, thinking somewhat that I had made fun of him, told me he would report everything I had said to the Pope; and so he did.

The Pope, going into a bestial fury, said he wanted to see whether I was such a madman as not to finish it. So more than two months went by; and although I had said I did not want to lift a finger to it, I had not done that, but on the contrary had worked at it continually with the greatest love. Seeing that I did not bring it, he began to show me a great deal of disfavour, saying he would punish me at all costs.

There was present at these words a Milanese jeweller of his called Pompeo, who was a close relation of a certain Messer Traiano, the most favoured servant Pope Clement had. These two agreed together and said to the Pope: "If your Holiness were to take the mint away from him, perhaps you would give him the wish to finish the chalice." Then the Pope said: "On the contrary, there would be two evils: one, that I should be badly served in the mint, which matters so much to me; and the other, that I should certainly never get the chalice."

These two Milanese, seeing the Pope ill disposed towards me, in the end had so much power that he did take the mint from me and gave it to a certain young Perugian called Fagiuolo for a nickname. Pompeo came to tell me, on the Pope's behalf, that his Holiness had taken the mint from me, and that if I did not finish the chalice he would take other things from me. To this I answered: "Tell his Holiness that he has taken the mint from himself and not from me, and that the same would happen with those other things; and that when his Holiness wants to give it back to me, I shall not want it back in any way."

This graceless and unlucky man thought a thousand years had gone by before he could get to the Pope to repeat all these things to him, and he put something in out of his own mouth. A week later the Pope sent by this same man to tell me that he no longer wanted me to finish that chalice for him, and that he wanted it back exactly in the way and at the point to which I had brought it. To this Pompeo I answered: "This is not like the mint, that he can take from me; but the five hundred scudi I had are his Holiness's, and I shall give them back at once; and the work is mine, and I shall do with it what I please." Pompeo ran off to report that, with some other biting words which I had said to him, and with good reason.

Book One, Chapters 61 to 65

Chapter 61

Three days later, on a Thursday, two of his Holiness's most favoured chamberlains came to me — one of them is still alive today and is a bishop, and was called Messer Pier Giovanni, and was his Holiness's wardrobe keeper; the other was of even greater family than he, but I do not remember his name. When they came to me they said: "The Pope sends us, Benvenuto: since you would not understand him by the easier road, he says either that you give us his work, or that we take you to prison."

Then I looked them in the face very cheerfully and said: "Gentlemen, if I gave the work to his Holiness, I should be giving him my work and not his; and I do not want to give him my work either, because, having brought it a long way forward with my great labour, I do not want it to go into the hands of some ignorant beast who will spoil it for me with little trouble." There was present, while I said this, that goldsmith called Tobbia I have mentioned, who recklessly asked me for the models of the work as well: the words I said to such a wretch, worthy of him, need not be repeated here.

And because those gentlemen chamberlains were pressing me to make up my mind what I wanted to do, I told them I had made it up. Having taken my cloak, and before I went out of my shop, I turned to an image of Christ with great reverence and with my cap in my hand, and said: "O our kind and immortal, just and holy Lord, everything you do is according to your justice, which is without equal. You know that I am just arriving at the age of thirty in my life, and that never until now has prison been promised me for anything: since now you are content that I should go to prison, I thank you for it with all my heart."

Then, turning to the two chamberlains, I said this, with a certain rather ruffled look of mine: "A man like me did not deserve constables of less worth than you gentlemen; so put me between you and take me as a prisoner where you like." Those two most well-bred men burst out laughing, put me between them, and, talking pleasantly the whole way, took me to the Governor of Rome, who was called Il Magalotto.

When I got to him, the fiscal Procurator was there with him, and they were waiting for me; and those gentlemen chamberlains, still laughing, said to the Governor: "We hand you over this prisoner, and take good care of him. We have been very glad to take the office from your officers, because Benvenuto has told us that since this is his first arrest, he did not deserve constables of less worth than we are." As soon as they had gone they came to the Pope; and having told him precisely everything, at first he made as if to fly into a fury, and afterwards forced himself to laugh, because there were present some lords and cardinals who were friends of mine and who greatly favoured me.

Meanwhile the Governor and the Fiscal partly bullied me, partly exhorted me and partly advised me, telling me that reason required that a man who has a work made by another can take it back at his pleasure, and in any way he likes. To which things I said that this was not what justice promised, and that a pope could not do it; because he was not a pope of the sort those little tyrant lordlings are, who do the worst they can to their people, keeping neither law nor justice: therefore a Vicar of Christ cannot do any of these things.

Then the Governor, with certain constable-like ways and words, said: "Benvenuto, Benvenuto, you are asking me to do to you what you deserve." "You will do me honour and courtesy if you do to me what I deserve." Again he said: "Send for the work at once, and see that you do not wait for a second word." To this I said: "Gentlemen, do me the favour of letting me say four more words about my case."

The Fiscal, who was a much more discreet constable than the Governor, turned to the Governor and said: "Monsignor, let us do him the favour of a hundred words; as long as he gives up the work, that is quite enough for us."

I said: "If there were any sort of man who was having a palace or a house built, he could justly say to the master who was building it, 'I do not want you to work on my house or my palace any more'; and paying him justly for his labour, he can send him away. Again, if there were a lord who was having a jewel of a thousand scudi set, and saw that the jeweller was not serving him according to his wish, he can say, 'Give me my jewel, because I do not want your work.' But in this present business there is none of these heads; because it is neither a house nor a jewel; nothing else can be said to me except that I should give back the five hundred scudi I have had. So, monsignori, do everything you can, because you will get nothing else from me but the five hundred scudi. And so you may tell the Pope. Your threats do not frighten me in the world at all; because I am a decent man, and I am not afraid of my sins."

The Governor and the Fiscal stood up and told me they were going to the Pope, and that they would come back with orders, and that it would go badly with me. So I was left under guard. I walked about a hall, and they were nearly three hours coming back from the Pope. In the meantime all the nobility of our nation of merchants came to visit me, begging me urgently not to stand and argue with a Pope, because it might be my ruin. To whom I answered that I had quite made up my mind about what I wanted to do.

Chapter 62

As soon as the Governor and the Fiscal had come back from the Palace, having had me called, he spoke in this strain: "Benvenuto, certainly I am sorry to have come back from the Pope with such orders as I have; so either you find the work at once, or you look to your own affairs." Then I answered that, since I had never believed until that hour that a holy Vicar of Christ could do an injustice, "I want to see it before I believe it; so do what you can."

Again the Governor spoke, saying: "I have two more words to say to you from the Pope, and then I shall carry out the orders given me. The Pope says that you should bring the work here to me, and that I should see it put in a box and sealed; then I am to take it to the Pope, who promises on his faith not to move it from its seal, but to keep it shut, and will give it straight back to you; but he wants it done this way so that he too may have his share of the honour."

At these words I answered, laughing, that I would very willingly give him my work in the way he said, because I wanted to be able to say what a Pope's faith is made of. And so, having sent for my work and sealed it up in the way he said, I gave it him.

The Governor having gone back to the Pope with the work in that way, the Pope took the box — according to what the Governor told me — and turned it over several times; then he asked the Governor whether he had seen it, and he said he had seen it and that it had been sealed like that in his presence; then he added that it had seemed to him a very marvellous thing. At which the Pope said: "You will tell Benvenuto that Popes have authority to loose and to bind much greater things than this." And while he was saying these words, with some little indignation he opened the box, taking off the cords and the seal it had been tied with; then he looked at it a long while, and as far as I gathered he showed it to that goldsmith Tobbia, who praised it a great deal. Then the Pope asked him whether he had the nerve to make a work in that way; the Pope told him to follow exactly that arrangement; then he turned to the Governor and said to him: "See whether Benvenuto will give it to us; because if he gives it us like this he shall be paid everything it is valued at by able men; or else, if he really wants to finish it himself, let him name a term; and if you see that he wants to do it, let him be given the reasonable facilities he asks for."

Then the Governor said: "Most Blessed Father, knowing the terrible quality of that young man, give me authority to give him a good dressing-down in my own way." At this the Pope said he should do what he liked with words, although he was sure he would make it worse; then, when he saw he could do nothing else, he should tell me to take his five hundred scudi to that Pompeo, his jeweller mentioned above.

The Governor came back and had me called into his room, and with a constable's look he said: "Popes have authority to loose and to bind the whole world, and it is immediately confirmed in Heaven as well done: there is your work, loosed and seen by his Holiness." Then I raised my voice at once and said: "I thank God, for now I know what a Pope's faith is made of." Then the Governor said and did a great deal of extravagant bullying; and afterwards, seeing that it came to nothing at all, quite despairing of the business, he took up a somewhat sweeter manner and said to me: "Benvenuto, I am very sorry that you will not understand your own good; so go, and take the five hundred scudi when you like to the Pompeo I mentioned."

Having taken my work, I went off and at once took the five hundred scudi to Pompeo. And because the Pope, perhaps thinking that through inconvenience or some other occasion I should not bring the money so soon, wanting to tie up the thread of my service again — when he saw that Pompeo came before him smiling with the money in his hand, the Pope spoke rudely to him and was very sorry that the thing had turned out that way; then he said to him: "Go and find Benvenuto in his shop, and make as much of him as your ignorant beastliness can; and tell him that if he will finish that work for me to make a reliquary of it, to carry the Corpus Domini in when I go in procession with it, I shall give him the facilities he wants to finish it; as long as he works."

Pompeo came to me, called me out of the shop, and made me the sickliest donkey's caresses, telling me everything the Pope had ordered him. To which I answered at once that the greatest treasure I could wish for in the world was to have got back the favour of so great a Pope, which had been lost by me not through my own fault, but through the fault of my immeasurable illness, and through the wickedness of those envious men who take pleasure in doing harm; "and since the Pope has plenty of servants, let him not send you round me any more, for your own health's sake; so mind your own business. I shall never fail, night or day, to think of and do everything I can in the Pope's service; and remember well that when you have told this about me to the Pope, you must in no way mix yourself up in any of my affairs, because I shall make you know your errors by the penance they deserve."

This man reported everything to the Pope in a much more beastly way than I had put it to him. So the business stood for a while, and I attended to my shop and my own affairs.

Chapter 63

That goldsmith Tobbia mentioned above attended to finishing that setting and ornament for the unicorn's horn; and besides that the Pope had told him to begin the chalice in the way he had seen mine. And when he began to have Tobbia show him what he was doing, finding himself badly satisfied, he was very sorry to have broken with me, and blamed that man's works and the men who had put him forward; and several times Baccino della Croce came to speak to me on the Pope's behalf, that I should make that reliquary. To whom I said that I begged his Holiness to let me rest after the great illness I had had, of which I was not yet quite out of danger; but that I would show his Holiness that in the hours in which I could work, I would spend them all in his service.

I had set myself to take his portrait, and I was making him a medal in secret; and those steel dies to strike the medal I made at home; and in my shop I kept a partner who had been my apprentice, called Felice.

At this time, as young men do, I had fallen in love with a little Sicilian girl who was very beautiful; and because she too showed that she was very fond of me, her mother, noticing it, and suspecting what might come of it — for I had arranged to run away with the girl for a year to Florence, in the greatest secrecy from her mother — noticing it, she left Rome secretly one night and went off towards Naples; and she gave out that she had gone from Civitavecchia, and she went from Ostia. I went after her to Civitavecchia and did unreckonable mad things to find her. It would be too long to tell such things exactly: it is enough that I was on the point either of going mad or of dying. At the end of two months she wrote to me that she was in Sicily and very unhappy. In this time I had attended to all the pleasures anyone can imagine, and had taken up another love, only to put out the first one.

Chapter 64

It happened to me, through certain odd chances, that I made friends with a certain Sicilian priest, who was of the loftiest wit and had a very good knowledge of Latin and Greek letters. Coming once to the point in a conversation where we happened to speak of the art of necromancy, I said: "I have had the greatest wish all my life to see or hear something of this art." At which words the priest added: "A man who sets himself to such an undertaking must have a strong and steady mind." I answered that I should have more than enough strength and steadiness of mind, if only I could find a way to do such a thing. Then the priest answered: "If you have the nerve for that, I shall satisfy you fully as to all the rest." And so we agreed to make a beginning of the business.

One evening among others the priest got himself in order, and told me to find a companion or two. I called Vincenzio Romoli, a very great friend of mine, and he brought with him a man of Pistoia who also went in for necromancy. We went off to the Colosseum, and there the priest, having dressed himself in the manner of a necromancer, set about drawing circles on the ground with the finest ceremonies anyone in the world could imagine; and he had made us bring precious perfumes and fire, and foul perfumes too. When he was ready, he made the door to the circle; and taking us by the hand, he put us into the circle one by one; then he shared out the duties: he gave the pentacle into the hand of that other companion of his, the necromancer, and to the others he gave the care of the fire for the perfumes; then he set his hand to the conjurations.

This thing lasted more than an hour and a half; several legions appeared, so that the Colosseum was quite full. I, who was attending to the precious perfumes, when the priest knew there was so great a quantity of them, turned to me and said: "Benvenuto, ask them for something." I said they should bring it about that I might be with my Sicilian Angelica. That night we had no answer at all; but I had very great satisfaction from what I had wanted of such a thing.

The necromancer said we must go there another time, and that I should be satisfied in everything I asked for, but that he wanted me to bring a virgin boy with me. I took an errand boy of mine of about twelve, and called that Vincenzio Romoli again with me; and, a certain Agnolino Gaddi being a familiar companion of ours, we brought him too to this business.

Having got to the appointed place again, the necromancer having made the same preparations with the same arrangement and even more marvellous, he put us into the circle, which he had made again with more admirable art and more admirable ceremonies; then he gave my Vincenzio the care of the perfumes and the fire, and Agnolino Gaddi took it with him; then he put the pentacle into my hand, which he told me to turn according to the places he signalled to me, and under the pentacle I held that little errand boy of mine.

The necromancer began to make those most terrible invocations, calling by name a great quantity of those demons who are heads of the legions, and commanding them by the power and might of the uncreated, living and eternal God, in Hebrew words, and a good many Greek and Latin ones as well; so that in a short space of time the whole Colosseum was filled a hundred times more than they had been that first time. Vincenzio Romoli attended to making fire together with Agnolino, and a great quantity of precious perfumes.

I, by the necromancer's advice, again asked to be able to be with Angelica. The necromancer turned to me and said: "Do you hear what they have said? That within a month you will be where she is." And again he added that he begged me to hold him firm, because the legions were a thousand times more than he had asked for, and were the most dangerous; and that now they had settled what I had asked for, it was necessary to make much of them and dismiss them patiently.

On the other side the boy, who was under the pentacle, said in the greatest terror that there were a million of the fiercest men in that place, all threatening us; and besides that he said that four measureless giants had appeared, who were armed and were making as if to come in among us. At this the necromancer, who was trembling with fear, attended as best he could, in a sweet and gentle way, to dismissing them. Vincenzio Romoli, who was shaking like a reed, attended to the perfumes. I, who was as frightened as they were, made an effort to show it less, and gave them all the most marvellous heart; but certainly I had given myself up for dead, from the fear I saw in the necromancer.

The boy had stuck his head between his knees, saying: "I want to die this way, because we are dead men." Again I said to the boy: "These creatures are all beneath us, and what you see is smoke and shadow; so lift up your eyes." When he had lifted his eyes, he said again: "The whole Colosseum is burning, and the fire is coming at us"; and putting his hands over his face, he said again that he was dead, and that he would not look any more.

The necromancer commended himself to me, begging me to hold him firm, and to have perfumes of asafoetida made; so, turning to Vincenzio Romoli, I told him to perfume with asafoetida quickly. While I was saying this, looking at Agnolino Gaddi, who was so terrified that the light of his eyes had gone out of focus, and he was more than half dead, I said to him: "Agnolo, in places like this one must not be afraid, but must set about doing something and helping oneself; so put some of that asafoetida on quickly." As Agnolo was about to move, he let off a blast of wind with such an abundance of shit that it had more power than the asafoetida. The boy, at that great stink and that noise, lifted his face a little, and hearing me laugh a bit, was reassured a little in his fear and said that they were beginning to go off in a great hurry.

So we stayed on until the bells began to ring for matins. Again the boy told us that few of them were left, and far off. When the necromancer had done all the rest of his ceremonies and had undressed and packed up a great bundle of books he had brought, we all came out of the circle together with him, sticking one under another; above all the boy, who had got into the middle and had taken hold of the necromancer by his robe and of me by my cloak. And all the time, while we were going home towards the Banchi, he kept telling us that two of the ones he had seen in the Colosseum were skipping along in front of us, now running along the roofs and now on the ground.

The necromancer said that of all the times he had gone into circles, so great a thing had never happened to him; and he tried to persuade me to be willing to be with him in consecrating a book, from which we should draw endless riches, because we should ask the demons to teach us where the treasures are that the earth is full of, and in that way we should become extremely rich; and that these things about love were vanities and follies which came to nothing.

I told him that if I knew Latin letters I would very willingly do such a thing. Still he tried to persuade me, telling me that Latin letters would be no use to me at all, and that if he had wanted he could have found plenty of men with good Latin letters; but that he had never found anyone with as steady a mind as mine, and that I ought to follow his advice. With this talk we got home, and each of us dreamed of devils all that night.

Chapter 65

Seeing each other afterwards day by day, the necromancer pressed me to attend to that undertaking; so I asked him how much time it would take to do such a thing, and where we should have to go. To this he answered that in less than a month we should come out of the business, and that the most suitable place was in the mountains of Norcia; although a master of his had consecrated one near a place called the Abbey of Farfa; but that he had had some difficulties there which would not arise in the mountains of Norcia; and that those Norcian peasants are men to be trusted, and have some practice in this thing, so that at need they can give marvellous help.

This priest necromancer had certainly persuaded me so much that I had willingly disposed myself to do such a thing; but I said I wanted first to finish those medals I was making for the Pope, and I had spoken about them to him and to nobody else, begging him to keep them secret for me.

All the same, I kept asking him whether he believed that by that time I should be with my Sicilian Angelica; and seeing the time coming very close, it seemed a very great thing to me that I heard nothing of her. The necromancer told me that I should certainly find myself where she was, because they never fail when they promise in the way they had then; but that I should keep my eyes open and guard against some scandal that might come to me because of that case; and that I should force myself to put up with something against my nature, because he saw a very great danger in it; and that it would be good for me if I went with him to consecrate the book, because by that road that great danger of mine would pass over, and I should be the cause of making him and me extremely happy.

I, who was beginning to have more appetite for it than he had, told him that a certain Master Giovanni da Castel Bolognese had come to Rome, a very able man at making medals of the sort I made, in steel, and that I wanted nothing else in the world but to compete with this able man and come out on the world with such a piece of work, by which I hoped with that skill, and not with the sword, to kill those several enemies of mine.

This man still went on at me, saying: "For pity's sake, my Benvenuto, come with me and escape a great danger which I see in you." Having disposed myself in every way to want to finish my medal first, we were already near the end of the month; and by then, being so taken up with the medal, I no longer remembered either Angelica or anything else of that sort, but was entirely intent on my work.

Book One, Chapters 66 to 72

Chapter 66

One day among others, near the hour of vespers, an occasion came for me to go out of my usual hours from my house to my shop; because I had my shop in the Banchi and kept a little house behind the Banchi, and I seldom went to the shop, since I left all the business to that partner of mine called Felice.

Having been in the shop a little while, I remembered that I had to go and speak to Alessandro del Bene. Getting up at once and arriving in the Banchi, I ran into a certain great friend of mine, whose name was Ser Benedetto. He was a notary and had been born in Florence, the son of a blind man from Siena who used to recite prayers. This Ser Benedetto had been at Naples many and many years; then he had come back to Rome and did business for certain Sienese merchants, the Chigi.

And because that partner of mine had asked him again and again for certain money he was owed for some little rings he had let him have on trust, this day, meeting him in the Banchi, he asked him for his money in a somewhat rough way, which was his habit; and Ser Benedetto was with his masters, so that, seeing such a thing done in such a way, they shouted at Ser Benedetto a great deal, telling him they wanted to use somebody else, so as not to have to hear any more of such rubbish. This Ser Benedetto defended himself with them as well as he could, and said that he had paid that goldsmith, and that he was not able to bridle the fury of madmen. The Sienese took that remark badly and dismissed him at once.

Having got away from them, he went off in a hurry to my shop, perhaps to do Felice some harm. It happened that we met exactly in the middle of the Banchi; and I, who knew nothing about it, greeted him most pleasantly in my usual way, and he answered me with a great many rude words. At which everything the necromancer had told me came back to me; so, holding the rein as hard as I could on what his words were forcing me to do, I said: "Ser Benedetto, brother, do not be angry with me, because I have done you no harm, and I know nothing about these affairs of yours; and whatever you have to do with Felice, go and settle it with him, if you please; he knows perfectly well what he has to answer you. So, as I know nothing about it, you do me wrong to bite at me like this, all the more knowing that I am not a man who puts up with insults."

At this he said that I knew all about it, and that he was a man fit to make me carry a heavier load than that, and that Felice and I were two great scoundrels. By now a good many people had gathered to see the argument. Driven on by his ugly words, I quickly stooped to the ground and took a lump of mud, because it had been raining, and with it I quickly swung my hand up to give it him in the face. He ducked his head, so that I got him with it in the middle of his head. In this mud there was a stone of hard rock with many sharp corners, and catching him with one of those corners in the middle of the head, he fell down senseless like a dead man; and, seeing such an abundance of blood, all the bystanders judged that he was dead.

Chapter 67

While he was still on the ground, and some people were setting about carrying him away, that Pompeo the jeweller I mentioned above came by. The Pope had sent for him about some business of jewels. Seeing that man in that bad state, he asked who had done it to him. He was told: "Benvenuto has done it, because this beast asked for it."

Pompeo, having got quickly to the Pope, said to him: "Most Blessed Father, Benvenuto has this very minute killed Tobbia; I saw it with my own eyes." At this the Pope in a fury ordered the Governor, who was there present, to take me and hang me at once in the place where the homicide had been done, and to use every diligence to get me, and not to come before him until he had hanged me.

When I had seen that unlucky man on the ground, I thought at once about my own affairs, considering the power of my enemies and what such a thing might bring forth. Leaving there, I withdrew to the house of Messer Giovanni Gaddi, clerk of the Chamber, wanting to get myself ready as quickly as I could to go off with God. At which Messer Giovanni advised me not to be in such a fury to leave, since it might be that the harm was not as great as it seemed to me; and having sent for Messer Annibal Caro, who lived with him, he told him to go and find out about the case.

While these orders were being given, there appeared a Roman gentleman who was with the Cardinal de' Medici and had been sent by him. This gentleman, having called Messer Giovanni and me aside, told us that the Cardinal had told him the words he had heard the Pope say, and that he had no remedy at all to be able to help me, and that I should do everything in my power to escape this first fury, and that I should not trust myself in any house in Rome.

As soon as the gentleman had gone, Messer Giovanni, looking me in the face, made as if to weep, and said: "Alas, wretched me, that I have no remedy at all to be able to help you!" Then I said: "By the help of God, I shall help myself well enough; I only ask you to lend me one of your horses." A black Turkish horse was already got ready, the most beautiful and the best in Rome. I mounted him with a wheel-lock arquebus in front of the saddle bow, ready to defend myself with it.

When I got to the Ponte Sisto, I found there the whole of the sheriff's guard, on horseback and on foot; so, making a virtue of necessity, boldly urging my horse on quietly, by God's mercy their eyes were darkened, and I passed free; and with as much haste as I could I went off to Palombara, a place belonging to Signor Giovanbattista Savelli, and from there I sent the horse back to Messer Giovanni, and would not even let him know where I was.

Signor Giambattista, when he had made much of me for two days, advised me that I ought to get away from there and go off towards Naples, until this fury had passed; and having given me company, he set me on the road to Naples, on which I found a sculptor friend of mine who was going to San Germano to finish Piero de' Medici's tomb at Monte Cassino. His name was Il Solosmeo. He gave me news of how that very evening Pope Clement had sent a chamberlain of his to find out how Tobbia was; and, having found him at work, and that nothing at all had happened to him, and that he did not even know anything about it, when this was reported to the Pope, he turned to Pompeo and said to him: "You are a wretch; but I tell you plainly, you have stirred up a snake that will bite you and serve you right." Then he turned to Cardinal de' Medici and ordered him to take some care of me, because he would not have wanted to lose me for anything.

So Solosmeo and I went off singing towards Monte Cassino, to go on to Naples together.

Chapter 68

When Solosmeo had gone over his business at Monte Cassino, we went off together towards Naples. When we had got to within half a mile of Naples, an innkeeper came to meet us who invited us to his inn, and told us that he had been in Florence many years with Carlo Ginori; and that if we went to his inn he would make a great deal of us, since we were Florentines. We told this innkeeper several times that we did not want to go with him. This man kept going on ahead of us and then dropping behind, saying the same things over and over, that he would like us at his inn.

So, becoming a nuisance to me, I asked him whether he could tell me of a certain Sicilian woman called Beatrice, who had a beautiful young daughter called Angelica, and they were courtesans. This innkeeper, thinking I was making fun of him, said: "God send bad luck to courtesans and to those who like them"; and, giving his horse his heel, he made as if to go off from us for good.

Thinking I had got that beast of an innkeeper off my back in a good way — although I got no capital out of it, because that great love I bore Angelica had come back to me, and I was talking about it to Solosmeo, not without some lover's sighs — we see the innkeeper come back to us in a great hurry; and when he got to us he said: "It is either two or three days since a woman and a young girl came to lodge next to my inn, who have those names; I do not know whether they are Sicilians or from some other country." Then I said: "That name Angelica has such power over me that I want to come to your inn at all costs."

We went off together with the innkeeper into the city of Naples, and dismounted at his inn; and it seemed to me a thousand years before I could get my things in order, which I did very quickly; and going into the house next to the inn, I found there my Angelica, who made the most immeasurable fuss of me that anyone in the world can imagine. So I stayed with her from that hour, the twenty-second, until the following morning, with such pleasure as I never had the like of. And while I was rejoicing in this pleasure, I remembered that that very day the month expired which had been promised me in the circle of necromancy by the demons. So let every man who meddles with them consider the unreckonable dangers I have been through.

Chapter 69

I happened to have a diamond in my purse, which I showed among the goldsmiths; and although I was still young, I was so well known in Naples as a man worth something that a great deal was made of me. Among the others was a certain most gallant jeweller called Messer Domenico Fontana. This decent man left his shop for the three days I was in Naples, and never left my side, showing me a great many beautiful antiquities that were in Naples and outside Naples; and more than that, he took me to pay my respects to the Viceroy of Naples, who had let him know that he had a fancy to see me.

When I got to his Excellency, he gave me a very honourable welcome; and while we were doing this, that diamond caught his Excellency's eye; and having had it shown to him, he said that if I had to part with it, I should not exchange it with anyone else, if I pleased. To which I, having taken the diamond back, handed it to his Excellency again, and told him that the diamond and I were both at his service. Then he said that he liked the diamond very much, but that it would please him far more if I stayed with him; that he would make me such terms that I should have reason to praise him. We used a good many courteous words to each other; but coming afterwards to the merits of the diamond, and his Excellency ordering me to name a price for it, whatever seemed right to me, in a single word, I told him that two hundred scudi was exactly its price.

At this his Excellency said that it seemed to him I was not at all far from what was right; but that, being set by my hand, since he knew me for the first man in the world, it would not turn out, if somebody else set it, with the excellence it showed. Then I said that the diamond was not set by my hand, and that it was not well set; and that what it did, it did by its own goodness; and that if I reset it for him I should improve it a great deal from what it was. And putting my thumbnail to the edges of the diamond, I drew it out of its ring, and having cleaned it a little I handed it to the Viceroy; who, satisfied and astonished, made me out an order that I should be paid the two hundred scudi I had asked for.

Coming back to my lodging, I found letters from Cardinal de' Medici, telling me to come back to Rome with great diligence, and to go straight to dismount at his most reverend lordship's house. Having read the letter to my Angelica, with little loving tears she begged me to be so good as to stay in Naples, or else to take her with me. To whom I said that if she wanted to come with me I would give her those two hundred ducats I had had from the Viceroy to keep.

Her mother, seeing us in this close conversation, came up to us and said: "Benvenuto, if you want to take my Angelica to Rome, leave me fifteen ducats so that I can have my baby, and then I will come along too." I told the old bawd that I would leave her thirty willingly if she was content to give me my Angelica. So, having settled it, Angelica begged me to buy her a gown of black velvet, because velvet was cheap in Naples. I was content about it all; and having sent for the velvet and made the bargain and everything, the old woman, who thought I was more cooked than raw, asked me for a gown of fine cloth for herself, and a great many other expenses for her children, and a good deal more money than I had offered her. To whom I turned pleasantly and said: "My dear Beatrice, is what I have offered you enough?" She said no. Then I said that what was not enough for her would be enough for me; and having kissed my Angelica, she with tears and I with laughter, we parted, and I went straight back to Rome.

Chapter 70

Leaving Naples at night with the money on me, so as not to be waylaid or murdered, as is the custom of Naples, when I got to the Selciata, with great cunning and bodily courage I defended myself against several riders who had come to murder me. Then, some days later, having left Solosmeo to his business at Monte Cassino, I arrived one morning for dinner at the inn at Anagni.

Being near the inn, I fired at some birds with my arquebus and killed them; and a little iron pin that was in the lock of my gun tore my right hand. Although the injury was of no importance, it looked a good deal, because of the great quantity of blood my hand poured out.

Having gone into the inn and put my horse in its place, and having gone up onto a rough gallery, I found a number of Neapolitan gentlemen who were about to sit down at table; and with them was a young gentlewoman, the most beautiful I ever saw. When I came up, behind me came a very bold young servant of mine with a great partisan in his hand: so that we, the weapons and the blood, put such terror into those poor gentlemen — above all because that place was a nest of murderers — that, getting up from the table, they prayed God in great fright to help them. To whom I said, laughing, that God had helped them, and that I was a man to defend them from anyone who wanted to hurt them; and asking them for a little help to bandage my hand, that most beautiful gentlewoman took a handkerchief of hers richly worked in gold, meaning to bandage me with it; I would not have it: at once she tore it across the middle, and with the greatest gentleness bandaged me with her own hand.

So, being somewhat reassured, we dined very cheerfully. After dinner we mounted and went along in company. Their fright was not yet over; and those gentlemen cunningly had me entertain that gentlewoman, dropping some way behind; and I rode beside her on a fine little horse of mine, having signalled my servant to keep a little away from me; so that we talked of those things the apothecary does not sell. And in that way I came to Rome with the greatest pleasure I ever had.

When I got to Rome, I went off to dismount at the Cardinal de' Medici's palace; and, finding his most reverend lordship, I greeted him and thanked him for having had me come back. Then I begged his lordship to make me safe from prison, and if possible from the fine as well. He was very glad to see me; he told me not to worry about anything; then he turned to a gentleman of his called Messer Pierantonio Pecci, a Sienese, telling him to tell the sheriff on his behalf not to dare touch me.

Then he asked him how the man was whose head I had hit with the stone. Messer Pierantonio said he was ill, and would be worse still, because, having heard that I was coming back to Rome, he said he wanted to die in order to spite me. At which words the Cardinal said with great laughter: "That man could not have found any other way than this to let us know that he was born of Sienese." Then he turned to me and said: "For our own honour and yours, have patience for four or five days and do not go about in the Banchi; after that go where you like, and let the madmen die at their convenience."

I went home and set myself to finish the medal I had already begun, of the head of Pope Clement, which I made with a reverse figuring a Peace. This was a little female figure dressed in the thinnest clothes, girt up, with a torch in her hand burning a heap of weapons tied together like a trophy; and there was figured the wall of a temple, in which Fury was figured bound with many chains; and round about was a motto in letters saying "Clauduntur belli portae" — the gates of war are shut.

While I was finishing the medal, the man I had struck got better, and the Pope did not stop asking for me; and because I avoided going about the Cardinal de' Medici — since every time I came before him his lordship gave me some important work to do, which greatly hindered the finishing of my medal — it happened that Messer Pier Carnesecchi, a great favourite of the Pope's, took on the care of looking after me: so in a neat way he told me how much the Pope wanted me to serve him. To whom I said that in a few days I would show his Holiness that I had never moved away from his service.

Chapter 71

A few days later, having finished my medal, I struck it in gold and in silver and in brass. Having shown it to Messer Piero, he brought me in to the Pope at once. It was a day after dinner in the month of April, and it was fine weather: the Pope was in the Belvedere.

When I came before his Holiness, I put the medals into his hands together with the steel dies. Having taken them, and having at once recognised the great force of art that was in them, looking Messer Piero in the face he said: "The ancients were never so well served with medals." While he and the others were considering them, now the dies and now the medals, I began most modestly to speak, and said:

"If the power of my perverse stars had not had a greater power to hinder what they violently showed me they meant to do, your Holiness would have lost, without any cause on your part or mine, a faithful and loving servant. Therefore, most Blessed Father, there is no error at all in those cases where everything is at stake in using the way certain poor simple men speak of, saying that one should mark seven and cut one. Since a wicked, lying tongue of a most evil adversary of mine had so easily made your Holiness angry that you came into such a fury, ordering the Governor to take me at once and hang me — seeing afterwards such an impropriety, doing so great a wrong to yourself in depriving yourself of a servant of yours such as your Holiness yourself says he is, I am quite sure that, both before God and before the world, your Holiness would afterwards have felt no small remorse. Therefore good and virtuous fathers, and masters of that kind too, ought not so hastily to let their arm fall on their sons and their servants; seeing that being sorry for it afterwards is no use at all. Since God has hindered this malignant course of the stars, and has saved me for your Holiness, I beg you once more not to be so easily angered with me."

The Pope, having stopped looking at the medals, listened to me with great attention; and because there were present a good many lords of the greatest importance, the Pope, blushing somewhat, showed that he was ashamed; and not knowing any other way of getting out of that tangle, he said he did not remember ever having given such an order. Then, seeing this, I went into other talk, so as to turn aside that shame he had shown.

His Holiness, having got onto the subject of the medals again, asked me what way I had used to strike them so marvellously, being so large; which he had never seen among the ancients, medals of such size. We talked about that a good while; and he, being afraid I would make him another little speech worse than that one, told me that the medals were most beautiful and that they were very welcome to him, and that he would have liked to make another reverse of his own fancy, if such medals could be struck with two reverses. I said they could. Then his Holiness ordered me to make the story of Moses when he strikes the rock and the water comes out of it, with a motto above saying "Ut bibat populus" — that the people may drink. And then he added: "Go, Benvenuto, for you will not have finished it before I have thought about your affairs."

When I had gone, the Pope boasted in front of everybody that he would give me so much that I should be able to live richly without ever having to work for anybody else again. I attended urgently to finishing the reverse of the Moses.

Chapter 72

In the meantime the Pope fell ill; and, the doctors judging that the illness was dangerous, that adversary of mine, being afraid of me, ordered certain Neapolitan soldiers to do to me what he was afraid I would do to him. So I had a great deal of trouble to defend my poor life.

Going on, I finished the reverse entirely; and having taken it up to the Pope, I found him in bed in a very bad way. All the same he made much of me, and wanted to see the medals and the dies; and having spectacles and lights brought, he could not make out anything at all. He set himself to grope over them a little with his fingers; then, having done that a while, he heaved a great sigh and said to some of them that he was sorry for me, but that if God gave him back his health he would settle everything.

Three days later the Pope died; and I, finding that I had lost my labour, put a good heart into myself and said to myself that by means of those medals I had made myself so well known that by any pope who came I should be employed, perhaps with better fortune. So I put heart into myself, cancelling entirely and completely the great injuries Pompeo had done me; and having put on my armour and my sword, I went off to Saint Peter's and kissed the dead Pope's feet, not without tears; then I came back into the Banchi to consider the great confusion that comes about on such occasions.

And while I was sitting in the Banchi with a good many friends of mine, Pompeo came by in the middle of ten very well armed men; and when he was exactly opposite where I was, he stopped a moment in the attitude of wanting to pick a quarrel with me. Those who were with me, bold and willing young men, signalled to me that I should put my hand to my sword; at which I immediately considered that if I put my hand to my sword some very great harm would follow to men who had not a fault in the world; therefore I judged it better that I alone should put my own life at risk. Having stood there for as long as it takes to say two Hail Marys, Pompeo laughed at me scornfully; and, going off, his men laughed too, shaking their heads; and with gestures of that sort they did a great deal of bullying. My companions wanted to put their hands to the quarrel; to whom I said angrily that I was a man to know how to finish my own quarrels by myself, and that I did not need braver men than I was; so let everybody mind his own business.

My friends, offended, went off from me grumbling. Among them was my dearest friend, whose name was Albertaccio del Bene, own brother of Alessandro and of Albizzo, who is today in Lyons and extremely rich. This Albertaccio was the most admirable young man I ever knew, and the most spirited, and he loved me as he loved himself; and because he knew perfectly well that that act of patience had not been out of smallness of spirit but out of the boldest courage — for he knew me very well — he came back to the point and begged me to do him the favour of calling on him to be with me in whatever I had a mind to do. To whom I said: "My Albertaccio, dearest to me of all the rest: the time will certainly come when you will be able to help me; but in this case, if you love me, do not look at me, and mind your own business, and get away quickly as the others have done, because this is no time to lose." These words were said quickly.

Book One, Chapters 73 to 78

Chapter 73

Meanwhile my enemies had made their way at a slow pace from the Banchi towards the Chiavica, as the place is called, and had arrived at a crossing of streets which go off in different directions; but the one where my enemy Pompeo's house was, was the street that leads straight to the Campo di Fiore. And for some business of his, Pompeo had gone into the apothecary's shop on the corner of the Chiavica, and had stayed there with the apothecary a while over some affairs of his; although I was told that he had been boasting about that piece of bullying he thought he had done me. But at all events it was his bad fortune; because when I got to that corner he was just coming out of the apothecary's, and his bravoes had opened out and had already taken him into the middle of them.

I put my hand to a small sharp dagger, and forcing the line of his bravoes, I laid my hands on his chest with such quickness and steadiness of mind that none of them could do anything about it. Having drawn to give it him in the face, the fright he took made him turn his face away, so that I pricked him exactly under the ear; and there I struck home two blows only, at the second of which he fell dead out of my hands — which was never my intention; but, as they say, blows are not given by agreement.

Having taken the dagger back in my left hand, and with my right drawn my sword to defend my life, all those bravoes ran to the dead body and made no move at all against me; and so, all alone, I withdrew along the Strada Giulia, thinking where I could save myself.

When I had gone three hundred paces, Il Piloto the goldsmith, a very great friend of mine, caught up with me and said: "Brother, since the harm is done, let us see about saving you." To whom I said: "Let us go to Albertaccio del Bene's house, because a little while ago I told him the time would soon come when I should need him."

When we got to Albertaccio's house the fuss made of me was beyond all reckoning, and soon the noblest of the young men of the Banchi of every nation appeared, except the Milanese; and they all offered to lay down their lives for the saving of mine. Messer Luigi Rucellai also sent to offer me marvellously that I should make use of anything of his, and so did many other great men like him; because they all agreed in blessing my hands, thinking that man had murdered me too far, and wondering a great deal that I had put up with so much.

Chapter 74

At this moment Cardinal Cornaro, having heard of the thing, sent thirty soldiers of his own accord, with as many partisans, pikes and arquebuses, to take me to his own rooms for greater safety; and I accepted the offer and went with them, and more than as many again of those young men kept me company.

In the meantime Messer Traiano, that relation of his, the Pope's first chamberlain, heard of it and sent to Cardinal de' Medici a great Milanese gentleman to tell the Cardinal of the great harm I had done, and that his most reverend lordship was bound to punish me. The Cardinal answered at once and said: "He would have done great harm if he had not done this smaller harm: thank Messer Traiano on my behalf for having informed me of what I did not know." And turning at once, in the presence of the gentleman, to the Bishop of Forlì, a gentleman and member of his household, he said: "Look diligently for my Benvenuto, and bring him here to me, because I mean to help and defend him; and whoever acts against him will be acting against me."

The gentleman went off very red in the face, and the Bishop of Forlì came to find me at Cardinal Cornaro's house; and, having found the Cardinal, he said that Cardinal de' Medici was sending for Benvenuto, and that he wanted to be the one to keep him. Cardinal Cornaro, who was as odd-tempered as a bear cub, answered the bishop very angrily, telling him that he was as fit to keep me as Cardinal de' Medici was. At this the bishop said that he begged him to let him speak one word to me about something apart from that affair, on other business of the Cardinal's. Cornaro told him that for that day he should consider that he had spoken to me.

Cardinal de' Medici was very offended; but all the same I went the following night, without Cornaro's knowledge, very well accompanied, to visit him; and then I begged him to do me the favour of leaving me in Cornaro's house, and I told him of the great courtesy Cornaro had shown me; so that, if his most reverend lordship let me stay with Cornaro, I should come to have one more friend in my necessities; or else that he should dispose of me in whatever way pleased his lordship. He answered that I should do whatever seemed good to me.

Having gone back to Cornaro's house, a few days later Cardinal Farnese was made Pope; and, having at once given orders about matters of more importance, the Pope asked for me, saying he did not want anybody but me to make his coins. To these words a certain gentleman of his household, very close to him, called Messer Latino Juvinale, answered his Holiness, and said that I was a fugitive for a homicide committed on the person of a Milanese called Pompeo; and he added all my reasons very favourably. At which words the Pope said: "I did not know of Pompeo's death, but I did know Benvenuto's reasons; so let him have a safe conduct made out at once, with which he may be perfectly safe."

There was present a great friend of that Pompeo's, and very close to the Pope, who was called Messer Ambrogio and was a Milanese, and he said to the Pope: "In the first days of your papacy it would not be well to grant favours of this sort." To whom the Pope turned and said: "You do not know the case as well as I do. Know that men like Benvenuto, unique in their profession, ought not to be bound by the law; and this man all the more so, because I know how much right he has on his side." And having had the safe conduct made out for me, I began at once to serve him with the greatest favour.

Chapter 75

That Latino Juvinale came to find me and ordered me to make the Pope's coins. At which all those enemies of mine were roused: they began to hinder me from making them. At which the Pope, becoming aware of the thing, shouted at them all, and insisted that I should make them.

I began to make the dies for the scudi, on which I made a half figure of Saint Paul, with a motto in letters saying "Vas electionis" — a chosen vessel. This coin pleased far more than the ones by those who had made them in competition with me; so that the Pope said that nobody else should ever speak to him about coins again, because he wanted me to be the one who made them and nobody else.

So I attended to working freely; and that Messer Latino Juvinale brought me in to the Pope, because the Pope had given him that duty. I wanted to get back the grant of the office of striker of the mint. The Pope let himself be advised about this, saying that first I must have the pardon for the homicide, which I should get on the Feast of the Assumption in August by order of the wardmasters of Rome — for that is the custom every year at this solemn feast, to give these wardmasters twelve banished men; meanwhile another safe conduct would be made out for me, by which I could be safe until that time.

My enemies, seeing that they could in no way manage to keep me from the mint, took another course. Pompeo, when he was killed, had left three thousand ducats of dowry to a little bastard daughter of his; and they arranged that a certain favourite of Signor Pier Luigi, the Pope's son, should ask for her in marriage through that lord: and so it was done. This favourite was a little peasant brought up by that lord, and, from what was said, very little of that money came to him, because the lord laid his hands on it and meant to use it himself.

But because this husband of the young girl, to please his wife, had several times begged the lord to have me taken, which lord had promised to do it as soon as he saw the favour I had with the Pope fall off a little — things standing so for about two months, because that servant of his was trying to get his dowry, the lord did not answer him to the purpose, but let his wife know that he would take revenge for her father at all costs.

Although I knew something of it, and had presented myself several times before that lord, who made a show of doing me the greatest favours, on the other side he had arranged one of two ways: either to have me killed or to have me taken by the sheriff. He gave the commission to a certain little devil of a Corsican soldier of his, to do it as cleanly as he could; and those other enemies of mine, above all Messer Traiano, had promised to make this little Corsican a present of a hundred scudi; who said he would do it as easily as drinking a fresh egg.

I, hearing of such a thing, went about with my eyes open and in good company and very well armed with a coat of mail and sleeves, which I had licence for. This little Corsican, out of greed, thinking to get all that money safely for himself, believed he could do the business by himself alone; so that one day, after dinner, they had me called on Signor Pier Luigi's behalf; at which I went at once, because the lord had talked to me about wanting to make several large silver vessels.

Having left home in a hurry, still with my usual armour, I went quickly along the Strada Giulia, thinking I should meet nobody at that hour. When I was at the upper end of the Strada Giulia, to turn towards the Farnese palace, it being my habit to turn wide at corners, I saw that little Corsican get up from where he was sitting and come out into the middle of the street; so that I was not put out at all, but stood ready to defend myself; and slowing my pace somewhat, I went close to the wall to leave a wide road for the Corsican. He too came close to the wall; and, when we had already come well up to each other, having recognised expressly by his behaviour that he had a mind to do me harm, and seeing me alone like that, thinking it would come off for him — so that I began to speak and said: "Brave soldier, if it were night you might say you had taken me for somebody else; but because it is day, you know perfectly well who I am, and I have never had anything to do with you, and never did you any harm; but I should be quite ready to do you a good turn."

At these words he, in a bullying attitude, not getting out of my way, told me he did not know what I was talking about. Then I said: "I know perfectly well what you want and what you are saying; but the business you have undertaken to do is more difficult and dangerous than you think, and it might turn out backwards; and remember that you have to do with a man who would defend himself against a hundred. And this is not an honourable business for brave men, such as you are."

Meanwhile I too was looking at him like a dog, and both of us had changed colour. By now people had come up, who had already realised that our words were of iron; so that, not having the nerve to lay hands on me, he said: "We shall see each other again another time." To whom I said: "I always see decent men again, and men who look like it."

Having gone off, I went to the lord's house, and he had not sent for me. Coming back to my shop, that little Corsican let me know, through a very great friend of his and of mine, that I should not be on my guard against him any more, and that he wanted to be a good brother to me; but that I should be well on my guard against others, because I was in the greatest danger, since men of much importance had sworn my death. Having sent to thank him, I guarded myself as best I could.

Not many days later I was told by a great friend of mine that Signor Pier Luigi had given express orders that I should be taken that evening. This was told me at twenty hours; so I spoke about it with some friends of mine, who urged me to go away at once. And because the order had been given for one hour of the night, at twenty-three hours I got into the post saddle and rode off to Florence: because, since that little Corsican had not had the heart to do the business he had promised, Signor Pier Luigi had on his own authority given orders that I should be taken, only in order to quiet a little that daughter of Pompeo's, who wanted to know where her dowry was. Not being able to satisfy her with revenge in either of the two ways he had arranged, he thought of another, which we shall tell in its place.

Chapter 76

I got to Florence and paid my respects to Duke Alessandro, who made marvellous fuss of me and asked me to stay with him.

And because there was in Florence a certain sculptor called Il Tribolino, and he was my gossip, since I had stood godfather to a son of his, talking with him he told me that a certain Jacopo del Sansovino, once his first master, had sent for him; and because he had never seen Venice, and for the earnings he expected, he was going very willingly; and asking me whether I had ever seen Venice, I said no; so he begged me to go with him for a jaunt, which I promised him. So I answered Duke Alessandro that I wanted to go as far as Venice first, and afterwards I would willingly come back to serve him; and he made me promise it, and ordered me to speak to him before I left.

The next day, having got myself ready, I went to take leave of the Duke, whom I found in the Pazzi palace, at the time when the wife and daughters of Signor Lorenzo Cibo were lodged there. Having let his Excellency know that I wanted to go to Venice with his good favour, Cosimino de' Medici, today Duke of Florence, came back with the answer, telling me to go and find Niccolò da Monte Aguto, who would give me fifty gold scudi, which money his Excellency the Duke gave me, that I should enjoy them for love of him; and afterwards come back and serve him.

I had the money from Niccolò, and went to the house for Tribolo, who was ready; and he asked me whether I had tied up my sword. I told him that a man on horseback going on a journey ought not to tie up his sword. He said that in Florence that was the custom, because there was a certain Ser Maurizio who for the least thing would have given Saint John the Baptist himself the rope; so one had to carry one's sword tied until one was outside the gate. I laughed at it, and so we went off.

We joined up with the Venice courier, whose nickname was Lamentone. We went in company with him, and, having passed Bologna, one evening among others we arrived at Ferrara; and, having lodged at the inn on the Piazza, Lamentone went off to find some of the exiles to bring them letters and messages from their wives; for it was so by the Duke's consent that only the courier could speak to them, and nobody else, on pain of the same banishment they were under.

In the meantime, it being a little past twenty-two hours, Tribolo and I went off to see the Duke of Ferrara come back, who had gone to Belfiore to watch a joust. On his way back we met a good many exiles, who looked at us hard, almost forcing us to speak with them. Tribolo, who was the most fearful man I ever knew, kept telling me: "Do not look at them and do not speak with them, if you want to go back to Florence."

So we stayed to watch the Duke come back; then, having gone back to the inn, we found Lamentone there. And when it was getting on for one hour of the night, Niccolò Benintendi appeared with his brother Piero, and another old man, who I think was Jacopo Nardi, along with several other young men; and as soon as they came they asked the courier, each of them, about his own family in Florence. Tribolo and I stood some way off, so as not to speak with them.

After they had talked a while with Lamentone, that Niccolò Benintendi said: "I know those two perfectly well; why do they make such a fuss about not wanting to speak to us?" Tribolo kept telling me to keep quiet. Lamentone told them that the licence which had been given to him had not been given to us. Benintendi went on and said that it was asinine, and sent us cankers and a thousand fine things.

Then I raised my head as modestly as I could and knew how, and said: "Dear gentlemen, you can do us a great deal of harm, and we can do you no good at all; and although you have said some words to us which are not fitting, we do not for that wish to be angry with you." That old man of the Nardi said that I had spoken like the decent young man I was.

Niccolò Benintendi then said: "I have them and the Duke in my backside." I answered that he was in the wrong with us, that we had nothing to do with his affairs. That old man of the Nardi took our side, telling Benintendi that he was in the wrong; at which he still went on saying insulting words. So I told him that I would say and do things to him that he would not like; so let him mind his own business and leave us alone. He answered that he had the Duke and us in his backside again, and that we and he were a heap of asses.

At which words, giving him the lie in his throat, I drew my sword; and the old man, who wanted to be first at the stairs, fell a few steps down, and all of them on top of him, one on another. At which, jumping forward, I swung my sword along the walls in the greatest fury, saying: "I shall kill you all" — and I took very good care not to do them any harm, when I could have done far too much.

At this noise the innkeeper shouted; Lamentone said, "Do not do it"; some of them said, "Alas, my head!", others, "Let me get out of here." It was an unreckonable beating: they looked like a herd of pigs. The innkeeper came with a light; I went back up and put away my sword.

Lamentone told Niccolò Benintendi that he had done wrong; the innkeeper said to Niccolò Benintendi: "It is a matter of life to put your hand to a weapon here, and if the Duke knew of these insolences of yours he would have you hanged by the throat; so I will not do to you what you deserve; but never come to this inn again, or it will go badly with you." The innkeeper came up to me, and when I wanted to excuse myself he would not let me say anything, telling me that he knew I had a thousand reasons on my side, and that I should be well on my guard against them on the journey.

Chapter 77

When we had supped, a boatman came up to take us to Venice. I asked him whether he would give me the boat to ourselves; and he was content, and we made an agreement on it.

Very early in the morning we took horses to go to the port, which is I do not know how many miles from Ferrara; and when we got to the port we found there Niccolò Benintendi's brother with three other companions, waiting for me to arrive: among them were two shafted weapons, and I had bought a fine javelin in Ferrara. Being also very well armed, I was not dismayed at all, as Tribolo was, who said: "God help us: these men are here to kill us." Lamentone turned to me and said: "The best thing you can do is to go back to Ferrara, because I see the thing is dangerous. Please, my Benvenuto, let the fury of these mad beasts pass over."

Then I said: "Let us go forward, because God helps the man who is in the right; and you shall see how I shall help myself. Is that boat not hired for us?" "Yes," said Lamentone. "And we shall be in it without them, as far as my own powers can manage."

I urged my horse forward, and when I was within fifty paces I dismounted, and went boldly forward with my javelin. Tribolo had stopped behind and was huddled up on his horse, so that he looked like the cold itself; and Lamentone the courier was puffing and blowing like a wind, for that was his way of doing things — but he did it more then than usual, standing there considering what end that devilry was going to have.

When I got to the boat, the boatman came up to me and said that those several Florentine gentlemen wanted to come into the boat in our company, if I were content with it. To whom I said: "The boat is hired for us and not for anybody else, and it grieves me to my heart not to be able to be with them." At these words a bold young man of the Magalotti said: "Benvenuto, we shall see that you are able." Then I said: "If God and the right I have, together with my own strength, will it or can do it, you will not make me able to do what you say." And with the words together I jumped into the boat. Turning the point of my weapon at them, I said: "With this I shall show you that I am not able."

Wanting to make a little demonstration, having put his hand to his weapon and come forward, that man of the Magalotti — I jumped up onto the edge of the boat and gave him such a great blow that if he had not fallen over backwards on the ground I should have run him through from side to side. The other companions, instead of helping him, pulled back; and, seeing that I could have killed him, instead of striking him I said: "Get up, brother, and take your weapon and go away; you have seen well enough that I cannot do what I do not want to do, and that I have not wanted to do what I could have done."

Then I called Tribolo and the boatman and Lamentone into the boat; and so we went off towards Venice. When we had gone ten miles up the Po, those young men had got into a light boat and caught up with us; and when they came opposite us, that fool Pier Benintendi said to me: "Get along, Benvenuto, for we shall see each other again in Venice." "Get on your way, and I am coming," I said; "and everywhere I let myself be seen again."

So we arrived in Venice. I took advice from a brother of Cardinal Cornaro, asking him to do me the favour of being able to carry a weapon; who told me to carry it freely, since the worst that could happen was to lose the sword.

Chapter 78

So, carrying our weapons, we went to visit Jacopo del Sansovino the sculptor, who had sent for Tribolo; and he made a great fuss of me and wanted to give us dinner, and we stayed with him. Talking with Tribolo, he told him that he did not want to employ him for the present, and that he should come back another time.

At these words I burst out laughing, and said pleasantly to Sansovino: "Your house is much too far from his, for him to have to come back another time." The poor dismayed Tribolo said: "I have here the letter you wrote me to come." At this Sansovino said that men of his sort, decent and able men, could do that and more.

Tribolo shrugged his shoulders and said "Patience" several times. At this, and not looking at the abundant dinner Sansovino had given me, I took my companion Tribolo's part, since he was in the right. And because at that table Sansovino had never stopped chattering about his own great performances, speaking ill of Michelangelo and of everybody else who worked in that art, and only praising himself marvellously, this had become such a nuisance to me that I had not eaten a mouthful I liked, and I only said these two words: "Oh, Messer Jacopo, decent men do the things of decent men; and those able men who do beautiful and good works are much better known when they are praised by others than when they praise themselves so confidently on their own account." At these words both he and we got up from the table grumbling.

That same day, being in Venice near the Rialto, I ran into Piero Benintendi, who was with several men; and, seeing that they were looking to do me some harm, I withdrew into an apothecary's shop until I had let that fury pass by. Afterwards I heard that that young man of the Magalotti, to whom I had shown courtesy, had shouted at them a great deal; and so it passed off.

Book One, Chapters 79 to 83

Chapter 79

A few days later we came back towards Florence; and being lodged at a certain place which is on this side of Chioggia, on the left hand as you come towards Ferrara, the innkeeper wanted to be paid in his own way before we went to sleep; and when we told him that in other places the custom was to pay in the morning, he said: "I mean to be paid in the evening, and in my own way." I said, at those words, that men who wanted to do things their own way ought to make a world of their own, because that was not the custom here. The innkeeper answered that I should not bother his head, because he meant to do it his way. Tribolo was trembling with fear, and kept nudging me to keep quiet, so that they should not do us worse: so we paid him his way and went off to sleep.

We had the good luck of very beautiful beds, everything new and genuinely clean; for all that, I never slept, thinking all that night how I had to go about revenging myself. At one moment it came into my mind to set fire to his house; at another to cut the throats of four good horses he had in his stable; I saw that all of it was easy for me to do, but I did not see that it would be easy to save myself and my companion. In the end I took as my expedient to put the baggage and my companions in the boat, and so I did; and having hitched the horses to the tow rope which pulled the boat, I said they should not move the boat until I came back, because I had left a pair of my slippers in the place where I had slept.

So, going back into the inn, I asked for the innkeeper; and he answered me that he had nothing to do with us, and that we should go to the brothel. There was a great lout of a stable boy of his there, all half asleep, who said to me: "The innkeeper would not move for the Pope, because he is sleeping with a certain little slut he has wanted for a long time"; and he asked me for a tip; so I gave him some of those small Venetian coins, and told him to hold back the man with the tow rope a little while I looked for my slippers and came back.

Having gone up, I took a little knife that shaved, and the four beds that were there I cut them all to shreds with that knife; so that I knew I had done more than fifty scudi of damage. And coming back to the boat with some scraps of that serge in my pocket, I told the man who was guiding the tow rope in a hurry to get moving quickly.

When we had got a little way from the inn, my gossip Tribolo said that he had left certain straps behind that tied up his little valise, and that he wanted to go back for them at all costs. To which I said that he should not bother about two little straps, because I would make him as many big ones as he could want. He told me I was always joking, but that he wanted to go back for his straps at all costs; and, insisting to the man with the tow rope that he stop, while I said that he should keep going, I told him meanwhile of the great damage I had done to the innkeeper; and having shown him a sample of certain scraps of the serge and so on, such a trembling came over him that he never stopped saying to the man with the rope: "Get on, get on quickly." And he never felt safe from this danger until we had got back to the gates of Florence.

When we got there, Tribolo said: "Let us tie up our swords for the love of God, and do not do this to me any more; because I have felt the whole time as if my guts were in a basin." To which I said: "My gossip Tribolo, you do not need to tie up your sword, because you have never untied it" — and I said this by chance, having never seen him give a sign of being a man on that whole journey. At which, looking at his sword, he said: "By God, you are right, because it is tied in the way I fixed it before I left my house."

My gossip felt that I had kept him bad company, because I had taken offence and defended myself against those who had wanted to do us harm; and I felt that he had kept me much worse company, in not setting himself to help me in such needs. Let a bystander without passion judge it.

Chapter 80

When I had dismounted, I went at once to find Duke Alessandro and thanked him a great deal for the present of the fifty scudi, telling his Excellency that I was fully ready for whatever I was good for in serving his Excellency. He at once ordered me to make the dies for his coins; and the first I made was a coin of forty soldi, with his Excellency's head on one side and on the other a Saint Cosmas and a Saint Damian. These were silver coins, and they pleased so much that the Duke ventured to say that they were the most beautiful coins in Christendom. So said the whole of Florence, and everybody who saw them.

So I asked his Excellency to settle a salary on me, and to have the rooms at the mint made over to me; and he told me to attend to serving him and that he would give me a great deal more than I was asking; and meanwhile he told me he had given the order to the master of the mint, who was a certain Carlo Acciaiuoli, and that I should go to him for all the money I wanted. And so I found it was; but I drew the money so sparingly that I was always left with something owing to me, by my own reckoning.

Then I made the dies for the giulio, on which was a Saint John in profile, seated, with a book in his hand, which seemed to me the most beautiful work I ever made; and on the other side were Duke Alessandro's arms. After this I made the die for the half giulios, on which I made a head facing forward of a young Saint John. This was the first coin with a head facing forward on so thin a silver flan that was ever made; and this difficulty does not show, except to the eyes of those who are excellent in such a profession.
