After this I made the dies for the gold scudi, on which was a cross on one side with certain little cherubim, and on the other side his Excellency's arms.

When I had made these four sorts of coins, I begged his Excellency to settle my salary and make over to me the rooms mentioned, if my service pleased him. At which words his Excellency told me kindly that he was very content, and that he would give the orders.

While I was speaking to him, his Excellency was in his wardrobe and was looking at a marvellous gun which had been sent him from Germany. Seeing that I was looking at that fine instrument with great attention, he put it into my hands, telling me that he knew perfectly well how much I delighted in such a thing, and that as an earnest of what he had promised me I should take from his wardrobe an arquebus of my own choosing, apart from that one, since he well knew that there were many there as fine and as good. At which words I accepted and thanked him; and, seeing me start looking round with my eyes, he ordered his wardrobe keeper, a certain Pretino of Lucca, to let me take whatever I wanted. And having gone off with the most pleasant words, I stayed behind and chose the most beautiful and the best arquebus I ever saw or ever had, and I took it home.

Two days later I brought him certain small drawings his Excellency had asked me for, to make some works in gold which he wanted to send as a present to his wife, who was still in Naples. Again I asked him about that same business of mine, that he should settle it for me. Then his Excellency told me that first he wanted me to make the dies for a fine portrait of himself, as I had done for Pope Clement. I began that portrait in wax; on account of which his Excellency ordered that at whatever hours I came to take his likeness, I should always be let in.

I, seeing that this business of mine was dragging on, called a certain Pietro Paolo of Monterotondo, of the Roman country, who had been with me from a small boy in Rome; and, finding that he was with a certain wretched Bernardone, a goldsmith who did not treat him very well, on that account I took him away from him and taught him very well to put in those dies for the coins; and meanwhile I took the Duke's likeness. And many times I found him dozing after dinner with that Lorenzino of his who afterwards killed him, and nobody else; and I wondered a great deal that a Duke of that sort should trust himself so.

Chapter 81

It happened that Ottaviano de' Medici, who seemed to govern everything, wanting to favour, against the Duke's wish, the old master of the mint, whose name was Bastiano Cennini, an old-fashioned man of little knowledge, had had those clumsy dies of his mixed in with mine for the dies of the scudi; on which account I complained to the Duke, who, seeing the truth, took it very badly, and said to me: "Go and tell it to Ottaviano de' Medici, and show it to him." So I went at once; and having shown him the injury that had been done to my beautiful coins, he said to me like an ass: "That is how we please to do it." To whom I answered that it was not what was right, and it did not please me. He said: "And if it pleased the Duke?" I answered: "It would not please me; because such a thing is neither just nor reasonable." He said that I should get out of his sight, and that I should swallow it that way if I burst.

Having gone back to the Duke, I told him everything that Ottaviano de' Medici and I had discussed so unpleasantly; so I begged his Excellency not to let a wrong be done to the beautiful coins I had made for him, and to give me leave to go. Then he said: "Ottaviano wants too much of it; and you shall have what you want, because this is an injury done to me."

That same day, which was a Thursday, there came to me from Rome an ample safe conduct from the Pope, telling me to come quickly for the pardon of the Feast of the Assumption in the middle of August, so that I could free myself of that charge for the homicide I had done. Having gone to the Duke, I found him in bed, because they said he had disordered himself; and having finished in a little more than two hours what I needed for his wax medal, I showed it to him finished, and it pleased him a great deal. Then I showed his Excellency the safe conduct I had had by the Pope's order, and how the Pope was asking me to make him certain works; for this reason I should go and win back that beautiful city of Rome, and meanwhile I would serve him with his medal.

At this the Duke said, half in anger: "Benvenuto, do as I say, do not go; because I shall settle your salary, and give you the rooms at the mint, with a great deal more than you would know how to ask me for, because you ask what is just and reasonable: and whom would you have put in my beautiful dies that you have made me?" Then I said: "My lord, everything has been thought of, because I have here a pupil of mine, a young Roman whom I have taught, who will serve your Excellency very well until I come back with your medal finished, to stay with you always afterwards. And because I have my shop open in Rome with workmen and some business, when I have had my pardon I shall leave all my Roman devotions to a man I have brought up who is there, and afterwards, with your Excellency's good favour, I shall come back to you."

Present at this was that Lorenzino de' Medici I have mentioned, and nobody else; the Duke signalled to him several times that he too should encourage me to stay; on account of which Lorenzino never said anything but: "Benvenuto, you would do better to stay." To whom I said that I wanted to win back Rome at all costs. He said nothing else, and kept looking at the Duke with a very evil eye.

I, having finished the medal in my own way and having shut it in its little box, said to the Duke: "My lord, be of good cheer, because I shall make you a far more beautiful medal than I made for Pope Clement; because reason requires that I should do better, that one being the first I ever made; and Messer Lorenzo here will give me some very beautiful reverse, being a learned person and of the greatest wit." At these words Lorenzo at once answered, saying: "I was thinking of nothing else but of giving you a reverse that would be worthy of his Excellency." The Duke smirked, and, looking at Lorenzo, said: "Lorenzo, you shall give him the reverse, and he shall make it here, and shall not go." Lorenzo answered quickly, saying: "I shall do it as soon as I can, and I hope to do something to make the world marvel." The Duke, who took him now for a bit of a madman and now for a lazy fellow, rolled over in bed and laughed at the words he had said to him.

I left without any other ceremony of leave-taking, and left them alone together. The Duke, who did not believe I would go, said nothing more to me. When he heard afterwards that I had gone, he sent a servant after me who caught me up at Siena, and gave me fifty gold ducats from the Duke, telling me that I should enjoy them for love of him and come back as soon as I could. "And from Messer Lorenzo I tell you that he is getting ready a marvellous reverse for that medal you want to make."

I had left all instructions with Pietro Paolo the Roman about how he was to put in the dies; but because it was a very difficult thing, he never put them in very well. I was left a creditor of the mint, for the making of my dies, for more than seventy scudi.

Chapter 82

I went off to Rome, and took with me that most beautiful wheel-lock arquebus the Duke had given me, and with the greatest pleasure I used it many times on the way, doing unreckonable feats with it.

I got to Rome; and because I kept a little house in the Strada Giulia which was not in order, I went to dismount at the house of Messer Giovanni Gaddi, clerk of the Chamber, with whom I had left in trust when I left Rome many fine weapons of mine and many other things I set great store by. So I did not want to dismount at my shop; and I sent for that Felice, my partner, and had that little house of mine put in very good order at once. Then the next day I went to sleep in it, being very well provided with clothes and everything I needed, wanting the following morning to go and visit the Pope to thank him.

I had two boy servants, and under my house there was a laundress who cooked for me very cleanly. Having given supper that evening to several friends of mine, and that supper having passed with the greatest pleasure, I went off to sleep; and hardly had the night passed when in the morning, more than an hour before day, I heard someone knocking with the greatest fury at the door of my house, one blow not waiting for the next.

So I called my elder servant, whose name was Cencio — it was he I had taken into the necromantic circle — and told him to go and see who was the madman who was beating on it so beastly at that hour. While Cencio was going, I lit another lamp, since I always keep one burning at night, and at once put on over my shirt a marvellous shirt of mail, and over it an old coat, anyhow.

Cencio came back and said: "Alas, master, it is the sheriff with the whole guard, and he says that if you do not come quickly he will throw the door down; and they have torches and a thousand things with them." To whom I said: "Tell them I am putting on an old coat, and that I am coming out in my shirt like this."

Imagining that it was a murder, like the one Signor Pier Luigi had already arranged for me, I took in my right hand a marvellous dagger I had, and in my left the safe conduct; then I ran to the back window, which looked out over certain gardens, and there I saw more than thirty constables; from which I knew that I could not escape on that side. Having put the two boys in front of me, I told them to open the door when I told them exactly.

Having got myself ready, the dagger in my right and the safe conduct in my left, in an attitude of real defence, I said to the two boys: "Do not be afraid; open." Vittorio the sheriff jumped inside at once with two others, thinking he could easily lay hands on me; and, seeing me ready in that way, they drew back and said: "This calls for something more than jokes." Then I said, throwing the safe conduct at them: "Read that; and since you cannot take me, still less do I want you to touch me."

The sheriff then told several of them to take me, and that the safe conduct could be looked at afterwards. At this, boldly, I pushed my weapon forward and said: "God be for the right; either I escape alive, or I am taken dead." The room was narrow; they made as if to come at me by force, and I made a great show of defence; on account of which the sheriff knew that he could not have me in any way but the one I had said. Having called the clerk, while he was having the safe conduct read he made as if two or three times to have hands laid on me; at which I never moved from that resolution I had made. Giving up the business, they threw the safe conduct on the ground and went off without me.

Chapter 83

Having gone back to rest, I felt badly upset, and could never take up sleep again. I had made up my mind that as soon as it was day I would have myself bled; so I took advice about it from Messer Giovanni Gaddi, and he from a little apology of a doctor of his, who asked me whether I had been frightened. Now consider what a doctor's judgement that was, having been told so great a case, and asking me such a question! This was a certain young jackanapes who laughed almost continually and at nothing; and, laughing in that way, he told me to take a good glass of Greek wine and to attend to being cheerful and not to be afraid.

Messer Giovanni kept saying: "Master, a man of bronze or of marble would be frightened in such cases; how much more a man." At this the little doctor said: "Monsignor, we are not all made in one way: this is a man neither of bronze nor of marble, but of solid iron"; and, putting his hands on my pulse, with that senseless laughing of his he said to Messer Giovanni: "Now feel here; this is not the pulse of a man, but of a lion or a dragon." At which I, whose pulse was strongly disturbed — perhaps beyond the measure that stupid doctor had learned either from Hippocrates or from Galen — knew my own trouble well enough; but so as not to frighten myself more, or do myself more harm than I had already had, I showed a good spirit.

Meanwhile Messer Giovanni had dinner got ready, and we all ate in company: which was, along with Messer Giovanni, a certain Messer Lodovico da Fano, Messer Antonio Allegretti, Messer Giovanni the Greek, all most learned persons, and Messer Annibal Caro, who was very young. And nothing else was talked of at that dinner but this bold affair. And they had it told over and over again by Cencio, my little servant, who was beyond measure quick-witted, bold and very handsome of body; and every time he told this raging business of mine, doing the attitudes I had done and saying very well the words I had said, something new always came back to me. And they often asked him whether he had been frightened; to which words he answered that they should ask me whether I had been frightened, because he had had exactly what I had had.

This chatter becoming a nuisance to me, and because I felt strongly upset, I got up from the table, saying that I wanted to go and dress myself and him in new clothes of blue cloth and silk; that I wanted to go in the procession in four days' time, when the Feast of the Assumption came, and that I wanted Cencio to carry the lighted white torch for me. So, having gone off, I went to cut out the blue clothes, with a fine little gown of blue watered silk as well and a doublet of the same; and for him I made a doublet and a gown of taffeta, also blue.

When I had cut out these things, I went off to the Pope, who told me to speak to his Messer Ambrogio, since he had given orders that I should make a great work in gold. So I went to find Messer Ambrogio; who was very well informed about the sheriff business, and had been in agreement with my enemies about making me come back, and had shouted at the sheriff for not having taken me; and he excused himself, that against a safe conduct in that form he could not do it. Messer Ambrogio began to talk to me about the business the Pope had ordered; then he told me to make the designs for it and that everything would be provided.

Meanwhile the day of the Assumption came; and because the custom is that those who have such pardons must give themselves up as prisoners, on that account I went back to the Pope and told his Holiness that I did not want to put myself in prison, and that I begged him to do me the favour of not going to prison. The Pope answered me that that was the custom, and so it should be done. At this I knelt down again and thanked him for the safe conduct his Holiness had made me; and said that with it I would go back to serve my Duke of Florence, who was waiting for me with so much eagerness. At these words the Pope turned to a trusted man of his and said: "Let Benvenuto be given the pardon without the prison; and let his grant be drawn up for him so that it is in good form."

The grant having been drawn up, the Pope signed it; it was registered at the Capitol; then, on the appointed day, between two gentlemen, I went most honourably in the procession, and had the full pardon.

Book One, Chapters 84 to 87

Chapter 84

Four days later a very great fever took me with an unreckonable chill; and, having gone to bed, I judged at once that I was a dead man. I had the first doctors of Rome called, among them a Master Francesco da Norcia, a very old doctor and of the greatest credit in Rome. I told these doctors what I thought had been the cause of my great illness, and that I had wanted to be bled, but that I had been advised against it; and if I was still in time, I begged them to bleed me. Master Francesco answered that bleeding now would not be good, but then it would have been, and I should have had no trouble in the world; now I must be treated another way.

So they set their hands to treating me with all the diligence they could and knew in the world; and every day I got furiously worse, so that at the end of a week the illness grew so much that the doctors, despairing of the business, gave orders that I should be humoured and given everything I asked for. Master Francesco said: "As long as there is breath in him, call me at any hour, because nobody can imagine what nature can do in a young man of this sort; therefore, if he faints, give him these five remedies one after another, and send for me, and I shall come at any hour of the night; because it would please me more to save this man than any cardinal in Rome."

Messer Giovanni Gaddi came to visit me two or three times a day, and every time he took up one of my fine guns and my coats of mail and my swords, and kept saying: "This thing is beautiful, and this other one is more beautiful" — and so with my little models and small things: so that he had become a nuisance to me. And with him came a certain Mattio Franzesi, who seemed to think that a thousand years had gone by before I died as well; not because anything of mine would come to him, but he seemed to want what Messer Giovanni was showing such a great appetite for.

I had that Felice, my partner, who gave me the greatest help that one man in the world can ever give another. My nature was weakened and thoroughly beaten down; I had no strength left, once the breath had gone out, to take it in again; but the soundness of my brain stood firm, just as it did when I had nothing wrong with me. So, being in my right mind like that, a terrible old man came to find me at the bedside and wanted to drag me by force into a great boat of his; on account of which I called that Felice of mine to come near me and drive that scoundrelly old man away. Felice, who was most loving to me, ran up weeping and said: "Get away, you traitor of an old man, who want to rob me of all my good."

Messer Giovanni Gaddi, being there present, said: "The poor fellow is raving, and there are only a few hours left in him." That other man, Mattio Franzesi, said: "He has been reading Dante, and in this great illness that wandering has come over him"; and he said, laughing: "Get away, you old scoundrel, and do not bother our Benvenuto."

Seeing myself mocked, I turned to Messer Giovanni Gaddi and said to him: "My dear master, know that I am not raving, and that it is true about this old man who is giving me this great trouble. But you would do much better to take that wretch Mattio out of my sight, who laughs at my illness; and since your lordship thinks me worthy to see him, you ought to come here with Messer Antonio Allegretti or with Messer Annibal Caro, or with others of your able men, who are persons of another discretion and another wit than this beast."

Then Messer Giovanni said jokingly to Mattio that he should get out of his sight for good; but because Mattio laughed, the joke became earnest, because Messer Giovanni never wanted to see him again, and had Messer Antonio Allegretti called, and Messer Lodovico, and Messer Annibal Caro.

When these decent men had come, I took the greatest comfort from it, and talked with them a good while in my right mind, still urging Felice to drive the old man away. Messer Lodovico kept asking me what it was that I seemed to see, and what he was like. While I was drawing him for them well in words, this old man took me by an arm and pulled me to him by force; on account of which I shouted for them to help me, because he wanted to throw me under the deck in that frightening boat of his. When I had said that last word, a very great faintness came on me, and it seemed to me that he threw me into that boat.

They say that then, in this faint, I threw myself about and said ugly words to Messer Giovanni Gaddi, that he was coming to rob me and not out of any charity; and a great many other very ugly words which made Messer Giovanni ashamed. Then they said that I lay still like a dead man; and having waited more than an hour, and thinking that I was growing cold, they left me for dead. And having gone back to their houses, that Mattio Franzesi heard of it, and wrote to Florence to Messer Benedetto Varchi, a very dear friend of mine, that at such and such an hour of the night they had seen me die. On account of which that great and able man Messer Benedetto, and my great friend, made a marvellous sonnet on my death, which was not real but was well believed, and it will be put in its place.

More than three long hours went by before I came to myself; and, all the remedies of Master Francesco having been given, and seeing that I did not come round, my dearest Felice set off at a run to Master Francesco da Norcia's house, and knocked so hard that he woke him and got him up, and weeping begged him to come to the house, because he thought I was dead. To whom Master Francesco, who was extremely choleric, said: "Son, what do you think I would do by coming? If he is dead, I am sorrier for it than you are; do you think that with my medicine, if I came, I could blow up his backside and give him back to you alive?" Seeing that the poor young man was going off weeping, he called him back and gave him a certain oil to rub on my wrists and my heart, and told them to squeeze the little fingers of my feet and hands very tight; and that if I came round they should send for him at once.

Felice, having gone off, did everything Master Francesco had told him; and, it being nearly broad daylight, and thinking themselves without hope, they gave orders to make the shroud and to wash me. In a moment I came round and called Felice to drive that old man away quickly, quickly, who was bothering me. Felice wanted to send for Master Francesco, and I said he should not send, and that he should come here to me, because the old man went off at once and was afraid of him. Felice having come up to me, I touched him and it seemed to me that the old man went off in a fury; so I begged him always to stay by me.

Master Francesco, having appeared, said that he meant to save me at all costs, and that he had never in his life seen greater strength in a young man than that; and, setting his hand to writing, he ordered me perfumes, washings, ointments, plasters and a great many unreckonable things. Meanwhile I came round with more than twenty leeches on my backside, pricked, bandaged and altogether ground down.

A good many friends of mine having come to see the miracle of the resurrected dead man, men of great importance had come, and plenty of them; in front of whom I said that the little gold and money I had, which might be about eight hundred scudi between gold, silver, jewels and money, I wanted to go to my poor sister who was in Florence, whose name was Monna Liperata; and all the rest of my goods, weapons and everything else, I wanted to go to my dearest Felice, and fifty gold ducats over, so that he could clothe himself. At these words Felice threw himself on my neck, saying that he wanted nothing, only that he wanted me alive. Then I said: "If you want me alive, touch me like that, and shout at that old man, who is afraid of you." At these words there were some who were frightened, knowing that I was not raving but was speaking to the purpose and in my right mind.

So my great illness went on, and I improved little. That most excellent man Master Francesco came four or five times a day; Messer Giovanni Gaddi, who was ashamed, did not come before me any more.

My brother-in-law appeared, my sister's husband: he was coming from Florence for the inheritance; and because he was a very decent man, he was very glad to have found me alive; and it gave me an unreckonable comfort to see him, and he at once made much of me, saying that he had come only to look after me with his own hands; and so he did for several days. Then I sent him away, having an almost certain hope of health. Then he left the sonnet of Messer Benedetto Varchi, which is this:

	On the believed and untrue death
	of Benvenuto Cellini
	Who will console us, Mattio? Who is there
	to stop us dying of our weeping, since
	it is true, alas, that without us
	that clear friendly soul has climbed to Heaven
	so early — the soul in which such worth
	flowered that down to his own time
	the world saw no equal, nor will see one,
	I think, since the best go first?
	Gentle spirit, if outside the mortal veil
	one loves, look down from Heaven on those you loved
	on earth, weeping not for your good but for their own harm.
	You have gone to contemplate up in Heaven
	the high Maker, and now see him living, just as
	with your learned hands you formed him down here.

Chapter 85

The illness had been so beyond all reckoning that it did not seem possible to come to an end of it; and that decent man Master Francesco da Norcia laboured harder over it than ever, and every day brought me new remedies, trying to make the poor disordered instrument firm again; and with all those unreckonable labours it did not seem possible to come to the end of this indignation, so that all the doctors had almost despaired of it and did not know what more to do.

I had an unreckonable thirst, and had been on my guard, as they had ordered me, for many days; and Felice, who felt he had done a fine piece of work in saving me, never left me; and that old man no longer gave me so much trouble, but sometimes visited me in dreams.

One day Felice had gone out, and there had been left to look after me an errand boy of mine and a servant girl called Beatrice. I asked the boy what had become of my lad Cencio, and what it meant that I had never seen him in my need. The boy told me that Cencio had had a far greater illness than I had, and that he was at the point of death. Felice had ordered them not to tell me. When he had told me such a thing I took the greatest sorrow from it.

Then I called that servant Beatrice, who was from Pistoia, and begged her to bring me full of clear cold water a great crystal cooler that was there near by. This woman ran at once and brought it to me full. I told her to hold it to my mouth, and that if she let me drink a draught of it my own way, I would give her a gown. This servant, who had stolen certain little things of some importance from me, and who out of fear that the theft would be found out would have been very glad if I had died, let me drink as much of that water as I could in two goes, so that in fact I drank more than a flask of it; then I covered myself up and began to sweat, and fell asleep.

Felice came back after I must have slept about an hour, and asked the boy what I was doing. The boy said: "I do not know: Beatrice brought him that cooler full of water, and he has drunk nearly all of it; I do not know now whether he is dead or alive." They say that this poor young man nearly fell to the ground for the great sorrow he took; then he took a bad stick and with it beat the servant desperately, saying: "Alas, you traitress, you have killed him for me!"

While Felice was beating her and she was shouting, I was dreaming; and it seemed to me that the old man had cords in his hands, and, wanting to give orders to tie me up, Felice had come on him and was hitting him with an axe, so that this old man ran off, saying: "Let me go, and I shall not come back for a long time."

Meanwhile Beatrice, shouting loudly, had run into my room; on account of which, waking up, I said: "Let her be, because perhaps in wanting to do me harm she has done me so much good that you, with all your labours, never managed to do anything of what she has done entirely: attend to helping me, because I am sweating; and be quick."

Felice took heart again, dried me and comforted me; and I, feeling the greatest improvement, promised myself my health. Master Francesco, appearing, and seeing the great improvement, and the servant weeping, and the errand boy running back and forth, and Felice laughing, this confusion gave the doctor to believe that some strange thing had happened which was the cause of that great improvement of mine.

Meanwhile that other one, Master Bernardino, appeared, who at the beginning had not wanted to bleed me. Master Francesco, a very able man, said: "Oh, the power of nature! She knows her own needs, and the doctors know nothing." That little brain of a Master Bernardino answered at once and said: "If he had drunk another flask he would have been cured at once." Master Francesco da Norcia, an old man and of great authority, said: "It was the bad luck that God send you." And then he turned to me and asked me whether I could have drunk any more; to whom I said no, because I had entirely taken away my thirst. Then he turned to Master Bernardino and said: "Do you see that nature took exactly what she needed, and no more and no less? So she was asking for what she needed when the poor young man asked you to bleed him: if you knew that his health lay in drinking two flasks of water now, why did you not say so before? And you would have had the credit."

At these words the little apology of a doctor went off sulking, and never came there again.

Then Master Francesco said that I should be taken out of that room, and that they should have me carried towards one of the hills of Rome. Cardinal Cornaro, hearing of my improvement, had me carried to a place of his that he had on Monte Cavallo: that same evening I was carried there with great care in a chair, well covered and steady. When I got there I began to vomit; and in that vomiting there came out of my stomach a hairy worm a quarter of an arm long: the hairs were long and the worm was very ugly, spotted with different colours, green, black and red. It was kept for the doctor, who said he had never seen such a thing, and then said to Felice: "Now take care of your Benvenuto, who is cured, and do not let him make any disorder; because although that has saved him, another disorder now would kill him. You see, the illness has been so great that when they brought him the holy oil we were too late; now I know that with a little patience and time he will make other beautiful works."

Then he turned to me and said: "My Benvenuto, be wise and make no disorder at all; and when you are cured I want you to make me a Madonna by your own hand, because I want to adore her always for love of you." Then I promised it him; then I asked him whether it would be a good thing for me to go as far as Florence. Then he told me to make myself a little safer first, and to see what nature did.

Chapter 86

A week having passed, the improvement was so little that I had almost become a nuisance to myself, because I had been more than fifty days in that great trouble; and, having made up my mind, I got ready; and in a pair of panniers my dear Felice and I went off towards Florence.

And because I had written nothing, I arrived in Florence at my sister's house, where I was wept over and laughed over at the same time by my sister. That day a good many friends of mine came to see me; among the others Pier Landi, who was the greatest and dearest I ever had in the world. The next day a certain Niccolò da Monte Aguto came, who was a very great friend of mine; and because he had heard the Duke say, "Benvenuto would have done much better to die, because he has come here to put his head in a halter, and I shall never forgive him" — Niccolò, coming to me, said desperately: "Alas, my dear Benvenuto, what have you come here to do? Did you not know what you have done against the Duke? Because I have heard him swear, saying that you have come to put your head in a halter at all costs."

Then I said: "Niccolò, remind his Excellency that Pope Clement once wanted to do the same to me, and just as wrongly; let him have me looked after and let me get well; because I shall show his Excellency that I have been the most faithful servant he will ever have in his life; and since some enemy of mine will have done me this bad turn out of envy, let him wait for my recovery, and as soon as I can I shall give him such an account of myself that I shall make him wonder."

This bad turn had been done me by Giorgetto Vasari of Arezzo, the painter, perhaps in payment for so many kindnesses done him; for I had kept him in Rome and paid his keep, and he had turned my house upside down for me; because he had a dry sort of leprosy, and had got his hands used to scratching all the time; and, sleeping with a good apprentice I had, called Manno, thinking he was scratching himself he had flayed Manno's leg with those dirty little hands of his, whose nails he never cut. Manno took his leave of me, and wanted to kill him at all costs: I made peace between them; then I got Giorgio a place with the Cardinal de' Medici, and always helped him. This is the reward: that he had told Duke Alessandro that I had spoken ill of his Excellency, and that I had boasted that I would be the first to leap onto the walls of Florence, in agreement with his Excellency's exiled enemies.

These words, according to what I heard afterwards, that fine gentleman Ottaviano de' Medici had made him say, wanting to revenge himself for the anger the Duke had shown him over the coins and over my leaving Florence; but I, being innocent of that falsehood laid on me, had no fear in the world at all. And that able man Master Francesco da Montevarchi treated me with the greatest skill, and my dearest friend Luca Martini had brought him to me, who stayed with me the greater part of the day.

Chapter 87

In the meantime I had sent the most faithful Felice back to Rome to look after my business there. Having lifted my head a little off the pillow, which was at the end of a fortnight, although I could not go on my own feet, I had myself carried into the Medici palace, up to where the little terrace is; and so I had myself set down to sit and wait for the Duke to pass.

And when a good many friends of mine at Court greeted me, they wondered a great deal that I had taken the trouble to have myself carried there in that way, being so badly reduced by the illness; telling me that I ought to have waited to be cured and to visit the Duke afterwards. A good many being gathered together, they all looked at me as a miracle; not so much because they had heard I was dead, but it seemed to them more of a miracle that I looked to them like a dead man.

Then I said, in front of them all, that some wicked scoundrel had told my lord the Duke that I had boasted that I meant to be the first to climb onto his Excellency's walls, and that afterwards I had spoken ill of him; on account of which I had not the nerve either to live or to die until I had first purged myself of this disgrace, and known who the reckless scoundrel was who had made that false report.

At these words a great number of those gentlemen had gathered; and showing the greatest compassion for me, one said one thing and one another; I said that I would never leave that place until I knew who it was who had accused me. At these words there came up, among all those gentlemen, Master Agostino, the Duke's tailor, and said: "If you want to know nothing else but that, you shall know it this very minute." Just then that Giorgio the painter came by: then Master Agostino said: "There is the man who accused you: now you can tell whether it is true or not."

I boldly, unable to move as I was, asked Giorgio whether such a thing were true. Giorgio said no, that it was not true, and that he had never said such a thing. Master Agostino said: "Oh, you gallows-bird, do you not know that I know it for certain?" Giorgio went off at once, and said no, that it had not been him.

Not long afterwards the Duke passed by; at whom I at once had myself held up before his Excellency, and he stopped. Then I said that I had come there in that way only to justify myself. The Duke looked at me and wondered that I was alive; then he told me to attend to being a decent man and getting well.

Having gone back home, Niccolò da Monte Aguto came to find me and told me that I had got through one of the greatest storms in the world, which he had never believed I would; because he had seen my trouble written in an unchangeable ink; and that I should attend to getting well quickly, and then go off with God, because it came from a place and from a man who would have done me harm. And then, having said "Take care", he said to me: "What harm have you done that scoundrel Ottaviano de' Medici?" I told him that I had never done him any harm, but that he had done plenty to me; and, having told him the whole business of the mint, he said: "Go off with God as soon as you can, and be of good cheer, because sooner than you think you will see your revenge."

I attended to getting well; I gave advice to Pietro Paolo about the business of the coin dies; then I went off with God, going back to Rome, without a word to the Duke or anybody else.

Book One, Chapters 88 to 92

Chapter 88

When I got to Rome, having made much of my friends, I began the Duke's medal; and I had already made in a few days the head in steel, the most beautiful work I ever made in that line; and there came to see me at least once every day a certain great fool called Messer Francesco Soderini. Seeing what I was doing, he said to me several times: "Alas, you cruel man, so you mean to make this raging tyrant immortal for us. And because you never made so beautiful a work, it can be seen by this that you are our sworn enemy and so great a friend of theirs — since the Pope and he both wanted to have you hanged twice, and wrongly: that was the father and the son; now beware of the Holy Ghost." It was certainly held that Duke Alessandro was Pope Clement's son. Messer Francesco also said, and swore it expressly, that if he could he would have stolen those dies of that medal from me. To whom I said that he had done well to tell me, and that I would guard them in such a way that he would never see them again.

I sent word to Florence that they should tell Lorenzino to send me the reverse of the medal. Niccolò da Monte Aguto, to whom I had written it, wrote back to me, telling me that he had asked that mad melancholy philosopher Lorenzino, who had told him that day and night he thought of nothing else, and that he would do it as soon as he could; therefore he told me not to put any hope in his reverse, and to make one myself of my own pure invention; and that when I had finished it I should take it freely to the Duke, and it would be good for me.

Having made a design for a reverse which seemed to me to the purpose, I carried it forward with all the urgency I could; but because I was not yet safe from that measureless illness, I took a good deal of pleasure in going shooting with my gun along with that dear Felice of mine, who knew nothing at all of my art; but because we were continually together, day and night, everybody imagined that he was most excellent in the art. On account of which he, who was the pleasantest of men, and I laughed together a thousand times over this great credit he had won; and because he was called Felice Guadagni — Felix Gains — he said to me jokingly: "I should call myself Felice Gains-little, if you had not made me acquire so great a credit that I can call myself Gains-a-great-deal." And I told him that there are two ways of gaining: the first is what one gains for oneself, the second is what one gains for others; so that I praised in him that second way far more than the first, since he had gained me my life.

We had these conversations again and again; but among the rest, one day at Epiphany, we were together near the Magliana, and the day was already almost done; on which day I had killed a good many ducks and geese with my gun; and having almost made up my mind not to shoot any more that day, we were coming back urgently towards Rome. Calling my dog, whom I called by the name of Barucco, and not seeing him in front of me, I turned and saw that the well-trained dog was watching certain geese that had settled down in a ditch. On account of which I got down at once; and having got my good gun ready, I fired at them from a long way off and got two of them with a single ball; because I never wanted to fire with anything but a single ball, with which I shot two hundred arms' lengths and hit the mark most of the time; which one cannot do those other ways. So, having hit the two geese, one almost dead and the other wounded, which, wounded like that, was flying badly, my dog went after this one and brought it to me; the other, seeing that it was diving down into the ditch, I came up on it. Trusting to my boots, which were quite high, pushing my foot forward, the ground gave way under me: and although I got the goose, I had the boot of my right leg quite full of water. Having lifted my foot in the air I emptied out the water, and, having mounted, we hurried to get back to Rome.

But because it was very cold, I felt my leg freezing in such a way that I said to Felice: "This leg needs help here, because I no longer know any way of being able to bear it." The good Felice, without saying anything else, got down off his horse, and, having taken thistles and small sticks and set about making a fire, meanwhile, while I was waiting, having put my hands into the feathers of the breast of one of those geese, I felt a good deal of warmth; on account of which I did not have the fire made after all, but filled that boot of mine with those goose feathers, and at once I felt so much comfort that it gave me back my life.

Chapter 89

I mounted, and we came on urgently towards Rome. When we had got to a certain little rise, it being already night, looking towards Florence we both together let out a great cry of astonishment, saying: "O God of heaven, what is that great thing to be seen over Florence?" It was like a great beam of fire, which sparkled and gave out a very great brightness. I said to Felice: "Certainly we shall hear tomorrow that some great thing has happened in Florence."

So, having come on to Rome, it was very dark; and when we had got near the Banchi and near our house, I had a little horse under me which went at a very furious amble; so that, a heap of rubble and broken tiles having been made in the middle of the street that day, my horse, not seeing the heap, nor I, went up it at that speed, and then, coming down, stumbled, so as to turn a somersault: it put its head between its legs; whereby, by the express power of God, I did not do myself any harm in the world. Lights having been brought out by the neighbours at that great noise, I, who had jumped to my feet, without mounting again ran home laughing, having escaped a chance of breaking my neck.

When I got home I found there certain friends of mine, to whom, while we were supping together, I told the tight places of the shooting and that devilry of the beam of fire that we had seen; and they said: "What on earth is that going to mean?" I said: "Something new must have happened in Florence." So, having passed our supper pleasantly, the next day late the news came to Rome of Duke Alessandro's death.

On account of which many acquaintances of mine came saying to me: "You said rightly that some great thing had happened over Florence." At this there came trotting up on a certain wretched little mule that Messer Francesco Soderini; laughing wildly and madly along the way, he said: "This is the reverse of the medal of that wicked tyrant, that your Lorenzino de' Medici promised you." And besides he added: "You wanted to make dukes immortal for us: we do not want any more dukes." And there he made fun of me as if I had been a head of one of those factions that make dukes.

At this there came up a certain Baccio Bettini, who had a great head like a basket, and he too made fun of me about these dukes, telling me: "We have un-duked them, and we shall have no more dukes; and you wanted to make them immortal for us" — with a great many of these annoying words. Which becoming too much of a nuisance to me, I said to them: "Oh, you great fools, I am a poor goldsmith who serves whoever pays me, and you make fun of me as if I were a head of a party; but I do not for that want to reproach you with the insatiability, madness and worthlessness of your forefathers. But I do say plainly to all this foolish laughter you are making, that before two or three days at the most have gone by you will have another duke, perhaps much worse than the one who has gone."

The next day that man Bettini came to my shop and said to me: "There would be no need to spend money on couriers, because you know things before they happen: what spirit is it that tells you them?" And he told me that Cosimo de' Medici, son of Signor Giovanni, had been made Duke; but that he had been made so with certain conditions which would hold him in, so that he would not be able to flutter about in his own way. Then it was my turn to laugh at them, and I said: "These men of Florence have put a young man on a marvellous horse, then they have put spurs on him and given him the bridle in his hand at his own liberty, and put him in a most beautiful field, where there are flowers and fruits and a great many delights; and then they have told him not to go beyond certain marked bounds. Now tell me, who is there who can hold him back when he wants to go beyond them? Laws cannot be given to the man who is master of them." So they left me alone and did not bother me.

Chapter 90

Having attended to my shop, I went on with some business of mine, not of much moment, because I was attending to the restoration of my health, and it did not yet seem to me that I was safe from the great illness I had been through.

In the meantime the Emperor was coming back victorious from the Tunis expedition, and the Pope had sent for me and was taking counsel with me about what sort of honourable present I advised him to give the Emperor. To whom I said that what seemed to me most to the purpose was to give his Majesty a gold cross with a Christ, for which I had almost made an ornament, which would be very much to the purpose and would do the greatest honour to his Holiness and to me. I had already made three little gold figures, in the round, about a palm high: these figures were the ones I had begun for Pope Clement's chalice; they were figured for Faith, Hope and Charity. So I added in wax all the rest of the foot of the cross; and having taken it to the Pope with the Christ in wax and with many very beautiful ornaments, it greatly satisfied the Pope; and before I left his Holiness we came to agreement about everything that was to be done, and afterwards we valued the making of the work.

This was one evening at four hours of the night; the Pope had given orders to Messer Latino Juvinale to have money given me the following morning. It seemed good to Messer Latino, who had a great streak of madness in him, to want to give the Pope a new invention which should come from him alone; and he upset everything that had been arranged. And in the morning, when I thought I was going for the money, he said with that beastly presumption of his: "It is for us to be the inventors, and for you to be the workmen. Before I left the Pope in the evening, we thought of something much better." At which first words, not letting him go any further, I said: "Neither you nor the Pope can ever think of anything better than the things where Christ comes in; so now say all the courtiers' rubbish you know." Without saying anything else, he went off from me in a rage, and tried to give the work to another goldsmith; but the Pope would not have it, and sent for me at once and told me that I had spoken well, but that they wanted to use a Book of Our Lady, which was illuminated marvellously, and which had cost Cardinal de' Medici more than two thousand scudi to have illuminated: and this would be to the purpose to make a present to the Empress, and for the Emperor they would afterwards make what I had planned, which was truly a present worthy of him; but this was being done because there was little time, since the Emperor was expected in Rome within a month and a half.

For this book he wanted a cover made of solid gold, richly worked and adorned with many jewels. The jewels were worth about six thousand scudi: so that, having given me the jewels and the gold, I set my hand to the work, and, urging it on, in a few days I made it come out with such beauty that the Pope marvelled and showed me the greatest favours, on condition that that beast Juvinale should not come about me.

Having the work near its end, the Emperor appeared, for whom many marvellous triumphal arches had been made; and having come into Rome with marvellous pomp, which it falls to others to write about, because I want to deal only with what concerns me, on his arrival he at once gave the Pope a diamond which he had bought for twelve thousand scudi. The Pope sent for me about this diamond and gave it me, that I should make him a ring to the measure of his Holiness's finger; but he said he wanted me first to bring the book to the point it had got to.

When I had brought the book to the Pope, it greatly satisfied him; then he took counsel with me about what excuse could be found with the Emperor that would be valid for that work being unfinished. Then I said that the valid excuse was that I should speak of my ill health, which his Majesty would very easily believe, seeing me as thin and dark as I was. At this the Pope said that he liked it very much; but that I should add, on his Holiness's behalf, in making him the present of the book, to make him a present of myself as well; and he told me the whole manner I was to keep to, and the words I was to say, which words I said to the Pope, asking him whether he was pleased that I should say it so. He said to me: "You said it far too well, if you had the nerve to speak in that way to the Emperor as you speak to me."

Then I said that with far greater confidence I had the nerve to speak with the Emperor; seeing that the Emperor went dressed as I went, and it would seem to me that I was speaking to a man who was made as I was; which did not happen to me speaking with his Holiness, in whom I saw a far greater divinity, both because of the ecclesiastical ornaments, which showed me a kind of halo, together with his Holiness's fine old age: all these things made me more afraid than those of the Emperor did. At these words the Pope said: "Go, my Benvenuto, for you are an able man: do us honour, and it will be good for you."

Chapter 91

The Pope ordered two Turkish horses, which had been Pope Clement's, and were the most beautiful that ever came into Christendom. These two horses the Pope ordered Messer Durante his chamberlain to lead down to the corridors of the palace, and there to give them to the Emperor, saying certain words that he laid on him.

We went down together; and when we came before the Emperor, those two horses came in through those rooms with such majesty and such spirit that the Emperor and everybody marvelled. At this Messer Durante came forward in such a graceless way, and with certain words of his in the Brescian manner, his tongue knotting itself in his mouth, that a worse thing was never seen or heard: it moved the Emperor somewhat to laughter.

Meanwhile I had already uncovered my work; and, having noticed that the Emperor had turned his eyes on me in the most gracious way, I came forward at once and said: "Sacred Majesty, our most holy Pope Paul sends this Book of Our Lady as a present to your Majesty, which is written by hand and illuminated by the hand of the greatest man who ever practised that profession; and this rich cover of gold and jewels is unfinished on account of my ill health: for which reason his Holiness, together with the book, presents me as well, that I may come along with your Majesty to finish your book for you; and moreover, whatever you may have a mind to have made, for as long as I live I shall serve you in it."

At this the Emperor said: "The book is welcome to me and so are you; but I want you to finish it for me in Rome; and when it is finished and you are well, bring it to me and come and find me." Then, talking with me, he called me by my name; on account of which I wondered, because no words had passed in which my name could have come up; and he told me he had seen that clasp for Pope Clement's cope, on which I had made so many marvellous figures. So we stretched out our conversation for a whole half hour, talking of a good many different things, all of them skilful and pleasant; and because it seemed to me that I had come out of it with far greater honour than I had promised myself, having made a little cadence in the talk, I made my bow and went off.

The Emperor was heard to say: "Let Benvenuto be given five hundred gold scudi at once" — so that the man who took them up asked which was the Pope's man who had spoken to the Emperor. Messer Durante came forward, and robbed me of my five hundred scudi. I complained of it to the Pope; who said that I should not worry; that he knew everything, how well I had borne myself in speaking to the Emperor, and that of that money I should have my share at all events.

Chapter 92

Having gone back to my shop, I set my hand with great urgency to finishing the diamond ring; about which there were sent to me four of the first jewellers of Rome; because it had been said to the Pope that that diamond was set by the hand of the first jeweller in the world, in Venice, whose name was Master Miliano Targhetta, and, the diamond being somewhat thin, it was too difficult a business to do without great counsel.

I was glad of the four jeweller men, among whom was a Milanese called Gaio. He was the most presumptuous beast in the world, and the one who knew least and thought he knew most; the others were most modest and very able men. This Gaio began to speak before all the rest and said: "Save Miliano's foil, and to that, Benvenuto, you will take off your cap; because just as tinting a diamond is the most beautiful and the most difficult thing in the jeweller's art, Miliano is the greatest jeweller there ever was in the world, and this is the most difficult diamond."

Then I said that so much the greater glory it was to me to fight with so brave a man of such a profession; then I turned to the other jewellers and said: "See, I am saving Miliano's foil; and I shall try whether by making one I can improve on it; and if not, we shall tint it again with that same one." The beastly Gaio said that if I did it that way he would willingly take off his cap to it. To whom I said: "Then if I do it better, it deserves the cap off twice." "Yes," he said; and so I began to make my foils.

Having set about making the foils with the greatest diligence — which I shall teach in its place how they are made — that diamond was certainly the most difficult that ever came before me either before or since, and Miliano's foil was skilfully made; still it did not dismay me. I, having sharpened up the little tools of my wits, managed not only to come up to it but to pass it very well. Then, knowing that I had beaten him, I went looking to beat myself, and with new methods I made a foil that was better than the one I had made, by a long way.

Then I sent to call the jewellers, and, having tinted the diamond with Miliano's foil, then having cleaned it well, I tinted it again with mine. Having shown it to the jewellers, one of the first able men among them, called Raffaello del Moro, took the diamond in his hand and said to Gaio: "Benvenuto has passed Miliano's foil." Gaio, who would not believe it, took the diamond in his hand and said: "Benvenuto, this diamond is two thousand ducats better than with Miliano's foil."

Then I said: "Since I have beaten Miliano, let us see whether I can beat myself"; and having begged them to wait for me a little, I went up to a little upper room of mine, and out of their sight tinted the diamond again, and, having brought it to the jewellers, Gaio said at once: "This is the most marvellous thing I ever saw in my life, because this diamond is worth better than eighteen thousand scudi, where we hardly valued it at twelve." The other jewellers, turning to Gaio, said: "Benvenuto is the glory of our art, and deservedly we ought to take off our caps both to his foils and to him." Gaio then said: "I mean to go and tell the Pope, and I want him to have a thousand gold scudi for the setting of this diamond." And running off to the Pope, he told him everything; on account of which the Pope sent three times that day to see whether the ring was finished.

At twenty-three hours I took the ring up; and because the door was not kept against me, having lifted the curtain discreetly, I saw the Pope together with the Marquis del Vasto, who must have been pressing him about things he did not want to do; and I heard him say to the Marquis: "I tell you no, because it is my part to be neutral and nothing else." Having drawn back quickly, the Pope himself called me; so I went in quickly, and having handed him that beautiful diamond, the Pope drew me aside, so that the Marquis moved away.

The Pope, while he was looking at the diamond, said to me: "Benvenuto, start a conversation with me that looks as if it were important, and do not stop as long as the Marquis is here in this room." And having begun to walk about, the thing he was doing for my sake pleased me, and I began to talk with the Pope about the way I had gone about tinting the diamond. The Marquis stood upright at one side, leaning against a tapestry, and shifted now onto one foot and now onto the other.

The theme of this conversation was of such importance, if one wanted to tell it properly, that one could have talked of it for three whole hours. The Pope took such great pleasure in it that it went beyond the displeasure he had at the Marquis being there. I had mixed into the talk that part of philosophy which belonged to that profession; so that, having talked for close on an hour, the Marquis, becoming bored, went off half in a rage. Then the Pope made me the most familiar caresses anyone in the world can imagine, and said: "Wait, my Benvenuto, and I shall give you another reward for your skills than the thousand scudi that Gaio has told me your labour deserves."

So, when I had gone, the Pope praised me in front of those familiars of his, among whom was that Latino Juvinale I have spoken of. Who, having become my enemy, tried with every study to do me harm; and, seeing that the Pope spoke of me with so much affection and admiration, said: "There is no doubt at all that Benvenuto is a person of marvellous wit; but although every man is naturally bound to love those of his own country more than others, one ought still to consider well in what way one should speak of a Pope. He has had it to say that Pope Clement was the finest prince there ever was, and just as able, but with bad fortune; and he says that your Holiness is quite the opposite, and that the tiara weeps on your head, and that you look like a truss of straw dressed up, and that there is nothing in you but good fortune."

These words had such force, said by a man who knew perfectly well how to say them, that the Pope believed them: I not only had not said them, but such a thing never came into my consideration. If the Pope could have done it with his honour, he would have done me the greatest harm; but, being a person of the greatest wit, he made a show of laughing at it: nevertheless he kept inside himself so great a hatred of me that it was beyond all reckoning. And I began to notice it, because I did not get into his rooms with the same ease as before, but with the greatest difficulty. And because I had been many years practised in those courts, I imagined that somebody had done me a bad turn; and, making neat enquiries about it, I was told the whole thing, but I was not told who it had been; and I could not imagine who had said such a thing, for if I had known it I should have taken revenge for it by the shovelful.

Book One, Chapters 93 to 97

Chapter 93

I attended to finishing my little book; and when I had finished it I took it to the Pope, who really could not help praising it to me a great deal. To whom I said that he should send me to take it, as he had promised me. The Pope answered that he would do what it suited him to do, and that I had done what belonged to me. So he gave orders that I should be well paid.

Out of those works, in a little more than two months, I made five hundred scudi: the diamond was paid me at the rate of a hundred and fifty scudi and no more; all the rest was given me for the making of that little book, which making deserved more than a thousand, being a work rich in a great many figures and leafwork and enamels and jewels. I took what I could get, and made up my mind to go off with God out of Rome.

At this the Pope sent the little book to the Emperor by a nephew of his called Signor Sforza; who, presenting the book to the Emperor, the Emperor was most grateful for it and at once asked about me. The young Signor Sforza, having been coached, said that I had not come because I was ill. All of it was repeated to me.

Meanwhile I got ready to go off towards France; and I wanted to go quite alone, but I could not, because of a young man who was with me, called Ascanio. This young man was of very tender years and was the most marvellous servant there ever was in the world; and when I took him he had left a master of his called Francesco, who was a Spaniard and a goldsmith. I, who would not have wanted to take this boy so as not to come into a quarrel with the Spaniard, said to Ascanio: "I do not want you, so as not to displease your master." He managed so that his master wrote me a note saying I might freely take him.

So he had been with me many months; and because he had come to me thin and pinched, we called him the Little Old Man; and I thought he was a little old man, both because he served so well, and because he was so knowing, since it did not seem reasonable that at the age of thirteen, which he said he was, there should be so much wit.

Now, to come back: in those few months he filled out, and, having recovered from his hardship, became the handsomest young man in Rome; and both because he was that good servant I have described, and because he learned the art marvellously, I set the greatest love on him as on a son, and I kept him dressed as if he had been my son. Seeing himself restored, the young man felt that he had had a great piece of luck to fall into my hands. He often went to thank his master, who had been the cause of his great good; and because this master of his had a beautiful young wife, she used to say: "Surgetto, what have you done that you have become so handsome?" — for that is what they called him when he was with them. Ascanio answered her: "Madonna Francesca, it has been my master who has made me so handsome and much better besides."

This spiteful little woman took it very badly that Ascanio should say so; and because she had the name of being an unchaste woman, she knew how to make the young man some caresses perhaps beyond the use of decency; on account of which I noticed that this young man went to see his mistress a good deal more often than was usual with him.

It happened that, having one day beaten a shop boy badly, when I came in, coming from outside, the boy complained, weeping, telling me that Ascanio had beaten him for no reason at all. At which words I said to Ascanio: "With reason or without reason, never let me hear that you have struck anybody in my house again, because you will feel how I know how to strike." He answered me back, and I threw myself on him at once and gave him with fists and kicks the roughest beating he ever felt. As soon as he could get out of my hands, without cloak and without cap he ran out; and for two days I never knew where he was, nor did I look for him.

Only at the end of two days there came to speak to me a Spanish gentleman called Don Diego. He was the most generous man I ever knew in the world; I had made him, and was making him, certain works, so that he was very much a friend of mine. He told me that Ascanio had gone back to his old master, and that, if I thought fit, I should give him back his cap and cloak that I had given him. At these words I said that Francesco had behaved badly, and had acted like an ill-bred person; because if he had told me at once when Ascanio went to him, since he was in his house, I would very willingly have given him leave; but for having kept him two days and then not letting me know, I did not want him to stay with him, and let him see to it that I did not see him in his house in any way. Don Diego reported it; on account of which Francesco made a joke of it.

The next morning I saw Ascanio working at some rubbish of wirework beside his master. As I passed, Ascanio made me a bow, and his master almost laughed at me. He sent to tell me, by that gentleman Don Diego, that if I thought fit I should send back to Ascanio the clothes I had given him; and if not, he did not care, and Ascanio would not go short of clothes.

At these words I turned to Don Diego and said: "Signor Don Diego, in all your affairs I never saw a more generous or a more decent man than you; but this Francesco is quite the opposite of what you are, because he is a dishonoured renegade. Tell him this from me: that if before vespers ring he has not himself brought Ascanio back to me here at my shop, I shall kill him at all costs; and tell Ascanio that if he does not get away from there at that hour consecrated to his master, I shall do him little less."

At these words Signor Don Diego answered me nothing, but went and put such terror into Francesco that he did not know what to do. Meanwhile Ascanio had gone to look for his father, who had come to Rome from Tagliacozzo, where he came from; and, hearing of this confusion, he too advised Francesco that he ought to take Ascanio back to me. Francesco said to Ascanio: "Go there yourself, and your father will go with you." Don Diego said: "Francesco, I see some great scandal coming: you know better than I who Benvenuto is; take him back to him safely, and I will come with you."

I, having got myself ready, was walking about the shop waiting for the stroke of vespers, having disposed myself to do one of the most ruinous things I ever did in my life. At this Don Diego appeared, and Francesco and Ascanio, and the father, whom I did not know. Ascanio having come in, I looked at them all with an angry eye, and Francesco, pale of colour, said: "Here is Ascanio brought back to you, whom I kept, not thinking to displease you." Ascanio said reverently: "My master, forgive me; I am here to do everything you order me."

Then I said: "Have you come to finish the time you promised me?" He said yes, and never to leave me again. Then I turned and told that shop boy whom he had beaten to hand me that bundle of clothes; and to him I said: "Here are all the clothes I had given you, and with them take your freedom and go where you like."

Don Diego was left astonished at this, since he had expected anything else. At this, Ascanio together with his father begged me to forgive him and take him back. Having asked who it was who was speaking for him, he told me it was his father; to whom, after many entreaties, I said: "And because you are his father, for love of you I take him back."

Chapter 94

Having made up my mind, as I said a little while ago, to go off towards France — both because I had seen that the Pope did not think of me as he had before, since through evil tongues my long service had been muddied for me, and for fear that those who had the power might do me worse — for that reason I had disposed myself to look for another country, to see whether I could find better fortune; and I would willingly have gone off with God alone.

Having made up my mind one evening to leave in the morning, I told that faithful Felice that he should enjoy all my things until my return; and that if it happened that I did not come back, I wanted everything to be his.

And because I had a Perugian apprentice who had helped me finish those works for the Pope, I gave him his leave, having paid him for his labour. He told me that he begged me to let him come with me, and that he would come at his own expense; that if it happened that I stopped to work with the King of France, it was still better for me to have some of my Italians with me, and above all people whom I knew would know how to help me. He knew how to beg me so well that I was content to take him with me in the way he had said.

Ascanio, being also present at this conversation, said half in tears: "Since you took me back, I said that I meant to stay with you for life, and so I mean to do." I told him that I did not want him on any account. The poor young man began to get ready to come after me on foot. Seeing him take such a resolution, I took a horse for him as well, and having put a little valise of mine on the crupper, I loaded myself with far more baggage than I would have done.

And having left Rome, I came to Florence, and from Florence to Bologna, and from Bologna to Venice, and from Venice I went to Padua; where I was carried off from the inn by that dear friend of mine called Albertaccio del Bene. The next day I went to kiss the hands of Messer Pietro Bembo, who was not yet a cardinal. Messer Pietro made me the most extravagant caresses that can ever be made to any man in the world; then he turned to Albertaccio and said: "I want Benvenuto to stay here with all his people, even if he had a hundred of them; so make up your mind, if you want Benvenuto too, to stay here with me, otherwise I do not mean to give him back to you."

And so I stayed to enjoy myself with this most able lord. He had had a room got ready for me that would have been too honourable for a cardinal, and he insisted always that I should eat beside his lordship. Then he came, with the most modest talk, to show me that he would have liked me to take his portrait; and I, who wanted nothing else in the world, having made myself some very white stucco in a little box, began it; and the first day I worked two hours running, and sketched out that able head with such good grace that his lordship was left astonished; and, being a very great man in his letters and in poetry to a superlative degree, but understanding nothing at all in the world of this profession of mine, the reason is that it seemed to him I had finished it at that time, when I had hardly begun it: so that I could not make him understand that it needed a long time to do well.

At last I made up my mind to do it as well as I knew how, with the time it deserved; and because he wore his beard short, in the Venetian way, it gave me a great deal of trouble to make a head that satisfied me. Still, I finished it, and it seemed to me that I had made the most beautiful work I ever made, as far as my art was concerned. On account of which I saw that he was dismayed, because he thought that, having made it in wax in two hours, I ought to make it in ten in steel. Then, seeing that I had not been able to do it in two hundred hours in wax, and that I was asking leave to go off towards France, he was much put out by it, and asked me at least to make him a reverse for his medal; and this was a Pegasus in the middle of a wreath of myrtle. This I made in about three hours' time, giving it very good grace; and being very well satisfied, he said: "This horse seems to me a ten times greater thing than making a little head, over which you have laboured so long: I cannot grasp this difficulty."

Still he kept telling me and begging me that I ought to do it for him in steel, saying: "Please do it for me, because you will do it very quickly if you want to." I promised him that I did not want to do it there; but that wherever I settled to work I would do it for him without fail.

While we were keeping to this subject, I had gone off to bargain for three horses to go off towards France; and he had me kept an eye on secretly, because he had the greatest authority in Padua; so that when I wanted to pay for the horses, which I had bargained for at fifty ducats, the owner of the horses said to me: "Able man, I make you a present of the three horses." To whom I answered: "It is not you who are giving them me; and from the man who is giving them me I do not want them, because I have not been able to give him anything for my labour." The good man told me that if I did not take those horses I should not get any other horses out of Padua and would have to go on foot. At this I went off to the magnificent Messer Pietro, who made a show of knowing nothing, and still made much of me, telling me to stay on in Padua. I, who wanted nothing of it and was disposed to go at all costs, was forced to accept the three horses; and with them I went off.

Chapter 95

I took my road through the land of the Grisons, because no other road was safe, on account of the wars. We crossed the mountains of the Alba and the Bernina: it was the eighth of May and the snow was very deep. With the greatest danger to our lives we crossed these two mountains. When we had crossed them, we stopped at a place which, if I remember rightly, is called Wallenstadt: there we lodged.

That night a Florentine courier turned up there, who was called Il Busbacca. I had heard this courier spoken of as a man of credit and able in his profession, and I did not know that he had come down in the world through his own knaveries. When he saw me at the inn he called me by name and told me that he was going to Lyons about matters of importance, and that he begged me to lend him money for the journey. At this I said that I had no money to be able to lend him, but that if he wanted to come along with me I would pay his expenses as far as Lyons.

This scoundrel wept and made me fine speeches, telling me how, "over affairs of importance for the nation, a poor courier having run out of money, a man like you is bound to help him"; and besides he told me that he was carrying things of the greatest importance for Messer Filippo Strozzi; and because he had a case for a cup covered in leather, he told me in my ear that in that case was a silver cup, and that in that cup were jewels worth many thousands of ducats, and that there were letters of the greatest importance which Messer Filippo Strozzi was sending.

At this I told him to let me shut up the jewels on his own person, where they would be in less danger than carrying them in that cup; and to leave the cup to me, which might be worth about ten scudi, and I would serve him with twenty-five. At these words the courier said that he would come along with me, since he could do nothing else, because to leave that cup would be no honour to him. So we cut it short.

And in the morning, setting off, we came to a lake which is between Wallenstadt and Wesen; this lake is fifteen miles long, and one comes out at Wesen. Seeing the boats on this lake, I was afraid; because the boats are of fir, not very big and not very thick, and they are not nailed, nor even pitched; and if I had not seen four German gentlemen get into another like it with their horses, I should never have got into this one; on the contrary I would sooner have turned back; but I thought, from the beastly things I saw them do, that those German waters did not drown people as ours in Italy do.

Those two young men of mine kept saying: "Benvenuto, this is a dangerous thing to get into with four horses." To whom I said: "Do you not consider, you cowards, that those four gentlemen have gone in ahead of us, and are going off laughing? If this were wine, as it is water, I should say they were going cheerfully to drown in it; but because it is water, I know very well that they have no more pleasure in drowning in it than we have."

This lake was fifteen miles long and about three wide; on one side was a very high mountain full of caves, on the other it was flat and grassy. When we had gone about four miles in, the lake began to get up a storm, so that those who were rowing asked us for help, that we should help them row; and so we did for a while. I signalled, and said that they should throw us onto that further shore; they said it was not possible, because there is no water there to hold the boat up, and there are certain shallows on which the boat would break up at once and we should all be drowned; and still they urged us to help them. The boatmen were calling to each other, asking for help.

Seeing them dismayed, having a wise horse, I fixed the bridle round his neck and took a part of the halter in my left hand. The horse, who was, as they are, with some intelligence, seemed to have noticed what I meant to do, since, having turned his face towards that fresh grass, I wanted him to drag me with him as he swam.

At this there came so great a wave from that lake that it swamped the boat. Ascanio, shouting: "Mercy, my father, help me!" — meant to throw himself on me; on account of which I put my hand to my little dagger and told them to do what I had taught them, because the horses would save their lives just as I hoped to save mine that way; and that if he threw himself on me again I would kill him. So we went on several miles in this mortal danger.

Chapter 96

When we were halfway across the lake, we found a bit of flat ground where we could rest, and on this flat ground I saw those four German gentlemen had got out. When we wanted to get out, the boatman would not have it at any price. Then I said to my young men: "Now is the time to make some trial of ourselves: so put your hands to your swords, and let us make them put us on land by force." So we did, with great difficulty, because they made the greatest resistance.

Still, when we were put on land, we had to climb two miles up that mountain, which was harder than going up a ladder. I was all armed in mail, with heavy boots and with a gun in my hand, and it was raining as much as God knew how to send. Those devils of German gentlemen, with their little horses led by hand, worked miracles, because our horses were no good for this purpose, and we were bursting with the effort of making them climb that difficult mountain.

When we had got up a good way, Ascanio's horse, which was a most marvellous Hungarian horse, was a little in front of Busbacca the courier, and Ascanio had given him his javelin to help him carry; it happened that at the bad places that horse slipped and went so staggering, not being able to help himself, that he struck on the point of that scoundrel courier's javelin, who had not known how to get it out of the way; and having gone through the horse's throat from side to side — that other apprentice of mine, wanting to help his horse too, which was a black horse, slipped towards the lake and caught hold of a bramble which was very thin. On this horse was a pair of saddlebags, in which was all my money with everything of value that I had. I told the young man to save his own life and let the horse go to the devil: the fall was more than a mile and went sheer down and fell into the lake. Exactly under this place our boatmen had stopped; so that if the horse fell, it would land right on them.

I was ahead of them all, and we stood watching the horse tumbling, which seemed to be going certainly to perdition.
