At this I said to my young men: "Do not worry about anything, let us save ourselves and thank God for everything; I am only sorry for this poor man Busbacca, who has tied his cup and his jewels, which are worth several thousand ducats, to the saddle bow of that horse, thinking that was safer; and mine are a few hundred scudi, and I am not afraid of anything in the world, as long as I have God's grace."

Busbacca then said: "I am not sorry for mine, but I am very sorry for yours." I said to him: "Why are you sorry for my little, and not sorry for your own great deal?" Busbacca then said: "I shall tell you in God's name: in these cases, and in the pass we are in, one must tell the truth. I know that yours are scudi, and are real; but that cup case of mine, where I said there were so many jewels and so many lies, is quite full of caviar."

Hearing this, I could not help laughing; my young men laughed; he wept.

That horse helped himself, when we had given him up for lost. So, laughing, we took up our strength again and set ourselves to keeping on up the mountain. Those four German gentlemen, who had got to the top of that steep mountain before us, sent us some men who helped us; so that we got to that wildest of lodgings: where, being wet, exhausted and starving, we were most pleasantly received; and there we dried ourselves, rested ourselves, satisfied our hunger, and the wounded horse was treated with certain herbs; and we were shown that sort of herb, which the hedges were full of, and we were told that by keeping the wound continually full of those herbs the horse would not only get well but would serve us as if he had nothing wrong with him at all: and so we did.

Having thanked the gentlemen, and being very well restored, we left there and went on, thanking God for having saved us from that great danger.

Chapter 97

We arrived at a place beyond Wesen: here we rested for the night, where all through the night we heard a watchman singing in a very pleasant way; and because all the houses of that town are of fir wood, the watchman said nothing except that people should look out for fire.

Busbacca, who was terrified by the day, at every hour when the man sang, Busbacca shouted in his sleep, saying: "Alas, God, I am drowning!" — and this was the fright of the day just past; and added to that, he had got drunk that evening, because he wanted to have a drinking match with all the Germans who were there; and sometimes he said, "I am burning," and sometimes, "I am drowning": it seemed to him at times that he was in hell being martyred with that caviar round his neck. This night was so pleasant that all our troubles had been turned into laughter.

Getting up in the morning in very fine weather, we went to dine at a cheerful place called Lachen. There we were treated marvellously; then we took guides, who were going back to a place called Zurich. The guide who was leading went along an embankment of a lake, and there was no other road, and this embankment was itself covered with water, so that the beastly guide slipped, and he and his horse went under the water. I, who was exactly behind the guide, having stopped my horse, stood watching the beast come out of the water; and, as if nothing had happened, he began singing again and signalled to me to go on. I threw myself off to the right hand and broke through some hedges; and so I guided my young men and Busbacca. The guide shouted, telling me in German that if those people saw me they would kill me. We went on and escaped that other fury.

We arrived at Zurich, a marvellous city, as clean as a jewel. There we rested a whole day, and then early one morning we left; we came to another beautiful city called Solothurn: from there we came to Lausanne, from Lausanne to Geneva, from Geneva to Lyons, always singing and laughing.

At Lyons I rested for four days; I was very glad to be with some friends of mine; I was repaid the expense I had been at for Busbacca; then at the end of the four days I took the road towards Paris. This was a pleasant journey, except that when we got to La Palisse a band of adventurers tried to murder us, and it was not without some courage that we saved ourselves. Then we went on as far as Paris without a single trouble in the world: always singing and laughing we arrived safely.

Book One, Chapters 98 to 103

Chapter 98

After resting a little in Paris, I went to find Rosso the painter, who was in the King's service. This Rosso I believed to be the greatest friend I had in the world, because in Rome I had done him the greatest kindnesses one man can do another; and since these kindnesses can be told in a few words, I do not want to leave off telling them, to show how shameless ingratitude is. Because of his bad tongue, while he was in Rome, he had spoken so much ill of the works of Raphael of Urbino that Raphael's pupils meant to kill him at all costs: I saved him from that, guarding him day and night with enormous trouble. Again, for having spoken ill of Master Antonio da San Gallo, a most excellent architect, Rosso had a commission taken away from him which San Gallo had got him from Messer Agnolo de' Cesi; after that San Gallo began to work against him so hard that he had brought him to the point of starving to death; on account of which I lent him many tens of scudi to live on. And not having had them back yet, knowing that he was in the King's service, I went, as I have said, to visit him: I was not so much thinking that he would give me back my money, but I thought he would give me help and favour to get me into the service of that great King. When the man saw me, he was upset at once and said to me: "Benvenuto, you have come at too great an expense on so long a journey, especially at this time, when the mind is on war and not on trifles like our works." Then I said that I had brought money enough to be able to get back to Rome in the same way as I had come to Paris; and that this was not the return for the trouble I had gone through for him; and that I was beginning to believe what Master Antonio da San Gallo had told me about him. When he tried to turn the thing into a joke, having seen his own wretchedness, I showed him a bill of exchange for five hundred scudi on Ricciardo del Bene. This wretched man was ashamed then, and when he tried to keep me almost by force, I laughed at him and went off along with a painter who was there present. This man was called lo Sguazzella: he too was a Florentine; and I went to lodge in his house with three horses and three servants at so much a week. He treated me very well, and I paid him better. Then I looked for a way to speak to the King, to whom I was introduced by a certain Messer Giuliano Buonaccorsi, his treasurer. Over this I was held up a long while, because I did not know that Rosso was using every diligence to keep me from speaking to the King. Once the said Messer Giuliano had noticed it, he took me straight to Fontainebleau and put me in front of the King, from whom I had a whole hour of most gracious audience. And because the King was making ready to go towards Lyons, he told the said Messer Giuliano to bring me with him, and that on the road they would talk over certain fine works His Majesty had it in mind to have made. So I went along with the train of the Court; and on the way I did a great deal of service to the Cardinal of Ferrara, who did not have his hat yet. And since every evening I had long talks with the said Cardinal, His Lordship said I ought to stay at Lyons at an abbey of his, and there I could enjoy myself until the King came back from the war, since he was going towards Grenoble, and at his abbey in Lyons I should have every comfort. When we reached Lyons I had fallen ill, and that young man of mine Ascanio had caught a quartan fever; so that the French and their Court had become tiresome to me, and it seemed a thousand years until I got back to Rome. When the Cardinal saw me set on going back to Rome, he gave me money enough to make him a basin and a jug of silver in Rome. So we went back towards Rome on very good horses, coming over the mountains of the Simplon; and I had joined company with certain Frenchmen, with whom we travelled a good stretch, Ascanio with his quartan and I with a dull little fever which seemed as if it would never leave me at all; and my stomach had turned against food so much that I do not believe I managed to eat a whole loaf in a week, and I very much wanted to reach Italy, wanting to die in Italy and not in France.

Chapter 99

When we had passed the mountains of the said Simplon, we came on a river near a place called Indevedro. This river was very wide and quite deep, and over it there was a long narrow little bridge with no parapets. It being a morning of very heavy hoarfrost, when I reached the bridge, finding myself ahead of everybody, and seeing it to be very dangerous, I ordered my young men and servants to dismount and lead their horses by the bridle. So I crossed the said bridge very happily, and came along talking with one of those two Frenchmen, who was a gentleman: the other one was a notary, who had stayed a little way behind, and was jeering at that French gentleman and at me for having chosen the discomfort of going on foot out of fear of nothing at all. I turned round to him, seeing him in the middle of the bridge, and begged him to come on gently, because he was in a very dangerous place. This man, who could not fail his French nature, told me in French that I was a man of little spirit, and that there was not a scrap of danger there. While he was saying these words, he chose to prick his horse a little, and at that the horse at once slid off the bridge, and with its legs towards the sky fell beside a very large rock. And because God is often merciful to madmen, this beast together with the other beast, his horse, plunged into a very deep pool, where they both went under, he and the horse. As soon as I saw this, with the greatest speed I set off running, and with great difficulty jumped onto that rock, and hanging over from it I caught hold of a corner of a cloak the man had on him, and by that corner I pulled him up, for he was still under the water; and because he had drunk a great deal of water, and was very near being drowned, when I saw him out of danger I congratulated him on my having saved his life. To which this man answered me in French and said that I had done nothing; that the important thing was his papers, which were worth many tens of scudi: and it seemed as if he said these words to me in a rage, all wet and spluttering. At that, I turned to certain guides we had, and told them to help that beast, and that I would pay them. One of those guides set to helping him with skill and great labour, and fished up his papers for him, so that he lost nothing; the other guide would never take the least trouble to help him. When we had then got to the place mentioned above — we had made up a purse, and it had fallen to me to do the spending — after we had dined I gave several coins out of the company's purse to that guide who had helped to pull the man out of the water; on account of which the man said to me that I would be giving that money out of my own, since he did not mean to give him anything beyond what we had agreed on for doing a guide's office. At that, I said a great many insulting words to him. Then the other guide came at me, the one who had taken no trouble, and wanted me to pay him too; and because I said, "This one deserves a reward as well, for having carried the cross," he answered me that he would soon show me a cross I should weep at. I told him that I would light a candle-end at that cross, by which I hoped it would fall to him to weep first. And because this is a place on the borders between the Venetians and the Germans, the man ran to fetch people, and came back with them, a great boar-spear in front of him. I, who was on my good horse, lowered the cock of my arquebus: turning to my companions, I said, "At the first shot I kill that one; and the rest of you do your duty, because these are highway assassins, and they have taken this small occasion only to murder us." That innkeeper, where we had eaten, called one of those headmen, who was a great old fellow, and begged him to put a stop to so much trouble, saying to him: "This is a very brave young man, and even if you cut him to pieces, he will kill so many of you, and after doing the harm he means to do he might well slip out of your hands." The thing quieted down, and that old chief of theirs said to me: "Go in peace, for you would not make a salad of it, if you had a hundred men with you." I, who knew he was telling the truth, and had already made up my mind and counted myself a dead man, hearing no further insulting words said to me, shook my head and said: "I should have done all I could, to show I was a live animal and a man." And taking the road, that evening at the first lodging we made up the account of the purse, and I parted from that brutish Frenchman, remaining great friends with the other one who was a gentleman; and with my three horses, alone, we came to Ferrara. When I had dismounted, I went to the Duke's Court to pay my respects to His Excellency, so as to be able to leave in the morning towards Santa Maria of Loreto. I had waited until two hours into the night, and then the Duke appeared: I kissed his hands; he made me a great welcome, and gave orders that water should be brought for my hands. At which I said pleasantly: "Most excellent lord, it is more than four months since I have eaten enough to make anybody believe a man could live on so little; so, knowing that I should not be able to enjoy the royal dishes of your table, I will stay here talking with you while Your Excellency has supper, and both you and I at one and the same time will have more pleasure than if I supped with you." So we struck up conversation, and went on until five hours into the night. At five hours I took my leave, and going off to my inn I found a marvellous meal laid out, because the Duke had sent me the choice pieces off his own dish with very good wine; and having been kept in that way more than two hours past my hour for eating, I ate with the greatest appetite, which was the first time in four months that I had been able to eat.

Chapter 100

Leaving in the morning, I went to Santa Maria of Loreto, and from there, having said my prayers, I went on to Rome; where I found my most faithful Felice, to whom I left the shop with all its furnishings and fittings, and I opened another one next to Sugherello the perfumer, much bigger and roomier; and I thought to myself that great King Francis would not be remembering me. On account of which I took on a great many works from various lords, and meanwhile I was working on that jug and basin I had undertaken to make for the Cardinal of Ferrara. I had a great many workmen and a great deal of big business in gold and silver. I had made an agreement with that Perugian workman of mine, who had written down for himself all the money that had been spent on his account, which money had been spent on his clothing and on many other things; with the expenses of the journey it came to about seventy scudi: of which we had agreed that he should work off three scudi a month; for I was making him earn more than eight scudi. At the end of two months this rascal went off out of my shop with a God-be-with-you, and left me embarrassed by a great deal of business, and said he did not mean to give me anything more. For this reason I was advised to help myself by way of justice, because I had made up my mind to cut off one of his arms; and I would certainly have done it, but my friends told me it was not right for me to do such a thing, seeing that I would lose my money and perhaps Rome another time, because blows are not struck on terms; and that with that paper I had in his own hand I could have him arrested at once. I took the advice, but I chose to go about the thing more openly. I brought the suit before the auditor of the Chamber properly, and won it; and by virtue of it, which took several months, I afterwards had him put in prison. I found my shop loaded with very large commissions, and among the rest all the gold ornaments and jewels of the wife of Signor Gerolimo Orsino, father of Signor Paolo, today son-in-law of our Duke Cosimo. These works were very near finishing, and all the while more of the most important kind kept coming in. I had eight workmen, and along with them, for honour and for profit, I worked day and night.

Chapter 101

While I was pushing on my undertakings so vigorously, there came to me a letter sent with all speed by the Cardinal of Ferrara, which ran to this effect: "Benvenuto, our dear friend. In these last days this great Most Christian King remembered you, saying that he wished to have you in his service. To which I answered that you had promised me that whenever I sent for you on His Majesty's service you would come at once. To these words His Majesty said: 'I want the means of coming to be sent to him, as a man of his rank deserves,' and at once ordered his Admiral to have a thousand gold scudi paid me by the treasurer of savings. Present at this conversation was Cardinal de' Gaddi, who at once came forward and told His Majesty that there was no need for His Majesty to give that order, because he said he had already sent you money enough, and that you were on the road. Now if by chance the contrary is the case, as I believe, when you have had this letter of mine, answer at once, because I will pick up the thread again and get you the promised money from this magnanimous King."

Now let the world and those who live in it take notice of how much the malignant stars with adverse fortune can do in us human beings! I had not spoken twice in my life to that silly little creature of a small cardinal, Gaddi; and he did not do this piece of officiousness to do me any harm in the world, but did it only out of scattiness and worthlessness of his own, making a show of taking care himself of the affairs of the talented men the King wanted, as the Cardinal of Ferrara did. But he was such a fool afterwards that he sent me no word of it; for certainly, rather than shame a silly puppet, for love of my country, I would have found some excuse to patch up that foolish officiousness of his. As soon as I had the most reverend Cardinal of Ferrara's letter, I answered that of Cardinal de' Gaddi I knew nothing in the world, and that even if he had made me some such offer, I would not have moved out of Italy without His most reverend Lordship's knowing it; and all the more so since I had in Rome a greater quantity of business than I had ever had before; but that at a single word from His Most Christian Majesty, told me by so great a lord as His most reverend Lordship, I would set off at once, throwing everything else aside.

When I had sent my letters off, that traitor of a Perugian workman of mine thought up a piece of malice, which succeeded for him at once, given the avarice of Pope Paul of the Farnese family, and still more that of his bastard son, then called Duke of Castro. This workman I speak of let one of the secretaries of the said Signor Pier Luigi understand that, having been with me as a workman several years, he knew all my affairs; on the strength of which he gave the said Signor Pier Luigi his word that I was a man worth more than eighty thousand ducats, and that most of this money I had in jewels; and that the jewels belonged to the Church, and that I had stolen them at the time of the sack of Rome in the Castel Sant'Angelo, and that they should see to having me arrested at once and secretly.

One morning among others, I had worked more than three hours before daybreak on the works for the bride I spoke of above, and while my shop was being opened and swept out I had put my cloak on to take a little turn; and taking my way along the Strada Giulia, I came out at the corner of the Chiavica; where Crespino the constable with his whole troop of catchpoles came up against me, and said to me: "You are the Pope's prisoner." To whom I said: "Crespino, you have taken me for somebody else." "No," said Crespino, "you are the talented Benvenuto, and I know you very well, and I have to take you to the Castel Sant'Angelo, where lords and men of talent like you go." And because four of his corporals threw themselves on me and tried by force to take from me a dagger I had at my side and certain rings I had on my finger, the said Crespino said to them: "Let none of you touch him: it is quite enough that you do your office, and see that he does not escape me." Then, coming up to me, he asked me for my weapons with courteous words. While I was giving him my weapons, it struck me that in that very place I had killed Pompeo. From there they took me into the Castle, and shut me up as a prisoner in a room high up, in the keep. This was the first time I ever tasted prison, up to that age of mine of thirty-seven years.

Chapter 102

When Signor Pier Luigi, the Pope's son, had considered the great quantity of money I was accused of, he at once asked as a favour of that father of his the Pope that he should make him a gift of this sum of money. Which the Pope willingly granted him, and moreover told him that he would also help him to recover it: so that, having kept me prisoner eight whole days, at the end of the eight days, to bring the matter to some conclusion, they sent to examine me. So I was called into one of those halls which are in the Castle, the Pope's own, a very honourable place; and the examiners were the Governor of Rome, who was called Messer Benedetto Conversini of Pistoia, who was afterwards Bishop of Iesi; the second was the Procurator Fiscal, whose name I do not remember; the third was the judge of criminal cases, who was called Messer Benedetto da Cagli. These three men began to examine me, first with kindly words, then with the harshest and most frightening words, brought on because I said to them: "My lords, it is more than half an hour that you have not stopped asking me about fables and about matters of which it can truly be said that you are chattering, or that you are gossiping. A way of speaking, chattering, that has no tone to it, or gossiping, that means nothing; so I beg you to tell me what you want from me, and to let me hear reasoning come out of your mouths, and not fables and chatter." At these words of mine the Governor, who was from Pistoia, no longer able to cover up his hot-tempered nature, said to me: "You speak very confidently, indeed too haughtily; so that I will make this haughtiness of yours humbler than a puppy's at the reasoning you will hear me tell you; which will be neither chatter nor fables, as you say, but will be a setting out of arguments, to which you will certainly need to do your best to give us an answer." And so he began: "We know for a certainty that you were in Rome at the time of the Sack that was made in this unhappy city of Rome; and at that time you were in this Castel Sant'Angelo, and were employed here as a gunner; and because your trade is that of goldsmith and jeweller, Pope Clement, having known you before, and there being nobody else here of that profession, called you into his privacy and had you take out all the jewels of his tiaras and mitres and rings; and then, trusting you, he wanted you to sew them onto him: on account of which you kept back for yourself, hidden from His Holiness, the value of eighty thousand scudi. This has been told us by a workman of yours, in whom you confided and to whom you boasted of it. Now we tell you plainly to find the jewels, or the value of those jewels: and then we will let you go free."

Chapter 103

When I heard these words I could not keep myself from bursting into enormous laughter; then, having laughed a while, I said: "I thank God greatly that for this first time it has pleased His Majesty that I should be imprisoned, and that I am at least happy not to be imprisoned for some feeble thing, as most often seems to happen to young men. If what you say were true, there is no danger here of my having to be punished with a bodily penalty, since the laws at that time had lost all their authority; so that I could excuse myself by saying that as an officer I had kept that treasure for the sacred and holy Apostolic Church, waiting to hand it over to a good Pope, or truly to whoever should ask me for it, which now would be you, if it were so." At these words that furious Governor of Pistoia did not let me finish saying my reasons, but said in a rage: "Arrange it as you please, Benvenuto, since it is enough for us to have found our own again; and be quick about it, if you do not want us to do more than talk." And as they were about to get up and go away, I said to them: "My lords, I have not finished being examined, so finish examining me, and then go where you please." They sat down again at once, in a fine rage, almost showing that they did not want to hear another word I might say then, and half risen, thinking they had found out all they wanted to know. On account of which I began in this fashion: "Know, my lords, that it is about twenty years that I have lived in Rome, and never here nor anywhere else was I imprisoned." At these words that ruffian of a Governor said: "But you have killed men." Then I said: "You say it, and not I; but if a man came to kill you, priest as you are, you would defend yourself, and in killing him the holy laws allow it: so let me say my reasons, since you will want to be able to report to the Pope and to be able to judge me justly. I tell you again that it is close on twenty years that I have lived in this marvellous Rome, and in it I have done very great business in my profession: and because I know that this is the seat of Christ, I would certainly have promised myself that if a temporal prince had wanted to do me some violence, I should have had recourse to this holy Chair and to this Vicar of Christ, that he might defend my rights. Alas! where am I to go then? and to what prince, to defend me from so wicked an assault? Ought you not, before you took me, to have found out where I was turning over these eighty thousand ducats? And ought you not to have looked at the list of the jewels which this Apostolic Chamber has had written down carefully these five hundred years? Then, if you had found anything missing, that was the moment to seize all my books, along with me. I tell you that the books in which all the jewels of the Pope and of the tiaras are written down are all in existence, and you will not find anything missing of what Pope Clement had that is not carefully written down. The only thing there might be is that, when that poor man Pope Clement wanted to come to terms with those thieves of Imperials, who had robbed Rome and shamed the Church, there came to negotiate this agreement a man called Cesare Iscatinaro, if I remember rightly; who, having as good as concluded the agreement with that plundered Pope, in order to make a little show of affection to him let fall from his finger a diamond worth about four thousand scudi: and because the said Iscatinaro bent down to pick it up, the Pope told him to keep it for love of him. I was actually present at these doings: and if the said diamond is missing, I can tell you where it has gone; but I am quite certain that you will find this one written down too. After that you may be ashamed at your leisure of having assaulted a man like me, who has done so many honourable services for this Apostolic See. Know that if I had not been what I was, the morning the Imperials came into the Borgo they would have got into the Castle without any hindrance at all; and I, without being rewarded on that account, threw myself vigorously on the artillery, which the gunners and the soldiers of the garrison had abandoned, and put heart into a poor little companion of mine called Raffaello da Montelupo, a sculptor, who had abandoned it too and had got himself into a corner all terrified, doing nothing: I roused him up; and he and I alone killed so many of the enemy that the soldiers took another way. I was the one who fired an arquebus shot at Scatinaro, for seeing him talk with Pope Clement without a bow, but with the ugliest mockery, Lutheran and impious as he was. Pope Clement over this had a search made in the Castle for whoever had done it, to hang him. I was the one who wounded the Prince of Orange in the head with an arquebus shot, down here under the trenches of the Castle. Besides that, I have made for the holy Church so many ornaments of silver, of gold and of jewels, so many medals and coins so fine and so honourable. Is this then the rash priestly reward that is used towards a man who has served and loved you with such faith and such skill? Oh, go and tell the Pope again everything I have told you, telling him that he has all his jewels, and that I never had anything else from the Church except certain wounds and stonings in that time of the Sack; and that I counted on nothing except a little reward from Pope Paul, which he had promised me. Now I am clear about His Holiness and about you, his officers." While I was saying these words they stood astonished to hear me; and looking each other in the face, with a gesture of amazement they went away from me. All three went in agreement to report to the Pope everything I had said. The Pope, ashamed, gave orders with the greatest diligence that all the accounts of the jewels should be gone over again. Then, when they had seen that nothing was missing, they left me in the Castle without saying anything more: Signor Pier Luigi, thinking himself too that he had done badly, they set about diligently trying to make me die.

Book One, Chapters 104 to 108

Chapter 104

During this little stir of the time, King Francis had already learned in detail how the Pope was holding me prisoner, and so very wrongfully: having sent to the Pope as ambassador a certain gentleman of his who was called Monsignor de Morluc, he wrote to him to ask me of the Pope, as a man of His Majesty's. The Pope, who was a very able and marvellous man, but in this affair of mine behaved like a mean and foolish one, answered the King's said envoy that His Majesty should not concern himself about me, because I was a man very troublesome with weapons, and for this reason he warned His Majesty to let me alone, because he was holding me prisoner for homicides and other devilries of mine of that kind. The King answered again that in his kingdom very good justice was kept; and that just as His Majesty rewarded and favoured men of talent marvellously, so on the other hand he punished troublesome ones; and that since His Holiness had let me go, not caring for the service of the said Benvenuto, and had seen him gladly in his kingdom, he had taken him into his own service; and asked for him as his own man. These things were the greatest annoyance and harm to me, although they were the most honourable favours a man of my sort could wish for. The Pope had fallen into such a fury out of his fear that I might go and tell of that wicked piece of villainy used towards me, that he was thinking of every way he could, with his honour, to have me put to death. The Castellan of the Castel Sant'Angelo was a Florentine of ours, who was called Messer Giorgio the knight, of the Ugolini. This honest man used towards me the greatest courtesies that can be used in the world, letting me go free about the Castle on my word alone; and because he understood the great wrong that had been done me, when I wanted to give him security for going about the Castle at my ease, he told me that he could not take it, seeing that the Pope set too much store by this affair of mine; but that he would freely trust my word, because he heard from everybody what an honest man I was: and I gave him my word, and so he gave me the chance of doing a little work on something. At this, thinking that this indignation of the Pope's would come to an end, both because of my innocence and because of the King's favours, and still keeping my shop open, Ascanio my apprentice came to me in the Castle, and brought me some things to work on. Though I could work little, seeing myself imprisoned in that way and so wrongfully; still I made a virtue of necessity: cheerfully, as best I could, I put up with this perverse fortune of mine. I had made the greatest friends of all those guards and of many soldiers of the Castle. And because the Pope came sometimes to supper in the Castle, and during the time the Pope was there the Castle kept no guards, but stayed freely open like an ordinary palace; and because during the time the Pope was there in that way all the prisoners were usually shut up with greater diligence; yet none of these things was done to me, but freely at all these times I went about the Castle; and several times some of those soldiers advised me that I ought to run away, and that they would back me up, knowing the great wrong that had been done me: to whom I answered that I had given my word to the Castellan, who was such an honest man, and who had done me such great kindnesses. There was a soldier there, very brave and very clever; he said to me: "My Benvenuto, know that a man who is a prisoner is not bound, and cannot bind himself, to keep faith, any more than to anything else; do what I tell you; run away from this rascal of a Pope and from this bastard son of his, who will take your life from you in any case." I, who had made up my mind rather to lose my life than to fail of my promised word to that honest man the Castellan, put up with this inestimable unpleasantness, along with a friar of the Pallavicini house, a very great preacher.

Chapter 105

This one had been arrested as a Lutheran: he was a very good companion to live with, but as a friar he was the greatest rascal in the world, and lent himself to every sort of vice. His fine qualities I admired, his ugly vices I greatly abhorred, and I reproved him for them freely. This friar never did anything but remind me that I was not bound to keep faith with the Castellan, since I was in prison. To which I answered that as a friar he was quite right, but as a man he was not right, because a man who was a man and not a friar had to keep his word in every sort of accident he might find himself in: therefore I, who was a man and not a friar, was never going to fail of that plain and honest word of mine. When the said friar saw that he could not manage to corrupt me by way of his most subtle and virtuous reasonings, so marvellously put by him, he thought of trying me another way; and letting a good many days pass like that, while he was reading me the sermons of Fra Girolamo Savonarola, he gave them a commentary so admirable that it was finer than the sermons themselves; by which I was left enchanted, and there was nothing in the world I would not have done for him, apart from failing of my word, as I have said. When the friar saw me astonished at his talents, he thought of another way; for he began, in a pretty fashion, to ask me what course I would take if the wish came on me, once they had shut me up, to open those prison doors and escape. I too, wanting to show some subtlety of my wit to this talented friar, told him that I would certainly open any lock, however difficult, and all the more those of these prisons, which would be like eating a little fresh cheese to me. The said friar, to make me tell my secret, belittled me, saying that there are a great many things said by men who have got some credit for being ingenious; that if they then had to put in practice the things they boasted of, they would lose so much credit that it would go hard with them: and that he was hearing me say things so far from the truth that if I were ever put to it, he thought I would come out of it with little honour. At this, feeling myself pricked by this devil of a friar, I told him that I always undertook far less in words about myself than I knew how to do, and that this thing I had promised about the keys was the feeblest of them; and that in a few words I would make him quite sure it was as I said; and inconsiderately, as I have said, I showed him easily everything I had spoken of. The friar, making a show of not caring about it, took the whole thing in at once, most cleverly and thoroughly.

And as I have said above, that honest man the Castellan let me go freely all about the Castle; and he did not even lock me up at night, as he did all the others; he also let me work at whatever I wanted, in gold and silver and wax; and although I had worked several weeks on a certain basin I was making for the Cardinal of Ferrara, finding myself sick of the prison, working at that sort of thing had become tiresome to me; and I worked only, for less unhappiness, at some little figures of mine in wax: of which wax the said friar got hold of a piece from me, and with the said piece he put into practice that way with the keys which I had inconsiderately taught him. He had taken as companion and helper a chancellor who was with the said Castellan. This chancellor was called Luigi, and was a Paduan. When they wanted to have the said keys made, the locksmith gave them away; and because the Castellan came sometimes to see me in my room, and having seen me working with that wax, he recognized the said wax at once and said: "Even if this poor man Benvenuto has had one of the greatest wrongs done him that was ever done, he ought not to have carried on this kind of business with me, who was doing him the one kindness I could do him. Now I shall keep him shut up very close and never do him another kindness in the world." So he had me shut up again with some unpleasantness, especially of words said to me by certain devoted servants of his, who wished me exceedingly well, and every hour told me all the good offices this lord Castellan had done for me; so that over this incident they called me an ungrateful man, empty and without faith. And because one of those servants said these insults to me more boldly than became him, I, feeling myself innocent, answered boldly, saying that I had never failed of my word, and that I would maintain such words at the risk of my life, and that if he or anybody else said such unjust words to me again, I would say that whoever said such a thing lied in his throat. Unable to bear the insult, he ran to the Castellan's room and brought me back the wax with that model made of the key. As soon as I saw the wax, I told him that he and I were both in the right; but that he should let me speak to the lord Castellan, because I would tell him frankly how the case stood, which was of far more importance than they thought. At once the Castellan had me called, and I told him the whole business; on account of which he took the friar in hand, and the friar gave away that chancellor, who was within an inch of being hanged. The said Castellan quieted the matter, which had already come to the Pope's ears; he saved his chancellor from the gallows, and gave me the same freedom I had before.

Chapter 106

When I saw this affair go forward with so much severity, I began to think about my own case, saying: "If another one of these furies came on, and this man did not trust me, I should no longer be under any obligation to him, and I should want to use my wits a little, which I am sure would come out for me differently from that wretched friar's." And I began to have new thick sheets brought to me, and I did not send the dirty ones back. When my servants asked me for them, I told them to keep quiet, because I had given them away to certain poor soldiers; and that if such a thing got known, those poor fellows would be in danger of the galleys: so that my young men and servants, most faithfully, and Felice above all, kept the matter of the said sheets very well hidden for me. I set about emptying a straw mattress, and burnt the straw, because in my prison there was a fireplace where a fire could be made. I began to make out of these sheets strips a third of a braccio wide: when I had made as much as seemed to me enough to get down from that great height of the keep of the Castel Sant'Angelo, I told my servants that I had given away as many as I wanted, and that they should see to bringing me the thin ones, and that I would always give them back the dirty ones. The matter was forgotten.

As for those workmen and servants of mine, Cardinal Santiquattro and Cardinal Cornaro had my shop shut up, telling me plainly that the Pope would not hear a word of letting me go, and that those great favours of the King's had done me far more harm than good; because the last words Monsignor de Morluc had said on the King's behalf had been that Monsignor de Morluc told the Pope he ought to hand me over to the ordinary judges of the court; and that, if I had done wrong, he could punish me, but if I had not done wrong, reason required that he should let me go. These words had annoyed the Pope so much that he had a mind never to let me go again. This Castellan most certainly helped me as much as he could.

At this time, when those enemies of mine saw that my shop had been shut, they said in mockery some insulting word every day to those servants and friends of mine who came to visit me in prison. It happened one day among others that Ascanio, who came to me twice every day, asked me to make him a certain little coat for himself out of a blue satin gown of mine, which I never wore: it had served me only that once, when I went in procession in it: so I told him that these were not the times, nor was I in a place, for wearing clothes of that sort. The young man took it so badly that I did not give him this wretched gown, that he told me he wanted to go off to Tagliacozzo to his home. All worked up, I told him that he would be doing me a kindness by getting out of my sight; and he swore in a great passion never to come in front of me again. While we were saying this, we were walking round the keep of the Castle. It happened that the Castellan was walking too: meeting His Lordship at exactly that point, Ascanio said: "I am going, and goodbye for ever." At this I said: "And for ever I want it to be, and so let it truly be: I will order the guards never to let you pass again," and turning to the Castellan, with all my heart I begged him to order the guards never to let Ascanio pass again, saying to His Lordship: "This little peasant comes to add to my great trouble; so I beg you, my lord, never to let him in again." The Castellan was very sorry about it, because he knew him for a young man of marvellous talent: besides which he was of such a fine shape of body that it seemed as if everybody who saw him even once was particularly fond of him. The said young man went off in tears, and carried with him a little curved sword of his, which he sometimes carried secretly under his clothes. Going out of the Castle, with his face all in tears, he met two of my greatest enemies, one of whom was that Girolamo of Perugia I spoke of, and the other a certain Michele, both of them goldsmiths. This Michele, being a friend of that rascal the Perugian and an enemy of Ascanio's, said: "What does it mean that Ascanio is crying? Perhaps his father is dead? I mean that father of his in the Castle." Ascanio said to this: "He is alive, but you will be dead in a moment;" and raising his hand, with that curved sword of his he dealt him two blows, both of them on the head, so that with the first he laid him on the ground, and with the second he then cut off three fingers of his right hand, while still striking at his head. He was left there as if dead. It was reported to the Pope at once; and the Pope in great anger said these words: "Since the King wants him to be tried, go and give him three days to defend his case." At once they came, and did the said office the Pope had charged them with. That honest man the Castellan went straight to the Pope, and made it clear to him that I had known nothing of the matter, and that I had driven him away. He defended me so admirably that he saved my life from that great fury. Ascanio fled to Tagliacozzo to his home, and from there he wrote to me asking my pardon a thousand times, saying that he knew he had been wrong to add unpleasantness to my great troubles; but that if God gave me the grace to get out of that prison, he would never leave me again. I let him know that he should attend to learning his trade, and that if God gave me liberty, I would call him back in any case.

Chapter 107

This Castellan had every year certain illnesses which took his wits away altogether; and when this thing began to come on him, he talked a great deal: a way of chattering; and these humours of his were different every year, because one time it seemed to him that he was an oil jar; another time it seemed to him that he was a frog, and he hopped like a frog; another time it seemed to him that he was dead, and he had to be buried: so every year he came into one or another of these different humours. This time he began to imagine that he was a bat, and while he went about at his ease he sometimes squeaked as dully as bats do; he also made a little play with his hands and his body, as if he had meant to fly. His doctors, who had seen how it was, and so had his old servants, gave him every pleasure they could imagine: and because it seemed to them that he took great pleasure in hearing me talk, every little while they came for me and brought me to him. On account of which this poor man sometimes kept me four and five whole hours, in which I had never left off talking with him. He kept me at his table to eat opposite him, and never left off talking or making me talk; but in all that talking I ate very well all the same. He, poor man, neither ate nor slept, so that he had worn me out and I could bear no more; and looking him in the face sometimes, I saw that the pupils of his eyes were frightening, because one looked one way and the other another.

He began to ask me if I had ever had a fancy for flying: to whom I said that all those things which had been most difficult for men, I had most gladly tried to do and had done; and this business of flying, since the God of nature had presented me with a body very fit and disposed for running and jumping far more than the ordinary, and then with that little wit of mine which I would apply with my hands, I had the heart to fly for certain. This man began to ask me what methods I would use: to whom I said that, considering the animals that fly, and wanting to imitate by art what they had from nature, there was none that could be imitated except the bat. As soon as this poor man heard that name of bat, which was the humour he was suffering from that year, he let out an enormous cry, saying: "He speaks the truth, he speaks the truth; this is it, this is it;" and then he turned to me and said to me: "Benvenuto, if somebody gave you the means, would you have the heart to fly?" To whom I said that if he would afterwards give me my liberty, it would be enough for me to have the appearance of flying as far as the Prati, if he would make me a pair of wings of waxed Rheims linen. Then he said: "And the appearance would be enough for me too; but because the Pope has ordered me to take care of you as of his own eyes, I know that you are an ingenious devil who would run away; so I mean to have you shut up with a hundred keys, so that you shall not escape me." I set about begging him, reminding him that I could have escaped and, for love of the word I had given him, would never have failed him; so I begged him for the love of God, and for so many kindnesses as he had done me, that he would not choose to add a greater trouble to the great trouble I already had. While I was saying these words to him, he was giving express orders that they should bind me and take me to prison and shut me in well. When I saw that there was no other remedy, I said to him in front of all his people: "Shut me up well and guard me well, because I shall run away in any case." So they took me off, and shut me up with marvellous diligence.

Chapter 108

Then I began to think of the way I should take to escape. As soon as I saw myself shut in, I went examining how the prison stood in which I was locked; and when it seemed to me that I had certainly found the way to get out of it, I began to think in what way I should get down from that great height of the keep, for so that high tower was called: and taking those new sheets of mine, which I have already said I had made into strips and sewn together very well, I went examining how much bulk of them would be enough for me to be able to get down. Having judged what would serve me, and having put everything in order, I found a pair of pincers which I had taken from a Savoyard who was one of the Castle guards. This man had charge of the barrels and the cisterns; he also took pleasure in working with wood; and because he had several pairs of pincers, among them there was one pair very thick and large: thinking they were the thing for me, I took them from him and hid them inside that straw mattress. When the time came that I wanted to use them, I began with them to try at those nails which held the hinges; and because the door was double, the clinching of the said nails could not be seen; so that when I tried to draw one out, I went through the greatest labour; still, in the end I managed it. When I had drawn out this first nail, I went thinking what way I should take so that they should not notice it. At once I fixed up, with a little scraping of rusty iron, a little wax, which was exactly the same colour as those nail heads I had drawn out; and with that wax I began carefully to counterfeit those nail heads on their hinges: and one after another, as many as I drew out, so many I counterfeited in wax. I left the hinges fastened, each of them at top and bottom, with certain of the same nails I had drawn out, and then put back; but they were cut, then put back lightly, just enough to hold the hinges for me. I did this thing with the greatest difficulty, because the Castellan dreamed every night that I had escaped, and so he sent to look at the prison from hour to hour; and the man who came to look at it had the name and the ways of a catchpole. This one was called il Bozza, and he always brought another with him, who was called Giovanni, nicknamed Pedignone; this one was a soldier, and Bozza was a servant. This Giovanni never came a single time to that prison of mine without saying some insult to me. He was from the district of Prato and had been in Prato at the apothecary's: he looked carefully every evening at those hinges and at the whole prison, and I said to him: "Guard me well, because I mean to escape in any case." These words bred the greatest enmity between him and me; so that with the greatest diligence I put away carefully in my straw mattress all those bits of iron of mine, such as the pincers, and a dagger quite a big one and other things belonging to the business; and so those strips I had made, these too I kept in this straw mattress; and as soon as it was day, I swept out my room myself: and although by nature I take pleasure in cleanliness, at that time I was cleanliness itself. When I had swept out, I made my bed so daintily, and with some flowers, which almost every morning I had brought me by a certain Savoyard. This Savoyard had charge of the cistern and of the barrels; and he too took pleasure in working with wood; and it was from him that I stole the pincers with which I unfastened the nails of those hinges.

Book One, Chapters 109 to 113

Chapter 109

To come back to my bed: when Bozza and Pedignone came, I never said anything else to them except that they should keep away from my bed, so that they should not dirty it for me or spoil it; telling them, on some occasion when out of mockery they touched my bed a little, ever so lightly: "Ah, you filthy loafers! I will lay my hand on one of those swords of yours, and do you such a mischief that I will make you wonder. Do you think you are fit to touch the bed of a man like me? Over this I will have no regard for my own life, because I am certain I shall take yours; so leave me alone with my troubles and my tribulations, and do not give me any more grief than I have already; if not, I will let you see what a desperate man can do." These words they repeated to the Castellan, who expressly ordered them never to come near that bed of mine, and that when they came to me they should come without swords, and take very good care of me in everything else. Having made my bed safe, I felt I had done everything: because in that lay the whole importance of my business.

One holiday evening among others, the Castellan feeling himself very badly disposed and those humours of his grown stronger, never said anything except that he was a bat, and that if they heard that Benvenuto had flown away, they should let him go, for he would catch me up, since he too would certainly fly by night, and stronger than I, saying: "Benvenuto is a counterfeit bat, and I am a real bat; and since he has been given into my keeping, just leave it to me, and I shall certainly catch him." Having been several nights in this humour, he had worn out all his servants; and I by various ways heard everything, above all from that Savoyard who was fond of me.

Having made up my mind that holiday evening to escape in any case, first of all I said my prayers to God most devoutly, begging His divine Majesty to defend and help me in so dangerous an undertaking; then I set my hand to all the things I meant to use, and worked all that night. When it was two hours before daybreak, I got those hinges out with the greatest labour, because the wooden jamb of the door, and the bolt as well, made an obstruction, and so I could not open it: I had to hack away the wood; still, in the end I opened it, and putting on me those strips, which I had wound like skeins of yarn on two little sticks, I got out and went to the privies of the keep; and having uncovered two roof tiles from the inside, I easily jumped up onto the roof at once. I was in a white doublet and a pair of white hose and likewise a pair of half boots, in which I had put that little dagger of mine already mentioned. Then I took one end of those strips of mine and made it fast to a piece of old tile which was built into the said keep: by chance this stuck out barely four fingers. The strip was arranged like a stirrup. When I had hooked it to that piece of tile, turning to God I said: "Lord God, help my right, because I have right on my side, as You know, and because I am helping myself." Letting myself go very gently, holding myself up by the strength of my arms, I got right down to the ground. There was no moonlight, but it was a fine clear night. When I was on the ground, I looked at the great height I had come down so boldly, and went off happy, thinking I was free. It turned out not to be so, because on that side the Castellan had had two walls built, quite high, and used the place as a stable and a hen house: this place was closed with heavy bolts on the outside. When I saw that I could not get out of there, it gave me the greatest unhappiness.

While I was walking up and down thinking about my situation, my feet struck against a great pole which was covered by the straw. This with great difficulty I set upright against that wall; then by the strength of my arms I climbed it right to the top of the wall. And because that wall came to a sharp edge, I had no strength to pull the said pole up; so I made up my mind to fasten on a piece of those strips, which was the second skein, because one of the two skeins I had left attached to the keep of the Castle: so I took a piece of this other strip, as I have said, and having tied it to that beam, I got down this wall, which gave me the greatest labour and had tired me very much, and besides that I had flayed the insides of my hands, which were bleeding; on account of which I had settled down to rest, and had bathed my hands with my own urine. When it seemed to me that my strength had come back, I climbed to the last circuit of the walls, which looks towards the Prati; and having laid down that skein of strips of mine, with which I meant to take a turn round a battlement, and to do at this lesser height what I had done at the greater; having, as I say, laid down my strip, one of those sentries who were on guard came upon me. Seeing my design blocked, and seeing myself in danger of my life, I resolved to face that guard; who, seeing my determined spirit and that I was coming towards him with a weapon in my hand, quickened his pace, making as though to get out of my way. Having got a little distance from my strips, very quickly I turned back; and although I saw another guard, that time he chose not to see me. When I got to my strips, having tied them to the battlement, I let myself go; on account of which, whether it was that it seemed to me I was near the ground and I opened my hands to jump, or whether my hands were simply tired and could not stand up to that labour, I fell, and in this fall of mine I struck my head and lay senseless more than an hour and a half, as far as I can judge. Then, as the day was beginning to break, that little coolness that comes an hour before the sun brought me back to myself; but I was still out of my wits, because it seemed to me that my head had been cut off, and it seemed to me that I was in purgatory. Lying like that, little by little my faculties came back to their proper state, and I realized that I was outside the Castle, and at once I remembered everything I had done. And because I felt the blow on my head before I noticed that my leg was broken, putting my hands to my head I brought them away all bloody: then, having felt myself over well, I knew and judged that I had no injury of any importance; but when I tried to get up from the ground, I found my right leg broken three fingers above the heel. Not even this dismayed me: I drew out my little dagger together with its sheath; for this having a metal tip with quite a big ball at the end of the tip, that was the cause of my having broken my leg; because the bone coming up against that thickness of that ball, and the bone not being able to bend, made it break in that place: so that I threw away the dagger's sheath, and with the dagger I cut a piece of that strip which I had left over, and as best I could I put the leg together again, and then on all fours, with the said dagger in my hand, I went off towards the gate. When I got to the gate, I found it shut; and seeing a certain stone right under the gate, which, judging that it was not very firmly set, I tried to loosen; then I put my hands to it, and feeling it shift, it obeyed me easily, and I drew it out; and by that way I got in.

Chapter 110

It had been more than five hundred paces walking from the place where I fell to the gate where I got in. When I had got inside Rome, certain mastiff dogs threw themselves on me and bit me badly; and when they came at me several times to worry me, I struck at them with that dagger of mine and pricked one of them so soundly that it yelped loudly, so that the other dogs, as their nature is, ran to that dog: and I made haste, going off towards the church of the Traspontina still on all fours. When I had reached the mouth of the street that turns towards Sant'Angelo, from there I took the way to go towards St. Peter's, so that as broad day came on me, I considered that I was in danger; and meeting a water carrier who had loaded his donkey and filled his jars with water, I called him to me and begged him to lift me up bodily and carry me onto the level at the top of the steps of St. Peter's, saying to him: "I am a poor young man who, over a love affair, tried to get down from a window; and so I fell, and broke my leg. And because the place I came out of is of great importance, and I should be in danger of being cut to pieces, I beg you to take me quickly, and I will give you a gold scudo," and I put my hand to my purse, where I had a good quantity of them. At once the man took hold of me, and willingly got me on his back, and carried me onto the said level at the top of the steps of St. Peter's; and there I made him set me down, and told him to run back to his donkey.

At once I took my way, still on all fours, and was going to the house of the Duchess, wife of Duke Ottavio and daughter of the Emperor, natural, not legitimate, who had been the wife of Duke Alessandro, Duke of Florence; and because I knew for certain that about this great princess there were many friends of mine, who had come from Florence with her; and also because she had done me a favour by means of the Castellan; who, wanting to help me, had told the Pope, when the Duchess made her entry into Rome, that I had been the cause of saving more than a thousand scudi of damage which a heavy rain was doing them: on account of which he said that he was in despair, and that I put heart into him, and told how I had trained several heavy pieces of artillery towards that quarter where the clouds were thickest, and where a very heavy rain had already begun; on account of which, when these pieces of artillery began to be fired, the rain stopped, and at the fourth round the sun came out; and that I had been the entire cause of that festival passing off very well; on account of which, when the Duchess heard it, she had said: "That Benvenuto is one of those men of talent who were with the blessed memory of Duke Alessandro my husband, and I shall always take account of such men, when the occasion comes of doing them a kindness;" and she had also spoken about me to Duke Ottavio her husband. For these reasons I was going straight to the house of Her Excellency, who was living in the Borgo Vecchio in a very fine palace that is there; and there I should have been quite safe from the Pope's touching me; but because what I had done up to that point had been too marvellous for a human body, and God not wanting me to fall into so much vainglory, for my own good chose to give me yet a greater discipline than the one before; and the cause was this, that while I was going on all fours like that up those steps, a servant who was with Cardinal Cornaro recognized me at once; which cardinal was lodged in the Palace. This servant ran to the Cardinal's room, and having woken him, said: "Most reverend Monsignor, your Benvenuto is down there, having escaped from the Castle, and he is going along on all fours all covered in blood: by the look of him he has broken a leg, and we do not know where he is going." The Cardinal said at once: "Run, and carry him bodily up here to my room." When I came to him, he told me not to be afraid of anything; and at once he sent for the first doctors of Rome; and by them I was treated: and this was a Master Iacomo of Perugia, a most excellent surgeon. He set the bone for me admirably, then bandaged me, and with his own hand let my blood; for my veins being swollen far beyond the ordinary, and also because he chose to make the opening somewhat wide, the rush of blood came out so great that it caught him in the face, and covered him in such abundance that he could not get on with treating me: and having taken this for a very bad omen, he treated me with great difficulty; and several times he wanted to leave me, remembering that he too was risking no small penalty for having treated me, or even for having finished treating me. The Cardinal had me put in a private room, and went off at once to the Palace with the intention of asking the Pope for me.

Chapter 111

Meanwhile a very great noise had gone up in Rome: for the strips had already been seen hanging from the great tower of the keep of the Castle, and all Rome was running to see this astonishing thing. Meanwhile the Castellan had come into the highest of his humours of madness, and wanted, in spite of all his servants, to fly from that keep himself, saying that nobody could recapture me but he, by flying after me. At this Messer Roberto Pucci, father of Messer Pandolfo, having heard of this great affair, went in person to see it; then he came on to the Palace, where he met Cardinal Cornaro, who told him the whole business, and how I was in one of his rooms and already treated. These two honest men went in agreement to kneel down before the Pope, who, before he let them say anything, said: "I know everything you want from me." Messer Roberto Pucci said: "Most blessed Father, we ask you as a favour for that poor man, who for his talents deserves to have some consideration shown him, and besides those, has shown such bravery together with such ingenuity that it has not seemed a human thing. We do not know for what sins Your Holiness has kept him so long in prison; so, if those sins were too outrageous, Your Holiness is holy and wise, and may do your will in the matter, high and low; but if they are things that can be forgiven, we beg you to grant them to us as a favour." The Pope at this, ashamed, said that he had kept me in prison at the request of certain of his people, "he being a little too bold; but knowing his talents and wanting to keep him here beside us, we had arranged to give him so much good that he should have had no cause to go back to France. I am very sorry for his great injury; tell him to attend to getting well; and when he is well, we will make him amends for his troubles." These two great men came and gave me this good news on the Pope's behalf.

Meanwhile the nobility of Rome came to visit me, young and old and of every sort. The Castellan, being out of his mind as he was, had himself carried to the Pope; and when he was before His Holiness he began to shout, saying that if the Pope did not give me back to him in prison he was doing him a great wrong, saying: "He escaped from me under the word he had given me; alas, he has flown away from me, and he promised me not to fly away!" The Pope, laughing, said: "Go, go, for I will give him back to you in any case." The Castellan added, saying to the Pope: "Send the Governor to him, to find out who helped him escape, because if it is one of my men, I want to hang him by the throat from that battlement Benvenuto escaped by." When the Castellan had gone, the Pope called the Governor, smiling, and said: "This is a brave man, and this is a marvellous thing; for all that, when I was young, I too came down from that very place." Here the Pope was telling the truth, because he had been a prisoner in the Castle for having forged a brief, when he was an abbreviator of the Parco Maggiore: Pope Alexander had kept him prisoner a long time; then, the thing being too ugly, he had made up his mind to cut off his head; but wanting to let the feast of Corpus Domini go by, Farnese, knowing all about it, had Pietro Chiavelluzzi come with several horses, and in the Castle bribed certain of those guards with money; so that on the day of Corpus Domini, while the Pope was in procession, Farnese was put in a basket and lowered by a rope right down to the ground. The circuit of walls at the Castle was not made yet, but only the tower, so that he did not have the great difficulties in escaping that I had: besides, he had been imprisoned justly and I unjustly. Enough that he chose to boast to the Governor of having been spirited and brave himself in his youth, and did not notice that he was disclosing his own great villainies. He said: "Go and tell him he may freely say who helped him: so, let it have been whoever it likes, it is enough that he is pardoned, and promise him that freely yourself."

Chapter 112

This Governor came to me, who had been made Bishop of Iesi two days before: when he reached me, he said: "My Benvenuto, although my office is one that frightens men, I come to you to reassure you; and I have authority to promise you so by the express commission of His Holiness, who has told me that he escaped too, but that he had a great deal of help and a great deal of company, for otherwise he could not have done it. I swear to you by the Sacraments I bear — for I have been made a Bishop these two days — that the Pope has set you free and pardoned you, and is very sorry for your great injury; but attend to getting well, and take everything for the best, for this imprisonment, which you have certainly suffered as an entirely innocent man, will have been the making of you for ever, because you will tread poverty underfoot, and you will not need to go back to France, wearing out your life travelling here and there. So tell me freely how the case went, and who gave you help; then take comfort and rest and get well." I went back to the beginning and told him the whole thing exactly as it had happened, and I gave him the fullest details, down to the water carrier who had carried me on his back. When the Governor had heard the whole of it, he said: "Truly these are too great things for one man alone: they are not worthy of any man but you." So, having had my hand brought out, he said: "Be of good cheer and take comfort, for by this hand that I touch you are free and, if you live, will be happy."

Having left me, since he had kept waiting a crowd of great gentlemen and lords who were coming to visit me, saying among themselves, "Let us go and see the man who works miracles," these stayed with me; and some of them made me offers and some brought me presents. Meanwhile the Governor, having got to the Pope, began to tell over the thing I had told him; and it just happened that Signor Pier Luigi his son was present; and they all wondered greatly. The Pope said: "Certainly this is too great a thing." Signor Pier Luigi then added, saying: "Most blessed Father, if you set him free, he will do you greater ones, because this is far too bold a spirit of a man. I want to tell you another one, which you do not know. This Benvenuto of yours, before he was a prisoner, had words with a gentleman of Cardinal Santa Fiore's; which words came out of a small thing this gentleman had said to Benvenuto, so that he answered most insolently and with such daring that he even made as if to pick a quarrel; the said gentleman reported it to Cardinal Santa Fiore, who said that if he laid hands on him he would knock the madness out of his head; Benvenuto, hearing this, kept a fowling piece of his in readiness, with which he hits a farthing every time: and one day, when the Cardinal came to the window, the said Benvenuto's shop being under the Cardinal's palace, he took his fowling piece and got himself ready to fire at the Cardinal. And because the Cardinal was warned of it, he moved away at once. Benvenuto, so that the thing should not be noticed, fired at a house pigeon that was brooding in a hole high up on the palace, and hit the said pigeon in the head: a thing impossible to believe. Now let Your Holiness do with him whatever you please; I did not want to fail to have told you of it. It might also come into his head, thinking he has been in prison wrongfully, to take a shot one day at Your Holiness. This is far too fierce and too confident a spirit. When he killed Pompeo, he gave him two dagger thrusts in the throat in the middle of ten men who were guarding him, and then got away, to no small discredit of those men, who were honest men of standing."

Chapter 113

Present at these words was that gentleman of Santa Fiore's with whom I had had words, and he confirmed to the Pope everything his son had said. The Pope sat swollen up and said nothing. I will not fail to state my case justly and honestly. This gentleman of Santa Fiore's came to me one day and handed me a little gold ring, which was all smeared with quicksilver, saying: "Get the quicksilver off this little ring for me, and be quick." I, who had before me many works in gold with jewels of the greatest importance, and also feeling myself ordered about so confidently by a man to whom I had never spoken and whom I had never seen, told him that I had no tool for the job just then, and that he should go to somebody else. The man, with no cause in the world, told me that I was an ass. To which words I answered that he was not telling the truth, and that I was in every respect a better man than he; but that if he provoked me, I would kick him harder than an ass would. The man reported it to the Cardinal and painted him a picture of hell.

Two days later I fired, behind the palace into a very high hole, at a wild pigeon that was brooding in that hole; and at that same pigeon I had seen a goldsmith called Giovan Francesco della Tacca, a Milanese, fire several times, and he had never hit it. This day when I fired, the pigeon was showing just its head, being suspicious because of the other times it had been fired at; and because this Giovan Francesco and I were rivals at shooting, some gentlemen and friends of mine being in my shop, they pointed it out to me, saying: "There up above is Giovan Francesco della Tacca's pigeon, that he has fired at so many times: now look, the poor creature is suspicious; it hardly shows its head." Raising my eyes, I said: "That little bit of head alone would be enough for me to kill it, if it would only wait until I got my gun up to my face." Those gentlemen said that the inventor of the gun himself would not hit it. To which I said: "Let there be a jug of Greek wine on it, of that good wine of Palombo the innkeeper, and if it waits until I get my marvellous Broccardo to my face" — for so I called my gun — "I will plant a shot in that little bit of head it is showing me." At once getting it up to my face, at arm's length, without a rest or anything else, I did what I had promised, thinking neither of the Cardinal nor of anybody else; on the contrary, I held the Cardinal to be very much my patron. So let the world see, when fortune wants to set about assassinating a man, how many different ways she takes. The Pope, swollen up and sulking, sat considering what his son had told him.

Book One, Chapters 114 to 119

Chapter 114

Two days later Cardinal Cornaro went to ask the Pope for a bishopric for a gentleman of his, who was called Messer Andrea Centano. It is true that the Pope had promised him a bishopric: one having fallen vacant in this way, when the Cardinal reminded the Pope how he had promised him the thing, the Pope agreed that it was true and that he meant to give it him; but that he wanted a favour from His most reverend Lordship, and this was that he wanted him to put Benvenuto back into his hands. Then the Cardinal said: "Oh, if Your Holiness has pardoned him and given him to me free, what will the world say both of Your Holiness and of me?" The Pope replied: "I want Benvenuto, and let everybody say what they like, if you want the bishopric." The good Cardinal said that His Holiness should give him the bishopric, and that for the rest he should consider it himself and afterwards do everything His Holiness both wanted and could do. The Pope said, still somewhat ashamed of the wicked word he had already given: "I will send for Benvenuto, and for a little satisfaction of my own I will put him down in those rooms in the private garden, where he will be able to attend to getting well, and none of his friends will be forbidden to go and see him, and I will also have his expenses paid, until this little fancy of ours passes off."

The Cardinal went home and sent word to me at once, by the man who was waiting for the bishopric, that the Pope wanted me back in his hands; but that he would keep me in a ground-floor room in the private garden; where I should be visited by everybody just as I was in his house. Then I begged this Messer Andrea to be so good as to tell the Cardinal not to give me to the Pope and to leave it to me; because I would have myself wrapped up in a mattress and have myself carried out of Rome to a safe place; because if he gave me to the Pope, most certainly he was giving me to death. The Cardinal, when he heard it, I believe would have been willing to do it, but that Messer Andrea, who was to have the bishopric, disclosed the matter. Meanwhile the Pope sent for me at once and had me put, as he had said, in a ground-floor room in his private garden. The Cardinal sent to tell me that I should eat nothing of those dishes the Pope sent me, and that he would send me food; and that what he had done he could not do otherwise, and that I should be of good cheer, for he would help me so much that I should be free.

Being in that position, I was visited every day, and many great things were offered me by many great gentlemen. From the Pope came the food, which I did not touch; instead I ate what came from Cardinal Cornaro, and so I lived. Among my other friends I had a young Greek of twenty-five years of age: this man was exceedingly strong and played at the sword better than any other man in Rome: he was faint-hearted, but he was a most faithful honest man and very ready to believe. He had heard it said that the Pope had said he wanted to make up to me for my troubles. This was true, that the Pope had said such things at the beginning, but at the end afterwards he was saying something else. On account of which I confided in this young Greek and said to him: "Dearest brother, these men mean to assassinate me, so now is the time to help me: for they think I do not see it, doing me these extraordinary favours, which are all done to betray me." This honest young man said: "My Benvenuto, it is said all over Rome that the Pope has given you an office worth five hundred scudi of income; so I beg you as a favour not to let this suspicion of yours rob you of so great a good." And still I begged him, with my arms crossed on my breast, to take me away from there, because I well knew that a Pope like that could do me a great deal of good, but that I knew for certain that he was studying to do me, secretly, for his honour's sake, a great deal of harm; so let him be quick and try to save my life from this man: for if he got me out of there in the way I would tell him, I should always owe my life to him; and if the need came, I would spend it for him. This poor young man said to me, weeping: "Oh my dear brother, you insist on ruining yourself, and I cannot fail you in what you order me; so tell me the way and I will do everything you say, even if it is against my will." So we had made up our minds and I had given him the whole plan, which was working out very easily for us. When I thought he was coming to put in practice what I had arranged with him, he came to tell me that for my own good he meant to disobey me, and that he well knew what he had heard from men who were about the Pope and who knew the whole truth of my case. I, who could not help myself in any other way, was left discontented and in despair. This was the day of Corpus Domini in 1539.

Chapter 115

After this argument, time went by all that day until night, and from the Pope's kitchen came a plentiful meal: from Cardinal Cornaro's kitchen too came a very good supply: several friends of mine happening to be there, I made them stay to supper with me; and so, keeping my leg in splints in the bed, I made merry with them; and they stayed on with me. When an hour of the night had passed they went away; and two servants of mine settled me down to sleep, and then went into the outer room. I had a dog as black as a mulberry, one of the shaggy sort, and he served me marvellously at shooting, and never stayed a step away from me. In the night, being under my bed, three good times I called my servant to take him out from under the bed, because he was howling frighteningly.
