When the servants came, this dog threw himself on them to bite them. They were terrified and were afraid the dog had gone mad, because he howled continually. So we went on until four hours into the night.

At the stroke of four hours of the night the constable came into my room with a large troop: then the dog came out and threw himself on them with such fury, tearing their cloaks and their hose, and had put them into such fear that they thought he was mad. On account of which the constable, as an experienced person, said: "The nature of good dogs is this, that they always guess and foretell the harm that is coming to their masters: take a couple of sticks and defend yourselves from the dog, and let the others tie Benvenuto onto this chair, and carry him where you know." As I have said, the day of Corpus Domini had just passed, and it was about four hours of the night. They carried me gagged and covered up, and four of them went ahead, making the few men who were still to be found in the street get out of the way. So they carried me to the Torre di Nona, as the place is called, and put me in the condemned cell, laid me down on a bit of a mattress and gave me one of those guards, who all night lamented my bad fortune, saying to me: "Alas! poor Benvenuto, what have you done to these people?" So I saw very well what was going to happen to me, both because the place was what it was and also because that man had let me know it. I spent a part of that night tormenting myself with wondering what could be the reason it pleased God to give me such a penance; and because I could not find it, I struggled hard. That guard had then set about comforting me as best he knew how; on account of which I entreated him for the love of God not to say anything to me and not to speak to me, seeing that by myself I should come to such a resolution sooner and better. So he promised me.

Then I turned my whole heart to God; and I prayed Him most devoutly that it might please Him to receive me into His kingdom; and that although I had complained, this departure in this way seeming to me a very innocent one, so far as the ordinances of the laws promised; and although I had committed homicides, that Vicar of His had called me from my own country and pardoned me by the authority of the laws and of his own; and what I had done had all been done in defence of this body which His Majesty had lent me: so that I did not see, according to the ordinances by which men live in the world, that I deserved that death; but that it seemed to me there was happening to me what happens to certain unfortunate people who, going along the street, have a stone fall on their head from some great height and are killed by it: which is plainly seen to be the power of the stars: not that they are conspiring against us to do us good or harm, but it comes about in their conjunctions, to which we are subject; though I know that I have free will: and if my faith had been exercised in a holy way, I am quite certain that the angels of Heaven would carry me out of that prison and would save me surely from all my trouble; but because it does not seem to me that I have been made worthy by God of such a thing, therefore these heavenly influences must needs work out their malignity upon me. And having struggled a while with this, afterwards I made up my mind, and fell asleep at once.

Chapter 116

When dawn came, the guard woke me and said: "Oh unlucky honest man, now it is no longer time for sleeping, because the man has come who has to give you bad news." Then I said: "The sooner I get out of this worldly prison, the more welcome it will be to me, all the more since I am sure my soul is saved, and that I am dying wrongfully. The glorious and divine Christ makes me the companion of His disciples and friends, who, both He and they, were put to death wrongfully: so wrongfully am I being put to death, and I thank God for it in all holiness. Why does the man not come forward who has to pass sentence on me?" Then the guard said: "He is too sorry for you and is weeping." Then I called him by name, whose name was Messer Benedetto da Cagli. I said: "Come forward, my Messer Benedetto, now that I am very well disposed and resolved; it is much more to my glory that I should die wrongfully than if I died justly: come forward, I beg you, and give me a priest, so that I may say four words to him; although there is no need, because I have made my holy confession to my Lord God; but only to observe what holy mother Church has ordained for us; for although she is doing me this wicked wrong, I freely forgive her. So come, my Messer Benedetto, and dispatch me before my senses begin to trouble me." When I had said these words, this honest man told the guard to shut the door, because without him the office could not be performed.

He went off to the house of the wife of Signor Pier Luigi, who was together with the Duchess I spoke of above; and coming before them, this man said: "My most illustrious patroness, be pleased, I beg you for the love of God, to send word to the Pope to send somebody else to pass that sentence on Benvenuto and do this office of mine, because I resign it and never want to do it again;" and sighing with the greatest grief he went away. The Duchess, who was there present, twisting her face aside, said: "This is the fine justice that is kept in Rome by the Vicar of God! The Duke who was my husband was very fond of this man for his goodness and for his talents, and did not want him to go back to Rome, holding him very dear about his own person;" and she went off, muttering a great many angry words. The wife of Signor Pier Luigi, who was called Signora Girolama, went off to the Pope, and throwing herself on her knees — several Cardinals were present — this woman said so many great things that she made the Pope blush, and he said: "For love of you we will let him be, though we never had any bad intention towards him." These words the Pope said because those Cardinals were present, who had heard the words that marvellous and daring woman had said.

I stayed on in the greatest discomfort, my heart beating continually. And all those men who were appointed to that ugly office stayed in discomfort too, until it was late at the hour for dinner; at which hour every man went off to his other business, so that dinner was brought to me: whereat, wondering, I said: "Here truth has been able to do more than the malignity of the heavenly influences; so I pray God, if it be His pleasure, to save me from this fury." I began to eat, and just as before I had made up my mind to my great harm, so now I made it up to the hope of my great good. I dined with a good appetite. So I stayed without seeing or hearing anybody until an hour of the night. At that hour the constable came with a good part of his troop, and put me back on that chair on which the evening before he had carried me to that place, and from there, with many kind words to me not to be afraid, he ordered his catchpoles to take care not to knock that leg of mine that I had broken, as they valued their eyes. So they did, and carried me to the Castle, from which I had escaped; and when we were up at the top of the keep, where there is a little courtyard, they stopped me there for a while.

Chapter 117

Meanwhile the Castellan spoken of above had himself carried to the place where I was, and ill and afflicted as he was he said: "See, I have taken you again?" "Yes," I said, "but see, I escaped, as I told you I would? And if I had not been sold, under the papal word, for a bishopric by a Venetian cardinal and a Roman one by Farnese, both of whom have clawed the face of the sacred holy laws, you would never have taken me again. But since they have now set up this bad custom, do you too the worst you can, for I care about nothing in the world." This poor man began to cry out very loudly, saying: "Alas! alas! this man cares neither about living nor about dying, and he is bolder than when he was well: put him down there under the garden, and never speak to me about him again, for he is the cause of my death."

I was carried down under a garden into a very dark room, where there was a great deal of water, full of tarantulas and many venomous worms. A wretched little mattress of tow was thrown down on the ground for me, and that evening I was given no supper, and I was shut in behind four doors: so I stayed until nineteen hours the next day. Then food was brought to me: and I asked them to give me some of those books of mine to read. Not one of these men spoke to me, but they reported it to that poor man the Castellan, who had asked what I was saying. The next morning then a book of mine was brought me, a Bible in Italian, and a certain other book in which were the Chronicles of Giovanni Villani. When I asked for certain other books of mine, I was told that I should have nothing else and that I already had too many of those.

So I lived on wretchedly on that mattress, which was all rotten, for in three days everything was water; so that I was continually unable to move, because I had my leg broken; and when I had to get out of the bed for the necessity of relieving myself, I went on all fours with the greatest distress, so as not to make filth in the place where I slept. I had an hour and a half of the day of a little reflected light which came into that unhappy cave through a very small hole; and only in that little space of time did I read, and the rest of the day and the night I was always in the dark, patiently, never out of thoughts of God and of this human frailty of ours; and it seemed to me certain that within a few days I should end my unlucky life there and in that way. Still, as best I could I comforted myself, considering how much greater a suffering it would have been for me, in passing out of my life, to feel that inestimable agony of the knife, whereas being in that state I was passing out of it with a sleeping draught, which had become much pleasanter to me than the first one had been: and little by little I felt myself going out, until my good constitution had accommodated itself to that purgatory. Then, when I felt that it had accommodated and accustomed itself, I took heart to bear that inestimable suffering as long as my constitution itself bore it for me.

Chapter 118

I began at the beginning of the Bible, and read it devoutly and thought it over, and I was so enchanted with it that if I had been able I would never have done anything but read: but as soon as the light failed me, all my miseries jumped on me at once and gave me such torment that several times I had made up my mind to put an end to myself somehow; but because they did not let me have a knife, I had little means of doing such a thing. Still, once among other times I had arranged a thick beam that was there, propped up like a trap; and I meant to make it come down on my head; which would have crushed it at the first blow: so that, when I had got this whole engine ready, and moved resolutely to set it off, when I tried to strike it with my hand, I was caught hold of by something invisible and thrown four braccia away from that place, and so terrified that I was left in a swoon: and so I lay from the dawn of day until nineteen hours, when they brought me my dinner. They must have come several times without my hearing them; because when I did hear them, Captain Sandrino Monaldi came in, and I heard him say: "Oh! unhappy man, see what an end so rare a talent has come to!" Hearing these words I opened my eyes: and so I saw priests with their gowns on, who said: "Oh, you said he was dead!" Bozza said: "I found him dead, and so I said so." At once they lifted me out of the place where I was, and lifting the mattress, which had become all rotten like macaroni, they threw it out of that room: and when these things had been repeated to the Castellan, he had another mattress given me. And so, remembering what that thing could have been which had held me back from that undertaking, I thought it must have been something divine and my defender.

Chapter 119

Afterwards there appeared to me in a dream by night a marvellous creature in the form of a most beautiful young man, and as though scolding me he said: "Do you know who it was that lent you that body, which you meant to spoil before its time?" It seemed to me that I answered him that I acknowledged the whole of it from the God of nature. "Then," he said to me, "you despise His works, wanting to spoil them? Let yourself be guided by Him and do not lose hope in His power," with many other words so admirable that I do not remember the thousandth part of them.

I began to consider that this figure of an angel had told me the truth; and casting my eyes about the prison, I saw a bit of rotten brick; so I rubbed one piece against another and made a sort of little paste of it: then on all fours I went up to a corner of that prison door and with my teeth I worked away until I got off a little splinter; and when I had done this, I waited for that hour of light which came into my prison, which was from twenty and a half hours until twenty-one and a half. Then I began to write as best I could on certain leaves that were left over in the book of the Bible; and I reproved the spirits of my intellect, angry at their not wanting to stay alive any longer; and they answered my body, excusing themselves for their misfortune: and my body gave them hope of good: so I wrote it in dialogue:

	"Afflicted spirits of mine,
	alas, how cruel, that life should weary you!"
	"If you are set against Heaven,
	who will be for us? who will bring us any help?
	Let us go, let us go to a better life."
	"Ah, do not go yet,
	for Heaven promises you more happiness and joy
	than you have ever known."
	"We will stay some little while,
	provided that great God grants you
	the grace that we come to no worse trouble."

Having taken my vigour back again, after I had comforted myself by my own means, going on with reading my Bible, my eyes had grown so used to that darkness that where before I used to read an hour and a half, I now read three whole hours. And I considered with such wonder the force of the power of God in those very simple men, who believed with such fervour that God granted them everything they imagined: promising myself too the help of God, both for His divinity and mercy and also for my innocence; and continually, now with prayer and now with reasonings turned to God, I was always in these high thoughts about God; so that there began to come to me such a great delight in these thoughts about God that I no longer remembered any unhappiness I had ever had before; on the contrary, I sang psalms all day and many other compositions of my own, all directed to God.

The only thing that gave me great distress was my nails, which kept growing; because I could not touch myself without wounding myself with them: I could not dress myself, because they turned back either inwards or outwards, giving me a great deal of pain. My teeth were dying in my mouth as well; and I knew this, because the dead teeth being pushed by the living ones, little by little they perforated the gums, and the points of the roots came through the bottom of their sockets. When I noticed it I pulled them out, like drawing them out of a sheath, without any pain or blood: so I had lost a good many of them. Still, having come to terms with these new distresses too, I spent the time now singing, now praying, and now writing with that pounded brick I have spoken of; and I began a poem in praise of the prison, and in it I told all those things that had happened to me there; which poem shall be written out later in its own place.

Book One, Chapters 120 to 125

Chapter 120

The good Castellan often sent secretly to find out what I was doing: and because on the last day of July I made great rejoicing by myself, remembering the great festival they hold in Rome on that first day of August, I said to myself: "All these past years I have kept this pleasant festival with the frailties of the world; this year I shall keep it from now on with the divinity of God;" and I said to myself: "Oh, how much happier I am with this one than with those!" Those who heard me say these words reported the whole of it to the Castellan; who with wonderful annoyance said: "Oh God! that man triumphs and lives in so much misery; and I suffer in so many comforts, and am dying solely because of him! Go quickly and put him in that deepest underground cave, where the preacher Foiano was starved to death: perhaps seeing himself in such wretchedness the maggot might get out of his head."

At once Captain Sandrino Monaldi came to my prison with about twenty of those servants of the Castellan; and they found me on my knees, and I did not turn towards them, but went on worshipping a God the Father adorned with Angels and a Christ rising victorious, which I had drawn for myself on the wall with a bit of charcoal I had found covered by the earth, after four months of my having lain on my back in bed with my leg broken; and so many times I dreamed that the Angels came to treat it for me, that after four months I had become as strong as if it had never been broken. Still, they came at me so heavily armed, almost as if they were afraid I was a venomous dragon. The said captain said: "You surely hear that there are a good many of us, and that we come to you with a great noise; and you do not turn towards us." At these words, imagining very well the worst that could happen to me, and having made myself practised and steady under trouble, I said to them: "To this God who carries me to the God of the heavens I have turned my soul and my contemplations and all my vital spirits; and to you I have turned exactly what belongs to you, because what is good in me you are not worthy to look at, nor can you touch it: so do to what is yours everything you can." This hard captain, in fear, not knowing what I meant to do, said to four of the strongest of them: "Take off all your weapons from your sides." When they had taken them off, he said: "Quick, quick, jump on him and take him. Is this man the devil, that so many of us should be afraid of him? Hold him tight now so that he does not get away from you."

I, forced and roughly handled by them, imagining much worse than what afterwards happened to me, raising my eyes to Christ said: "O just God, You surely paid all our debts on that high wood: why then must my innocence pay the debts of men I do not know? Oh, still let Your will be done." Meanwhile they were carrying me away with a great flaring torch alight; I thought they meant to throw me into the oubliette of Sammalò, as they call a fearful place which has swallowed a good many men alive, because they come down into the foundations of the Castle into a well. This did not happen to me: on account of which it seemed to me I had made a very good bargain; because they put me in that filthiest cave I spoke of, where Foiano died of hunger, and left me there, doing me no other harm. When they had left me, I began to sing a De profundis clamavi, a Miserere, and In te Domine speravi. All that first day of August I kept festival with God, and my heart was always jubilant with hope and faith.

The second day they drew me out of that hole and carried me back to where those first drawings of mine of those images of God were. And when I got there, in front of them I wept a great deal out of sweetness and gladness. After that the Castellan wanted to know every day what I was doing and what I was saying. The Pope, who had heard the whole business, and the doctors having already given the said Castellan up for dead, said: "Before my Castellan dies, I want him to put that Benvenuto to death in his own way, since he is the cause of his death, so that he shall not die unavenged." When the Castellan heard these words from the mouth of Duke Pier Luigi, he said to him: "Then the Pope makes me a present of Benvenuto, and wants me to take my revenge? Let him think no more about it and leave it to me." Just as the Pope's heart was bad towards me, at first sight the Castellan's was the worst and most grievous; and at that point that Invisible One, who had turned me aside from wanting to kill myself, came to me, invisibly again but with a clear voice; and shook me and lifted me from where I lay and said: "Alas! my Benvenuto, quickly, quickly turn to God with your usual prayers, and cry out loudly, loudly." At once, terrified, I got on my knees, and said many prayers of mine aloud: then all of them, and a Qui habitat in adiutorium; then after this I talked with God a while: and in an instant that same voice, open and clear, said to me: "Go and rest, and be afraid no more." And this was because the Castellan, having given the foulest order for my death, had at once taken it back and said: "Is not this Benvenuto the man I have defended so much, and the man I know for certain is innocent, and that all this harm has been done him wrongfully? Oh, how shall God ever have mercy on me and on my sins, if I do not forgive those who have done me the greatest offences? Oh, why must I offend an honest man, an innocent one, who has done me service and honour? Let him go; and instead of putting him to death, I give him life and liberty; and I leave it in my will that nobody shall ask him anything for the debt of the heavy expense he would have to pay here." The Pope heard this and took it very badly.

Chapter 121

Meanwhile I went on with my usual prayers and wrote my poem, and I began to have every night the happiest and pleasantest dreams that can ever be imagined; and it always seemed to me that I was visibly together with the One I had heard invisibly, and heard very often, of whom I asked no other favour except that I begged Him, and pressingly, to take me where I could see the sun, telling Him that this was the whole of my desire; and that if I could see it just once, I would die content afterwards. Of all the things I had in this prison that were disagreeable, all of them had become my friends and companions, and nothing disturbed me. Although those devoted servants of the Castellan, who were expecting the Castellan to hang me from that battlement I had come down by, as he had said, seeing afterwards that the said Castellan had come to another resolution altogether contrary to that one; these men, who could not bear it, were always giving me some different fright, meant to make me take alarm for the loss of my life. As I say, I had grown so used to all these things that I was no longer afraid of anything and nothing moved me any more; only this desire, this dreaming of seeing the disc of the sun. So that, going on with my long prayers, always with my heart turned to Christ, always saying: "O true Son of God, I beg You by Your birth, by Your death on the cross and by Your glorious resurrection, to make me worthy to see the sun, if not otherwise, at least in a dream; but if You made me worthy to see it with these mortal eyes of mine, I promise to come and visit You at Your holy Sepulchre." This resolution and these greatest prayers of mine to God I made on the second day of October in 1539.

The next morning came, which was the third day of the said October, and I had woken at the point of day, almost an hour before sunrise; and getting up from that unhappy den of mine, I put on a bit of a wretched gown I had, because it had begun to turn cold: and standing up like that, I made prayers more devout than any I had ever made in the past; for in the said prayers I said with great entreaty to Christ that He would grant me at least so much grace that I might know by divine inspiration for what sin of mine I was doing so great a penance; and since His divine Majesty had not chosen to make me worthy of the sight of the sun even in a dream, I begged Him by all His power and might to make me worthy to know what was the cause of that penance.

Chapter 122

When I had said these words, by that Invisible One I was caught up as by a wind and carried away, and I was taken into a room where that Invisible One of mine then showed himself to me visibly in human form, like a young man with his first beard; with a most marvellous face, beautiful but stern, not wanton; and he showed me things in the said room, saying to me: "All those many men you see are all those who up to now have been born and then have died." So I asked him for what reason he was taking me there: and he said to me: "Come forward with me and you will soon see." I found I had a little dagger in my hand and a coat of mail on my body; and so he led me through that great room, showing me those who in infinite thousands were walking now one way, now another. Having led me forward, he went out ahead of me by a little door into a place like a narrow street; and when he drew me after him into the said street, on coming out of that room I found myself unarmed, and I was in a white shirt with nothing on my head, and I was on the right hand of my said companion. Seeing myself like that, I wondered, because I did not recognize that street; and raising my eyes, I saw that the brightness of the sun was striking on a face of wall, like the front of a house, above my head. Then I said: "Oh my friend, how must I manage so as to raise myself high enough to see the very disc of the sun?" He showed me several steps that were there on my right hand, and said to me: "Go there by yourself."

Getting a little apart from him, I climbed those several steps backwards on my heels, and began little by little to make out the neighbourhood of the sun. I hurried to climb; and I went so far up in that way that I made out the whole disc of the sun. And because the force of its rays, as they usually do, made me shut my eyes, realizing my mistake, I opened my eyes and looking fixedly at the sun, I said: "O sun of mine, that I have wanted so much, I want never to look at anything else again, even if your rays blind me." So I stayed with my eyes fixed on it; and having been a little while like that, I saw in a moment all that force of those great rays throw itself over onto the left side of the said sun; and the sun being left clear, without its rays, with the greatest pleasure I looked at it; and it seemed to me a marvellous thing that those rays should have taken themselves off in that way. I stayed considering what a divine grace this was that I had that morning from God, and I said aloud: "Oh, how wonderful is Your power! oh, how glorious Your might! How much greater a grace You do me than I looked for!" This sun without its rays seemed to me neither more nor less than a bath of purest molten gold.

While I was considering this great thing, I saw that in the middle of the said sun there began to be a swelling; and this shape of this swelling grew, and in a moment there was made a Christ on the cross of the same substance as the sun; and He was of such beautiful grace and of so kindly an aspect that human wit could not imagine the thousandth part of it; and while I was considering the thing, I said aloud: "Miracles, miracles! O God, O Your mercy, O Your infinite power, of what things You make me worthy this morning!" And while I was considering and saying these words, this Christ moved towards that quarter where His rays had gone, and in the middle of the sun there swelled up again as it had done before; and when the swelling had grown, at once it was turned into the form of a most beautiful Madonna, who seemed to be seated very high up with the said Son in her arms in a most pleasing attitude, almost laughing; on this side and on that she had in the middle of two Angels so beautiful that the imagination does not reach them. I also saw in that same sun, on the right hand, a figure dressed like a priest: this one had his back to me and kept his face turned towards that Madonna and that Christ. All these things I saw truly, clearly and living, and continually I thanked the glory of God in the loudest voice.

When this marvellous thing had been before my eyes a little more than an eighth of an hour, it went away from me, and I was carried back to that den of mine. At once I began to shout out loud, in a loud voice saying: "The power of God has made me worthy to be shown all His glory, which perhaps no other mortal eye has ever seen: so by this I know myself to be free and happy and in the grace of God; and you rascals will remain rascals, unhappy and in the disgrace of God. Know that I am quite certain that on All Saints' day, which was the day I came into the world, in exactly 1500, the first day of November, on the night following, at four hours, that day when it comes you will be forced to take me out of this dark prison; and you will not be able to do otherwise, because I have seen it with my own eyes and in that throne of God. That priest, who was turned towards God and who showed his back to me, was Saint Peter, who was pleading for me, ashamed that in his house such foul wrongs should be done to Christians. So tell it to whom you like, that nobody has the power to do me any more harm; and tell that lord who keeps me here that if he gives me either wax or paper, and the means of my expressing to him this glory of God which has been shown me, most certainly I shall make him clear about what he is perhaps in doubt of."

Chapter 123

The Castellan, although the doctors had no hope at all of his recovery, still had a firm spirit left in him, and those humours of madness which used to trouble him every year had gone off: and having given himself altogether and entirely to his soul, his conscience gnawed at him, and it certainly seemed to him that I had received and was receiving a very great wrong; and when he let the Pope know those great things I was saying, the Pope sent word back to him, like a man who believed in nothing, neither in God nor in anybody else, saying that I had gone mad, and that he should attend as much as he could to his own health. When the Castellan heard these answers, he sent to comfort me and sent me writing materials and some wax and certain little sticks made for working in wax, with many courteous words, which were said to me by a certain one of his servants who was fond of me. This man was quite the opposite of that faction of those other rascals, who would have liked to see me dead. I took those papers and that wax, and began to work: and while I worked I wrote this sonnet addressed to the Castellan:

	If I could show you, my lord, the truth
	of the eternal light, here in this low life,
	which I have from God, then my faith in your eyes
	would be worth far more than any high empire.
	Ah, if the great Shepherd of the fold believed
	that God has shown Himself in His infinite glory,
	as no soul ever saw Him before it left
	this low kingdom, harsh and unsparing;
	then you would see the sacred and holy gates
	of Justice thrown open, and wicked fury
	fall bound, and send its cries up to heaven.
	If I had light — alas — at least I might carve
	the footprints of Heaven's great worth!
	My great trouble would not be so heavy a cross.

Chapter 124

When the next day that servant of the Castellan came to bring me my food, the one who was fond of me, I gave him this sonnet written out; which, secretly from those other malicious servants who wished me ill, he gave to the Castellan: who would gladly have let me go, because it seemed to him that the wrong which had been done me was a great cause of his death. He took the sonnet, and having read it more than once, said: "These are neither the words nor the ideas of a madman; but rather of a good and honest man;" and at once he ordered a secretary of his to carry it to the Pope, and to give it into his own hand, begging him to let me go. While the said secretary carried the sonnet to the Pope, the Castellan sent me light for the day and for the night, with all the comforts that could be wished for in that place; on account of which I began to improve from the disorder of my health, which had become very great. The Pope read the sonnet several times; then he sent word to the Castellan that he would very soon do something that would please him. And certainly the Pope would afterwards have let me go willingly; but the said Signor Pier Luigi, his son, almost against the Pope's will, kept me there by force.

The Castellan's death drawing near, while I had drawn and modelled that marvellous miracle, on the morning of All Saints' he sent Piero Ugolini, his nephew, to me to show me certain jewels; which, when I saw them, I said at once: "This is the sign of my deliverance." Then this young man, who was a person of very little sense, said: "Never think of that, Benvenuto." Then I said: "Take your jewels away, because I am brought to such a state that I see no light except in this dark cave, in which the quality of jewels cannot be made out; but as for getting out of this prison, this whole day will not end before you come to fetch me out: and it must needs be so, and you cannot do otherwise." The man went away and had me shut up again; and having gone, he stayed away more than two hours by the clock; then he came for me without armed men, with two boys to help hold me up, and so took me into those large rooms I had had before (this was in 1538), giving me all the comforts I asked for.

Chapter 125

A few days later the Castellan, who thought I was outside and free, hard pressed by his great illness passed out of this present life, and in his place there remained Messer Antonio Ugolini his brother, who had given the late Castellan, his brother, to understand that he had let me go. This Messer Antonio, as far as I heard, had orders from the Pope to leave me in that roomy prison until such time as he should tell him what was to be done with me.

That Messer Durante of Brescia already spoken of came to an agreement with that soldier, the apothecary of Prato, to give me among my food some liquor to eat that should be deadly, but not at once; it should take effect within four or five months. They took to thinking of putting powdered diamond among the food; which has no poison in it of any kind, but by reason of its inestimable hardness it keeps the sharpest edges, and does not behave as other stones do; for that very fine sharpness in all stones does not survive their being pounded, but they are left as it were rounded; and the diamond alone keeps that sharpness; so that getting into the stomach along with the other food, in that turning about which the food does to make digestion, this diamond catches on the cartilages of the stomach and of the bowels, and as the new food comes pushing it always forward, that diamond caught on them in no long space of time pierces them; and for that reason a man dies; whereas every other sort of stone or glass mixed with food has not the power of catching on, and so goes off with the food. So this Messer Durante mentioned above gave a diamond of some little value to one of these guards. It was said that a certain Lione of Arezzo, a goldsmith, a great enemy of mine, had the handling of it. This Lione got the diamond to pound it; and because Lione was very poor and the diamond might be worth several tens of scudi, the man gave that guard to understand that the powder he gave him was that pounded diamond which had been ordered to be given me; and that morning when I had it, they put it in all my dishes; which was a Friday: I had it in salad and in sauces and in soup.

I set to eating with a good appetite, because in the evening I had fasted. This day was a holiday. It is quite true that I felt the food crunch under my teeth, but I never thought of such villainy. When I had finished dining, a little salad being left in the dish, my eyes fell straight on certain very fine splinters which were left over. At once I took them, and going up to the light of the window, which was very bright, while I was looking at them I was reminded of that crunching the food had made for me that morning more than usual: and having considered them well, as far as my eyes could judge, I firmly believed that this was pounded diamond. At once I gave myself up for dead, absolutely; and so, full of grief, I ran devoutly to holy prayers; and having made up my mind, it seemed to me certain that I was finished and dead: and for a whole hour I made great prayers to God, thanking Him for so pleasant a death. Since my stars had so destined me, it seemed to me I had had a good bargain in getting out of it by that easy road; and I was content, and had blessed the world and the time I had spent in it. Now I was going back to a better kingdom with the grace of God, which it seemed to me I had most surely gained: and while I was in these thoughts, I held in my hand certain very fine little grains of that supposed diamond, which I judged for a certainty to be such.

Now, because hope never dies, it seemed to me that I was prompted by a little vain hope; which was the cause of my taking a little knife, and taking some of the said little grains, and putting them on an iron bar of the prison; then setting the point of the knife on them flatwise, pressing hard, I felt the said stone break up; and looking well with my eyes, I saw that this was really so. At once I clothed myself in new hope and said: "This is not my enemy Messer Durante, but a soft bit of stone which cannot do me any harm in the world." And whereas I had made up my mind to keep quiet and to die in peace in that way, I made a new resolution, but first thanking God and blessing poverty, which just as it is often the cause of men's death, that time had itself been the cause of my life; because that Messer Durante my enemy, or whoever it was, having given a diamond worth more than a hundred scudi to Lione, to pound it for me, the man out of poverty kept it for himself, and pounded for me a yellow beryl worth two carlins, thinking perhaps that, being a stone too, it would have the same effect as the diamond.

Book One, Chapters 126 to 128

Chapter 126

At this time the Bishop of Pavia, brother of the Count of San Secondo, called Monsignor de' Rossi of Parma, this bishop was a prisoner in the Castle over certain troubles he had had at Pavia; and because he was a great friend of mine, I put myself out at the hole of my prison and called to him in a loud voice, telling him that to kill me those thieves had given me pounded diamond: and I had some of those little powders that were left me shown him by a servant of his; but I did not tell him that I had found out that it was not diamond; instead I told him that most certainly they had poisoned me after the death of that honest man the Castellan; and for the little while I might live, I begged him to give me one of his loaves a day, because I never wanted to eat anything again that came from them. So he promised to send me some of his own food. That Messer Antonio, who certainly knew nothing of the business, made a very great noise about it and insisted on seeing that pounded stone, thinking too that it was diamond; and supposing that such an enterprise came from the Pope, he passed it over lightly, once he had considered the case.

I set myself to eating the food the Bishop sent me, and went on continually writing that poem of mine about the prison, putting into it day by day all those things that newly happened to me, point by point. The said Messer Antonio also sent me my food by a certain Giovanni mentioned above, the apothecary from the district of Prato, and a soldier there. This man, who was my greatest enemy and who had been the one who brought me that pounded diamond, I told that I would eat nothing of what he brought me unless he first tasted it before me: on account of which he told me that tasters are provided for Popes. To whom I answered that just as gentlemen are bound to taste for the Pope, so he, a soldier, an apothecary, a peasant from Prato, was bound to taste for a Florentine like me. He said some big words, and I said some to him. That Messer Antonio, being somewhat ashamed, and also planning to make me pay those expenses which the poor dead Castellan had made me a present of, found another of those servants of his who was a friend of mine; and he sent me my food, which the man just mentioned pleasantly tasted for me with no further argument. This servant told me how the Pope was pestered every day by that Monsignor de Morluc, who on the King's behalf was continually asking for me; and that the Pope had little mind to give me up; and that Cardinal Farnese, once so much my patron and friend, had said that I should not plan on getting out of that prison for a good while: to whom I said that I would get out of it in spite of them all. This decent young man begged me to keep quiet, and not to be heard saying such a thing, because it would do me a great deal of harm; and that with that trust I had in God I ought to wait for His grace, keeping quiet. I told him that the powers of God have no need to be afraid of the malignity of injustice.

Chapter 127

So a few days went by, and the Cardinal of Ferrara arrived in Rome; who, going to pay his respects to the Pope, was kept by the Pope so long that the supper hour came. And because the Pope was a very able man, he wanted plenty of leisure to talk with the Cardinal about French affairs. And because at table things get said which outside such an occasion would sometimes not be said; so that, that great King Francis being most generous in everything of his, and the Cardinal, who well knew the King's taste, on his side satisfied the Pope far more fully than the Pope had imagined; so that the Pope had come into such gaiety, both from this and because he was in the habit once a week of making a really heavy debauch, since afterwards he vomited it up. When the Cardinal saw the Pope's disposition, fit for granting favours, he asked for me on the King's behalf with great insistence, showing that the King had a great desire for the thing. Then the Pope, feeling the hour of his vomit coming on, and the too great abundance of wine also doing its office, said to the Cardinal with a great laugh: "This minute, this minute I want you to take him home with you;" and having given the express orders, he got up from the table; and the Cardinal sent for me at once, before Signor Pier Luigi should know of it, because he would never have let me out of prison on any account. The Pope's messenger came together with two great gentlemen of the said Cardinal of Ferrara, and at four hours past nightfall they took me out of the said prison and led me before the Cardinal; who gave me an inestimable welcome; and there, well lodged, I stayed to enjoy myself.

Messer Antonio, the Castellan's brother and in his place, insisted that I should pay him all the expenses, with all those extras that constables and people of that kind are in the habit of wanting, and would not observe anything of what the late Castellan had left instructions should be done for me. This thing cost me many tens of scudi, and afterwards the Cardinal told me that I should be well on my guard if I valued my life, and that if he had not got me out of that prison that evening, I should never have got out at all; for he had already heard it said that the Pope was very sorry to have let me go.

Chapter 128

I must go back a step, because in my poem all these things I am telling come in. When I stayed those several days in the Cardinal's room and afterwards in the Pope's private garden, among my other dear friends there came to find me a cashier of Messer Bindo Altoviti, who by name was called Bernardo Galluzzi; to whom I had entrusted the value of several hundreds of scudi; and this young man came to find me in the Pope's private garden and wanted to give me back everything; and I told him that I did not know how to give my property either to a dearer friend or into a place where I could have thought it safer; and this friend of mine seemed to twist about at not wanting it, and I almost by force made him keep it. When I had come out of the Castle for the last time, I found that that poor young man Bernardo Galluzzi had been ruined; on account of which I lost my property.

Again: at the time I was in prison, a terrible dream came to me, in which as it were with a reed pen there were written on my forehead words of the greatest importance; and the one who wrote them repeated to me three good times that I should keep silent and not report them to anybody. When I woke up, I felt my forehead defiled. So in my poem about the prison a great many things of this kind come in.

Again: it came to me to say, without knowing what I was saying, everything that afterwards happened to Signor Pier Luigi, so clearly and so exactly that I have considered by myself that an Angel of Heaven must have dictated them to me.

Again: I do not want to leave out one thing, the greatest that ever happened to any other man; which is in justification of the divinity of God and of His secrets, of which He deigned to make me worthy: that from then on, since I saw that thing, there has remained a splendour — a marvellous thing! — above my head; which is plain to every sort of man to whom I have wanted to show it, and those have been very few. This can be seen above my shadow in the morning at sunrise until two hours of sun, and it is seen much better when the grass has that soft dew on it; it can also be seen in the evening at sunset. I noticed it in France, in Paris, because the air in that part of the world is so much clearer of mists that there it was seen far more distinctly than in Italy, where the mists are much more frequent; but that does not mean that I do not see it in any case; and I can show it to others, but not so well as in that part I have spoken of.

I want to write out my poem made in prison and in praise of the said prison; then I shall go on with the good and the bad things that happened to me from time to time, and those too that shall happen to me in my life.

The Capitolo

I write this poem to Luca Martini, calling on him in it as you will hear.

	Anyone who wants to know how great God's power is,
	and how far a man resembles that Good,
	had better spend time in prison, to my mind;
	let him be loaded with worries and a household,
	and some pain in his own body,
	and have come a thousand miles from home.
	Now if you want to be able to do something good,
	be arrested wrongfully, and then stay there a long while,
	and get help from nobody;
	let them rob you as well of the little you have:
	danger of your life; be roughly handled,
	with never a hope of rescue.
	And let them drive you into desperation,
	to break the prison, to jump the Castle:
	then be put back in a worse place still.
	Listen, Luca, now the fine part is coming:
	to have broken a leg, to have been tricked,
	the prison soaking, and no cloak to your back.
	And let nobody ever speak to you,
	and let your food be brought, with bad news,
	by a soldier, apothecary, peasant from Prato.
	Now hear where the glory really shows:
	nowhere to sit but on the privy;
	and still always awake to make something new.
	Express orders given to the servant
	not to hear you speak, nor give you anything;
	and to open the door the tiniest crack.
	Now this is where a fine brain amuses itself:
	no paper, pen, ink, iron or fire,
	and full of fine thoughts since the cradle.
	The great pity of it — little has been told of it,
	but for every one of them imagine a hundred,
	for I have kept a share and a place for them all.
	Now, to come back to what we first meant,
	and speak the praise the prison deserves:
	everyone in Heaven would not be enough for it.
	Good people are never put in here
	except by officials, or by bad government,
	envy, spite, or over some quarrel.
	To tell the truth of what I make out about it,
	here a man knows God and calls on Him always,
	feeling all the while the pains of Hell.
	Let a man be as bad in repute as the world allows,
	and let him stay in prison a couple of wretched years:
	he comes out holy and wise, and everybody loves him.
	Here the soul is refined, and the body, and the clothes;
	and every coarse lump of a man is made fine,
	and Heaven is seen right up to its benches.
	I want to tell you a great marvel:
	the fancy taking me to write a caprice,
	what things a man lays hold of at a pinch.
	I go about the room, knitting my brows and my head,
	then I make for an edge of the door,
	and with my teeth I get off a splinter of wood;
	and I took a piece of brick as it came,
	and broke a little of it down into powder;
	then I made a paste of it with stagnant water.
	Just then the fire of poetry
	came into my body, and I think by the road
	the bread goes out by; for there was no other way in.
	To come back to my first notion:
	whoever wants to know what good is
	must first know the evil God sends him.
	The prison knows and keeps every trade:
	if it were the apothecary's you wanted,
	it makes you sweat the blood out of your veins.
	Then it has a certain natural power in it:
	it makes you eloquent, spirited and bold,
	loaded with fine thoughts for good and for ill.
	It is good for the man who lies a long time
	in a dark prison, and then at last gets out:
	he can talk about war, truce and peace.
	Everything is bound to come out right for him;
	for the place fills a man so full of skill
	that his brain does not go dancing afterwards.
	You might say to me: "You are those years the poorer."
	It is not true, for it teaches you a way
	of filling your breast and your bosom with them later.
	As for me, so far as I know, I praise it;
	but I would like to see a law in use:
	that whoever deserves it most should not get off.
	Every man who is given charge of the poor flock
	ought to take his doctorate in the prison,
	because then he would know how governing is done.
	He would do things as people should,
	and would never stray out of the sown field,
	nor would such great confusion be seen.
	In this time that I have been here
	I have seen friars, priests and other people,
	and those who deserved it most stay the least.
	If you knew the great grief a man feels
	when one of them walks out ahead of you!
	He almost repents of having been born.
	I will say no more: I have turned into gold,
	which is not spent so easily,
	nor would very good work be made of it.
	Another thing has come into my mind
	which I have not told you, Luca: where I wrote it
	was in a book belonging to a relative of ours,
	for I set down along the length of the margins
	this great grief that has twisted my limbs,
	and the paste would not run, as I told you;
	so that to make one O I had to dip
	the stick three times; and I do not think there is grief
	like it in Hell among the souls wrapped up there.
	Now since I am not the first here to be wronged,
	I say nothing of it; and come back to the prison,
	where I file my brain and my heart away with grief.
	I praise it more than other people do;
	and if you want to make a learned man of one who knows nothing,
	without it no good work can be done.
	Oh, if only there were, as I read a while ago,
	somebody to say as at the Pool:
	"Take up your clothes, Benvenuto, and go!"
	I would sing the Creed and the Salve Regina
	and the Our Father, and then hand out alms
	to the blind, the poor and the lame every morning.
	Oh, how many times these lilies have made my cheek
	pale and bloodless, so much so
	that I want neither Florence nor France any more!
	And if I happen to go to the hospital
	and the Annunciation is painted there,
	I shall run away and look like an animal.
	I do not say this against Her, worthy and consecrated,
	nor against her glorious and holy lilies,
	which have lit up heaven and earth;
	but because at every hour I see, up on the street corners,
	some of those that have their petals hooked,
	I shall be afraid they belong to that great crowd.
	Oh, how many go wretched as I do,
	some born to it, some brought to this business,
	spirits clear, graceful, high and divine!
	I saw the deadly emblem fall
	swiftly from the sky among the empty people,
	then a new lamp lit in the stone;
	I saw the Castle bell break
	before I got out of it; and He had told me so,
	He who lays the truth flat in Heaven and on earth;
	after this, a bier draped in mourning
	and decked with broken lilies; weeping and a cross,
	and many laid in bed afflicted with grief.
	I saw her who torments and burns souls,
	frightening now this man, now that; then she said:
	"I mean to carry in my breast whoever harms you."
	Then that Worthy One wrote on my forehead
	words to me with Peter's reed pen,
	and three good times told me to keep silent.
	I saw Him who drives and reins in the sun,
	clothed in it in the midst of His Court,
	as no mortal eye is ever used to seeing.
	A solitary sparrow was singing loudly
	above the fortress; so that I said: "Certainly
	that bird foretells life for me, and death for you."
	And I sang and wrote out my great case,
	asking of God only pardon and help,
	for I felt my eyes going out, fixed in death.
	There never was wolf, lion, tiger or bear
	more bristling than that man for human blood,
	nor viper with a more venomous bite;
	this was a cruel thieving captain,
	the greatest rascal, with certain other bad men;
	but so that not everyone shall know it, I will say it low.
	If you have ever seen starving catchpoles
	going in to distrain on some poor wretch,
	throwing Our Ladies and Christs on the ground —
	on the August day they came out of spite
	to move me into a more wretched tomb:
	"November: every one of them scattered and cursed."
	I had at my ears such a truthful trumpet
	that it told me everything, and I told them,
	without thinking, because grief empties a man out.
	And when they were left without hope,
	they gave me, to kill me, a diamond
	pounded up to eat, and not set in gold.
	I asked that peasant rogue to taste it,
	the one who brought my food; and I said to myself:
	"That was not my enduring enemy after all."
	But first I gave my thoughts up to God,
	begging Him to forgive my sin;
	and weeping I said a Miserere.
	Somewhat quieted from the great grief,
	giving up this soul to God willingly,
	content to go to a better kingdom and another state,
	I saw an Angel come down from Heaven
	with a glorious palm; and then with a glad face
	he promised my life a longer span,
	saying to me: "By God, first every enemy of yours
	shall be taken away in bitter war,
	and you shall be left fortunate, happy and free,
	in the grace of Him who is Father in heaven and on earth."

Book Two

Book Two, Chapters 1 to 6

Chapter 1

Staying in the palace of the Cardinal of Ferrara mentioned above, very well thought of by everybody in general, and visited much more than I used to be, every man wondering all the more at my having come out and stayed alive among such immeasurable troubles; while I was getting my breath back and trying to remember my trade, I took the greatest pleasure in writing out this poem given above. Then, the better to get my strength back, I decided to go out for some days into the open air, with leave and with horses from my good Cardinal, together with two young Romans, one of whom was a workman of my trade; the other, his companion, was not of the trade, but came to keep me company. Leaving Rome, I went off towards Tagliacozzo, thinking to find there Ascanio, my apprentice spoken of above; and when I got to Tagliacozzo, I found the said Ascanio together with his father and brothers and sisters and stepmother. By them for two days I was made so much of that it would be impossible to tell it: I left for Rome, and took Ascanio with me. On the road we began to talk about the trade, so that I was burning to get back to Rome to begin my works again.

When we had got to Rome, I at once set myself up to work; and I found again a silver basin which I had begun for the Cardinal before I was imprisoned: along with the said basin a very beautiful little jug had been begun: this had been stolen from me along with a great quantity of other things of much value. On the said basin I set Pagolo, mentioned above, to work. I also began the jug again, which was composed of little figures in the round and in low relief; and likewise the said basin was composed of figures in the round and fishes in low relief, so rich and so well arranged that everybody who saw it was left wondering, both for the strength of the design and for the invention and for the neatness those young men used on the said works. The Cardinal came at least twice a day to stay with me, together with Messer Luigi Alamanni and with Messer Gabriello Cesano, and there we passed some hours cheerfully. Although I had plenty to do, still new works came in on me in abundance; and he gave me to make his pontifical seal, which was the size of the hand of a boy of twelve; and on the said seal I carved two little scenes in intaglio; one of which was Saint John preaching in the desert, the other Saint Ambrose driving out those Arians, shown on horseback with a whip in his hand, with such daring and such good design, and so neatly worked, that everybody said I had surpassed that great Lautizio who did nothing but this branch of the trade; and the Cardinal, out of his own pride, compared it with the other seals of the cardinals of Rome, which were almost all from the hand of the said Lautizio.

Chapter 2

The Cardinal further charged me, together with those two men mentioned above, to make him a model for a saltcellar; but he would have liked to get outside the usual run of those who had made saltcellars. Messer Luigi, on this subject of the salt, said many admirable things; Messer Gabriello Cesano too said very fine things to the same purpose. The Cardinal, a most kindly listener and satisfied beyond measure with the designs these two great talents had made in words, turned to me and said: "My Benvenuto, Messer Luigi's design and Messer Gabriello's please me so much that I should not know which of the two to take; so I leave it to you, who have to put it into execution." Then I said: "See, gentlemen, how important the sons of kings and emperors are, and that marvellous splendour and divinity which appears in them. Nevertheless, if you ask a poor humble shepherd which he has more love and affection for, those sons or his own, for certain he will say he has more love for his own sons. So I too have great love for my sons, whom I bring to birth in this profession of mine: so the first thing I shall show you, my most reverend Monsignor and patron, will be my own work and my own invention; because many things are fine to describe which, when you come to make them, do not go well together as a work." And turning to those two great talents, I said: "You have spoken and I shall do."

Messer Luigi Alamanni then, laughing, with the greatest pleasantness added many gracious words in my favour: and they suited him, because he was handsome in his face and in the proportion of his body, and had a gentle voice. Messer Gabriello Cesano was quite the reverse, so ugly and so disagreeable; and so he spoke according to his shape. Messer Luigi had designed in words that I should make a Venus with a Cupid, together with many pretty conceits, all to the purpose; Messer Gabriello had designed that I should make an Amphitrite, Neptune's wife, together with some of Neptune's Tritons and a great many other things very fine to describe, but not to make.

I made an oval form of a size well over half a braccio, almost two thirds, and on the said form, as the Sea shows itself embracing the Land, I made two figures well over a palm high, which sat with their legs entwined in each other, as one sees certain long arms of the sea that run into the land; and in the hand of the male Sea I put a most richly worked ship: in this ship a great deal of salt sat conveniently and well; underneath him I had arranged those four sea horses: in the right hand of the said Sea I had put his trident. The Land I had made a woman of as beautiful a form as I could and knew how, beautiful and full of grace; and in the hand of the said figure I had put a rich and adorned temple, resting on the ground; and she leaned on it with the said hand; this I had made to hold the pepper. In her other hand I had put a horn of plenty, adorned with all the beauties I knew in the world. Under this Goddess, and in that part which showed itself to be land, I had arranged all those most beautiful animals the earth produces. Under the part of the Sea I had figured all the fine sorts of fishes and little snails that the small space would allow: for the rest of the oval, in its thickness, I made many very rich ornaments.

Then, having waited for the Cardinal, who came with those two talented men, I brought out this work of mine in wax: at which the first with a great noise was Messer Gabriello Cesano, and he said: "This is a work not to be finished in the lifetime of ten men; and you, most reverend Monsignor, who would like it, will never have it in your lifetime; so Benvenuto has chosen to show you his sons, but not to give them, as we were doing, who spoke of things that could be made; and he has shown you things that cannot be made." At this, Messer Luigi Alamanni took my side. The Cardinal said that he did not want to go into so great an undertaking. Then I turned to them and said: "Most reverend Monsignor, and to you gentlemen full of talent, I say that I hope to make this work for whoever is to have it, and each of you will see it finished a hundred times richer than the model; and I hope there will still be plenty of time left us to make things much greater than this." The Cardinal said in annoyance: "If you do not make it for the King, where I am taking you, I do not think you can make it for anybody else;" and having shown me the letters in which the King wrote in one paragraph that he should soon come back, bringing Benvenuto with him, I raised my hands to heaven, saying: "Oh, when will this soon come?" The Cardinal said that I should give my orders and wind up the business I had in Rome, within ten days.

Chapter 3

When the time of departure came, he gave me a fine good horse; and it was called Tornon, because Cardinal Tournon had given it to him. Pagolo and Ascanio too, my apprentices, were provided with mounts. The Cardinal divided his Court, which was a very large one: the nobler part he took with him: with it he took the road through the Romagna, to go and visit Our Lady of Loreto, and from there on to Ferrara, his home; the other part he directed towards Florence. This was the larger part; and it was a great number, with all the beauty of his horsemen. To me he said that if I wanted to go safely, I should go with him: if not, I was in danger of my life. I gave His most reverend Lordship to understand that I would go with him; and as what is ordained by the Heavens must needs be, it pleased God to bring back into my memory my poor sister of my own blood, who had had such great unhappiness over my great troubles. There also came back into my memory my cousins, who were nuns at Viterbo, one an abbess and the other the treasurer, so that they were the governesses of that rich convent; and they had had such heavy trouble on my account and had said so many prayers for me, that I held it for certain that it was through the prayers of those poor virgins that I had obtained from God the grace of my deliverance. So, all these things coming into my memory, I turned towards Florence; and where I should have gone free of expense either with the Cardinal or with the other part of his train, I chose to go by myself; and I joined company with a most excellent master of clocks, who was called Master Cherubino, a great friend of mine. Meeting by chance, we made that journey very pleasantly together.

Having left Rome on Holy Monday, we came along just the three of us, and at Monterosi I found the said company; and because I had given people to understand that I was going off with the Cardinal, I did not think that any of those enemies of mine would have been keeping watch on me otherwise. Certainly I came off badly at Monterosi, because ahead of us a troop of well-armed men had been sent, to do me a mischief; and God willed it that while we were dining, they, having had word that I was coming without the Cardinal's train, had got themselves in order to do me harm. At just this point the said train of the Cardinal came up, and with it I went on happily and safe as far as Viterbo; for from there onwards I saw no more danger, and all the more since I always went several miles ahead; and those better men who were in that train took a great deal of account of me. I arrived, God be thanked, safe and sound at Viterbo, and there I was made a great deal of by those sisters of mine and by the whole convent.

Chapter 4

Having left Viterbo with the men spoken of, we came on riding, now ahead and now behind the said train of the Cardinal, so that on Holy Thursday at twenty-two hours we found ourselves near Siena at a post house; and seeing that there were some mares there to go back, and that the post men were waiting to give them to some traveller, for some small profit, so that he should take them back to the post at Siena; seeing this, I dismounted from my horse Tornon, and put my cushion and my stirrups on that mare, and gave a giulio to one of those post boys. Leaving my horse to my young men to bring on for me, I went ahead at once so as to get to Siena half an hour earlier, both to visit a friend of mine and to do some other business of my own: so, although I came on quickly, I did not race the said mare. When I got to Siena I took rooms at the inn, good ones such as we needed for five people, and by the innkeeper's boy I sent the said mare back to the post, which was outside the Camollia gate; and on the said mare I had forgotten my stirrups and my cushion.

We passed the evening of Holy Thursday very cheerfully: then in the morning, which was Good Friday, I remembered my stirrups and my cushion. When I sent for them, that postmaster said he would not give them back to me, because I had raced his mare. Several times word was sent back and forth and the man always said he would not give them back to me, with many insulting and unbearable words; and the innkeeper where I was lodged said to me: "You get off well if he does nothing worse to you than not give you back your cushion and stirrups;" and he added: "Know that he is the most brutish man this city ever had; and he has two grown sons there, very fierce soldiers, more brutish than he is; so buy again what you need and go on your way without saying anything to him." I bought a pair of stirrups again, still thinking to get my good cushion back with friendly words: and because I was very well mounted, and well armed with a coat of mail and sleeves, and with a wonderful arquebus at my saddlebow, those great brutalities the man said that mad brute had in him did not frighten me. I had also accustomed those young men of mine to wearing mail and sleeves, and I trusted very much in that young Roman, whom I thought never took his off while we were in Rome. Ascanio too, although he was still a boy, wore his as well: and because it was Good Friday, I thought the madness of madmen ought at least to keep a little holiday.

We came to the said Camollia gate; and there I saw and knew, by the marks that had been given me, being blind in the left eye, this postmaster. Coming up to him, and leaving my young men and my companions to one side, I said pleasantly: "Postmaster, if I assure you that I did not race your mare, why will you not be content to give me back my cushion and my stirrups?" To this he answered exactly in that mad, brutish way I had been told of. On account of which I said to him: "How is this? are you not a Christian? Do you want to scandalize both yourself and me on a Good Friday?" He said that neither Good Friday nor Devil's Friday was any trouble to him, and that if I did not get out of his sight, with a boar spear he had taken up he would tumble me on the ground together with that arquebus I had in my hand.

At these fierce words there came up an old gentleman of Siena, dressed in a citizen's clothes, who was coming back from performing those devotions that are usual on such a day; and having heard from a distance the whole of my case very well, he boldly came up to reprove the said postmaster, taking my side, and scolded his two sons for not doing what was right by the strangers who passed, and that in that way they were acting against God and bringing shame on the city of Siena. Those two young sons of his, shaking their heads without saying anything, went off inside their house. The furious father, envenomed by the words of that honourable gentleman, at once with abusive blasphemies lowered his boar spear, swearing that with it he meant to kill me in any case. Seeing this brutish resolution, to keep him back a little, I made as if to show him the mouth of my arquebus. The man, more furious, threw himself on me, and the arquebus which I had in my hand, although it was ready for my defence, I had not yet lowered enough for it to be pointed at him, but its mouth was still high; and it went off of itself. The ball struck the arch of the gate, and being beaten back, caught him in the windpipe, and he fell dead to the ground.

His two sons ran up quickly, and one of them took a weapon from a rack, the other took his father's boar spear; and throwing themselves on those young men of mine, that son who had the boar spear ran the first of them, Pagolo the Roman, through above the left breast; the other ran at a Milanese who was in our company, who had the face of a madman: and it did him no good to plead that he had nothing to do with me, and to defend himself from the point of a partisan with a little stick he had in his hand: with which he could not fence well enough to avoid being caught a little in the mouth. That Messer Cherubino was dressed as a priest, and although he was a most excellent master of clocks, as I have said, he had had benefices from the Pope with good incomes. Ascanio, although he was very well armed, made no sign of running away, as that Milanese had done; so that these two were not touched.

I had set spurs to my horse, and while he galloped had quickly put my arquebus in order again and loaded it, and was coming back in a rage, feeling I had been playing at it and now meaning to be in earnest; and I thought those young men of mine had been killed, and I was going, resolved, to die there too. The horse had not run many paces back before I met them coming towards me, and I asked them if they were hurt. Ascanio answered that Pagolo was wounded to death with a boar spear. Then I said: "Oh Pagolo my son! Then the spear went through your coat of mail?" "No," he said, "for I put my coat of mail in the saddlebag this morning." "So coats of mail are worn about Rome to look fine to the ladies? and in dangerous places, where there is need to have them, they are kept in the saddlebag? All the harm you have suits you very well, and you are the reason I mean to go and get killed there myself this minute;" and while I was saying these words, I kept going stoutly back. Ascanio and he begged me to be content, for the love of God, to save myself and to save them, because we were surely going to our deaths.

At this I met that Messer Cherubino, together with that wounded Milanese: at once he shouted at me, saying that nobody was hurt, and that Pagolo's blow had gone so straight along that it had not gone through; and that that old postmaster had been left dead on the ground, and that the sons, with a good many other people, had got themselves ready, and would certainly have cut us all to pieces: "So, Benvenuto, since fortune has saved us from that first fury, do not tempt her any more, for she would not save us again." Then I said: "Since you are content with it that way, I am content too;" and turning to Pagolo and Ascanio, I said to them: "Set spurs to your horses, and let us gallop as far as Staggia without ever stopping, and there we shall be safe." That wounded Milanese said: "A plague on sins! for this hurt I have came only from the sin of a little meat soup I ate yesterday, having nothing else for dinner." With all these great tribulations we were in, we were forced to make some show of laughing at that beast and at those foolish words he had said. We set spurs to our horses, and left Messer Cherubino and the Milanese to come on at their leisure.

Chapter 5

Meanwhile the dead man's sons ran to the Duke of Melfi, to have him give them several light horsemen to overtake us and take us. The said Duke, learning that we were men of the Cardinal of Ferrara's, would give them neither horses nor leave. Meanwhile we got to Staggia, where we were safe. When we got to Staggia, we looked for a doctor, the best that could be had in that place: and when the said Pagolo had been looked at, the wound ran between skin and skin, and I saw that he would come to no harm. We had dinner got ready.

Meanwhile there appeared Messer Cherubino and that madman of a Milanese, who never left off sending a plague on quarrels, and said he was excommunicated, because on that holy morning he had not been able to say a single Our Father. Being ugly in the face, and his mouth by nature large; and then, from the wound he had got in it, his mouth had grown more than three fingers larger; and with that yowling Milanese speech of his, and with that silly tongue, the words he said gave us so much occasion to laugh that instead of grieving over our fortune, we could not help laughing at every word the man said. When the doctor wanted to sew up that wound in his mouth, having already put in three stitches, he told the doctor to hold off a bit, because he would not have liked him out of some enmity to sew the whole of it up: and he laid hold of a spoon, and said he wanted him to leave it so much open that the spoon would go in, so that he might get back alive to his own people. These words the man said, with certain shakings of the head, gave such great occasion for laughing that instead of grieving over our bad fortune, we never left off laughing; and so, laughing all the way, we got ourselves to Florence.

We went to dismount at the house of my poor sister, where we were made marvellously much of by my brother-in-law and by her. That Messer Cherubino and the Milanese went off about their business. We stayed in Florence four days, during which Pagolo got well; but it was a strange thing, that every time we spoke of that beast of a Milanese it moved us to as much laughter as the other misfortunes that had happened moved us to tears; so that continually, at one and the same time, we laughed and wept. Pagolo got well easily: then we went off towards Ferrara, and we found that our Cardinal had not yet arrived at Ferrara, and had heard all about our adventures; and grieving over them he said: "I pray God to give me grace enough to bring you alive to that King I have promised you to."

The said Cardinal made over to me in Ferrara a palace of his, a very beautiful place, called Belfiore: it borders on the walls of the city: there he had me fitted up to work. Then he gave orders to leave for France without me; and seeing that I was left very discontented, he said to me: "Benvenuto, all that I do is for your safety; because before I take you out of Italy, I want you to know very well first what you are coming to do in France: meanwhile press on as fast as you can with this basin and little jug of mine; and everything you need, I will leave orders with a steward of mine to give you." And when he had gone, I was left very discontented, and several times I had a mind to go off with God: but the only thing that held me was his having got me free from Pope Paul, because for the rest I was discontented and much out of pocket. Still, clothing myself in the gratitude the benefit received deserved, I made up my mind to have patience and to see what end this business was to have; and setting to work with those two young men of mine, I carried that jug and that basin marvellously far forward.

Where we were lodged the air was bad, and as the summer came on, we all fell a little ill. In these indispositions of ours we went looking about the place where we were, which was very large, and had almost a mile of open ground left wild, in which there were so many of our native peacocks that they brooded there like wild birds. Noticing this, I fitted up my fowling piece with a certain powder that made no noise; then I lay in wait for some of those young peacocks, and every two days I killed one, which fed us very amply, and with such virtue in it that all our illnesses left us: and for those several months we attended most cheerfully to our work, and carried forward that jug and that basin, which was a work that took a very long time.

Chapter 6

At this time the Duke of Ferrara came to terms with Pope Paul of Rome over certain old differences of theirs, which they had about Modena and certain other cities; over which, the Church having a right in them, the Duke made this peace with the said Pope by force of money: and the amount was a great one: I believe it came to more than three hundred thousand ducats of the Chamber. The Duke at this time had an old treasurer of his, a man brought up by Duke Alfonso his father, who was called Messer Girolamo Giliolo. This old man could not bear this outrage of so much money going to the Pope, and went shouting about the streets, saying: "Duke Alfonso his father, with this money, would sooner have taken Rome with it than shown it to him;" and there was no way of making him pay it. In the end, when the Duke forced him to have it paid, this old man got such a great flux of the body that it brought him close to death.

While he was ill, the said Duke called me and wanted me to make his portrait, which I did on a round of black stone, as large as a small serving trencher. The Duke liked these labours of mine together with a great deal of pleasant conversation; which two things often caused him to stay four and five hours at the least, attentive to being portrayed, and sometimes he made me sup at his table. In the space of eight days I finished this portrait of his head for him: then he ordered me to make the reverse; which was figured as Peace, a woman with a little torch in her hand, burning a trophy of arms: which I made, this said woman, in a joyful posture, with the finest of draperies, of most beautiful grace; and under her feet I figured, afflicted and downcast, and bound with many chains, despairing Fury. This work I made with much study, and it did me the greatest honour. The Duke could not have enough of calling himself satisfied, and gave me the mottoes for the head of His Excellency and for the reverse. The one on the reverse said "Pretiosa in conspectu Domini". It meant that that peace had been sold for a price in money.

Book Two, Chapters 7 to 12

Chapter 7

At this time, when I set about making the said reverse, the Cardinal had written to me telling me to get myself ready, because the King had asked for me: and that with his first letters there would come the order for everything he had promised me. I had my basin and my jug well packed in a case; and I had already shown them to the Duke. The Cardinal's business was managed by a gentleman of Ferrara, who was called by name Messer Alberto Bendedio. This man had been in the house twelve years without ever going out of it, because of an infirmity of his. One day with the greatest haste he sent for me, telling me that I ought to get into the post saddle at once to go and find the King, who with great insistence had asked for me, thinking I was in France.
