The Cardinal, as an excuse for himself, had said that I had stayed at an abbey of his in Lyons, a little ill, but that he would see to it that I should soon be with His Majesty; so he was making all this haste for me to ride post.

This Messer Alberto was a very honest man, but he was proud, and because of his illness insufferably proud; and as I say, he told me to get ready quickly, to ride post. To whom I said that my art was not carried on in the post saddle, and that if I had to go there, I wanted to go by easy stages and to take with me Ascanio and Pagolo, my workmen, whom I had brought from Rome; and besides that I wanted a servant with us on horseback, for my service, and money enough to get me there. This sick old man answered me with the most arrogant words, that in the way I described, and no otherwise, the Duke's sons travelled. To him I answered at once that the sons of my art travelled in the way I had said; and never having been the son of a duke, I did not know how they travelled; and that if he used those outlandish words to my ears, I would not go there at all, both because the Cardinal had failed of his word to me and because, with these boorish words added, I should certainly make up my mind not to want anything to do with men of Ferrara; and turning my back on him, I muttering and he blustering, I went off.

I went to find the said Duke with his medal finished; who made me the most honourable welcome ever made to a man in the world: and he had ordered that Messer Girolamo Giliolo of his to find, for these labours of mine, a ring with a diamond worth two hundred scudi, and to give it to Fiaschino his chamberlain, who should give it to me. So it was done. The said Fiaschino, on the evening of the day when I had given him the medal, at one hour of the night handed me a ring with a diamond in it, which made a great show; and he said these words on his Duke's behalf: that that matchless skilled hand, which had worked so well, should adorn itself with that diamond in memory of His Excellency. When day came, I looked at the said ring, which was a thin wretched diamond, worth about ten scudi. And because of those marvellous words that Duke had had used to me, I, not wanting them to be dressed in so poor a reward, the Duke thinking he had satisfied me well; and I, imagining that it came from that rascally treasurer of his, gave the ring to a friend of mine to give back to the chamberlain Fiaschino in whatever way he could. This was Bernardo Saliti, who did this office admirably.

The said Fiaschino came at once to find me with the greatest outcry, telling me that if the Duke knew that I was sending him back a present in that way, which he had so kindly given me, he would take it very badly, and perhaps I might repent of it. To him I answered that the ring His Excellency had given me was worth about ten scudi, and that the work I had done for His Excellency was worth more than two hundred; but that to show His Excellency that I valued the act of his courtesy, let him send me only a crab ring, of those that come from England which are worth about a carlin; I would keep that in memory of His Excellency as long as I lived, together with those honourable words His Excellency had had conveyed to me; because I reckoned that His Excellency's splendour had amply paid for my labours, whereas that low-priced jewel disgraced them. These words were such an annoyance to the Duke that he called that said treasurer of his, and abused him worse than he had ever abused him in the past; and he had orders given me, under pain of his displeasure, that I was not to leave Ferrara unless he let me know; and to his treasurer he gave orders to give me a diamond that came to three hundred scudi. The miserly treasurer found one worth a little over sixty scudi, and gave it out that the said diamond was worth far more than two hundred.

Chapter 8

Meanwhile the said Messer Alberto had come round to the right course, and had provided me with everything I had asked for. I had made up my mind that day to leave Ferrara in any case; but that diligent chamberlain of the Duke's had arranged with the said Messer Alberto that for that day I should have no horses. I had loaded a mule with a good deal of my baggage, and with it I had packed in a case that basin and that jug I had made for the Cardinal. At this point there came up a gentleman of Ferrara, who was called by name Messer Alfonso de' Trotti. This gentleman was very old and was a most affected person, and took the greatest pleasure in works of art; but he was one of those people who are extremely hard to please; and if by chance they ever happen to see something that pleases them, they paint it so excellent in their brains that they never think to see anything else that pleases them again.

This Messer Alfonso arrived; on account of which Messer Alberto said to him: "I am sorry you have come late: because that jug and that basin we are sending to the Cardinal in France are already packed and shut up." This Messer Alfonso said that he did not care; and beckoning to a servant of his, he sent him to his house: who brought a jug of white clay, of those clays of Faenza, very delicately worked. While the servant went and came back, this Messer Alfonso said to the said Messer Alberto: "I want to tell you why I do not care to see any more vases: it is that once I saw one in silver, an antique, so beautiful and so marvellous that the human imagination would not reach to conceiving such excellence; and so I do not care to see anything else of the kind, for fear it should spoil that marvellous image of it for me. This was a great gentleman of taste, who went to Rome on some business of his own and was secretly shown this antique vase; which by force of a great quantity of scudi he got out of the man who had it, corrupting him, and brought it with him to these parts of ours; but he keeps it very secret, so that the Duke shall not know of it; because he would be afraid of losing it in any case."

This Messer Alfonso, while he was telling these long tales of his, was not on his guard against me, who was present, because he did not know me. Meanwhile, this blessed clay model appeared, uncovered with such pomposity, patter and self-importance that when I had seen it, turning to Messer Alberto, I said: "Well, I am lucky to have seen it!" Messer Alfonso, in anger, said with some insulting word: "And who are you, who do not know what you are saying?" To this I said: "Now listen to me, and then you will see which of us knows better what he is saying." Turning to Messer Alberto, a very serious and intelligent person, I said: "This is a little silver jug of such and such a weight, which I made at such and such a time for that quack Master Iacopo the surgeon of Carpi, who came to Rome and stayed there six months; and with an ointment of his he besmeared many tens of lords and poor gentlemen, from whom he drew many thousands of ducats. At that time I made him this vase and another different from this; and he paid me for both of them very badly, and now all those unlucky men he greased are in Rome, crippled and in a bad way. It is the greatest glory to me that my works should be so famous with you rich gentlemen; but I tell you plainly that in all these years since, I have attended as far as I could to learning; so that I think the vase I am taking to France is worthy of the Cardinal and of the King in a way that that little doctor of yours was not."

When I had said these words of mine, that Messer Alfonso seemed literally to be consumed with desire to see that basin and jug, which I continually refused him. When we had been at this a while, he said that he would go to the Duke and by means of His Excellency he would see it. Then Messer Alberto Bendedio, who was, as I have said, exceedingly proud, said: "Before you leave here, Messer Alfonso, you shall see it, without using the Duke's favours." At these words I went off and left Ascanio and Pagolo to show it to them; who said afterwards that they had said the greatest things in my praise. After that Messer Alfonso wanted me to be on familiar terms with him, and it seemed a thousand years to me until I could get out of Ferrara and away from them all. What I had had of good there had been the company of Cardinal Salviati and that of the Cardinal of Ravenna, and of one or two of those talented musicians, and of nobody else; because the Ferrarese are a most avaricious people and love other men's property in every way they can get it; they are all like that.

At twenty-two hours the said Fiaschino appeared, and handed me the said diamond worth about sixty scudi; telling me with a gloomy face and in few words that I should wear it for love of His Excellency. To whom I answered: "And so I will." Putting my foot in the stirrup in his presence, I took the road to go off with God. He noted the act and the words; and having reported them to the Duke, in his anger the Duke had the greatest wish to make me come back.

Chapter 9

That evening I went on more than ten miles, trotting all the way; and when the next day I was out of the Ferrarese territory, I had the greatest pleasure in it, because apart from those young peacocks I ate there, which were the cause of my health, I found nothing else good in it. We made the journey by way of Mont Cenis, not touching the city of Milan on account of the suspicion mentioned above; so that safe and sound we arrived at Lyons. Together with Pagolo and Ascanio and a servant, we were four with four quite good mounts. Having got to Lyons, we stopped several days to wait for the muleteer, who had that basin and silver jug together with the rest of our baggage: we were lodged in an abbey belonging to the Cardinal. When the muleteer had arrived, we put all our things in a cart and started it off towards Paris: and so we went on towards Paris, and had some troubles on the road, but not very notable ones.

We found the King's court at Fontainebleau: we showed ourselves to the Cardinal, who at once had lodgings assigned us, and that evening we did well. The next day the cart appeared; and when we had taken our things, the Cardinal, having heard of it, told the King, who at once wanted to see me. I went to His Majesty with the said basin and jug, and having come into his presence, I kissed his knee and he received me most graciously. While I was thanking His Majesty for having set me free from prison, saying that he was under an obligation, every good and matchless prince in the world, as His Majesty was, to free good men who are fit for something, and all the more innocent ones as I was; and that those benefits are written down in the books of God before any other that can be done in the world; this good King listened to me until I had done, with much gratitude and with certain words worthy only of himself. When I had finished, he took the vase and the basin, and then said: "Truly I do not believe such a fine style of work was ever seen even among the ancients: for I well remember having seen all the best works, and made by the best masters, of the whole of Italy; but I never saw anything that moved me more greatly than this." These words the said King spoke in French to the Cardinal of Ferrara, with many others greater than these. Then turning to me he spoke to me in Italian, and said: "Benvenuto, pass your time cheerfully for a few days, and comfort your heart and attend to living well; and meanwhile we will think of giving you good conveniences for making some fine work for us."

Chapter 10

The Cardinal of Ferrara mentioned above, seeing that the King had taken the greatest pleasure in my arrival; and seeing too that with those few works the King had promised himself he could satisfy his wish to make certain very great works which he had in mind; so at this time, while we were following the Court, it may be said in torment (the reason being that the King's train drags continually behind it twelve thousand horse; and that is the least of it: because when the Court in times of peace is complete, they are eighteen thousand, so that they always come to be more than twelve thousand; on account of which we went on following the said Court sometimes into places where there were barely two houses; and as the gypsies do, we made ourselves tents of canvas, and many times we suffered a great deal): I still pressed the Cardinal to urge the King to set me to work; the Cardinal told me that the best thing in this case was to wait for the King to remember it of his own accord; and that I should let myself be seen by His Majesty now and then, while he was eating.

Doing so, one morning at his dinner the King called me: he began to speak to me in Italian, and said that he had it in mind to make many great works, and that he would soon give me orders where I was to work, providing me with everything I needed; with much other pleasant talk about various things. The Cardinal of Ferrara was present, because almost continually he ate in the morning at the King's small table; and having heard all this talk, when the King had got up from the table the Cardinal of Ferrara said in my favour, as far as was reported to me: "Sacred Majesty, this Benvenuto has a very great wish to work; one might almost say it is a sin to make such a talent lose time." The King added that he had said well, and that he should settle with me everything I wanted for my salary. The said Cardinal, on the evening of the day whose morning had brought him the commission, after supper, having sent for me, told me on His Majesty's behalf that His Majesty had made up his mind that I should set my hand to work; but that first he wanted me to know what my salary was to be. On this the Cardinal said: "It seems to me that if His Majesty gives you a salary of three hundred scudi a year, you can very well keep yourself; besides, I tell you to leave the care of it to me, because every day occasion comes of being able to do well in this great kingdom, and I will always help you marvellously."

Then I said: "Without my asking Your most reverend Lordship, when you left me in Ferrara you promised me never to take me out of Italy unless I first knew all the terms on which I was to be with His Majesty; Your most reverend Lordship, instead of sending word to me of the terms I was to be on, sent express orders that I should come post, as if such an art were carried on in the post saddle: for if you had sent to tell me three hundred scudi, as you tell me now, I would not have stirred for six hundred. But for all of it I thank God and Your most reverend Lordship as well, because God has used you as an instrument for so great a good as my deliverance from prison. So I say to Your Lordship that all the great harm I might have from you now cannot come to the thousandth part of the great good I have received from you, and I thank you for it with all my heart, and take a good leave of you, and wherever I am, as long as I live, I shall pray God for you." The Cardinal, angry, said in his rage: "Go where you like, because you cannot do a man good by force." Certain of those loaf-eating courtiers of his said: "This fellow thinks himself something great, because he refuses three hundred ducats of income." Others, of those men of talent, said: "The King will never find another like him; and this Cardinal of ours wants to bargain for him as if he were a load of firewood." This was Messer Luigi Alamanni, who I was told said this. This was in the Dauphiné, at a castle whose name I do not remember: and it was the last day of October.

Chapter 11

Having left the Cardinal, I went off to my lodging three miles away from there, together with a secretary of the Cardinal's who was coming to the same lodging. All that journey that secretary never left off asking me what I meant to do with myself, and what my fancy would have been about the salary I wanted. I never answered him a single word, except to say: "I knew the whole of it already." Then, having got to the lodging, I found Pagolo and Ascanio who were staying there; and seeing me thoroughly upset, they forced me to tell them what I had; and seeing the poor young men dismayed, I said to them: "Tomorrow morning I will give you money enough for you to be able to get back to your homes comfortably; and I shall go about a most important business of my own, without you; which I have had it in mind to do for a long time." Our room was wall to wall with that of the said secretary, and it is possible that he wrote to the Cardinal everything I had it in mind to do; although I never knew anything about it.

The night passed without my sleeping at all: it seemed a thousand years to me until day came, so that I could follow out the resolution I had made about myself. When the dawn of day came, having given orders for the horses and got myself quickly ready, I gave those two young men everything I had brought with me, and fifty gold ducats besides: and I kept as much again for myself, and besides that the diamond the Duke had given me; I took only two shirts and certain not too good riding clothes, which I had on my back. I could not get away from the two young men, who wanted to come with me in any case; on account of which I greatly belittled them, saying to them: "One of you has his first beard and the other is beginning to get his little by little, and you have learned from me so much of this poor skill that I have been able to teach you that you are today the first young men in Italy; and are you not ashamed that you have not the heart to get out of your daddy's go-cart, which has always carried you? This is surely a mean thing! And if I let you go without money, what would you say? Now get out of my sight, and God bless you a thousand times: goodbye." I turned my horse and left them weeping.

I took a very fine road through a wood, so as to get forty miles away at the least that day, to the most unknown place I could think of. And I had already got about two miles off; and in that little journey I had made up my mind never again to practise in any place where I was known, nor ever to make any other work than a Christ three braccia high, coming as near as I could to that infinite beauty which had been shown me by Himself. Having quite made up my mind, I was going off towards the Sepulchre. Thinking I had got so far off that nobody could find me any more, at this point I heard horses running behind me; and they made me somewhat suspicious, because in those parts there is a certain breed of company, who are called adventurers, who gladly rob people on the road; and although every day a good many of them are hanged, they hardly seem to care about it. When these men had come nearer me, I saw that they were a messenger of the King's, together with that young man of mine Ascanio; and coming up to me he said: "On the King's behalf I tell you to come to him at once." To which man I said: "You come on the Cardinal's behalf; and for that reason I do not want to come." The man said that since I would not come willingly, he had authority to command the country people, who would bring me bound like a prisoner. Ascanio too, as much as he could, begged me, reminding me that when the King put a man in prison, he took five years at the least afterwards to make up his mind to get him out. This word "prison", reminding me of the one in Rome, gave me such a fright that I quickly turned my horse where the King's messenger told me. He, always muttering in French, never left off all that journey until he had brought me to the Court: now he blustered at me, now he said one thing, now another, enough to make me renounce the world.

Chapter 12

When we had got to the King's lodgings, we passed in front of those of the Cardinal of Ferrara. The Cardinal being at the door, he called me to him and said: "Our Most Christian King of his own accord has settled on you the same salary that His Majesty used to give Leonardo da Vinci the painter: which is seven hundred scudi a year; and besides that he pays you for all the works you make for him; and moreover for your coming he gives you five hundred gold scudi, which he wants paid you before you leave here." When the Cardinal had finished speaking, I answered that these were offers worthy of the King he was. That messenger of the King's, not knowing who I was, seeing such great offers made me on the King's behalf, asked my pardon many times. Pagolo and Ascanio said: "God has helped us back into so honourable a go-cart."

Then the next day I went to thank the King, who charged me to make him the models of twelve silver statues, which he wanted to serve as twelve candlesticks round his table: and he wanted six gods and six goddesses figured, of exactly the size of His Majesty, who was very little short of four braccia tall. When he had given me this commission, he turned to the treasurer of savings and asked him whether he had paid me the five hundred scudi. He said that nothing had been said to him about it. The King took it very badly, for he had ordered the Cardinal to tell him. He also told me to go to Paris, and to look for a workshop that would suit for making such works, because he would have it given to me.

I took the five hundred gold scudi and went off to Paris to a lodging of the Cardinal of Ferrara's; and there I began, in the name of God, to work, and made four small models, of two thirds of a braccio each, in wax: Jupiter, Juno, Apollo, Vulcan. Meanwhile the King came to Paris; on account of which I went at once to find him, and took the said models with me, together with those two young men of mine, that is Ascanio and Pagolo. When I saw that the King was satisfied with the said models, he charged me first of all to make him the Jupiter in silver of the said height. I explained to His Majesty that I had brought those two young men from Italy for His Majesty's service; and because I had brought them up myself, I should for these beginnings get much better help from them than from the men of the city of Paris. At this the King said that I should settle for the said two young men whatever salary seemed right to me, which should be enough for them to keep themselves. I said that a hundred gold scudi each would do well, and that I would certainly make them earn such a salary. So we agreed.

I also said that I had found a place which seemed to me very suitable for making such works in; the said place belonged to His Majesty in particular, called the Petit Nesle, and at that time it was held by the provost of Paris, to whom His Majesty had given it; but because this provost did not use it, His Majesty could give it to me, who would use it for his service. The King said at once: "That place is my house; and I know very well that the man I gave it to does not live in it, and does not use it; so you shall use it for our business;" and at once he ordered his lieutenant to put me into the said Nesle. The lieutenant made some resistance, telling the King that he could not do it. To this the King answered in anger that he meant to give his own things to whom he pleased and to a man who served him, because this other man was of no use to him at all: so he should say no more to him about the matter. The lieutenant added further that it would be necessary to use a little force. To whom the King said: "Go now, and if a little force is not enough, put in a great deal of it."

He took me at once to the place and did have to use force to put me in possession: then he told me to take very good care not to get killed there. I went in, and at once took on servants, and bought several great weapons on poles, and for several days I lived in the greatest unpleasantness; because this was a great gentleman of Paris, and the other gentlemen were all my enemies, so that they did me so many insults that I could not stand up to it. I do not want to leave out that at this time when I entered His Majesty's service the year was exactly 1540, which was exactly my fortieth year.

Book Two, Chapters 13 to 18

Chapter 13

Because of these great insults I went back to the King, begging His Majesty to settle me somewhere else: at which words the King said to me: "Who are you, and what is your name?" I was left quite bewildered and did not know what the King meant; and as I stayed quiet like that, the King repeated the same words a second time, almost angrily. Then I answered that my name was Benvenuto. The King said: "Then if you are that Benvenuto I have heard of, act after your own custom, for I give you full leave to do it." I told His Majesty that all I needed was to keep myself in his favour; for the rest I saw nothing that could hurt me. The King, laughing a little, said: "Go then, and my favour shall never fail you."

At once he gave orders to one of his chief secretaries, who was called Monsignor de Villeroy, to give orders for me to be provided for and fitted out with all I needed. This Villeroy was a very great friend of that gentleman called the provost, whose the said place of Nesle was. This place was in the shape of a triangle, and was attached to the city walls, and was an old castle, but no guard was kept in it: it was of a good size. This Monsignor de Villeroy advised me to look for something else, and to give it up in any case; because the man whose it was, was a man of the greatest power, and he would most certainly have me killed. To whom I answered that I had come from Italy to France only to serve that marvellous King, and as for dying, I knew for certain that I had to die; and a little sooner or a little later did not trouble me in the world.

This Villeroy was a man of the greatest spirit, and admirable in everything he did, and enormously rich: there is nothing in the world he would not have done to do me a mischief, but he showed nothing of it; he was a serious person, of handsome appearance, and spoke slowly. He gave charge of the matter to another gentleman, who was called Monsignor de Marmagne, who was treasurer of Languedoc. The first thing this man did was to look out the best rooms of that place and have them fitted up for himself: to whom I said that the King had given me that place so that I might serve him, and that I did not want anybody living there except me and my servants. This man was proud, bold and spirited; and he told me that he meant to do whatever he pleased, and that I was banging my head against a wall in wanting to stand up against him; and that everything he was doing, he had orders from Villeroy that he might do it. Then I said that I had orders from the King, and that neither he nor Villeroy could do such a thing. When I said this, this proud man said to me in his French tongue many foul words, to which I answered in my own tongue that he lied. Moved by anger, he made as if to lay his hand on a little dagger of his; on account of which I laid my hand on a great dagger of mine, which I always carried at my side for my defence, and said to him: "If you are bold enough to draw that weapon, I will kill you on the spot." He had two servants with him, and I had my two young men: and while the said Marmagne stood there uncertain, not knowing what to do, but rather inclined to mischief, he said, muttering: "I will never put up with such a thing." I saw the business going the bad way; at once I made up my mind and said to Pagolo and Ascanio: "As soon as you see me draw my dagger, throw yourselves on the two servants and kill them if you can: because I shall kill this man at the first blow; then we will go off with God together at once." When Marmagne heard this resolution, he thought he had done well enough to get out of that place alive. All these things, somewhat more modestly put, I wrote to the Cardinal of Ferrara, who at once told them to the King. The King in vexation gave me into the charge of another of those men of his, who was called Monsignor the Viscount of Orbec. This man, with as much pleasantness as can be imagined, provided me with everything I needed.

Chapter 14

When I had made all the fittings of the house and of the workshop, most conveniently arranged for serving me, and most honourably, for the services of the house, I at once set my hand to making three models, of exactly the size they were to be in silver: these were Jupiter and Vulcan and Mars. I made them of clay, very well armed with iron, and then I went to the King, who had given me, if I remember rightly, three hundred pounds of silver, so that I might begin work. While I was giving orders for these things, the little vase and the oval basin were being finished, which took several months. When I had finished them, I had them very well gilded. This seemed the most beautiful work that had ever been seen in France. I took it at once to the Cardinal of Ferrara, who thanked me a great deal; then without me he took it to the King and made him a present of it. The King held it very dear, and praised me more beyond measure than a man of my sort was ever praised; and for this present he gave the Cardinal of Ferrara an abbey of seven thousand scudi of income; and he meant to make me a present too. On account of which the Cardinal hindered it, telling His Majesty that he was doing it too soon, since I had not yet given him any work. And the King, who was most generous, said: "That is just why I want to give him courage, so that he can give me some." The Cardinal, ashamed at this, said: "Sire, I beg you to leave it to me; because I will make him a pension of three hundred scudi at the least, as soon as I have taken possession of the abbey." I never had them, and it would be far too long to tell all the devilries of this Cardinal; but I want to keep myself for things of greater importance.

Chapter 15

I went back to Paris. With so much favour done me by the King I was admired by everybody. I got the silver, and began the said statue of Jupiter. I took on many workmen, and with the greatest diligence day and night I never left off working; so that, having finished Jupiter, Vulcan and Mars in clay, and having already begun to carry the Jupiter in silver a good way forward, the workshop showed itself already very rich.

At this the King appeared in Paris: I went to visit him; and as soon as His Majesty saw me, he called me cheerfully and asked me whether there was anything fine at my house to show him, because he would come as far as there. To whom I recounted all I had done. At once the greatest wish came on him to come; and after his dinner he made arrangements with Madame d'Étampes, with the Cardinal of Lorraine, and certain others of those lords, such as the King of Navarre, brother-in-law of King Francis, and the Queen, sister of the said King Francis; the Dauphin came and the Dauphine; the fact is that on that day all the nobility of the Court came. I had gone off home, and had set myself to work. When the King appeared at the gate of my castle, hearing the beating of several hammers, he ordered everybody to be silent: in my house everybody was at work; so that I was taken by surprise by the King, whom I was not expecting. He came into my hall: and the first thing he saw, he saw me with a great plate of silver in my hand, which was to serve for the body of the Jupiter: another was making the head, another the legs, so that the noise was very great. While I was working, having a little French boy of mine about me, who had done me some little annoyance or other, on account of which I had given him a kick, and by my good luck, having caught him with my foot in the fork of his legs, I had driven him forward more than four braccia, so that at the King's entrance this boy fetched up against the King: at which the King laughed a great deal, and I was left much abashed. The King began to ask me what I was doing, and wanted me to work; then he told me that he would be much better pleased if I never tired myself, but rather took on as many men as I liked, and made them work: because he wanted me to keep myself healthy so as to be able to serve him longer. I answered His Majesty that I should fall ill at once if I did not work, nor would the works be of the kind "I wish to make for His Majesty". The King, thinking that what I said was said out of boasting, and not because it was the truth, made me say it over again to the Cardinal of Lorraine, to whom I set out my reasons so fully and so clearly that he was quite convinced by them: so he urged the King to let me work little or much, according to my own will.

Chapter 16

The King, left satisfied with my works, went back to his palace, and left me full of so many favours that it would take long to tell them. The next day, at his dinner, he sent to call me. The Cardinal of Ferrara was present, dining with him. When I got there, the King was still at the second course: coming up to His Majesty, he at once began to talk with me, saying that since he had so fine a basin and so fine a jug from my hand, he wanted a fine saltcellar to keep them company, and that he wanted me to make him a design for it; but he would like to see it soon. Then I added: "Your Majesty shall see such a design much sooner than you ask of me; because while I was making the basin I thought that a saltcellar ought to be made to keep it company," and that such a thing was already made; and that if he liked, I would show it to him at once. The King started up with great eagerness, and turning to those lords, who were the King of Navarre, the Cardinal of Lorraine and the Cardinal of Ferrara, he said: "This is truly a man to be loved and desired by every man who does not know him;" then he said to me that he would gladly see that design I had made on such a subject.

I set off, and quickly went and came back, because I only had to cross the river, that is the Seine: I brought with me a wax model, which I had already made at the request of the Cardinal of Ferrara in Rome. When I got to the King and had uncovered the model to him, the King, wondering, said: "This is a hundred times more divine a thing than I should ever have thought. This man is a great matter! He ought never to rest." Then he turned to me with a very happy face, and told me that it was a work which pleased him greatly, and that he wanted me to make it for him in gold. The Cardinal of Ferrara, who was present, looked me in the face and made me a sign, as one who recognized that this was the model I had made for him in Rome. At this I said that I had already said I would make that work for whoever was to have it. The Cardinal, remembering those same words, almost in indignation, thinking I had wanted to take my revenge, said to the King: "Sire, this is a very great work, and so I should suspect nothing except that I should never believe I should see it finished; because these able men, who have those great conceits of this art, gladly make a beginning of them, without considering well when they are to have an end. So, if I were having works of this kind made, I should want to know when I was to have them." To this the King answered, saying that whoever looked so narrowly for the end of works would never begin any of them; and he said it in a certain way, showing that works of that kind were not matter for men of little spirit. Then I said: "All princes who give their servants courage, in the way His Majesty does and says, make every great undertaking easy; and since God has given me such a marvellous master, I hope to give him a great many great and marvellous works finished." "And I believe it," said the King; and he got up from the table.

He called me into his room and asked me how much gold was needed for that saltcellar: "A thousand scudi," I said. At once the King called a treasurer of his, who was called Monsignor the Viscount of Orbec, and told him to provide me that very moment with a thousand old scudi of good weight, in gold. When we had left His Majesty, I sent to call those two clerks who had had the silver given me for the Jupiter and many other things, and having crossed the Seine, I took a very small basket which a cousin of mine, a nun, had given me on my way through Florence, and for my good luck I took that basket and not a bag: and thinking to get the business done by daylight, because it was still a good hour, and not wanting to draw off the workmen; and neither did I care to take a servant with me.

I got to the treasurer's house, and he already had the money before him, and was picking it out as the King had told him. As far as I could see, that thieving treasurer artfully delayed until three hours into the night in counting me out the said money. I, who was not lacking in caution, sent to call several of those workmen of mine to come and keep me company, because it was a matter of great importance. Seeing that they did not come, I asked the messenger whether he had done my errand. A certain thievish little servant said he had done it, and that they had said they could not come; but that he would willingly carry that money for me: to whom I said that I meant to carry the money myself. Meanwhile the receipt was made out, the money counted and everything done. Having put it in the said little basket, I then put my arm through the two handles; and because it went in with great difficulty, they were well closed, and I carried it more conveniently than if it had been a bag. I was well armed with mail and sleeves, and with my little sword and dagger at my side I quickly put the road between my legs.

Chapter 17

At that moment I saw certain servants who, whispering, quickly left the house too, making as if to go by another way than the one I was going by. I, walking briskly, having passed the Pont au Change, was coming along a little wall by the river, which brought me home to Nesle. When I was exactly at the Augustinians', a most dangerous place, although only five hundred paces from my house; the dwelling part of the castle being about as far again inside, my voice would not have been heard if I had set about calling out, but making up my mind in a moment when I saw four men with four swords out upon me, I quickly covered that little basket with my cloak, and laying my hand on my sword, seeing that they were closing on me briskly, I said: "From soldiers there is nothing to be got but a cloak and a sword; and this one, before I give it to you, I hope you will have it with little profit to yourselves." And fighting against them spiritedly, several times I opened my cloak, so that, if they were of those put up to it by those servants who had seen me take the money, they might have some reason to judge that I had no such sum of money on me. The fight lasted a short time, because little by little they drew back; and they said among themselves in their own tongue: "This is a brave Italian, and certainly he is not the man we were looking for; or truly, if it is he, he has nothing on him." I was speaking Italian, and I kept driving at them with thrusts and downward strokes, sometimes very close to their bodies; and because I have handled weapons very well, they judged rather that I was a soldier than anything else; and closing up together, little by little they drew away from me, always muttering under their breath in their own tongue; and I too kept saying, modestly enough, that whoever wanted my weapons and my cloak would not have them without trouble.

I began to hurry my pace, and they always came after me at a slow pace; on account of which my fear grew, thinking I might fall into some ambush of several others of the same kind, who would have put me in the middle; so that when I was within a hundred paces, I set off at a full run and shouted at the top of my voice: "To arms, to arms, out, out, for I am being murdered." At once four young men ran up with four weapons on poles: and when they wanted to follow those men, whom they could still see, I stopped them, saying, still loudly: "Those four cowards have not known how, against one man alone, to make a booty of a thousand gold scudi in gold, which have broken my arm with the weight of them; so let us first go and put them away, and then I will keep you company with my two-handed sword wherever you like."

We went to put the money away; and those young men of mine, grieving greatly over the great danger I had run, as though scolding me, said: "You trust too much in yourself, and one day you will make us all weep." I said a good many things; and they answered me back too; my adversaries had run off; and we all, merry and cheerful, had supper, laughing at those great squeezes fortune gives, as much in good as in bad; and when she misses, it is as if nothing had happened at all. It is quite true that people say: "You will learn for another time." That is worth nothing, because she always comes with different ways and never with ones you have imagined.

Chapter 18

The next morning I at once made a beginning on the great saltcellar, and with diligence had it and the other works carried forward. By now I had taken on a great many workmen, both for the art of sculpture and for that of goldsmith's work. These workmen were Italians, French and Germans, and sometimes I had a good number of them, according as I found good ones; because from day to day I changed them, taking those who knew most, and I urged them on so that, from the continual toil (seeing what I did, whose constitution served me a little better than theirs, and being unable to stand up to the great labours, thinking to refresh themselves with plenty of drinking and eating), some of those Germans, who knew better than the others, in wanting to keep up with me, found that their nature would not bear such abuse, and it killed them.

While I was carrying the Jupiter in silver forward, seeing that I had a good deal of silver over, I set my hand, without the King's knowing, to making a large vase with two handles, of about a braccio and a half in height. I also had a wish to cast in bronze that great model I had made for the silver Jupiter; and setting my hand to this new undertaking, which I had never done before, and having talked it over with certain of those old master men of Paris, I told them all the ways we in Italy were used to carry out such an undertaking. These men told me that they had never gone by that road, but that if I let them do it in their own ways, they would give it me cast as clean and as fine as the clay one was. I chose to make a bargain, giving this work over to them: and on top of the price they had asked me, I promised them several scudi more. They set their hands to the undertaking; and seeing that they were not taking the right road, I quickly began a head of Julius Caesar, with his breast, armed, much larger than life, which I copied from a small model I had brought from Rome, taken from a most marvellous antique head. I also set my hand to another head of the same size, which I copied from a very beautiful girl whom I kept about me for my carnal pleasure. To this one I gave the name Fontainebleau, which was the place the King had chosen for his own particular delight.

Having made a very fine little furnace for melting the bronze, and got ready and baked our moulds, they the Jupiter and I my two heads, I said to them: "I do not believe your Jupiter will come out, because you have not given it enough vents underneath for the air to circulate; so you are wasting your time." These men told me that if their work did not come out, they would give me back all the money I had given them on account, and would make good the whole of the lost expense; but that I should look out well, for those fine heads of mine, which I meant to cast in my Italian way, would never come out for me. Present at this argument were those treasurers and other gentlemen, who came to see me by the King's orders; and everything that was said and done, they reported it all to the King.

These two old fellows, who wanted to cast the Jupiter, held back a while from giving the order for the pouring; because they said they would have liked to fix those two moulds of my heads; because that way I was doing it, it was not possible they should come out, and it was a great pity to lose such fine works. When this was made known to the King, His Majesty answered that they should attend to learning and not try to teach the master. These men with great laughter put their work in the pit; and I, steady, without any show either of laughter or of anger — which I had — put my two moulds in the middle of the Jupiter: and when our metal was very well melted, with the greatest pleasure we gave the said metal its way, and the mould of the Jupiter filled very well; at the same time the mould of my two heads filled: so that they were glad and I was content; because I was very pleased to have told lies about their work, and they showed themselves very pleased to have told lies about mine. They asked, in the French way, with great cheerfulness for a drink: I very willingly had a rich collation made them. Then they asked me for the money they were to have, and those extra ones I had promised them. At this I said: "You have been laughing over something which I am much afraid you may have to weep over; because I have considered that into that mould of yours far more metal has gone than it ought; so I do not want to give you any more money than what you have had, until tomorrow morning."

These poor men began to consider what I had said to them, and without saying anything they went off home. When morning came, very quietly they began to dig out of the pit; and because they could not uncover their great mould without first getting out those two heads of mine, they got them out and they were very good, and they had set them up on end, so that they could be well seen. Then, when they began to uncover the Jupiter, they had not gone two braccia down when they and four of their workmen set up such a great shout that I heard it. Thinking it was a shout of gladness, I set off running, for I was in my room more than five hundred paces away. I got to them and found them in the attitude given to those who watched the sepulchre of Christ, afflicted and terrified. I cast my eyes on my two heads, and seeing that they were good, I balanced my pleasure against my displeasure: and they excused themselves, saying: "Our bad fortune!" At which words I said: "Your fortune has been very good, but what has certainly been bad is your want of knowledge. If I had seen you put the core into the mould, with a single word I would have taught you so that the figure would have come out very well; on account of which the greatest honour would have come to me and much profit to you: but I shall be excused about my honour, whereas you have no escape either as to honour or as to profit: so another time learn to work and do not learn to make fools of people."

Still they commended themselves to me, telling me that I was right, and that if I did not help them, having to pay that heavy expense and that loss, they would go begging together with their families. At this I said that if the King's treasurers wanted to make them pay what they had bound themselves to, I promised them to pay it out of my own pocket, because I had truly seen that they had done with a good heart everything they knew. These things gained me so much goodwill with those treasurers and with those officers of the King that it was beyond reckoning. The whole of it was written to the King, who, matchless and most generous, ordered that everything I said should be done.

Book Two, Chapters 19 to 26

Chapter 19

At this point the most marvellous and brave Piero Strozzi had arrived; and having reminded the King about his letters of naturalization, the King at once ordered that they should be made out. "And together with them," he said, "make out those for Benvenuto, mon ami, and take them at once on my behalf to his house, and give them to him without any expense." Those of the great Piero Strozzi cost him many hundreds of ducats; mine were brought me by one of his chief secretaries, who was called Messer Antonio Le Maçon. This gentleman handed me the letters with a marvellous show of ceremony on His Majesty's behalf, saying: "The King makes you a present of these, so that you may serve him with the greater courage. These are letters of naturalization;" and he told me how, after a long time and with many favours, he had given them to Piero Strozzi at his own request, and that these he was sending to present to me of his own accord: that such a favour had never been done before in that kingdom. At these words I thanked the King with great demonstrations; then I begged the said secretary to be so good as to tell me what those "letters of naturalization" meant. This secretary was very cultivated and courteous, and spoke Italian very well: first breaking into great laughter, then recovering his gravity, he told me in my own tongue, that is in Italian, what "letters of naturalization" meant, which was one of the greatest dignities given to a foreigner; and he said: "This is a greater thing than being made a Venetian gentleman." Having left me, when he got back to the King, he reported the whole of it to His Majesty, who laughed a while, then said: "Now I want him to know why I have sent him letters of naturalization. Go, and make him lord of the castle of the Petit Nesle which he lives in, which is mine by inheritance. He will find out what this is much more easily than he found out what letters of naturalization were." A messenger came to me with the said present, on account of which I wanted to show him a courtesy: he would not accept anything, saying that such were His Majesty's orders. The said letters of naturalization, together with those for the gift of the castle, when I came to Italy I brought with me; and wherever I go, and wherever I end my life, there I shall take care to have them.

Chapter 20

Now I go on with the account of my life that I had begun. Having in hand the works spoken of above, that is the silver Jupiter, already begun, the said gold saltcellar, the great silver vase spoken of, the two bronze heads, we worked on these works diligently. I also gave orders for the casting of the base of the said Jupiter, which I made of bronze most richly, full of ornaments, among which ornaments I carved in low relief the rape of Ganymede; and on the other side Leda and the swan: this I cast in bronze, and it came out very well. I also made another like it to put the statue of Juno on, waiting to begin this one too, if the King gave me the silver to be able to make such a thing.

Working diligently, I had already put the silver Jupiter together; I had also put the gold saltcellar together; the vase was far advanced; the two bronze heads were already finished. I had also made several small works for the Cardinal of Ferrara; besides that I had made a little silver vase, richly worked, to give as a present to Madame d'Étampes; for many Italian lords, that is Signor Piero Strozzi, the Count of Anguillara, the Count of Pitigliano, the Count of Mirandola and many others, I had made a great many works.

To come back to my great King, as I have said, having carried these works of his very well forward, at this time he came back to Paris, and on the third day he came to my house with a great number of the greatest nobility of his Court, and he wondered greatly at the many works I had in hand and had brought to so good a point; and because his Madame d'Étampes was with him, they began to talk about Fontainebleau. Madame d'Étampes said to His Majesty that he ought to have me make something beautiful for the ornament of his Fontainebleau. At once the King said: "What you say is well said, and this very minute I mean to make up my mind that something beautiful shall be made there;" and turning to me, he began to ask me what seemed to me the thing to make for that beautiful fountain. At this I put forward some fancies of my own: His Majesty too said what he thought; then he told me that he wanted to go for a change of air for fifteen or twenty days to Saint-Germain-en-Laye, which was twelve leagues from Paris; and that in that time I should make a model for this beautiful fountain of his with the richest inventions I knew, because that place was the greatest recreation he had in his kingdom; so he ordered and begged me to do my best to make something beautiful: and I promised him as much.

The King, seeing so many works in hand, said to Madame d'Étampes: "I have never had a man of this profession who pleased me more, nor who deserved more to be rewarded than this one; so we must think of fixing him here. Because he spends a great deal, and is a good companion and works a great deal, we must of ourselves remember him: for consider, Madame, how many times he has come to me, and how often I have come here, and he has never asked for anything: his heart is plainly wholly intent on his works; and we must do him some good soon, so that we do not lose him." Madame d'Étampes said: "I will remind you of it." They went off: I set myself with great diligence to the works I had begun, and moreover set my hand to the model of the fountain and carried it forward diligently.

Chapter 21

Within a month and a half the King came back to Paris; and I, who had been working day and night, went to find him, and took my model with me, so beautifully sketched out that it was plainly understood. The devilries of the war between the Emperor and himself had already begun again, so that I found him much distracted; still I spoke with the Cardinal of Ferrara, telling him that I had with me certain models which His Majesty had ordered me to make: so I begged him, if he saw a time to put in a word so that these models might be shown, "I believe the King would take great pleasure in them." The Cardinal did so much; he put the said models before the King; at once the King came to where I had the models.

First of all I had made the door of the palace of Fontainebleau: so as to alter as little as I could the order of the door that was already made at the said palace, which was large and squat, in that bad French manner of theirs, in which the opening was little more than a square, and above that square a flattened half circle in the fashion of a basket handle: in this half circle the King wanted to have a figure which should represent Fontainebleau. I gave the said opening very beautiful proportions; then I put over the said opening a true half circle; and at the sides I made certain pleasing projections, under which in the lower part, which came to correspond to the one above, I put a plinth; and as much above; and instead of two columns, which the mouldings made below and above showed to be required, I had made a satyr in each of the places of the columns. This was more than half relief, and with one of his arms he seemed to hold up that part which belongs to the columns: in the other arm he had a heavy club, with his head bold and fierce, which showed terror to those who looked at it. The other figure was like it in posture, but was different and various in the head and in certain other such things: it had in its hand a scourge with three balls fixed to it by certain chains. Although I say satyrs, these had nothing of the satyr except certain little horns and the goatish head; all the rest was human form. In the half circle I had made a woman lying in a beautiful attitude: she held her left arm over the neck of a stag, which was one of the King's devices: on one side I had made in half relief little roe deer, and certain wild boars and other game in lower relief; on the other side hounds and greyhounds of several sorts, because that is what that most beautiful wood produces, where the fountain rises. I had then enclosed the whole of this work in an oblong square, and in the upper corners of the square, in each of them, I had made a Victory in low relief, with those little torches in their hands, as the ancients used. Above the said square I had made the salamander, the King's own device, with many other most pleasing ornaments suited to the said work, which showed itself to be of the Ionic order.

Chapter 22

When the King saw this model, it at once cheered him up, and turned him aside from those tiresome discussions he had been in for more than two hours. Seeing him cheerful as I wanted, I uncovered the other model to him, which he was not expecting at all, thinking he had seen work enough in the first. This model was more than two braccia large, in which I had made a fountain in the form of a perfect square, with very beautiful stairways round it, which crossed one into the other: a thing which had never been seen in those parts, and is very rare in these. In the middle of the said fountain I had made a solid block, which stood a little higher than the said basin of the fountain: on this block I had made, to correspond with it, a naked figure of very beautiful grace. This one held a broken lance in his right hand raised on high, and his left hand he held on the hilt of a curved sword of his of a very beautiful form: he rested on his left foot and held the right on a helmet as richly worked as can be imagined; and on the four corners of the fountain I had made, on each one, a seated figure raised up, with many charming devices for each of them.

The King began to ask me to tell him what fine fancy this was that I had made, telling me that everything I had done at the door he had understood without asking me anything, but that this of the fountain, although it seemed to him most beautiful, he understood nothing of; and he well knew that I had not done as the other fools do, who, even when they make things with some little grace, make them without any meaning at all. At this I set myself in order; for having pleased him with my doing, I very much wanted my speaking to please him as much. "Know, sacred Majesty, that this whole small work is very exactly measured in small feet, so that when it is afterwards carried out, it will come out with this same grace you see. That figure in the middle is fifty-four feet" (at this word the King made a great show of wonder); "besides, it is made to figure the god Mars. These other four figures are made for the Virtues in which Your Majesty takes such delight and which you so favour: this one on the right hand is figured for the knowledge of all Letters: you see that she has her tokens, which show Philosophy with all her companion virtues. This other shows herself to be the whole Art of design, that is Sculpture, Painting and Architecture. This other is figured for Music, which is a fit companion for all these sciences. This other, who shows herself so gracious and kindly, is figured for Liberality; for without her none of these admirable Virtues that God shows us can be displayed. This great statue in the middle is figured for Your Majesty yourself, who are a god Mars, for you alone are brave in the world; and this bravery you use justly and holily in defence of your glory."

He hardly had patience enough to let me finish speaking before, raising his voice loudly, he said: "Truly I have found a man after my own heart;" and he called the treasurers appointed to me, and told them to provide me with everything I needed, and let it be as great an expense as it liked: then he clapped me on the shoulder with his hand, saying to me: "Mon ami" (which means "my friend"), "I do not know which is the greater pleasure, that of a prince who has found a man after his own heart, or that of the man of talent who has found a prince who gives him such conveniences that he can express his great and skilful conceptions." I answered that if I was the man His Majesty said, my luck had been much the greater. He answered, laughing: "Let us say it is equal." I went off in great joy, and went back to my works.

Chapter 23

My bad fortune willed that I was not warned to make as much of a play to Madame d'Étampes, who, having heard in the evening all these things that had happened from the King's own mouth, it bred such venomous rage in her breast that in scorn she said: "If Benvenuto had shown me his fine works, he would have given me cause to remember him at the right time." The King tried to excuse me, and nothing took hold. When I heard of this, a fortnight later — for having gone round through Normandy to Rouen and to Dieppe, they had then come back to Saint-Germain-en-Laye mentioned above — I took that fine little vase which I had made at the request of the said Madame d'Étampes, thinking that by giving it to her I ought to win back her favour. So I took it with me; and having let her know by a nurse of hers, and having shown the said nurse the fine vase I had made for her Lady, and how I wanted to give it to her, the said nurse made an immense fuss of me, and told me that she would say a word to Madame, who was not yet dressed, and that as soon as she had told her, she would put me in. The nurse told the whole of it to Madame, who answered scornfully: "Tell him to wait."

Hearing this, I clothed myself in patience, which is the hardest thing in the world for me; still I had patience until after her dinner: and then, seeing the hour late, hunger caused me such anger that, not being able to hold out any longer, and having devoutly wished a plague on her heart, I left the place and went off to find the Cardinal of Lorraine, and made him a present of the said vase, only commending myself to him that he should keep me in the King's good graces. He said there was no need, and that when there was need, he would gladly do it: then, calling a treasurer of his, he spoke to him in his ear. The said treasurer waited until I had left the Cardinal's presence; then he said to me: "Benvenuto, come with me, and I will give you a glass of good wine to drink." To whom I said, not knowing what he meant: "For pity's sake, Monsignor Treasurer, have a single glass of wine and a mouthful of bread given me, because truly I am fainting away, for I have been from early this morning until this hour you see, fasting, at the door of Madame d'Étampes, to give her that fine little vase of gilt silver, and I let her know all about it, and she, always to torment me, sent to tell me to wait. Now hunger has come on me, and I felt myself failing; and, as God has willed it, I have given my goods and my labours to somebody who deserved them much better, and I ask you for nothing but a little to drink; for being somewhat too choleric, fasting affects me in such a way that it would make me fall to the ground in a faint."

In the time it took me to say these words, there had appeared marvellous wine and other pleasant things for a collation, so that I refreshed myself very well: and having got my vital spirits back, my temper had left me. The good treasurer handed me a hundred gold scudi; which I resisted, not wanting them on any account. He went and reported it to the Cardinal; who, after abusing him roundly, ordered him to make me take them by force, and not to come before him again otherwise. The treasurer came to me in vexation, saying that he had never been shouted at by the Cardinal before; and wanting to give them to me, when I made a little resistance, he told me very crossly that he would have them taken by force. I took the money. When I wanted to go and thank the Cardinal, he let me know by a secretary of his that whenever he could do me a kindness, he would do it with a good heart: I went back to Paris that same evening.

The King heard the whole of it. They made a joke of Madame d'Étampes, which was the cause of her becoming still more envenomed against me, on account of which I was in great danger of my life, as shall be told in its place.

Chapter 24

I ought to have remembered much sooner the friendship I had gained of the most talented, the most affectionate and the most companionable honest man I ever knew in the world: this was Messer Guido Guidi, an excellent physician and doctor and a noble citizen of Florence; because of the infinite troubles put before me by perverse fortune, I had rather left him a little behind. Although this does not matter much, I thought that having him continually in my heart was enough; but seeing afterwards that my Life does not go well without him, I have brought him in among these greatest troubles of mine, so that just as he was a comfort and a help to me then, here he may put me in mind of that good.

The said Messer Guido came to Paris; and having begun to know him, I took him to my castle, and there gave him a set of rooms free to himself; and so we enjoyed ourselves together several years. There also came the Bishop of Pavia, that is Monsignor de' Rossi, brother of the Count of San Secondo. This lord I took out of the inn and put into my castle, giving him too a free set of rooms, where he was very well accommodated with his servants and horses for a great many months. At another time too I put up Messer Luigi Alamanni with his sons for some months; so God gave me the grace to be able, even I, to do some kindness to men both great and gifted. With the said Messer Guido we enjoyed our friendship for as many years as I stayed there, often glorying together that we were learning something of our arts at the expense of that so great and marvellous prince, each of us in his own profession. I can truly say that whatever I am, and whatever good and beautiful things I have made, all of it has been by reason of that marvellous King: so I pick up the thread to talk about him and the great works I made for him.

Chapter 25

I had in this castle of mine a tennis court to play at with the cord, from which I got a good deal of profit while I had it kept up. There were in the said place some small rooms where various sorts of men lived, among whom was a very able printer of books: this man kept almost his whole shop inside my castle, and it was he who printed that first fine book of medicine for Messer Guido. Wanting to make use of those rooms, I sent him away, though with no small difficulty.

There was also a maker of saltpetre living there; and because I wanted to use these small rooms for certain good German workmen of mine, this said maker of saltpetre would not move out; and I had told him pleasantly several times that he should give me up my rooms, because I wanted to use them as lodgings for my workmen for the King's service. The more humbly I spoke, the more proudly this beast answered me: in the end I gave him three days as a term. He laughed at it, and told me that at the end of three years he would begin to think about it. I did not know that this man was a household servant of Madame d'Étampes: and if it had not been that this matter of Madame d'Étampes made me think a little more about things than I used to do, I would have sent him away at once; but I chose to have patience for those three days; and when they had passed, without saying anything more, I took Germans, Italians and Frenchmen with weapons in their hands, and many labourers I had; and in a short time I dismantled the whole house, and threw his goods out of my castle. And I did this somewhat harsh act because he had told me that he knew of no Italian's power bold enough to have shifted a link of his out of its place. So, after the deed, this man arrived; to whom I said: "I am the least of the Italians of Italy, and I have done nothing to you compared with what I should have the heart to do to you, and what I will do to you, if you say a single word;" with other insulting words I said to him.

This man, astonished and terrified, put his goods in order as best he could; then he ran to Madame d'Étampes and painted her a picture of hell; and that great enemy of mine, so much the greater as she was more eloquent and far more able, painted it to the King; who twice, I was told, meant to be angry with me and to give harsh orders against me; but because Henry the Dauphin his son, today King of France, had received some unpleasantness from that too bold woman, together with the Queen of Navarre, sister of King Francis, they favoured me with such spirit that the King turned the whole thing into a laugh: on account of which, with the true help of God, I got through a great storm.

Chapter 26

I had to do the same again with another man like this one, but I did not pull the house down; I did throw all his goods outside. On account of which Madame d'Étampes was bold enough to say to the King: "I believe this devil will sack Paris for you one day." At these words the King, angry, answered Madame d'Étampes, telling her that I did very well to defend myself from that rabble, who wanted to hinder me in his service.

The rage of this cruel woman grew greater every hour: she called to her a painter, who lodged at Fontainebleau, where the King stayed almost continually. This painter was an Italian and a Bolognese, and was known as Il Bologna: by his own name he was called Francesco Primaticcio. Madame d'Étampes told him that he ought to ask the King for that work of the Fountain, which His Majesty had decided on for me, and that she with all her power would help him in it: so they came to an agreement. This Bologna had the greatest joy he had ever had, and promised himself the thing for certain, although it was not his profession; but because he had quite a good hand at design, and was set up with certain workmen who had been formed under the discipline of Rosso, our Florentine painter, truly a most marvellously able man: and whatever this man did that was good, he had taken it from the admirable manner of the said Rosso, who was already dead.

Those cunning arguments could do so much, with the great help of Madame d'Étampes and with the continual hammering day and night, now Madame, now Bologna, at the ears of that great King. And what was the powerful cause of making him give way, was that she and Bologna in agreement said: "How is it possible, sacred Majesty, that when you want Benvenuto to make you twelve statues in silver, he has not yet finished one of them? And if you employ him on so great an undertaking, you must certainly deprive yourself of these others which you so much desire; because a hundred most able men could not finish so many great works as this able man has laid out. It is plain to see that he has a great will to do them; which will be the cause of Your Majesty losing at one stroke both him and the works." These with many other like words, finding the King in a mood for it, obtained everything they had asked him: and as yet neither designs nor models of anything had been shown from the hand of the said Bologna.

Book Two, Chapters 27 to 32

Chapter 27

At this same time there had been stirred up against me in Paris that second tenant whom I had driven out of my castle, and he had brought a lawsuit against me, saying that I had stolen a great quantity of his goods when I turned him out of the house. This lawsuit gave me the greatest trouble and took up so much of my time that several times I was ready to give up in despair and go off with God.

They have a custom in France of making a great capital out of a lawsuit which they bring against a foreigner or against any other person whom they see to be somewhat careless about litigating; and as soon as they begin to see some advantage in the said suit, they find people to sell it to; and some have given a suit as a dowry to certain men who follow this trade entirely, of buying lawsuits. They have another ugly thing, that the men of Normandy, almost the greater part of them, make a trade of giving false witness; so that these men who buy the suit at once instruct four of these witnesses or six, according to need, and by way of these, a man who is not warned to produce as many on the other side, one who does not know the custom, at once has sentence given against him.

And these same things happened to me: and it seeming to me a very dishonest business, I appeared in the great hall of Paris to defend my rights; where I saw a judge, the King's lieutenant for civil cases, raised up on a great tribunal. This man was tall, big and fat, and of the sternest aspect: he had round him on one side and the other many attorneys and advocates, all set in order on the right and on the left: others came, one at a time, and put a case before the said judge. Those advocates who were at the side, I saw them at times all speaking at once; whereat I was left wondering that this admirable man, a true likeness of Pluto, with a manner plain to see gave his ear now to this one and now to that, and answered them all skilfully. And because it has always delighted me to see and taste every sort of skill, this seemed to me so admirable that I would not for a great deal have missed seeing it.

It happened that, this hall being very large and full of a great quantity of people, they also took care that nobody should come in who had no business there, and they kept the door shut and a guard at the said door; which guard sometimes, in resisting somebody he did not want to let in, hindered with that great noise that marvellous judge, who in anger abused the said guard. And I several times happened on it, and considered the accident; and the very words I heard were these, which the judge himself said, catching sight of two gentlemen who were coming to look on; and this doorkeeper making the greatest resistance, the said judge said, shouting at the top of his voice: "Be quiet, be quiet, Satan, get away from there, and be quiet." These words in the French tongue sound like this: "Phe phe Satan phe phe Satan alè phe." I, who had learned the French tongue very well, hearing this phrase, remembered what Dante Alighieri meant when he entered with Publius Virgilius Maro his master inside the gates of Hell. For Dante in the time of Giotto the painter were together in France and above all in Paris, where for the said reasons the place where lawsuits are pleaded may be called a Hell: so Dante too, understanding the French tongue well, made use of that phrase; and it has seemed to me a great matter that it has never been understood as such; so that I say, and believe, that these commentators make him say things he never thought of.

Chapter 28

To come back to my own affairs, when I saw certain sentences being given against me by the hands of these advocates, and saw no way of being able to help myself, I turned for my help to a great dagger I had, because I have always taken pleasure in keeping fine weapons; and the first man I began to notch was that principal one who had brought the unjust suit against me; and one evening I gave him so many blows, taking care all the same not to kill him, in the legs and in the arms, that I deprived him of the use of both his legs. Then I found that other man who had bought the suit, and I touched him up in such a way too that the suit came to a stop.

Thanking God for this and for everything else always, and thinking that for the moment I should be a while without being molested, I told the young men of my household, especially the Italians, that for the love of God each of them should attend to his own business and help me for some time, so that I could finish the works I had begun, because I would soon finish them; and after that I meant to go back to Italy, not being able to put up with the villainies of those Frenchmen; and that if that good King ever grew angry with me, he would have me come to a bad end, since I had done so many things of that sort in my own defence.

These Italians were: the first and the dearest, Ascanio, from the kingdom of Naples, from a place called Tagliacozzo; the next was Pagolo, a Roman, a person born very humbly and whose father was not known: these two were the ones I had brought from Rome, who in the said Rome had lived with me. Another Roman, who had also come from Rome on purpose to find me, this one too was called by name Pagolo and was the son of a poor Roman gentleman of the family of the Macaroni: this young man did not know much about the art, but he was very brave with weapons. I had another, who was of Ferrara, and by name Bartolomeo Chioccia. I had yet another: this one was a Florentine and his name was Pagolo Miccieri. And because his brother, who was called by nickname Il Gatta, was able at accounts, but had spent too much in handling the property of Tommaso Guadagni, a very rich merchant, this Gatta set in order for me certain books in which I kept the accounts of the great Most Christian King and of others; this Pagolo Miccieri, having learned from his brother the way of these books of mine, kept them up for me, and I gave him a very good salary.

And because he seemed to me a very good young man, since I saw him devout, hearing him continually now muttering psalms, now with the rosary in his hand, I promised myself a great deal from his pretended goodness. Calling him aside alone, I said to him: "Pagolo, dearest brother; you see how well off you are with me, and you know that you had no start in life, and moreover you are a Florentine; on account of which I trust you more, seeing you so devout in the acts of religion, which is a thing that pleases me greatly. I beg you to help me, because I do not trust any of these others so much: so I beg you to take care of these two things above all for me, which would give me a great deal of trouble: the one is that you keep very good watch over my property, so that it is not taken from me, and so that you do not touch it yourself; and then, you see that poor girl Caterina, whom I keep chiefly for the service of my art, since without her I could not manage: besides, because I am a man, I have made use of her for my carnal pleasures, and it might be that she would give me a child; and because I do not want to bear the expense of other men's children, nor would I put up with such an injury being done me. If anybody in this house were bold enough to do such a thing, and I found it out, for certain I believe I would kill both her and him. So I beg you, dear brother, to help me; and if you see anything, tell me at once, because I will send her and her mother and whoever went after such a thing to the gallows: so be the first to keep yourself from it."

This rascal crossed himself with a sign that went from his head to his feet, and said: "Oh blessed Jesus! God keep me from ever thinking of such a thing! first, because I am not given to those filthy doings; and then, do you not think I know the great good I have from you?" At these words, seeing them said to me in a simple and affectionate way, I believed that things stood exactly as he said.

Chapter 29

Then two days later, a holiday coming, Messer Mattia del Nazaro, also an Italian and a servant of the King's, a most able man in the same profession, had invited me with those young men of mine to enjoy ourselves in a garden. On account of which I got ready, and told Pagolo too that he ought to come out for a change and enjoy himself, since it seemed to me that I had somewhat quieted down that tiresome lawsuit. This young man answered me, saying: "Truly it would be a great mistake to leave the house so empty: see how much gold, silver and jewels you have here. Being in a city of thieves as we are, we must be on guard by day as by night: I will attend to saying certain prayers of mine, while I keep watch on the house; go with an easy mind to enjoy yourself and have a good time: another time somebody else will do this office."

Thinking I was going with an easy mind, together with Pagolo, Ascanio and Chioccia we went off to the said garden to enjoy ourselves, and passed a good part of that day cheerfully.
