When it began to draw on towards evening, past midday the humour took me, and I began to think about those words that wretch had said to me with pretended simplicity; I got on my horse and with two servants of mine went back to my castle; where I found Pagolo and that wretched Caterina all but in the act; because as soon as I arrived, the French bawd of a mother said in a loud voice: "Pagolo, Caterina, the master is here." Seeing the two of them come, terrified and caught out, all in confusion in front of me, not knowing what they were saying, and, like stupefied creatures, where they were going, their committed sin was plainly to be seen. On account of which, reason being overcome by anger, I laid my hand on my sword, resolved to kill them both. The one ran off, the other threw herself on the ground on her knees, and cried out for all the mercies of heaven.

I, who would rather have struck the man first, not being able to reach him at the first blow, when afterwards I had caught up with him had by then taken counsel with myself: the best thing for me was to drive them both away; because with so many other things that had happened close to this one, I should have had difficulty in saving my life. So I said to Pagolo: "If my eyes had seen what you, you rascal, make me believe, I would run this sword through your guts ten times over: now get out of my sight, and if you ever said the Our Father, know that this is Saint Julian's day for you." Then I drove the mother and the daughter out with shoves, kicks and blows of my fists.

They thought of avenging themselves for this injury, and having talked it over with a Norman advocate, he taught them that she should say that I had used her in the Italian fashion; which fashion was understood to be against nature, that is in sodomy; saying: "At the very least, as soon as this Italian hears of such a thing, and knows how great a danger it is for him, he will at once give you several hundred ducats, so that you shall not speak of it, considering the heavy punishment that is inflicted in France for that sin." So they came to an agreement: they laid the charge against me, and I was summoned.

Chapter 30

The more I looked for rest, the more tribulations showed themselves to me. Injured by fortune every day in different ways, I began to think which of the two things I ought to do: either go off with God and leave France in its own bad hour; or truly fight this battle too and see for what end God had created me. I had tormented myself a great while over this thing; in the end I took the resolution to go off with God, so as not to tempt my perverse fortune so far that she should make me break my neck. When I was disposed to it altogether and entirely, and had made a move to find a quick place for those goods I could not take with me, and to fit the more portable ones as best I could on my own back and my servants', still it was with very heavy unhappiness that I was making such a departure.

I had stayed alone in a little study of mine; because to those young men of mine, who had urged me that I ought to go off with God, I said that it was well that I should take counsel a little by myself; although I well knew that they were largely telling the truth; because once I was out of prison and had given a little room to this fury, I could much better excuse myself to the King, telling him in letters of this attempt to murder my name, made on me only out of envy. And as I have said, I had made up my mind to do so; and having made a move, I was taken by one shoulder and turned round, and a voice said spiritedly: "Benvenuto, do as you are used to do, and have no fear."

At once taking the opposite counsel to what I had settled, I said to those Italian young men of mine: "Take good weapons and come with me, and obey what I tell you, and think of nothing else, because I mean to appear. If I went away, the next day you would all go up in smoke; so obey and come with me." All those young men said in agreement: "Since we are here and live on his bread, we must go with him and help him as long as there is life in us in whatever he proposes; because he has spoken more truly than we thought. As soon as he was out of this place, his enemies would have us all sent away. Let us consider well the great works that are begun here, and how great is their importance: we should not have the heart to finish them without him, and his enemies would say that he had gone off because he had not the heart to finish undertakings of that kind." They said a great many words besides these, of importance. That young Roman of the Macaroni was the first to put heart into the others: he also called several of those Germans and Frenchmen who wished me well. We were ten in all: I took the road, resolved and disposed not to let myself be imprisoned alive.

When I came before the criminal judges, I found the said Caterina and her mother. I came upon them while they were laughing with an advocate of theirs: I went in and boldly asked for the judge, who, swollen up, big and fat, sat raised above the others on a tribunal. When this man saw me, threatening me with his head, he said in a low voice: "Although your name is Benvenuto — Welcome — this time you will be the ill-come." I understood, and answered once more, saying: "Dispatch me quickly: tell me what I have come here to do." Then the said judge turned to Caterina and said to her: "Caterina, say everything that you have had to do with Benvenuto." Caterina said that I had used her in the Italian fashion. The judge turned to me and said: "You hear what Caterina says, Benvenuto." Then I said: "If I had used her in the Italian fashion, I should have done it only from the wish to have a son, as you people do." Then the judge replied, saying: "She means that you have used her outside the vessel where children are made." At this I said that that was not the Italian fashion; on the contrary it must be the French fashion, since she knew of it and I did not; and that I wanted her to say exactly in what fashion I had had to do with her. This wretched whore wickedly said, openly and clearly, the foul fashion she meant. I made her repeat it three times over one after another; and when she had said it, I said in a loud voice: "My lord judge, lieutenant of the Most Christian King, I ask you for justice; because I know that the laws of the Most Christian King promise the fire for such a sin, to the doer and to the sufferer alike; so this woman confesses the sin: I have no acquaintance with it in any way: the bawd of a mother is here, who for the one offence and the other deserves the fire; I ask you for justice."

And these words I repeated so often and in so loud a voice, always demanding the fire for her and for her mother: telling the judge that if he did not put her in prison in my presence, I would run to the King, and would tell him of the injustice a criminal lieutenant of his was doing me. At this great noise of mine they began to lower their voices; then I raised mine higher: the little whore fell to weeping together with her mother, and I shouted at the judge: "Fire, fire." That great coward, seeing that the thing had not gone the way he had planned, began with gentler words to excuse the weak feminine sex. At this, I considered that it seemed to me I had won a great battle, and muttering and threatening, I went off with God gladly; for certainly I would have paid five hundred scudi never to have appeared there. Out of that deep water, I thanked God with all my heart, and went back happily with my young men to my castle.

Chapter 31

When perverse fortune, or if we would rather say that contrary star of ours, takes to persecuting a man, she never lacks new ways of bringing something into the field against him. Thinking I had come out of a deep water beyond reckoning, thinking too that for some little time this perverse star of mine ought to leave me alone, I had not yet got my breath back from that danger beyond reckoning before she gave me two more at once. Within three days two cases happened to me; in each of the two my life was in the balance.

This was that, as I was going to Fontainebleau to talk with the King, who had written me a letter by which he wanted me to make the dies for the coinage of his whole kingdom, and with that letter had sent me some little sketches to show me part of his wish; but he certainly gave me leave to do everything I pleased: I had made new designs, according to my own judgment and according to the beauty of the art. So when I got to Fontainebleau, one of those treasurers who had orders from the King to provide for me — this one was called Monsignor de la Fa — at once said to me: "Benvenuto, Bologna the painter has had orders from the King to make your great colossus, and all the commissions our King had given us for you, he has taken them all away from us and given them to him. We have been extremely sorry about it, and it has seemed to us that this Italian of yours has behaved very rashly towards you; because you had already got the work by virtue of your models and your labours; this man takes it from you only through the favour of Madame d'Étampes: and it is many months now since he had that commission, and it has not yet been seen that he gives orders about anything."

I, wondering, said: "How is it possible that I have never known anything of this?" Then he told me that the man had kept it most secret, and that he had got it with the greatest difficulty, because the King would not give it to him; but that only the pressing of Madame d'Étampes had got it for him. Feeling myself injured in this way and so wrongfully, and seeing a work taken away from me which I had won with my great labours, and disposing myself to do some great thing of consequence with weapons, I went straight off to find Bologna. I found him in his room and in his study: he had me called inside, and with certain Lombard greetings of his asked me what good business had brought me there. Then I said: "A very good and great business." This man ordered his servants to bring drink, and said: "Before we talk about anything, I want us to drink together; for that is the custom of France." Then I said: "Messer Francesco, know that the talk we have to have together does not call for drinking first: perhaps afterwards we may drink."

I began to talk with him, saying: "All men who make profession of being honest men do their works in such a way that by them they are known to be honest men; and doing the contrary, they no longer have the name of honest men. I know that you knew the King had given me that great colossus to make, which had been talked about for eighteen months, and neither you nor anybody else had ever come forward to say anything about it; on account of which with my great labours I had shown myself to the great King, who, being pleased with my models, had given me this great work to make; and it is so many months that I have heard nothing else: only this morning I have heard that you have got it and have taken it from me; which work I won for myself by my marvellous doings, and you take it from me only with your empty words."

Chapter 32

To this Bologna answered and said: "Oh Benvenuto, everybody tries to do his own business in every way he can: if the King wants it so, what have you to say against it? for you would be throwing away your time, because I have had it made out, and it is mine. Now say whatever you like, and I will listen to you." I said this: "Know, Messer Francesco, that I should have many words to say to you, by which with admirable and true reasoning I should make you confess that such ways as these you have used and spoken are not used among rational animals; but I will come in a few words quickly to the point of my conclusion; only open your ears and understand me well, because it matters."

The man tried to get up from his seat, because he saw me dark in the face and greatly changed: I said that it was not yet time to get up: that he should stay seated and listen to me. Then I began, saying this: "Messer Francesco, you know that the work was mine first, and that, by the reason of the world, the time had gone by when anybody could speak of it any more: now I tell you that I am content that you should make a model, and I, besides the one I have made, will make another; then quietly we will take them to our great King; and whoever wins by that road the credit of having worked the better, he will deservedly be worthy of the colossus; and if it falls to you to make it, I will lay aside all this great injury you have done me, and will bless your hands as worthier than mine of so great a glory. So let us leave it at that, and we shall be friends; otherwise we shall be enemies; and God, who always helps the right, and I, who make the way for it, would show you into what great error you had fallen."

Messer Francesco said: "The work is mine, and since it has been given me, I do not want to put what is mine in question." To this I answer: "Messer Francesco, since you will not take the good course, which is just and reasonable, I will show you this other one, which will be like yours, ugly and disagreeable. I tell you this, that if I ever hear in any way that you speak of this work of mine again, I will kill you on the spot like a dog: and because we are neither in Rome, nor in Bologna, nor in Florence — here men live in another fashion — if I ever know that you speak of it to the King or to anybody else, I will kill you in any case. Think which road you want to take: either that first good one, which I spoke of, or this last bad one, which I speak of now."

This man did not know either what to say or what to do, and I was ready to carry that out then and there rather than let any more time come between. The said Bologna said no other words than these: "When I do the things an honest man ought to do, I shall have no fear in the world." At this I said: "You have spoken well; but if you do the contrary, be afraid, because it matters to you;" and at once I left him, and went off to the King, and with His Majesty I argued the matter of the coinage a great while; over which we were not much in agreement; because his Council being there, they persuaded him that the coins ought to be made in that French manner in which they had been made up to that time. To whom I answered that His Majesty had made me come from Italy so that I might make him works that should be good; and if His Majesty ordered me to the contrary, my spirit would never bear my making them. On this a time was fixed to talk about it another time: I went straight back to Paris.

Book Two, Chapters 33 to 40

Chapter 33

I had hardly dismounted when a good person, one of those who take pleasure in seeing mischief, came to tell me that Pagolo Miccieri had taken a house for that little whore Caterina and for her mother, and that he was continually going back there, and that when he spoke of me he always said in mockery: "Benvenuto set the geese to guard the lettuce, and thought I would not eat it; it is enough that now he goes about blustering and believes I am afraid of him: I have put on this sword and this dagger at my side to let him see that my sword cuts too, and that I am a Florentine as he is, of the Miccieri, a much better family than his Cellini are." This rascal, who brought me this message, said it to me with such effect that I at once felt the fever leap on me — I mean the fever, without saying it by way of comparison. And because perhaps of such a brutish passion I should have died, I took as a remedy the giving of that outlet which the occasion had given me, according to what I felt in myself.

I told that workman of mine from Ferrara, who was called Chioccia, to come with me, and I had my horse led behind me by my servant; and when I got to the house where this wretch was, finding the door ajar, I went in: I saw him with his sword and dagger at his side, and he was sitting on a chest, and had his arm round Caterina's neck: just as I arrived, I heard him joking with her mother about my affairs. Pushing the door open and at the same moment laying my hand on my sword, I put the point of it to his throat, not having given him time to think that he too had a sword, and said all at once: "Vile coward, commend yourself to God, for you are a dead man." He, quite still, said three times: "Oh mother of mine, help me." I, who had a mind to kill him in any case, when I heard those words, so foolish, half my anger went off me. Meanwhile I had told that workman of mine Chioccia not to let either her or the mother go out, because if I struck him, I meant to do the same harm to those two whores.

Keeping the point of my sword continually at his throat, and pricking him a little now and then, always with terrifying words; then seeing that he made no defence in the world, and I not knowing what more to do, and it seeming to me that this piece of blustering had no end to it at all, it came into my head, as the least evil, to make him marry her, meaning to take my revenge afterwards. So having made up my mind, I said: "Take that ring off your finger, coward, and marry her, so that afterwards I can take the revenge you deserve." The man said at once: "Only do not kill me, and I will do everything." "Then," said I, "put the ring on her finger." Having taken the sword a little away from his throat, the man put the ring on her. Then I said: "This is not enough, because I want two notaries to be sent for, so that the thing may go through by contract." Having told Chioccia to go for the notaries, I turned at once to her and to her mother. Speaking in French I said: "The notaries and other witnesses will come here: the first of you I hear say anything about this business, I will kill her on the spot, and I will kill all three of you; so keep your wits about you." To him I said in Italian: "If you say a word against anything I put forward, at the least word you say I will give you so many dagger thrusts that I will make you empty out everything you have in your bowels." To this he answered: "It is enough for me that you do not kill me; and I will do whatever you want." The notaries and the witnesses came, the contract was made in due form and — marvellous! — my anger and my fever went off me. I paid the notaries, and went away.

The next day Bologna came to Paris on purpose, and had me called by Mattio del Nazaro: I went and found the said Bologna, who came to meet me with a cheerful face, begging me to take him for a good brother, and saying that he would never speak of that work again, because he knew very well that I was in the right.

Chapter 34

If in some of these things that happened to me I did not admit that I had done wrong, those others where I know I did right would not pass for true; so I acknowledge that I did wrong in wanting to revenge myself so strangely on Pagolo Miccieri. Although, if I had thought he was a man of so much weakness, such a shameful revenge as I took would never have come into my mind; for it was not enough for me to have made him take so wicked a little whore for his wife; but afterwards, wanting to finish the rest of my revenge, I used to send for her and draw her: every day I gave her thirty soldi; and making her stand naked, first she wanted me to give her her money beforehand; second she wanted a very good breakfast; third I, for revenge, had my will of her, reproaching her and her husband with the various horns I was giving him; fourth I made her stay in great discomfort for hours and hours. And staying in this discomfort was a great weariness to her, as much as it delighted me, because she was of a very beautiful form and did me the greatest credit.

And because it did not seem to her that I had that consideration for her which I used to have before she was married, and it becoming very wearisome to her, she began to grumble; and in that French way of hers she blustered in words, bringing in her husband, who had gone to stay with the Prior of Capua, brother of Piero Strozzi. And as I have said, she brought in this husband of hers; and as soon as I heard him spoken of, an unspeakable rage came over me; still I put up with it, most unwillingly, as best I could, considering that for my art I could not find anything more to my purpose than she was; and I said to myself: "I am taking two different revenges here: one because she is a wife: these are not empty horns, as his were when she was a whore of mine; so if I take this revenge so complete against him and against her too such strangeness, making her stay here in such discomfort, which, besides the pleasure of it, brings me so much honour and so much profit, what more can I want?"

While I was making this reckoning of mine, this wretch multiplied those insulting words, always speaking of her husband; and she did and said so much that she drove me out of the bounds of reason; and giving myself up a prey to anger, I took her by the hair and dragged her about the room, giving her so many kicks and so many blows of my fists until I was tired. And nobody could come in there to her help. Having beaten her very thoroughly, she swore never to come back to me again; on account of which for the first time it seemed to me that I had done very wrong, because it seemed to me I was losing a marvellous opportunity of doing myself credit. Besides, I saw her all torn, livid and swollen, thinking that even if she did come back, it would be necessary to have her treated for a fortnight before I could use her.

Chapter 35

To come back to her: I used to send a maidservant of mine to help her dress, which maidservant was an old woman called Ruberta, a most kindly one; and when she got to this wretched girl, she took her fresh food and drink; then she anointed those bad bruises I had given her with a little fat of roast bacon, and the rest of the fat that was left over they ate between them. Then when she was dressed, she went off swearing and cursing all the Italians and the King who kept them there: and so she went home weeping and muttering. Certainly this first time it seemed to me that I had done very wrong; and my Ruberta reproved me, and kept saying to me: "You are very cruel to strike so harshly at such a beautiful young girl." When I tried to excuse myself to this Ruberta of mine, telling her the villainies she and her mother had done while she was with me, at this Ruberta scolded me, saying that that was nothing, because it was the custom of France, and that she knew for certain that in France there was no husband who had not his little pair of horns. At these words I was moved to laughter, and then I told Ruberta to go and see how Caterina was, because I should have been glad to be able to finish that work of mine, making use of her.

My Ruberta reproved me, telling me that I did not know how to live; because "hardly will it be day before she will come here of her own accord, whereas, if you send to ask for her or to visit her, she will play the great lady and will not want to come." The next day came, and this said Caterina came to my door, and with great fury banged at the said door, so that, being downstairs, I ran to see whether this was a madman or somebody of the house. Opening the door, this beast, laughing, threw herself on my neck, embraced me and kissed me, and asked me whether I was still angry with her. I said no. She said: "Give me a good breakfast, then." I gave her a good breakfast, and ate with her as a sign of peace. Then I set to drawing her, and in the meantime the pleasures of the flesh came in, and then at that same hour as on the past day, she provoked me so much that I had to give her the same beating; and so we went on for several days, doing every day all these same things, as if from a stamp: it varied little more or less.

Meanwhile I, who had won myself the greatest credit and finished my figure, gave orders to cast it in bronze; in which I had some difficulty, which it would be a very fine thing to tell for the accidents of the art; but because I should go on too long, I shall pass it over. It is enough that my figure came out very well, and it was as fine a cast as was ever made.

Chapter 36

While this work was being carried forward, I divided off certain hours of the day and worked on the saltcellar, and sometimes on the Jupiter. Because the saltcellar was worked on by many more people than I had the convenience to have working on the Jupiter, by this time I had finished it completely. The King had come back to Paris, and I went to find him, taking him the said saltcellar finished; which, as I have said above, was oval in form and was about two thirds of a braccio in size, all of gold, worked by the strength of the chisel. And as I said when I spoke of the model, I had figured the Sea and the Land seated the one and the other, and they entwined their legs, as certain arms of the sea run into the land, and the land into the said sea: I had given them that grace exactly.

Into the hand of the Sea I had put a trident in his right; and in his left I had put a boat finely worked, in which the salt was put. Under the said figure were his four sea horses, which as far as the breast and the forelegs were horse; all the part from the middle backwards was fish: these fishes' tails were twined together in a pleasing way; on which knot the said Sea sat in a most fierce attitude: he had round him many sorts of fishes and other sea creatures. The water was figured with its waves; then it was very well enamelled in its own colour.

For the Land I had figured a most beautiful woman, with the horn of her plenty in her hand, entirely naked just as the male was; in her other, left hand I had made a little temple of the Ionic order, most finely worked; and in this I had arranged the pepper. Under this woman I had made the most beautiful animals that the earth produces; and her earthy rocks I had partly enamelled and partly left in gold. I had then set this said work and fixed it in a base of black ebony: it was of a certain suitable thickness, and had a little hollow moulding, in which I had distributed four gold figures, made in more than half relief: these were figured as Night, Day, Twilight and Dawn. There were also four other figures of the same size, made for the four chief winds, worked and partly enamelled with as much neatness as can be imagined.

When I set this work before the King's eyes, he let out a cry of astonishment, and could not have enough of looking at it: then he told me to take it back home, and that he would tell me in time what I was to do with it. I took it home, and at once invited several dear friends of mine, and with them I dined in the greatest cheerfulness, putting the saltcellar in the middle of the table; and we were the first to use it. Then I went on to finish the silver Jupiter, and a great vase already spoken of, worked all over with many most pleasing ornaments and with plenty of figures.

Chapter 37

At this time Bologna the painter mentioned above gave the King to understand that it would be well for His Majesty to let him go as far as Rome, and to give him letters of recommendation, by which he might mould those first fine antiques, that is the Laocoön, the Cleopatra, the Venus, the Commodus, the Gypsy and the Apollo. These are truly the most beautiful things there are in Rome. And he told the King that when His Majesty had afterwards seen those marvellous works, then he would know how to talk about the art of design; because everything he had seen of us moderns was very far from the good work of those ancients. The King was content, and did him all the favours he asked. So this beast went off to his own ruin. Not having had the heart to compete with me with his own hands, he took that other Lombard expedient, trying to belittle my works by making himself a moulder of antiques. And although he had them moulded very well, the effect for him came out quite contrary to what he had imagined; which shall be told later in its place.

Having driven that wretched Caterina right away, and that poor unlucky young man her husband having gone off with God out of Paris, wanting to finish cleaning up my Fontainebleau, which was already made in bronze, and also to do those two Victories well, which went in the corners at the sides of the half circle of the door, I took a poor young girl of about fifteen. She was very beautiful in the form of her body and was somewhat dark; and because she was rather wild and of very few words, quick in her going about, and with frowning eyes, these things caused me to give her the name Scorzone: her own name was Gianna. With this said girl I finished in bronze very well the said Fontainebleau, and those two Victories for the said door. This young girl was pure and a virgin, and I got her with child; she bore me a daughter on the seventh day of June, at thirteen hours of the day, 1544, which was exactly the course of my age of 44 years. To the said daughter I gave the name Costanza; and she was baptized for me by Messer Guido Guidi, the King's physician, my great friend, as I have written above. He alone was godfather, because in France it is the custom to have one godfather and two godmothers, of whom one was Signora Maddalena, wife of Messer Luigi Alamanni, a Florentine gentleman and a marvellous poet; the other godmother was the wife of Messer Ricciardo del Bene, our Florentine citizen and a great merchant there; she a great French lady. This was the first child I ever had, as far as I remember. I made over to the said girl as much money for a dowry as an aunt of hers was satisfied with, to whom I gave her back; and I never had anything to do with her again.

Chapter 38

I pressed on with my works, and had carried them a long way forward: the Jupiter was almost at its end, the vase likewise; the door was beginning to show its beauties. At this time the King came to Paris; and although I have said 1544 for the birth of my daughter, we had not yet passed 1543; but because it has come in for me to speak of this daughter of mine now, so as not to have to interrupt myself in these other things of more importance, I shall say no more of it until its own place.

The King came to Paris, as I have said, and at once came to my house, and found those many works so far forward that his eyes could very well be satisfied; as those of that marvellous King were, whom the said works satisfied as much as anybody who takes such pains as I had done could wish; at once of his own accord he remembered that the said Cardinal of Ferrara had given me nothing, neither pension nor anything else of what he had promised me; and muttering with his Admiral, he said that the Cardinal of Ferrara had behaved very badly in not giving me anything; but that he meant to put this thing right, because he saw that I was a man to make few words about it; and, between seeing and not seeing, one day I should have gone off with God without saying anything more to him.

Having gone home, after His Majesty's dinner, he told the Cardinal to tell the treasurer of savings in his own words to pay me as soon as he could seven thousand gold scudi, in three or four payments, according to what suited him, so long as he did not fail in this; and moreover he repeated to him, saying: "I gave you Benvenuto in charge, and you have forgotten him." The Cardinal said that he would willingly do everything His Majesty said. The said Cardinal, out of his bad nature, let this wish of the King's pass off.

Meanwhile the wars were growing; and it was at the time when the Emperor with his very great army was coming towards Paris. The Cardinal, seeing that France was in great want of money, having come one day to speak about me, said: "Sacred Majesty, for the best I have not had money given to Benvenuto; the one reason is that there is too much need of it now; the other reason is that so heavy a sum of money would sooner have made you lose Benvenuto; because, thinking himself rich, he would have bought himself property in Italy, and once the whim took him, he would the more readily have left you; so I have considered that the best thing is for Your Majesty to give him something in your kingdom, if you wish him to stay a longer time in your service." The King thought these reasons good, being in want of money; nevertheless, as a most noble spirit, truly worthy of the King he was, he considered that the said Cardinal had done this thing rather to make himself agreeable than out of any necessity, or that he could have foreseen so far in advance the necessities of so great a kingdom.

Chapter 39

And although, as I have said, the King showed that he had thought these reasons good, in his own secret mind he did not take it so; because, as I have said above, he came back to Paris, and the next day, without my going to urge him, of his own accord he came to my house: where, going to meet him, I led him through various rooms, where there were various sorts of works, and beginning with the lowest things, I showed him a great quantity of works in bronze, of which he had not seen anything like so many. Then I took him to see the silver Jupiter, and showed it to him as finished, with all its most beautiful ornaments: which seemed to him a much more admirable thing than it would have seemed to any other man, on account of a certain terrible occasion which had befallen him a few years before: that when, after the taking of Tunis, the Emperor passed through Paris on good terms with his brother-in-law King Francis, the said King, wanting to make a present worthy of so great an Emperor, had a Hercules made for him in silver, of exactly the size that I had made the Jupiter; which Hercules the King admitted was the ugliest work he had ever seen; and having said so plainly to those able men of Paris, who claimed to be the ablest men in the world in that profession, they having given the King to understand that this was all that could be done in silver, and for all that they wanted two thousand ducats for that swinish work of theirs; for this reason, having seen that work of mine, the King saw in it such neatness as he would never have believed. So he made a good judgment, and wanted my work of the Jupiter to be valued at two thousand ducats too, saying: "To those men I gave no salary at all: to this man, to whom I give about a thousand scudi of salary, he can certainly make it for me for the price of two thousand gold scudi, having the said advantage of his salary."

Next I took him to see other works of silver and gold, and many other models for inventing new works. Then, at the last of his going, in my meadow at the castle I uncovered that great giant, at which the King made a greater show of wonder than he had ever made at anything else; and turning to the Admiral, who was called Monsignor Annibale, he said: "Since this man has been provided with nothing by the Cardinal, since he is himself slow at asking, without saying anything more I want him to be provided for: for these men, who are not used to asking for anything, think it right that their labours should ask a great deal: so provide him with the first abbey that falls vacant, one worth up to two thousand scudi of income; and if it does not come in one piece, see that it is in two or three pieces, because it will be the same to him." I, being present, heard everything and thanked him at once, as if I had already had it, telling His Majesty that when this thing came, I would work for His Majesty without any other reward, neither of salary nor of any other value for my works, until such time as, forced by old age and being no longer able to work, I might rest my tired life in peace, living honourably on that income, remembering that I had served so great a King as His Majesty was. At these words of mine the King, most cheerful towards me and with great heartiness, said: "And so let it be done;" and His Majesty went away content, and I stayed behind.

Chapter 40

Madame d'Étampes, hearing of these affairs of mine, grew still more envenomed against me, saying to herself: "I govern the world today, and a little man like this makes nothing of me!" She set up shop altogether and entirely to work against me. And a certain man coming into her hands, who was a great distiller — this man gave her certain sweet-smelling and marvellous waters, which tightened her skin, a thing never used before in France — she brought him before the King: which man set forth some of these distillations, which greatly delighted the King; and in these pleasures she brought it about that he asked His Majesty for a tennis court that I had in my castle, with certain small rooms, which he said I did not use.

That good King, who knew where the thing came from, gave no answer at all. Madame d'Étampes set about pressing it by those ways in which women can work on men, so much that this design of hers easily succeeded, since, finding the King in an amorous mood, to which he was very subject, she got as much as she wanted from him. This said man came together with the treasurer Grolier, a very great gentleman of France; and because this treasurer spoke Italian very well, he came to my castle, and went into it in my presence, speaking to me in Italian, in a joking way. When he saw his moment, he said: "I put this man here in possession, on the King's behalf, of that tennis court together with those little houses which belong to the said court." At this I said: "Everything belongs to the sacred King; so you could have come in here more freely; because in this way, done through notaries and through the court, it looks more like a way of trickery than a straightforward commission from so great a King; and I declare to you that before I go to complain to the King, I will defend myself in the way His Majesty ordered me to do the other day; and I will pitch this man, whom you have put in here, out of the windows, unless I see some other express commission in the King's own hand."

At these words of mine the said treasurer went off threatening and muttering, and I, doing the same, stayed where I was, and would not for the time make any other demonstration: then I went off to find those notaries who had put the man in possession. These were well known to me, and they told me that this was a ceremony properly done with the King's commission, but that it did not matter much; and that if I had made a little resistance, he would not have taken possession, as he did; and that these were acts and customs of the court, which touched the King's authority not at all; so that if it suited me to put him out of possession in the same way as he had come into it, it would be well done, and there would be nothing more in it.

It was enough for me to have the hint given, and the next day I began to lay my hand on my weapons; and although I had some difficulty, I had taken it on for my pleasure. Every day I made one assault, with stones, with pikes, with arquebuses, though firing without ball; but I put them into such fright that nobody wanted to come and help him any more. On account of which, finding his battle weak one day, I went into the house by force and drove him out of it, throwing outside every single thing he had brought there. Then I went to the King and told him that I had done every single thing His Majesty had ordered me, defending myself from all those who wanted to hinder me in His Majesty's service. At this the King laughed, and made me out new letters, by which I should no longer be molested.

Book Two, Chapters 41 to 48

Chapter 41

Meanwhile with great diligence I finished the beautiful silver Jupiter, together with its gilded base, which I had set on a block of wood that showed very little; and in the said block of wood I had fitted four little balls of hard wood, which were more than half hidden in their sockets, in the fashion of the nut of a crossbow. These things were so nicely arranged that a small child could easily, in every direction and without any effort in the world, send the said statue of Jupiter backwards and forwards and turn it about. Having arranged it to my own liking, I went off with it to Fontainebleau, where the King was.

At this time the said Bologna had brought the said statues from Rome, and had with great diligence had them cast in bronze. I knew nothing of this, both because he had done this business of his very secretly, and because Fontainebleau is more than forty miles from Paris; so I could not have known anything. When I let the King know where I wanted the Jupiter put, Madame d'Étampes being present, she told the King that there was no more suitable place to put it than in his beautiful gallery. This was, as we should say in Tuscany, a loggia, or truly a hall: a hall it might better be called, because we call a loggia those rooms that are open on one side. This room was more than a hundred paces long, and was adorned and very rich with paintings from the hand of that admirable Rosso, our Florentine; and among the paintings a great many pieces of sculpture were set, some in the round, others in low relief: it was about twelve paces wide. The said Bologna had brought into this said gallery all the said antique works, made in bronze and very well brought to finish, and had set them in beautiful order, raised on their bases; and as I have said above, these were the most beautiful things taken from those antiques of Rome.

Into this said room I brought my Jupiter; and when I saw that great array, all done by design, I said to myself: "This is like running the gauntlet of the pikes. Now God help me." Having put it in its place and arranged it as well as I could, I waited for that great King to come. The said Jupiter had in his right hand his thunderbolt arranged in the attitude of about to hurl it, and in his left I had arranged the World. Among the flames I had very skilfully set a piece of a white torch. And because Madame d'Étampes had kept the King back until night, so as to bring about one of two mischiefs, either that he should not come, or truly that my work, because of the night, should show itself less beautiful; and as God promises to those creatures who have faith in Him, quite the contrary came about; because seeing that night had fallen, I lit the said torch which was in Jupiter's hand; and being somewhat raised above the head of the said Jupiter, the lights fell from above and made it a much finer sight than they would have made by day.

The said King appeared together with his Madame d'Étampes, with the Dauphin his son and with the Dauphine, today King, with the King of Navarre his brother-in-law, with Madame Marguerite his daughter, and several other great lords, who had been instructed on purpose by Madame d'Étampes to speak against me. Seeing the King come in, I had that boy of mine already spoken of, Ascanio, push it forward, so that he gently moved the fine Jupiter towards the King: and because I had made it too with a little art, that little movement given to the said figure, it being very well made, made it seem alive; and having left the said antique figures somewhat behind, I first gave great pleasure to their eyes with my work.

At once the King said: "This is a much more beautiful thing than any man ever saw, and I, who take pleasure in these things and understand them, would not have imagined the hundredth part of it." Those lords, who were to speak against me, seemed unable to have enough of praising the said work. Madame d'Étampes boldly said: "It certainly seems that you have no eyes. Do you not see how many beautiful antique figures in bronze are set further along, in which the true excellence of this art consists, and not in these modern trifles?" Then the King moved on, and the others with him; and having given a glance at the said figures, and those, because the lights were brought to them from below, not showing themselves at all well; at this the King said: "Whoever wanted to do this man a disservice has done him a great service; because by means of these admirable figures one sees and knows that this one of his is by far more beautiful and more marvellous than they are. So Benvenuto is to be greatly esteemed, since his works not only stand comparison with the antiques, but even surpass them."

At this Madame d'Étampes said that seeing such a work by day, it would not seem a thousandth part as beautiful as it seems by night; besides, it was to be considered that I had put a veil on the said figure, to cover the faults. This was a very fine veil which I had put with fine grace on the said Jupiter, so that it should increase his majesty: which at those words I took hold of, lifting it from below, uncovering those fine genital members, and with a little show of anger I tore the whole of it off. She thought I had uncovered that part in mockery of her. The King, noticing her anger and that I was overcome with passion, when I tried to begin to speak, at once the wise King said these very words in his own tongue: "Benvenuto, I cut off your speech; so be quiet, and you shall have a thousand times more treasure than you desire." Not being able to speak, I twisted about in great passion: which caused her to mutter still more scornfully; and the King, much sooner than he would have done, went away, saying loudly, to give me courage, that he had brought out of Italy the greatest man ever born, full of so many professions.

Chapter 42

Leaving the Jupiter there, and wanting to go away in the morning, he had a thousand gold scudi given me: part were of my salary, and part on accounts which I showed I had spent of my own. Having taken the money, happy and content I went back to Paris; and as soon as I arrived, having made merry in the house, after dinner I had all my clothes brought out, which were a great quantity of silk, of the finest furs and likewise of the finest cloth. These I made a present of to all those workmen of mine, giving to each according to the merits of those servants, down to the maids and the stable boys, giving them all courage to help me with a good heart.

Having taken up my vigour again, with the greatest study and diligence I set myself to finishing that great statue of Mars, which I had made of very well joined wood for an armature; and over that, its flesh was a crust an eighth of a braccio thick, made of plaster and carefully worked; then I had arranged to mould the said figure in many pieces, and afterwards to join them dovetailed, as the art requires; which I managed very easily.

I do not want to leave out giving one token of this great work, a thing truly worth a laugh: because I had commanded all those whose expenses I paid that they should not bring prostitutes into my house or into my castle; and about this I was very careful that such a thing should not happen. That young man of mine Ascanio was in love with a very beautiful girl, and she with him: on account of which this said girl having run away from her mother, and having come one night to find Ascanio, and then not wanting to go away, and he not knowing where to hide her, as a last remedy, being an ingenious person, he put her inside the figure of the said Mars, and arranged her a place to sleep in the very head; and there she stayed a good while, and at night he sometimes quietly got her out.

Having left that head very near its finish, and out of a little vanity of mine, I left the said head uncovered, so that it could be seen from most of the city of Paris: those who lived nearest had begun to climb up on the roofs, and a great many people went on purpose to see it. And because there was a report about Paris that in that castle of mine a spirit had lived from of old, of which thing I saw one or two tokens that made me believe it was so — the said spirit was universally called by the common people of Paris by the name of Lemmonio Boreo — and because this girl, who lived in the said head, could sometimes not help a certain little movement being seen through the eyes; whereat some of those foolish people said that the said spirit had entered into the body of that great figure, and that it made the eyes of that head move, and the mouth, as if it wanted to speak; and many went away terrified, and some cunning ones, having come to see and not being able to persuade themselves out of that flickering of the eyes which the said figure made, they too maintained that there was a spirit in there, not knowing that there was a spirit and good flesh besides.

Chapter 43

In the meantime I was attending to putting together my beautiful door, with all the things described. And because I do not want to trouble to write in this Life of mine things which belong to those who write chronicles, I have left out the coming of the Emperor with his great army, and the King with all his armed force. And at these times he asked my advice about fortifying Paris quickly: he came to my house on purpose for me, and took me round the whole city of Paris; and having heard with what good reason I would quickly fortify Paris for him, he gave me express orders to do at once everything I had said; and he ordered his Admiral to command those people to obey me, on pain of his displeasure.

The Admiral, who had been made such by the favour of Madame d'Étampes and not by his own good works, being a man of little wit, and his name being Monsignor d'Annebault, although in our tongue it means Monsignor Annibale, in that tongue of theirs it sounds in such a way that most of the people called him Monsignor Ass-and-Ox; this beast, having reported the whole thing to Madame d'Étampes, she ordered him quickly to send for Girolamo Bellarmati. This was a Sienese engineer and was at Dieppe, little more than a day's journey from Paris. He came at once, and having set to work on the longest way of fortifying, I withdrew from the undertaking; and if the Emperor had pushed forward, Paris could very easily have been taken. It was indeed said that in that agreement made afterwards, Madame d'Étampes, who more than anybody else had put herself in the middle of it, had betrayed the King. I have nothing else to say about this, because it is not to my purpose.

I set myself with great diligence to putting together my bronze door, and to finishing that great vase, and two other middling ones made of my own silver. After these tribulations the good King came to rest a while in Paris. This cursed woman having been born almost for the ruin of the world, it seems to me that I must be something after all, since she took me for her mortal enemy. Having fallen to talking about my affairs with that good King, she said so much ill of me that this good man, to please her, set about swearing that he would never again take any account of me in the world, as if he had never known me. These words were told me at once by a page of the Cardinal of Ferrara's, who was called Il Villa, and he told me that he himself had heard them from the King's mouth.

This thing put me into such anger that, throwing all my tools aside, and all my works too, I got ready to go off with God, and went at once to find the King. After his dinner, I went into a room where His Majesty was with very few people; and when he saw me come in, and I had made him that due reverence that belongs to a King, at once with a cheerful face he bowed his head to me. On account of which I took hope, and little by little came near His Majesty, because certain things of my profession were being shown; and when we had talked a little while over the said things, His Majesty asked me whether I had anything fine to show him at my house, then he said when did I want him to come and see them. Then I said that I was in a position to show him something, if he had a mind for it, at that very moment. At once he said that I should set off home, and that he wanted to come then.

Chapter 44

I set off, waiting for this good King, who had gone to take leave of Madame d'Étampes. She wanting to know where he was going, since she said she would keep him company, when the King had told her where he was going, she told His Majesty that she did not want to go with him, and that she begged him to do her so much favour as not to go himself either that day. She had to come back to it more than twice, wanting to turn the King away from that undertaking: for that day he did not come to my house.

The next day I went back to the King at that same hour: as soon as he saw me, he swore he would come to my house at once. Having gone as usual to take leave of his Madame d'Étampes, and she seeing that with all her power she had not been able to turn the King aside, she set about with her biting tongue saying as much ill of me as can be said of a man who was a mortal enemy of that worthy Crown. At this that good King said that he wanted to come to my house only to scold me in such a way as would have terrified me; and so he gave his word to Madame d'Étampes to do.

And at once he came to the house, where I led him into certain great ground-floor rooms, in which I had put together all that great door of mine; and when he got to it the King was left so astonished that he could not find the way to say to me that great abuse he had promised Madame d'Étampes. Nor even so would he give up finding the occasion to say to me that promised abuse, and he began by saying: "It is a very great matter, Benvenuto, that you men, although you are talented, ought to know that you cannot show those talents by yourselves; and you only show yourselves great by means of the opportunities you receive from us. Now you ought to be a little more obedient, and not so proud and self-willed. I remember expressly ordering you to make me twelve statues in silver; and that was my whole desire. You have wanted to make me a saltcellar, and vases and heads and doors, and so many other things, that I am much bewildered, seeing all the desires of my wishes left behind, and you attending to satisfying all your own wishes: so if you think of going on in this fashion, I shall let you see how I am used to act when I want a thing done in my way. Therefore I tell you: attend to obeying what you are told, because if you stay obstinate in these fancies of yours, you will bang your head against a wall." And while he was saying these words, all those lords stood attentive, seeing that he shook his head, knitted his brows, and made gestures now with one hand and now with the other; so that all those men who were there present trembled with fear for me, because I had made up my mind not to have any fear in the world.

Chapter 45

And as soon as he had finished giving me that scolding which he had promised his Madame d'Étampes, I put one knee on the ground, and having kissed his robe on his knee, I said: "Sacred Majesty, I acknowledge everything you say to be true; only I say to you that my heart has been continually day and night with all my vital spirits intent only on obeying you and on serving you; and everything which to Your Majesty may seem contrary to what I say, let Your Majesty know that this has not been Benvenuto, but may have been some bad fate of mine or evil fortune, which has wanted to make me unworthy of serving the most marvellous prince the earth ever had: so I beg you to forgive me.

"It only seemed to me that Your Majesty gave me silver for a single statue: and having none of my own, I could not make more than that one; and out of that little silver which was left me over from the said figure, I made that vase, to show Your Majesty that beautiful manner of the ancients; which perhaps you had not seen of that kind before. As to the saltcellar, it seemed to me, if I remember rightly, that Your Majesty of your own accord asked me for one one day, having fallen to talking of one that was brought before you; on account of which, when I had shown you a model, which I had already made in Italy, only at your request you had a thousand gold ducats given me at once, so that I should make it, saying that you were much obliged to me for such a thing: and still more it seemed to me that you thanked me greatly when I gave it to you finished. As to the door, it seemed to me that, talking of it by chance, Your Majesty gave the orders to Monsignor de Villeroy your chief secretary, who ordered Monsignor de Marmagne and Monsignor de la Fa to press on this work with me, and to provide for me; and without these orders, by myself I could never have carried forward such great undertakings.

"As to the bronze heads and the base of the Jupiter and the rest, the heads I truly made of my own accord, to make trial of these clays of France, which I, as a foreigner, did not know at all; and without making trial of the said clays I would not have set about casting these great works. As to the bases, I made them because it seemed to me that such a thing was very fitting to keep those figures company; so everything I have done, I have thought to do for the best, and never to depart from Your Majesty's will. It is quite true that that great colossus I have made entirely, up to the point it has got to, at the expense of my own purse; only it seemed to me that you, so great a King, and I, the poor artist that I am, ought to make for your glory and mine a statue such as the ancients never had. Now, knowing that it has not pleased God to make me worthy of so honourable a service, I beg you that, instead of that honourable reward which Your Majesty had destined for my works, you would only give me a little of your good grace and with it your good leave to go; because at this moment, if you make me worthy of such things, I shall go, returning to Italy, always thanking God and Your Majesty for those happy hours I have been in your service."

Chapter 46

He took me with his hands, and raised me with great gentleness from my knees; then he told me that I should be content to serve him, and that everything I had done was good, and was most acceptable to him. And turning to those lords he said these very words: "I certainly believe that if Paradise were to have doors, it would never have a more beautiful one than this." When I saw the heartiness of those words a little settled, all of which were in my favour, again with the greatest reverence I thanked him, still repeating that I wanted my leave; because my anger had not yet gone off me. When that great King saw that I had not made the account those unusual and great caresses of his deserved, he ordered me with a great and terrible voice not to say another word, for it would go ill with me; and then he added that he would drown me in gold, and that he gave me leave, so that, after the works ordered by His Majesty, he was quite content with everything I did in between of my own accord, and that I should never have any difference with him again, because he had come to know me; and that I too should try to know His Majesty, as was only right. I said that I thanked God and His Majesty for everything, then I begged him to come and see the great figure, and how far I had carried it forward: so he came after me. I had it uncovered: which gave him such wonder as could never be imagined; and he at once ordered a secretary of his that he should immediately repay me all the money I had spent of my own, and let it be whatever sum I liked, so long as I gave it in writing in my own hand. Then he went away, and said to me: "Adieu, mon ami": which great word is not usual for a king.

Chapter 47

Having got back to his palace, he came to repeat those great words, so marvellously humble and so loftily proud, which I had used with His Majesty, which words had made him very angry; and he told some of the particulars of such words in the presence of Madame d'Étampes, where there was Monsignor de Saint-Pol, a great baron of France. This man had in the past made a great profession of being a friend of mine; and certainly this time he showed it very finely, in the French manner. For, after much talking, the King complained of the Cardinal of Ferrara, that having given me into his charge, he had never thought about my affairs again, and that it had not been his fault that I had not gone off with God out of his kingdom, and that he would truly think of giving me into the charge of somebody who knew me better than the Cardinal of Ferrara had done, because he did not want to give me any more occasion to be lost to him.

At these words Monsignor de Saint-Pol at once offered himself, telling the King to give me into his keeping, and that he would certainly do something that would give me no more cause to leave his kingdom. At this the King said that he was very content, if Saint-Pol would tell him the way he meant to take so that I should not leave. Madame, who was present, was very much put out, and Saint-Pol stood on his dignity, not wanting to tell the King the way he meant to take. The King asking him again, and he, to please Madame d'Étampes, said: "I would hang him by the throat, this Benvenuto of yours; and in that way you would not lose him out of your kingdom." At once Madame d'Étampes broke into a great laugh, saying that I well deserved it. At this the King for company set to laughing, and said that he was very content that Saint-Pol should hang me, if he first found another man like me; for although I had never deserved it, he gave him full leave. In the way described this day was ended, and I was left safe and sound; for which God be praised and thanked.

Chapter 48

At this time the King had quieted the war with the Emperor, but not with the English, so that these devils kept us in great tribulation. The King having his head on other things than pleasures, had ordered Piero Strozzi to take certain galleys into those seas of England; which was a very great and difficult thing to take them there, even for that admirable soldier, matchless in his time in such a profession, and as matchless in his ill luck.

Several months had gone by in which I had had neither money nor any orders to work; so that I had sent away all my workmen, except those two Italians, whom I set to make two little vases out of my own silver, because they did not know how to work in bronze. When they had finished the two vases, I went off with them to a city which belonged to the Queen of Navarre: this is called Argentan, and is many days' journey from Paris. I got to the said place and found that the King was unwell; the Cardinal of Ferrara told His Majesty that I had arrived in that place. At this the King answered nothing at all, which was the cause of my having to stay many days in discomfort. And truly I never had greater unhappiness: still at the end of several days I came before him one evening, and set those two fine vases before his eyes: which pleased him beyond measure.

When I saw the King very well disposed, I begged His Majesty to be content to do me so much favour as to let me go for a change as far as Italy, and that I would leave the seven months' salary I was owed, which money His Majesty would deign to have paid me afterwards, if I should need it for my return. I begged His Majesty to grant me such a favour, seeing that it was truly a time for making war, and not yet for making statues, and since His Majesty had granted such a thing to his Bologna the painter, so I most devoutly begged him to be content to make me worthy of it too.

The King, while I was saying these words to him, was looking with the greatest attention at those two vases, and now and then he wounded me with a terrible look of his; still I, as best I could and knew how, begged him to grant me this favour. All at once I saw him angry, and he got up from his seat and said to me in Italian: "Benvenuto, you are a great fool; take these vases off to Paris, because I want them gilded;" and giving me no other answer, he went away.

I went up to the Cardinal of Ferrara, who was present, and begged him that, since he had done me so much good in getting me out of prison in Rome, together with so many other benefits, he would grant me this too, that I might go as far as Italy. The said Cardinal told me that he would most willingly do everything he could to do me that kindness, and that I should freely leave the care of it to him; and also that, if I wished, I could freely go, because he would settle it very well with the King. I said to the said Cardinal that I knew His Majesty had given me into the charge of His most reverend Lordship, and that if he gave me leave, I would gladly go, to come back at a single least sign from His most reverend Lordship. Then the Cardinal told me that I should go off to Paris, and stay there a week, and in that time he would get leave from the King for me to go: and in case the King was not content that I should leave, he would without fail send me word; on account of which, if he wrote me nothing else, it would be a sign that I might freely go.

Book Two, Chapters 49 to 54

Chapter 49

Having gone off to Paris, as the Cardinal had told me, I made marvellous cases for those three silver vases. When twenty days had passed, I got myself ready, and put the three vases on a mule's pack, which had been lent me as far as Lyons by the Bishop of Pavia, whom I had lodged once more in my castle. I set off in my ill hour, together with Signor Ippolito Gonzaga, which lord was in the King's pay and maintained by Count Galeotto della Mirandola, and with certain other gentlemen of the said count. Leonardo Tedaldi, our Florentine, also joined company with us.

I left Ascanio and Pagolo in charge of my castle and of all my property, among which were certain little vases that had been begun, which I left so that those two young men should not be idle. There was also a great deal of household furniture of great value, because I lived very honourably: the value of these said goods of mine was more than fifteen hundred scudi. I told Ascanio to remember how many great benefits he had had from me, and that up to then he had been a boy of little sense: that it was time now to have a man's sense; so I meant to leave in his keeping all my property, together with all my honour; and that if he heard so much as one thing or another from those beasts of Frenchmen, he should let me know at once, because I would get into the post saddle and fly from wherever I was, both for the great obligation I had to that good King and for my own honour. The said Ascanio with pretended and thievish tears said to me: "I never knew a better father than you, and everything a good son ought to do towards his good father, I will always do towards you." So in agreement I set off with a servant and with a small French boy.

When midday had gone by, there came to my castle certain of those treasurers, who were not friends of mine at all. This rascally rabble said at once that I had gone off with the King's silver, and told Messer Guido and the Bishop of Pavia to send quickly for the King's vases; if not, they would send after me for them, to my very great unpleasantness. The Bishop and Messer Guido were much more frightened than there was any need for, and quickly sent that traitor Ascanio after me by post, who appeared about midnight. And I, who was not sleeping, was grieving to myself, saying: "To whom am I leaving my property, my castle? Oh what a destiny of mine this is, which forces me to make this journey? If only the Cardinal is not in agreement with Madame d'Étampes, who desires nothing else in the world except that I should lose that good King's favour!"

Chapter 50

While I was having this dispute with myself, I heard Ascanio call me; and at the first sound I got up out of bed and asked him whether he brought me good news or bad. The thief said: "I bring good news; but you only need to send back the three vases, because those rascals of treasurers are raising a hue and cry, so that the Bishop and Messer Guido say that you should send them back in any case: and for the rest let nothing trouble you, and go and enjoy this journey happily." I gave him back the vases at once, two of which were my own, with the silver and everything.

I was taking them to the Cardinal of Ferrara's abbey in Lyons; because although they gave out that I meant to take them off to Italy, everybody knows well that neither money, nor gold, nor silver can be taken out without great leave. Now it is well worth considering whether I could have taken out those three great vases, which with their cases took up a whole mule. It is quite true that, they being a very beautiful thing and of great value, I feared for the King's death, because certainly I had left him very unwell; and I said to myself: "If such a thing happened, having them in the Cardinal's hands, I cannot lose them."

Now, in conclusion, I sent back the said mule with the vases and other things of importance; and with the said company the next morning I attended to going on my way, nor could I ever, in all the journey, keep myself from sighing and weeping. Still, sometimes I comforted myself with God, saying: "Lord God, You who know the truth, know that this going of mine is only to carry alms to six poor wretched virgins and to their mother, my sister of my own blood; that although they have their father, he is so old and his trade earns nothing; and they might easily go the bad way; whereas by doing this pious work, I hope for help and counsel from Your Majesty." This was all the recreation I took as I went on.

Finding ourselves one day within a day's journey of Lyons, near twenty-two hours the sky began to make certain dry claps of thunder, and the air was very white: I was a crossbow shot ahead of my companions; after the thunder the sky made so great and so fearful a noise that I judged to myself that it was the day of Judgment; and having stopped a little, there began to fall a hail without a drop of water. This was larger than pellets from a blowpipe, and, striking on me, it hurt me a great deal: little by little this began to grow larger so that it was like crossbow bolts. Seeing that my horse was greatly frightened, I turned him back with the greatest fury at a gallop, until I found my companions, who from the same fear had stopped inside a pine wood. The hail grew as large as big lemons: I sang a Miserere; and while I was saying this devoutly to God, there came one of those stones so large that it broke off a very thick branch of that pine where I thought I was safe. Another of those stones struck the head of my horse, which made as if to fall to the ground; one caught me, but not full on, because it would have killed me. Likewise one caught that poor old Leonardo Tedaldi, in such a way that he, who was on his knees like me, was made to put his hands on the ground. Then I quickly, seeing that that great branch could no longer defend me and that with the Miserere it was necessary to do something as well, began to double my clothes over my head: and so I said to Leonardo, who was crying out for help, "Jesus, Jesus," that Jesus would help him if he helped himself. I had greater trouble in saving him than in saving myself.

This thing lasted a good while, but then it stopped, and we, who were all battered, got back on our horses as best we could; and while we were going on towards our lodging, showing each other our scratches and our bruises, a mile further on we found so much greater ruin than our own that it seems impossible to tell it. All the trees were stripped and broken, with as many beasts dead as it had found there; and many shepherds dead too: we saw a great quantity of those hailstones which two hands could not have gone round. So it seemed to us that we had made a good bargain, and we knew then that calling on God and those Misereres of ours had served us better than we could have done by ourselves. So thanking God, we went on to Lyons the next day, and there we rested for a week. When the week had passed, having refreshed ourselves very well, we took up the journey again, and very happily crossed the mountains. There I bought a small horse, because some little baggage had rather tired out my horses.

Chapter 51

When we had been a day in Italy, Count Galeotto della Mirandola came up with us, who was travelling post, and having stopped with us, he told me that I had made a mistake in leaving, and that I ought not to go any further, because my affairs, if I went straight back, would go better than ever; but if I went on, I was giving my enemies room and the chance of doing me harm; whereas, if I went straight back, I would have blocked their way to what they had planned against me; and those men in whom I had most faith were the ones who were deceiving me. He would not tell me any more, though he knew it very well: and the Cardinal of Ferrara had come to an agreement with those two rascals of mine whom I had left in charge of everything of mine. The said young count repeated to me several times that I ought to go back in any case. Having got into the post saddle he went on ahead, and I, because of the company I was in, made up my mind to go on too.

I had a torment in my heart, now to get to Florence very quickly, and now to go back to France. I was in such passion, undecided in that way, that in the end I made up my mind to take post so as to get quickly to Florence. I could not come to terms at the first post house; and so I settled my purpose absolutely to come and be tormented in Florence. Having left the company of Signor Ippolito Gonzaga, who had taken the road to go to Mirandola, and I that of Parma and Piacenza, when I got to Piacenza I met in a street Duke Pier Luigi, who looked me over and knew me. And I, who knew that all the harm I had had in the Castel Sant'Angelo in Rome, he had been the entire cause of it, felt great passion at seeing him; and knowing no remedy for getting out of his hands, I made up my mind to go and visit him; and I arrived just as the meat had been taken away, and with him were those men of the Landi family, who were afterwards the ones who killed him.

When I came to His Excellency, this man made the most immoderate fuss of me that can ever be imagined: and among these caresses he fell of himself into the subject, telling those who were present that I was the first man in the world of my profession and that I had been a long time in prison in Rome. And turning to me he said: "My Benvenuto, what happened to you grieved me a great deal; and I knew that you were innocent, and I could not help you otherwise, because my father, to satisfy certain enemies of yours, who had also given him to understand that you had spoken ill of him: which thing I know for a certainty was never true; and I was very sorry about your case;" and with these words he multiplied so many others like them that it seemed almost as if he were asking my pardon. Next he asked me about all the works I had made for the Most Christian King; and while I told him of them, he stayed attentive, giving me the most gracious audience possible in the world. Then he asked me whether I would serve him: to this I answered that with my honour I could not do it; that if I had left finished those many great works I had begun for that great King, I would leave any great lord, only to serve His Excellency.

Now here it is seen how the great power of God never leaves unpunished any sort of men who do wrongs and injustices to the innocent. This man asked my pardon, as it were, in the presence of those who a little afterwards took my revenge, together with that of many others who had been murdered by him; so let no lord, however great he may be, make a mockery of the justice of God, as certain of those men do whom I know, who have so foully murdered my name, of which I shall speak in its place. And these things of mine I do not write out of worldly boasting, but only to thank God, who has saved me from so many great troubles. And of those too who present themselves before me day by day, I complain of all of them to Him, and call on Him as my own defender and commend myself to Him. And always, besides helping myself as much as I can, when afterwards I am brought low where my weak forces do not reach, at once that great courage of God shows itself to me, which comes unlooked for upon those who wrongfully injure others, and upon those who take little care of the great and honourable charge which God has given them.

Chapter 52

I went back to the inn and found that the said Duke had sent me most abundant presents of food and drink, most honourable ones: I took my meal with a good appetite; then, having got on horseback, I came on towards Florence; where when I arrived, I found my sister of my own blood with six little daughters, one of whom was of an age to marry and one still at nurse: I found her husband, who because of various accidents in the city no longer worked at his trade. I had sent more than a year before jewels and French gilt work worth more than two thousand ducats, and with me I had brought about a thousand scudi' worth. I found that, although I gave them four gold scudi a month continually, they were also continually taking a good deal of money from those gilt works of mine which they sold from day to day. That brother-in-law of mine was such an honest man that, for fear I might grow angry with him, the money I sent him for his keep not being enough, and giving it him as alms, he had pawned almost everything he had in the world, letting the interest eat him up, only so as not to touch that money which was not meant for him. By this I knew that he was a very honest man and my wish grew to give him more alms: and before I left Florence I meant to make arrangements for all his little daughters.

Chapter 53

Our Duke of Florence at this time, which was the month of August in 1545, being at Poggio a Caiano, a place ten miles from Florence, I went to find him, only to do my duty, since I too was a Florentine citizen and because my forefathers had been great friends of the house of Medici, and I more than any of them loved this Duke Cosimo. As I say, I went to the said Poggio only to pay him my respects and never with any intention of settling with him, though as God, who does everything well, was pleased that it should be: for when the said Duke saw me, after making infinite fuss of me, he and the Duchess asked me about the works I had made for the King; on which I willingly, and all in order, told them.

When he had heard me, he said that he had already heard as much and that it was true; and then he added in an attitude of compassion, and said: "Oh, small reward for so many fine and great labours! My Benvenuto, if you would make something for me, I would pay you very differently from what that King of yours has done, whom out of your good nature you praise so much." To these words I added the great obligations I had to His Majesty, who had taken me out of so unjust a prison, and then had given me the chance of making the most marvellous works that any craftsman of my sort ever born had made. While I was speaking so, my Duke twisted about and seemed as if he could not bear to hear me. Then when I had finished, he said to me: "If you will make something for me, I will make so much of you that perhaps you will be left wondering, provided that your works please me; of which I have no doubt at all."

I, poor unlucky man, wishing to show in this marvellous School that since I had been away from it I had laboured in a profession other than that which the said school reckoned with, answered my Duke that I would willingly, either in marble or in bronze, make him a great statue for that fine square of his. To this he answered that he would have liked from me, as a first work, only a Perseus. This was what he had already wanted for a good while; and he begged me to make him a little model of it.
