I willingly set to making the said model, and in a few weeks I had finished it, about a braccio high: this was of yellow wax, quite well finished: it was certainly made with the greatest study and art.

The Duke came to Florence and before I could show him this said model, several days went by; so that it really seemed as if he had never seen or known me, so that I made a bad judgment of my affairs with His Excellency. Still afterwards, one day after dinner, having taken it into his wardrobe, he came to see it together with the Duchess and with a few other lords. As soon as he saw it he liked it and praised it beyond measure: on account of which he gave me a little hope that he understood something about it. After he had considered it a good while, his pleasure in it growing greatly, he said these words: "If you carried out this little model on a great scale, my Benvenuto, this would be the finest work in the square." Then I said: "My most excellent lord, in the square are the works of the great Donatello and of the marvellous Michelangelo, who have been the two greatest men since the ancients. So Your most illustrious Excellency gives great courage to my model, because I am confident I can make the work better than the model, more than three times over."

At this there was no small dispute, because the Duke kept saying that he understood these things very well and that he knew exactly what could be done. At this I told him that my works would decide that question and that doubt of his, and that most certainly I would perform for His Excellency far more than I promised him, and that he should only give me the conveniences for being able to do such a thing, because without those conveniences I could not perform for him the great thing I promised. At this His Excellency told me to make a petition of what I asked of him, and to put in it all my needs, for he would give the fullest orders about it.

Certainly if I had been shrewd enough to bind by contract everything I needed for these works of mine, I should not have had the great troubles which have come on me through my own fault: because his will was plainly very great both in wanting works made and in giving good orders about them. But not knowing that this lord had more of the ways of a merchant than of a duke, I proceeded most freely with His Excellency as with a duke and not as with a merchant. I made him the petitions, to which His Excellency answered most freely. At this I said: "My most singular patron, the true petitions and the true agreements between us do not consist in these words nor in these writings, but the whole of it consists in my succeeding with my works in what I have promised you; and if I succeed, then I promise myself that Your most illustrious Excellency will remember very well what you promise me." At these words His Excellency, taken with both my doing and my saying, he and the Duchess showed me the most immoderate favours that can be imagined in the world.

Chapter 54

Having the greatest desire to begin work, I told His Excellency that I needed a house, of such a kind that I could set myself up in it with my little furnaces, and work in it in clay and bronze, and then, separately, in gold and silver; because I knew that he knew how well fitted I was to serve him in these professions; and I needed convenient rooms to be able to do such a thing. And so that His Excellency should see how much I wanted to serve him, I had already found the house which suited my purpose, and it was in a place which pleased me greatly. And because I did not want to touch His Excellency for money or anything else before he had seen my works, I had brought two jewels from France, with which I begged His Excellency to buy me the said house, and to keep them until with my works and my labours I had earned it. The said jewels were very well worked by the hands of my workmen, under my designs.

When he had looked at them a good while, he said these spirited words, which clothed me in false hope: "Take back your jewels, Benvenuto, because I want you and not them; and you shall have your house free." Next he made me a rescript on a petition of mine, which I have always kept. The said rescript ran thus: "Let the said house be seen, and who has it to sell, and the price asked for it; because we wish to oblige Benvenuto in this." It seemed to me from this rescript that I was sure of the house; because I confidently promised myself that my works would please much more than I had promised.

Next to this, His Excellency had given express orders to a certain majordomo of his who was called Ser Pier Francesco Riccio. He was from Prato, and had been the said Duke's little schoolmaster. I spoke to this beast, and told him everything I needed, because where there was a garden in the said house I meant to make a workshop. At once this man gave the orders to a certain dry, thin paymaster, who was called Lattanzio Gorini. This little manikin, with certain little spidery hands of his and with a little gnat's voice, as quick as a little snail, in an unlucky hour had brought to the house stones, sand and lime enough as would have served, and grudgingly, to build a pigeon hole. Seeing things go so miserably cold, I began to be dismayed; but still I said to myself: "Small beginnings sometimes have great ends;" and I also took a little hope from seeing how many thousands of ducats the Duke had thrown away on certain ugly wretched sculptures, made by the hand of that beast Baccio Bandinello.

Having put heart into myself, I blew up the backside of that Lattanzio Gorini to make him move; I shouted at certain lame asses and at a little blind man who drove them; and with these difficulties, and then with my own money, I had marked out the site of the workshop, and had grubbed up trees and vines: still, as I usually do, boldly, and with a little fury, I went on getting things done.

On the other hand, I was in the hands of Tasso the carpenter, a great friend of mine, and I was having him make certain wooden armatures for beginning the great Perseus. This Tasso was a most excellent able man, I believe the greatest there ever was of his profession: on the other side he was pleasant and cheerful, and every time I went to him, he came to meet me laughing, with a little song in falsetto. And I, who was already more than half in despair, both because I was beginning to hear that things in France were going badly, and because I promised myself little of these things here for their coldness, forced myself always to listen to at least half of that little song of his: still in the end I cheered up somewhat with him, forcing myself to lose as much as I could of four of those desperate thoughts of mine.

Book Two, Chapters 55 to 61

Chapter 55

Having given orders for all the things spoken of above, and beginning to push on so as to get myself ready the sooner for this said undertaking — part of the lime was already slaked — all at once I was sent for by the said majordomo; and going to him, I found him after His Excellency's dinner in the hall called the Clock Hall; and when I came before him, I with the greatest reverence to him, and he with the greatest stiffness to me, he asked me who it was that had put me in that house, and by what authority I had begun building inside it; and that he wondered greatly at me, that I should be so bold and presumptuous. To this I answered that it was His Excellency who had put me in the house, and in the name of His Excellency his own Lordship, who had given the orders to Lattanzio Gorini; and the said Lattanzio had brought stone, sand and lime, and had given the orders for the things I had asked for — "and he said he had orders to that effect from Your Lordship."

When I had said these words, that said beast turned on me with greater sourness than before, and told me that neither I nor any of those I had cited were telling the truth. Then I took offence and said to him: "Majordomo, so long as Your Lordship speaks according to that most noble rank in which you are wrapped, I shall revere you and speak to you with that submission I use to the Duke; but if you do otherwise, I shall speak to you as to a Ser Pier Francesco Riccio." This man fell into such anger that I thought he would go mad on the spot, and gain time on what the heavens had determined for him; and he told me, along with some insulting words, that he wondered greatly at having made me worthy to speak to a man of his sort. At these words I was moved and said: "Now listen to me, Ser Pier Francesco Riccio, and I will tell you who are men of my sort, and who are men of your sort, masters at teaching children to read."

When I had said these words, this man with a hooked-up face raised his voice, repeating those same words more rashly. To which I too, setting my face in fighting order, clothed myself on his account with a little presumption, and said that men of my sort were worthy to speak to popes and to emperors and to great kings; and that of men of my sort perhaps one went about the world, but of men of his sort ten went to a doorway. When he heard these words, he got up on a window ledge that is in that hall; then he told me to repeat once more the words I had said to him; which I repeated more boldly than I had done before, and moreover I said that I no longer cared to serve the Duke, and that I would go back to France, where I could freely return. This beast was left stupefied and the colour of earth, and I in a rage went off with the intention of going off with God; would to God that I had carried it out.

His Excellency the Duke cannot have known at once about this devilment that had happened, because I stayed some few days having laid aside all thought of Florence, except those of my sister and of my little nieces, whom I was setting in order; for with the little I had brought I wanted to leave them settled as best I could, and as soon as possible afterwards I meant to go back to France, and never care to see Italy again. Having made up my mind to get myself away as quickly as I could, and to go without leave of the Duke or of anybody else, one morning the said majordomo of his own accord very humbly sent for me, and set his hand to a certain schoolmasterly oration of his, in which I never heard either method or grace, or skill, or beginning, or end: I only gathered that he said he made profession of being a good Christian, and that he did not want to bear hatred against anybody, and he asked me on the Duke's behalf what salary I wanted for my keep. At this I stayed a little within myself and did not answer, with the plain intention of not wanting to stay. Seeing me hold back without an answer, he still had enough sense to say: "Oh Benvenuto, one answers dukes; and what I say to you I say on His Excellency's behalf." Then I said that since he was saying it on His Excellency's behalf, I would very willingly answer; and I told him to tell His Excellency that I did not want to be made second to any of those men he kept of my profession. The majordomo said: "Bandinello is given two hundred scudi for his keep, so if you are content with that, your salary is settled." I answered that I was content, and that what I deserved beyond it should be given me after my works had been seen, and left the whole of it to the good judgment of His most illustrious Excellency: so against my will I picked up the thread and set myself to work, the Duke continually doing me the most immoderate favours that can be imagined in the world.

Chapter 56

I had had letters very often from France from that most faithful friend of mine Messer Guido Guidi: these letters as yet told me nothing but good; that Ascanio of mine also sent me word telling me to attend to giving myself a good time, and that if anything happened, he would let me know. It was reported to the King that I had set myself to work for the Duke of Florence; and because this man was the best in the world, he said several times: "Why does Benvenuto not come back?" And when those young men of mine were asked about it in particular, both of them told him that I wrote to them that I was so well off, and that they thought I no longer had any wish to come back and serve His Majesty. Finding the King in anger, and hearing these rash words, which never came from me, he said: "Since he has left us with no cause at all, I shall never ask for him again; so let him stay where he is." These thieving murderers, having brought the thing to the point they wanted, because every time I came back to France they went back to being workmen under me as they had been before, whereas, if I did not come back, they were left free and in my place, for this they made every effort so that I should not come back.

Chapter 57

While I was having the workshop built, so as to begin the Perseus in it, I worked in a ground-floor room, in which I made the Perseus in plaster, of the size it was to be, with the idea of moulding it from that plaster one. When I saw that doing it this way was taking me rather a long time, I took another expedient, because by now a bit of a wretched little workshop had been set up, brick upon brick, made with such meanness that it offends me too much to remember it. I began the figure of the Medusa, and made a skeleton of iron; then I began to make it in clay, and when I had made it in clay, I baked it. I was alone with a few little assistants, among whom there was one very beautiful: this was the son of a prostitute, called La Gambetta. I made use of this boy to draw from, because we have no other books that teach us the art except the natural model. I tried to get workmen so as to get this work of mine done quickly, and I could not find any, and by myself alone I could not do everything. There were a few in Florence who would gladly have come, but Bandinello at once prevented their coming; and after making me struggle like this a good while, he told the Duke that I was going about looking for his workmen, because by myself it was never possible that I should know how to put together a large figure. I complained to the Duke of the great annoyance this beast was giving me, and begged him to get me some of those workmen of the Cathedral Works. These words of mine were the cause of making the Duke believe what Bandinello was telling him. Realizing this, I disposed myself to do by myself as much as I could.

And having set myself down to the most extreme labours that can be imagined, while I was toiling day and night at this, my sister's husband fell ill, and in a few days he died. He left me my sister, a young woman, with six daughters small and grown: this was the first great trouble I had in Florence: to be left the father and guide of such a wreck.

Chapter 58

Wanting nothing to go wrong, my garden being loaded with a great deal of rubbish, I called two labourers, who were brought me from the Ponte Vecchio: of these one was an old man of sixty, the other a young man of eighteen. Having kept them about three days, that young man told me that the old man did not want to work and that I would do better to send him away, because not only did he not want to work, he prevented the young man from working: and he told me that the little there was to do, he could do it himself, without throwing money away on other people: this one was called Bernardino Mannellini of the Mugello. Seeing him work so willingly, I asked him whether he would take service with me: at the first word we agreed. This young man looked after a horse for me, worked in the garden, and then set himself to help me in the workshop, so that little by little he began to learn the art with such fineness that I never had a better helper than he.

And making up my mind to do everything with this man, I began to show the Duke that Bandinello was telling lies, and that I would do very well without Bandinello's workmen. At this time I got a little trouble in my kidneys; and because I could not work, I was glad to stay in the Duke's wardrobe with certain young goldsmiths, who were called Gianpagolo and Domenico Poggini, whom I set to making a little gold vase, worked all over in low relief, with figures and other fine ornaments: this was for the Duchess, and Her Excellency had it made to drink water from. She also asked me to make her a gold girdle; and this work too most richly, with jewels and with many pleasing inventions of little masks and other things: this was made for her.

The Duke came into this wardrobe every little while, and took the greatest pleasure in seeing the work go on, and in talking with me. Beginning to get a little better of my kidneys, I had some clay brought to me, and while the Duke was staying there passing the time, I made his portrait, making a head a good deal bigger than life. His Excellency took the greatest pleasure in this work and conceived such affection for me that he told me it would be the greatest pleasure to him if I would settle down to work in the Palace, looking for rooms in the said palace large enough, which I should have fitted up with the furnaces and with everything I needed; because he took the greatest pleasure in such things. To this I told His Excellency that it was not possible, because I should not have finished my works in a hundred years.

Chapter 59

The Duchess did me favours beyond reckoning, and would have liked me to attend to working for her, and not to care either about the Perseus or about anything else. I, who saw myself in these empty favours, knew for certain that my perverse and biting fortune could not hold off from doing me some new murder; because at every hour there came before me the great harm I had done in trying to do so great a good: I mean as to my affairs in France. The King could not swallow that great unhappiness he had at my leaving, and would indeed have liked me to come back, but with his own honour expressly saved: it seemed to me I had many great rights on my side, and I would not humble myself; because I thought that if I had humbled myself to write submissively, those men in the French way would have said that I had been a sinner and that certain foul charges, which had been wrongly laid on me, were true. For this I stood on my dignity, and, as a man who is in the right, wrote stiffly, which was the greatest pleasure those two traitors I had brought up could have: because in writing to them I boasted of the great caresses which had been made me in my own country by a Lord and a Lady, absolute masters of the city of Florence, my own country. As soon as they had one of these letters, they went to the King and pressed His Majesty to give them my castle, in the same way as he had given it to me.

The King, who was a good and admirable person, would never consent to the rash demands of these great little thieves, because he had begun to see what they were maliciously aiming at: and to give them a little hope and to give me occasion to come back at once, he had me written to somewhat angrily by a treasurer of his, who was called Messer Giuliano Buonaccorsi, a Florentine citizen. The letter contained this: that if I wanted to keep up that name of an honest man which I had carried there, since I had gone away with no cause at all, I was truly bound to render an account of everything I had handled and done for His Majesty.

When I had this letter, it gave me such pleasure that if I could have asked with my tongue, I would not have asked either more or less. Setting myself to write, I filled nine sheets of ordinary paper; and in them I narrated in detail all the works I had made and all the accidents I had had over them, and the whole quantity of money that had been spent on the said works, all of which had been paid out by the hands of two notaries and of a treasurer of his, and signed by all those very men who had had it, some of whom had given their goods and the others their labours; and that of this money I had not put a single farthing in my purse, and that for my finished works I had had nothing in the world; only I had brought with me to Italy certain favours and most royal promises, truly worthy of His Majesty. And although I could not boast of having got anything else out of my works except certain salaries appointed me by His Majesty for my keep, and of those too I was still owed more than seven hundred gold scudi, which I left there on purpose, so that they might be sent me for my good return; "so, knowing that certain malicious people out of their own envy have done me some ill service, the truth must always be uppermost: I glory in His Most Christian Majesty, and greed does not move me. Although I know that I have performed for His Majesty much more than I offered to do: and although the promised recompense has not come to me, I care for nothing else in the world, except to remain, in His Majesty's opinion, an honest and clean man, such as I always was. And if any doubt of this were in Your Majesty, at the least sign I will come flying to give account of myself, with my own life: but seeing so little account made of me, I have not wanted to come back and offer myself, knowing that I shall always have bread to spare wherever I go: and when I am called, I shall always answer." There were in the said letter many other particulars worthy of that marvellous King and of the saving of my honour. This letter, before I sent it, I took to my Duke, who was very pleased to see it; then I sent it at once to France, directed to the Cardinal of Ferrara.

Chapter 60

At this time Bernardone Baldini, His Excellency's broker in jewels, had brought from Venice a great diamond, of more than thirty-five carats in weight: Antonio di Vittorio Landi too had an interest in getting the Duke to buy it. This diamond had once been a point stone, but because it did not come out with that shining clearness which is to be desired in such a jewel, the owners of the said diamond had had this said point sliced off, which truly made it good neither for a table stone nor for a point stone. Our Duke, who took great pleasure in jewels, but did not understand them, gave this great rascal Bernardaccio sure hopes of wanting to buy this said diamond. And because this Bernardo was trying to have the honour of it to himself alone, of this cheat he meant to work on the Duke of Florence, he never told anything to his partner, the said Antonio Landi.

This said Antonio had been a great friend of mine ever since boyhood, and because he saw that I was so familiar with my Duke, one day among others he called me aside — it was near midday, and it was at the corner of the Mercato Nuovo — and said this to me: "Benvenuto, I am certain that the Duke will show you a diamond which he shows a wish to buy: you will see a great diamond. Help the sale on; and I tell you that I can let it go for seventeen thousand scudi: I am certain the Duke will want your advice; if you see him well inclined to wanting it, something will be done to enable him to take it." This Antonio showed that he had great confidence in being able to make a bargain of this jewel. I promised him that, if it were shown me and my opinion then asked, I would say everything I understood, without damaging the jewel.

As I have said above, the Duke came every day into that goldsmith's room for several hours; and more than a week after the day Antonio Landi had spoken to me, the Duke one day after dinner showed me this said diamond, which I recognized by the tokens Antonio Landi had told me of, both of the form and of the weight. And because this said diamond was of a water, as I said above, rather cloudy, and for that reason they had had that point sliced off, when I saw it to be of that kind I would certainly have advised against making such an outlay; so, when he showed it me, I asked His Excellency what he wanted me to say, because it was one thing for jewellers to price a jewel after a lord had bought it, and another to put a price on it so that he might buy it. Then His Excellency told me that he had bought it and that I should only say what I thought.

I did not want to fail to point out to him modestly the little I understood of that jewel. He told me to consider the beauty of those great facets it had. Then I said that this was not that great beauty His Excellency imagined and that it was a point stone with its point sliced off. At these words my lord, who saw that I was telling the truth, made a bad grimace and told me to attend to valuing the jewel and to judge what I thought it was worth. I, thinking that, Antonio Landi having offered it me for seventeen thousand scudi, I believed the Duke had had it for fifteen thousand at most, and because of this I, seeing that he took it badly that I told him the truth, thought to keep him in his false opinion, and handing him the diamond, I said: "Eighteen thousand scudi you have spent." At these words the Duke made an outcry, making an O bigger than the mouth of a well, and said: "Now I believe you know nothing about it." I said to him: "Certainly, my lord, you believe wrong: attend to keeping your jewel in credit and I will attend to understanding these things. Tell me at least what you have spent on it, so that I may learn to understand them according to the ways of Your Excellency." The Duke got up with a slightly scornful sneer, and said: "Twenty-five thousand scudi and more, Benvenuto, it costs me;" and went away.

At these words there were present Gianpagolo and Domenico Poggini, goldsmiths; and Bachiacca the embroiderer, who worked in a room next to ours, ran up too at that noise; where I said: "I would never have advised him to buy it; but if he had a mind for it all the same, Antonio Landi eight days ago offered it me for seventeen thousand scudi; I believe I could have got it for fifteen or less. But the Duke wants to keep his jewel in credit; for since Antonio Landi offered it to me for such a price, the devil that Bernardone should have played the Duke so shameful a trick!" And never believing that such a thing was true, as it was, we passed off that simplicity of the Duke's with a laugh.

Chapter 61

Having by now brought the figure of the great Medusa to that point, as I said, I had made its skeleton of iron: then having made it in clay, like an anatomy, and lean by half a finger, I baked it very well; then I put the wax over it and finished it in the way I wanted it to be. The Duke, who had come to see it several times, was so anxious lest it should not come out in bronze for me, that he would have liked me to call in some master to cast it for me. And because His Excellency spoke continually and with the greatest favour of my clever talk, his majordomo, who was continually looking for some snare to make me break my neck, and because he had the authority to command the constables and all the offices of the poor unlucky city of Florence — that a man of Prato, an enemy of ours, the son of a cooper, most ignorant, for having been a rotten schoolmaster to Cosimo de' Medici before he was duke, should have come into such great authority, as I have said — being as watchful as he could to do me harm, and seeing that in no way could he get a hook into me, he thought of a way of doing something.

And going to find the mother of that little assistant of mine, whose name was Cencio, and hers La Gambetta, they arranged, that rascally schoolmaster and that rogue of a whore, to give me a fright, so that by it I should have gone off with God. La Gambetta, following her own trade, came out on the orders of that mad rascal of a schoolmaster majordomo: and because they had also put up the constable, who was a certain Bolognese, whom the Duke afterwards drove out for doing things of this kind; on a Saturday evening, at three hours of the night, the said Gambetta came to find me with her son, and told me that she had kept him shut up several days for my safety. To whom I answered that on my account she should not keep him shut up: and laughing at her whorish trade, I turned to the son in her presence and said to him: "You know, Cencio, whether I have sinned with you;" and he, weeping, said no. Then the mother, shaking her head, said to her son: "Ah, you little rascal, do you think I do not know how it is done?" Then she turned to me, telling me to keep him hidden in the house, because the constable was looking for him, and would take him in any case outside my house; but that in my house they would not touch him. To this I told her that in my house I had my widowed sister with six holy little daughters, and that I did not want anybody in my house. Then she said that the majordomo had given the orders to the constable and that I would be arrested in any case; but since I would not take her son into the house, if I gave her a hundred scudi I need have no further fear of anything, because the majordomo being such a very great friend of hers, I might be sure that she would have him do everything she pleased, provided I gave her the hundred scudi.

I had come into such a fury that I said to her: "Get out of my sight, you shameful whore, for if it were not for the honour of the world and for the innocence of that unhappy son you have there, I would already have cut your throat with this little dagger, which I have laid my hand on two or three times." And with these words, with many rough shoves, I pushed her and her son out of the house.

Book Two, Chapters 62 to 68

Chapter 62

Then, having considered the villainy and the power of that bad schoolmaster, I judged that my best course was to give a little room to that devilment, and early in the morning, having made over to my sister jewels and things worth close on two thousand scudi, I got on horseback and went off towards Venice, and took with me that Bernardino of the Mugello. And when I got to Ferrara, I wrote to His Excellency the Duke that although I had gone without being sent, I would come back without being called.

Then, having got to Venice, considering in how many different ways my cruel fortune tormented me, nevertheless finding myself sound and strong, I made up my mind to skirmish with her as I usually do. And while I was going about thinking over my affairs like this, passing my time in that beautiful and very rich city, having paid my respects to that marvellous painter Titian and to Jacopo Sansovino, an able sculptor and architect of ours from Florence, very well kept by the Signory of Venice, and because we had known each other in our youth in Rome and in Florence as a Florentine of ours, these two men of talent made a great deal of me. The next day I met Messer Lorenzo de' Medici, who at once took me by the hand with the greatest welcome that can be seen in the world, because we had known each other in Florence when I was making the coins for Duke Alessandro, and afterwards in Paris, when I was in the King's service. He was staying in the house of Messer Giuliano Buonaccorsi, and having nowhere else to go and pass the time without the greatest danger to himself, he stayed most of the time in my house, watching me work at those great works.

And as I say, because of this old acquaintance, he took me by the hand and led me to his house, where there was the Prior of the Strozzi, brother of Signor Piero, and rejoicing, they asked me how long I meant to stay in Venice, believing that I meant to go back to France. To which lords I said that I had left Florence for such an occasion as I have described above, and that within two or three days I meant to go back to Florence to serve my great Duke. When I said these words, the Prior and Messer Lorenzo turned on me with such sternness that I was greatly afraid, and they said to me: "You would do better to go back to France, where you are rich and known; for if you go back to Florence, you will lose everything you had gained in France, and out of Florence you will get nothing but unhappiness." I did not answer their words, and having left the next day as secretly as I could, I went back towards Florence.

Meanwhile the devilments had ripened, because I had written to my great Duke all about the occasion which had carried me off to Venice. And with his usual prudence and severity, I visited him without any ceremony; having stayed a while with the said severity, he then turned to me pleasantly and asked me where I had been. To whom I answered that my heart had never moved a finger's breadth away from His most illustrious Excellency, though for certain just occasions it had been necessary for me to take my body a little way off wandering. Then, becoming more pleasant, he began to ask me about Venice and so we talked a while; then at last he told me to attend to working and to finish his Perseus for him. So I went home cheerful and happy, and cheered my family, that is my sister with her six daughters, and having taken up my works again, I carried them forward with as much diligence as I could.

Chapter 63

And the first work I cast in bronze was that great head, the portrait of His Excellency, which I had made in clay in the goldsmith's room while I had trouble in my back. This was a work that pleased, and I made it for no other reason than to make trial of the clays for casting bronze. And although I saw that that admirable Donatello had made his works in bronze, which he had cast with the clay of Florence, it seemed to me that he had brought them off with the greatest difficulty; and thinking that this came from a defect in the clay, before I set about casting my Perseus, I chose to take these first pains; by which I found that the clay was good, although it had not been well understood by that admirable Donatello, because I saw his works brought off with the greatest difficulty. So, as I say above, by the strength of art I compounded the clay, which served me very well; and, as I say, with it I cast the said head; but because I had not yet made the furnace, I made use of the furnace of Master Zanobi di Pagno the bellfounder.

And seeing that the head had come out clean, I at once set about making a little furnace in the workshop the Duke had made me, by my own order and design, in the very house he had given me; and having made the furnace at once, with all the diligence I could, I got ready to cast the statue of the Medusa, which is that twisted woman who is under the feet of the Perseus. And this cast being a most difficult thing, I did not want to fail in all those cares I had learned, so that no error should be made; and so the first cast I made in that little furnace of mine came out well in the highest degree, and it was so clean that it did not seem right to my friends that I should have to clean it up at all; a thing which certain Germans and Frenchmen have found, who talk and boast of very fine secrets for casting bronzes without cleaning up; a thing truly for madmen; because bronze, after it is cast, has to be closed up with hammers and with chisels, as the most marvellous ancients did, and as the moderns have done too, I mean those moderns who have known how to work in bronze.

This cast pleased His most illustrious Excellency greatly, and he came several times as far as my house to see it, giving me great courage to do well. But that raging envy of Bandinello's was able to do so much, with so much pressing at the ears of His most illustrious Excellency, that he made him think that even if I cast some of these statues, I should never put them together, because it was a new art with me; and that His Excellency ought to look out well not to throw away his money. These words were able to do so much in those glorious ears that some of my expenses for workmen were cut down; on account of which I was forced to complain boldly to His Excellency: and one morning, waiting for him in the Via de' Servi, I said to him: "My lord, I am not helped in my needs, so that I suspect Your Excellency has no confidence in me; therefore I tell you again that I am confident I can bring off this work three times better than the model was, just as I promised you."

Chapter 64

Having said these words to His Excellency, and seeing that they bore no fruit at all, because I got no answer out of them, at once my anger grew, together with an unbearable passion, and I began to speak to the Duke again and said to him: "My lord, this city has truly always been the school of the greatest talents; but once a man is known, having learned something, if he wishes to increase the glory of his city and of his glorious Prince, it is well for him to go and work elsewhere. And that this is true, my lord, I know that Your Excellency has known who Donatello was, and who the great Leonardo da Vinci was, and who the admirable Michelangelo Buonarroti is now. These men increase the glory of Your Excellency by their talents; on account of which I too hope to do my part; so, my lord, let me go. But let Your Excellency take good care not to let Bandinello go, rather give him always more than he asks you for; because if he goes abroad, his ignorance is so presumptuous that he is fit to bring disgrace on this most noble School. Now give me leave, my lord, and I ask nothing else for my labours up to now than the favour of Your most illustrious Excellency."

Seeing me so resolute, His Excellency turned to me with a little scorn, saying: "Benvenuto, if you have a mind to finish the work, nothing shall be wanting." Then I thanked him, and said that my only desire was to show those envious men that I was confident I could carry out the work I had promised. So having got away from His Excellency, I was given a little help; on account of which I was forced to put my hand in my own purse, wanting my work to go a little more than at a walk.

And because in the evening I always went to sit up in His Excellency's wardrobe, where there were Domenico and Gianpagolo Poggini, his brother, who were working on a gold vase, which has been spoken of before, for the Duchess, and a gold girdle; His Excellency had also had me make a little model of a pendant, in which that great diamond was to be set which Bernardone and Antonio Landi had made him buy. And although I dodged wanting to do such a thing, the Duke with so many fine pleasantries made me work on it every evening until four hours of the night. He also pressed me in the most pleasant ways to work on it by day as well; to which I would never consent; and for this I believed for certain that His Excellency was angry with me. And one evening among others, having arrived somewhat later than usual, the Duke said to me: "You are the ill-come." At which words I said: "My lord, that is not my name, because my name is Benvenuto, the welcome; and because I think Your Excellency is joking with me, I will go no further into it." At this the Duke said that he spoke in cursed earnest and was not joking, and that I should take good care what I was doing, because it had come to his ears that, taking advantage of his favour, I was having now this and now that got for me.

At these words I begged His most illustrious Excellency to be so good as to name me one single man from whom I had ever got anything in the world. At once he turned on me in anger and said: "Go and give back what you have of Bernardone's: there is one for you." At this I said: "My lord, I thank you, and I beg you to be so good as to hear me for four words: it is true that he lent me a pair of old scales and two anvils and three little hammers, which pieces of gear it is fifteen days ago today that I told his Giorgio of Cortona to send for them; on which the said Giorgio came for them himself; and if Your most illustrious Excellency ever finds that, from the day I was born until now, I have ever had anything of anybody's in that fashion, whether in Rome or in France, let those who have reported those things to you, or others, be asked; and if you find it true, punish me by the measure of the coals."

Seeing me in the greatest passion, the Duke, as a most discerning and affectionate Lord, turned to me and said: "It is not said to those who do not commit the faults; so, if it is as you say, I shall always be glad to see you, as I have been in the past." At this I said: "Let Your Excellency know that Bernardone's villainies force me to ask you and to beg you to tell me what you spent on the great diamond, the point stone with its point sliced off: because I hope to show you why this bad wretch of a man is trying to put me in your disgrace." Then His Excellency said to me: "The diamond cost me 25 thousand ducats: why do you ask me?" "Because, my lord, on such a day, at such an hour, at the corner of the Mercato Nuovo, Antonio di Vittorio Landi told me to try to make a bargain with Your most illustrious Excellency, and at the first asking he asked sixteen thousand ducats for it: now Your Excellency knows what you bought it for. And that this is true, ask Ser Domenico Poggini and Gianpagolo his brother, who are here; for I told them at once, and afterwards I never spoke of it again, because Your Excellency said that I knew nothing about it; and I thought you wanted to keep it in credit. Know, my lord, that I do understand these things; and as to the other matter, I make profession of being as honest a man as any born into the world, be he who he may. I shall not try to rob you of eight or ten thousand ducats at a time; rather I shall try to earn them by my labours: and I settled to serve Your Excellency as sculptor, goldsmith and master of the mint; and as for reporting other men's affairs to you, never. And what I am telling you now, I tell you in my own defence, and I do not want a quarter of it: and I tell you of it in the presence of so many honest men who are here, so that Your most illustrious Excellency may not believe everything Bernardone says."

At once the Duke rose in anger and sent for Bernardone, who was forced to run as far as Venice, he and Antonio Landi; which Antonio told me that he had not meant to speak of that diamond. They went and came back from Venice, and I found the Duke, and said: "My lord, what I told you is true, and what Bernardone told you about the gear was not true; and you would do well to make trial of it, and I will set off for the constable's." At these words the Duke turned to me, saying: "Benvenuto, attend to being an honest man, as you have done in the past, and never fear anything." The thing went off in smoke and I never heard it spoken of again.

I attended to finishing his jewel; and having taken it finished to the Duchess one day, she herself told me that she valued my workmanship as much as the diamond, which that wretch Bernardone had made her buy, and she wanted me to fasten it on her breast with my own hand, and she gave me a thickish pin into my hand, and with that I fastened it on her, and I went away with much good favour from her. Afterwards I heard that they had had it reset by a German or some other foreigner, if the truth be told, because the said Bernardone said that the said diamond would show better set with less work about it.

Chapter 65

Domenico and Gianpagolo Poggini, goldsmiths and brothers, were working, as I think I have said, in the wardrobe of His most illustrious Excellency, from my designs, on certain little gold vases chased with scenes of little figures in low relief and other things of much importance. And because I said several times to the Duke: "My lord, if Your most illustrious Excellency would pay me several workmen, I would make you the coins for your mint and the medals with the head of Your most illustrious Excellency, which I would make in competition with the ancients and would hope to surpass them: because since the time I made the medals of Pope Clement I have learned so much that I would do much better than those: and so I would do better than the coins I made for Duke Alessandro, which are still held to be beautiful; and so I would make you great vases of gold and silver, as I made so many for that admirable King Francis of France, only because of the great conveniences he gave me, and no time was ever lost over the great colossi or over the other statues." To these words of mine the Duke said: "Do it, and I shall see;" and he never gave me either conveniences or any help.

One day His most illustrious Excellency had several pounds of silver given me and said: "This is silver from my mines, make me a fine vase." And because I did not want to leave my Perseus behind and yet had a great wish to serve him, I gave it to be made, with my designs and little wax models, to a certain rascal called Piero di Martino, a goldsmith: who began it badly and moreover did not work at it, so that I lost more time over it than if I had made the whole of it with my own hand. So having tormented me for several months, and seeing that the said Piero was not working at it, nor having it worked at either, I had it given back to me, and I had great difficulty in getting back, along with the body of the vase badly begun, as I said, the rest of the silver I had given him. The Duke, who heard something of these disputes, sent for the vase and for the models and never told me either why or how; it is enough that with certain designs of mine he had them made by various people both in Venice and elsewhere, and he was very badly served.

The Duchess often told me to work for her at goldsmith's work: to whom I said several times that the world knew very well, and all Italy, that I was a good goldsmith; but that Italy had never seen works of sculpture from my hand: "and in the profession certain raging sculptors, laughing at me, call me the new sculptor; to whom I hope to show that I am an old sculptor, if God will give me so much grace that I may show my Perseus finished in that honoured square of His most illustrious Excellency."

And having withdrawn home, I attended to working day and night, and did not let myself be seen at the Palace. And thinking still to keep myself in the good graces of the Duchess, I had made for her certain little vases, as big as a two-farthing pot, in silver, with fine little masks in a very rare fashion, in the antique style; and having taken her the said little vases, she gave me the most gracious welcome that can be imagined in the world and paid me for my silver and gold that I had put into them. And I still commended myself to Her most illustrious Excellency, begging her to tell the Duke that I had little help for so great a work, and that Her most illustrious Excellency ought to tell the Duke not to give so much credit to that evil tongue of Bandinello's, with which he hindered me from finishing my Perseus. At these tearful words of mine the Duchess shrugged her shoulders and still said to me: "Certainly the Duke ought surely to know that this Bandinello of his is worth nothing."

Chapter 66

I stayed at home, and rarely presented myself at the Palace, and worked with great diligence to finish my work; and I had to pay the workmen out of my own pocket; because, the Duke having had certain workmen paid me by Lattanzio Gorini for about eighteen months, and having grown tired of it, he had the orders taken away from me, on account of which I asked the said Lattanzio why he did not pay me. He answered me, waving certain little spidery hands of his, with a little gnat's voice: "Why do you not finish this work of yours? People believe you will never finish it." I answered him at once in anger and said: "May a plague come on you and on all those who do not believe that I shall finish it."

And so in despair I went back home to my ill-fortuned Perseus, and not without tears, because there came back into my memory the fine state I had left in Paris in the service of that marvellous King Francis, with whom I had everything to spare, and here everything was wanting to me. And several times I was ready to throw myself into despair: and once among others I got on a good little horse of mine, and put a hundred scudi at my side, and went off to Fiesole to see a natural son of mine, whom I kept at nurse with a woman who was godmother to him, the wife of a workman of mine. And when I got to my little son I found him in good health, and I, so discontented, kissed him; and when I wanted to leave, he would not let me, because he held me tight with his little hands and with a fury of weeping and screaming, which at that age of about two years was a more than marvellous thing.

And because I had made up my mind that, if I found Bandinello, who used to go every evening to that farm of his above San Domenico, in my desperation I meant to throw him to the ground, so I tore myself away from my baby, leaving him with that broken weeping. And coming on towards Florence, when I got to the square of San Domenico, just then Bandinello was coming into the square from the other side. Having at once made up my mind to do that bloody work, I came up to him, and raising my eyes I saw him without weapons, on a wretched mule like an ass, and he had with him a little boy of about ten; and as soon as he saw me, he turned the colour of death, and trembled from his head to his feet. I, seeing how base the work would be, said: "Have no fear, vile coward, for I do not want to make you worthy of my blows." He looked at me submissively and said nothing. Then I took my courage back, and thanked God, who by His true power had not chosen that I should commit such a disorder.

So, freed from that devilish fury, my spirit rose and I said to myself: "If God gives me the grace to finish my work, I hope with it to kill all my rascally enemies; whereby I shall take a much greater and more glorious revenge than if I had vented myself on one alone;" and with this good resolution I went back home.

At the end of three days I heard that that woman, my child's godmother, had smothered my only little son; which gave me such grief as I never felt greater. Yet I knelt on the ground, and not without tears, as I usually do, thanked my God, saying: "My Lord, You gave him to me, and now You have taken him from me, and for all of it I thank You with all my heart." And although the great grief had all but bewildered me, still, as I usually do, making a virtue of necessity, I went about settling myself as best I could.

Chapter 67

At this time a young man had left Bandinello, whose name was Francesco, son of Matteo the smith. This said young man had me asked whether I would give him work; and I was content, and set him to cleaning up the figure of the Medusa, which was already cast. This young man, a fortnight later, told me that he had spoken with his master, that is Bandinello, and that he said to me on his behalf that, if I wanted to make a figure in marble, he sent to offer me the present of a fine piece of marble. At once I said: "Tell him that I accept it; and the marble might turn out ill for him, because he goes on provoking me, and does not remember the great danger he was in with me in the square of San Domenico: now tell him that I want it in any case. I never speak of him and this beast is always troubling me: and I believe that you have come to work with me sent by him, only to spy on my affairs. Oh go, and tell him that I shall want the marble in spite of him; and go back to him."

Chapter 68

Having gone many days without letting myself be seen at the Palace, I went there one morning, when that whim took me, and the Duke had almost finished dining, and, from what I heard, His Excellency had that morning been talking and speaking very well of me, and among other things he had praised me greatly for setting jewels; and because of this, as soon as the Duchess saw me, she had me called by Messer Sforza; and when I came near Her most illustrious Excellency, she begged me to set a little point diamond in a ring for her, and told me that she meant to wear it always on her finger; and she gave me the measure and the diamond, which was worth about a hundred scudi, and begged me to do it quickly.

At once the Duke began to talk with the Duchess and said to her: "Certainly Benvenuto was without an equal in that art; but now that he has left it off, I believe that making a little ring as you would want it would be too great a labour for him: so I beg you not to tire him with this small thing, which for him would be a great one, since he is out of practice." At these words I thanked the Duke, and then begged him to let me do this little service for the lady Duchess: and having set my hands to it at once, in a few days I had it finished. The ring was for the little finger of the hand: so I made four round boys with four little masks, which things made up the said little ring: and I also fitted in some enamelled fruits and little bindings; so that the jewel and the ring showed themselves very well together. And I took it at once to the Duchess: who with kindly words told me that I had made her a very beautiful piece of work, and that she would remember me. The said little ring she sent as a present to King Philip, and afterwards she was always ordering something from me, but so affectionately that I always did my best to serve her, although I saw little money; and God knows whether I had great need of it, because I wanted to finish my Perseus, and I had found certain young men who helped me, whom I paid out of my own pocket; and I began again to let myself be seen more often than I had done in the past.

Book Two, Chapters 69 to 75

Chapter 69

One holiday among others I went to the Palace after dinner, and having got to the Clock Hall, I saw the door of the wardrobe open, and having come a little nearer, the Duke called me, and with a pleasant welcome said to me: "You are welcome: look at that case, which Signor Stefano da Palestrina has sent me as a present; open it and let us see what it is." Opening it at once, I said to the Duke: "My lord, this is a figure of Greek marble and it is a marvellous thing: I say that for a boy figure I do not remember ever having seen among the antiques so beautiful a work, nor of so beautiful a manner; so that I offer Your most illustrious Excellency to restore it for you, both the head and the arms and the feet. And I will make it an eagle, so that it may be christened for a Ganymede. And although it is not fitting for me to patch up statues, because it is an art for certain cobblers, who do it very badly; still the excellence of this great master calls me to serve him." It pleased the Duke greatly that the statue should be so beautiful, and he asked me about a great many things, saying to me: "Tell me, my Benvenuto, exactly in what such great excellence of this master consists, which gives you such wonder." Then I showed His most illustrious Excellency in the best way I knew, so as to make him understand such beauty and such excellence of intelligence, and such a rare manner; on which things I had discoursed a great deal, and I did it all the more willingly, knowing that His Excellency took the greatest pleasure in it.

Chapter 70

While I was so pleasantly entertaining the Duke, it happened that a page came out of the wardrobe and that, as the said page came out, Bandinello came in. When the Duke saw him, he was half put out, and with a stern face said to him: "What are you doing?" The said Bandinello, without answering anything, at once cast his eyes on that case where the said statue was uncovered, and with an ugly sneer of his, shaking his head, he said, turning towards the Duke: "My lord, these are some of those things I have so often spoken of to Your most illustrious Excellency. Know that these ancients understood nothing about anatomy, and for this reason their works are all full of errors." I stayed quiet and paid no attention to anything he said, but rather turned my back on him. As soon as this beast had finished his disagreeable chatter, the Duke said: "Oh Benvenuto, this is quite the contrary of what with so many fine reasons you have just now shown me so well: so defend it a little."

At these ducal words, put to me with so much pleasantness, I answered at once and said: "My lord, Your most illustrious Excellency must know that Baccio Bandinelli is composed entirely of ill, and so he has always been; so that whatever he looks at, at once to his disagreeable eyes, although things are in the highest degree wholly good, at once they turn into the worst of ill. But I, who am drawn only to the good, see the truth more piously; so that what I have said of this most beautiful statue to Your most illustrious Excellency is all of it the pure truth, and what Bandinello has said of it is all of it that ill alone, of which he is composed." The Duke stood listening to me with much pleasure, and while I was saying these things, Bandinello twisted about and made the ugliest faces with his face, which was very ugly, that can be imagined in the world.

All at once the Duke moved off, setting out through certain ground-floor rooms, and the said Bandinello followed him. The chamberlains took me by the cloak and started me off after them and so we followed the Duke, until His most illustrious Excellency, having reached a room, sat down, and Bandinello and I stood one on the right and one on the left of His most illustrious Excellency. I stayed quiet, and those who were around, several servants of His Excellency, all stared at Bandinello, sneering a little at each other over those words I had said to him in that room upstairs.

So the said Bandinello began to speak and said: "My lord, when I uncovered my Hercules and Cacus, certainly I believe that more than a hundred wretched sonnets were made about me, which said the worst that can be imagined in the world by that vile mob." Then I answered and said: "My lord, when our Michelangelo Buonarroti uncovered his Sacristy, where so many beautiful figures were seen, this admirable and skilled School, the friend of truth and of good, made more than a hundred sonnets about it, each vying with the other to see who could say best of it: and just as Bandinello's work deserved all the ill he says was said of it, so Buonarroti's deserved all the good that was said of it."

At these words of mine Bandinello came into such a rage that he burst, and turned on me and said: "And what fault would you know how to find with it?" "I will tell you if you have patience enough to be able to listen to me." He said: "Now say on." The Duke and the others who were there all stood attentive. I began and first said: "Know that it grieves me to have to tell you the faults of that work of yours, but it is not I who will tell you such things, rather I will tell you everything that this most skilled School says." And because this wretched man now said something disagreeable and now made gestures with his hands and with his feet, he made me come into such anger that I began in a far more disagreeable fashion than, if he had behaved otherwise, I would have done:

"This skilled School says that if Hercules' hair were shorn off, there would not be skull enough left to hold his brains in; and that as for that face of his, it cannot be told whether it is a man's or a lion-ox's; and that it is not attending to what it is doing, and that it is badly stuck on the neck, with so little art and with so little grace that worse was never seen; and that those wretched shoulders of his are like the two saddlebows of an ass's packsaddle; and that his breasts and the rest of those muscles are not taken from a man, but are taken from a great sack full of melons, set upright and propped against a wall. So the back seems taken from a sack full of long gourds; the two legs, and it cannot be told in what way they are stuck onto that ugly trunk; because it cannot be told on which leg he is resting or on which he is making some show of strength; nor is it seen either that he rests on both, as those masters who know something have sometimes been used to do; it is plainly seen that it falls forward more than a third of a braccio: which alone is the greatest and the most unbearable error made by those wretched twopenny masters of the common sort. Of the arms they say that they are both of them stretched down without any grace, nor is any art seen in them, as if you had never seen living naked bodies, and that Hercules' right leg and Cacus's share the calves of their legs between them; so that if one of the two were to move away from the other, not only one of them, but both of them would be left without calves on the side where they touch; and they say that one of Hercules' feet is buried, and that the other seems to have a fire under it."

Chapter 71

This man could not stand still enough to have patience for me to tell the great faults of the Cacus as well; one reason being that I was telling the truth, the other that I was making it plainly known to the Duke and to the others who were present with us, who made the greatest signs and gestures of wonder and of knowing then that I was telling the very truth. All at once this wretched man said: "Ah, wicked tongue, and where do you leave my draughtsmanship?" I said that whoever drew well could never work badly — "so I shall believe that your draughtsmanship is like your works." Now, seeing those ducal faces and the others, who were tearing him to pieces with their looks and with their gestures, he let himself be too much overcome by his insolence, and turning on me with that most ugly face of his, all at once he said to me: "Oh, hold your tongue, you filthy sodomite."

The Duke at that word knitted his brows sharply at him, and the others closed their mouths and frowned at him. I, who felt myself so wickedly insulted, driven by fury, and all at once, ran for a remedy and said: "You madman, you go outside all bounds: but would to God that I knew how to practise so noble an art, because we read that Jove practised it with Ganymede in paradise, and here on earth the greatest emperors and the greatest kings of the world practise it. I am a low and humble little man, who neither could nor would know how to meddle with so marvellous a thing." At this nobody could contain himself, and the Duke and the others raised the greatest roar of laughter that can be imagined in the world.

And although I showed myself so pleasant, know, kind readers, that inside my heart was bursting, considering that a man, the filthiest villain that was ever born into the world, should be so bold, in the presence of so great a prince, as to say to me so great and so gross an insult; but know that he insulted the Duke and not me; because, if I had been out of so great a presence, I would have struck him down dead.

Seeing, this filthy clumsy rascal, that the laughter of those lords did not stop, he began, to turn them aside from so much mockery of him, to start on a new subject, saying: "This Benvenuto goes about boasting that I have promised him a marble." At these words I said at once: "What! did you not send to tell me by Francesco di Matteo the smith, your workman, that if I wanted to work in marble, you wanted to give me a marble? And I have accepted it, and I want it." Then he said: "Oh, reckon that you will never have it." At once I, who was full of rage over the unjust insults said to me before, having lost my reason and being blinded by the presence of the Duke, said in great fury: "I tell you expressly that if you do not send me the marble as far as my house, look for another world, because in this one I will let the wind out of you in any case." At once, realizing that I was in the presence of so great a Duke, I turned humbly to His Excellency and said: "My lord, one madman makes a hundred; the madnesses of this man had made me lose sight of the glory of Your most illustrious Excellency and of myself; so forgive me."

Then the Duke said to Bandinello: "Is it true that you promised him the marble?" The said Bandinello said that it was true. The Duke said to me: "Go to the Cathedral Works, and take one to your liking." I said that he had promised to send it to me at my house. The words were terrible; and I would not have it in any other way. The next morning a marble was brought me at home; and I asked who was sending it me: and they said that Bandinello was sending it me, and that this was the marble he had promised me.

Chapter 72

At once I had it carried into the workshop and began to chisel it; and while I worked, I was making the model: and so great was the wish I had to work in marble that I could not wait to settle on making a model with that judgment such an art requires. And because I heard it all creaking, I repented several times of ever having begun to work at it: still I got out of it what I could, which is the Apollo and Hyacinth, which can still be seen unfinished in my workshop. And while I was working at it, the Duke came to my house, and often said to me: "Leave the bronze alone a little and work a little in marble, so that I may see you." At once I took up the marble tools, and worked away with confidence. The Duke asked me about the model I had made for the said marble; to whom I said: "My lord, this marble is all cracked, but in spite of it I shall get something out of it; so I could not settle on a model, but I shall go on doing the best I can."

With great speed the Duke had a piece of Greek marble brought me from Rome, so that I might restore his antique Ganymede, which was the cause of the said quarrel with Bandinello. When the Greek marble came, I considered that it was a pity to make pieces of it to make the head and the arms and the other things for the Ganymede; and I provided myself with other marble, and for that piece of Greek marble I made a little model in wax, to which I gave the name Narcissus. And because this marble had two holes which went in more than a quarter of a braccio deep and were two good fingers wide, for this reason I made the attitude that is seen, to defend myself from those holes, so that I had got them out of my figure. But those many tens of years in which the rain had fallen on it, because those holes were always left full of water, the water had penetrated so far that the said marble was weakened; and as it were rotten in that part of the upper hole; and it showed itself afterwards, when there came that great flood of the Arno, which rose in my workshop more than a braccio and a half. And because the said Narcissus was set on a square of wood, the said water made it turn over, on account of which it broke across the breast, and I fastened it together again; and so that that crack of the joining should not be seen, I made it that garland of flowers which is seen that it has on its breast; and I went on finishing it at certain hours before daybreak, or truly on holidays, only so as not to lose time from my work of the Perseus.

And because one morning among others I was fitting up certain little chisels of mine to work at it, a very fine splinter of steel shot into my right eye; and it had gone so far inside the pupil that in no way could it be got out. I thought for certain that I should lose the sight of that eye. At the end of several days I called Master Raffaello de' Pilli, a surgeon, who took two live pigeons, and making me lie on my back on a table, he took the said pigeons and with a little knife pierced a small vein they have in their wings, so that the blood ran down into my eye; by which blood I at once felt myself relieved and in the space of two days the splinter of steel came out and I was left free and improved in my sight. And when the feast of Saint Lucy came, which we were within three days of, I made an eye of gold out of a French scudo, and had it presented to her by one of my six nieces, daughters of Liperata my sister, who was about ten years old, and with it I thanked God and Saint Lucy; and for a while I would not work on the said Narcissus, but carried the Perseus forward with the difficulties spoken of above, and I had made up my mind to finish it and go off with God.

Chapter 73

Having cast the Medusa, and it having come out well, with great hope I brought my Perseus, which I had in wax, to its finish, and I promised myself that it would come out for me as well in bronze as the said Medusa had done. And because, seeing it well finished in wax, it showed itself so beautiful that (the Duke seeing it in that state and it seeming beautiful to him; whether it was that somebody had given the Duke to believe that it could not come out so in bronze, or that the Duke imagined it of himself; and coming to the house more often than he used to) once among other times he said to me: "Benvenuto, this figure cannot come out for you in bronze, because the art does not promise you so much."

At these words of His Excellency I was greatly offended, saying: "My lord, I see that Your most illustrious Excellency has this very little faith in me: and this I believe comes because Your most illustrious Excellency believes too much those who say so much ill of me, or truly you do not understand these things." He hardly let me finish my words before he said: "I make profession of understanding them, and I understand them very well." I answered at once and said: "Yes, like a Lord, and not like an artist; because if Your most illustrious Excellency understood them in the way you believe you understand them, you would believe me by means of the fine bronze head I made you, so large, the portrait of Your most illustrious Excellency, which has been sent to Elba, and by means of my having restored for you the fine marble Ganymede with such extreme difficulty, in which I have endured much greater labour than if I had made the whole of it new; and again by my having cast the Medusa, which is to be seen here in Your Excellency's presence: a cast so difficult that in it I have done what no other man ever did before me, in this devilish art.

"See, my lord: I have made the furnace new, in a way different from other men's; because I, besides many other differences and skilful contrivances that are seen in it, have made two outlets in it for the bronze, because this difficult and twisted figure could not in any other way possibly have come out; and only by these understandings of mine has it come out so well, which thing none of these practised men of this art ever believed. And know, my lord, for a certainty, that all the great and most difficult works I made in France under that most marvellous King Francis, all of them came out for me very well, only because of the great courage that good King always gave me with those great allowances, and in satisfying me with as many workmen as I asked for; there were times when I made use of more than forty workmen, all of my own choosing; and for these reasons I made you such a quantity of works in so short a time. Now, my lord, believe me and help me with the help I need, because I hope to bring to an end a work that will please you; whereas, if Your most illustrious Excellency casts down my spirit and does not give me the help I need, it is impossible that either I or any man in the world should ever make anything that would be good."

Chapter 74

With great difficulty the Duke stood listening to these arguments of mine, for he kept turning now one way and now another; and I in despair, poor wretch, having remembered the fine state I had in France, was afflicting myself so. All at once the Duke said: "Now tell me, Benvenuto, how is it possible that that fine head of the Medusa, which is up there on high in the hand of the Perseus, can ever come out?" At once I said: "Now see, my lord, that if Your most illustrious Excellency had that knowledge of the art which you say you have, you would not be afraid for that fine head you speak of, that it should not come out; but you would rightly have to be afraid for this right foot, which is down here so far off."

At these words of mine the Duke, half angry, at once turned to certain lords who were with His most illustrious Excellency and said: "I believe this Benvenuto does it out of a know-all habit of contradicting everything;" and at once turning to me half in mockery, at which all those who were present did the same, he began to say: "I will have patience enough with you to listen to what reason you will be able to think up to give me, so that I may believe it." Then I said: "I will give you a reason so true that Your Excellency will be quite convinced by it;" and I began: "Know, my lord, that the nature of fire is to go upwards, and for this I promise you that that head of the Medusa will come out very well; but because the nature of fire is not to go downwards, and one has to force it down six braccia by strength of art, for this living reason I tell Your most illustrious Excellency that it is impossible that that foot should come out; but it will be easy for me to make it again."

The Duke said: "Oh why did you not think that that foot should come out in the way you say the head will come out?" I said: "It would have been necessary to make the furnace much larger, where I could have made a branch for the pouring, as thick as my leg, and with that weight of hot metal I would have forced it to go there, whereas my branch, which goes as far as the feet those six braccia I speak of, is not thicker than two fingers. Still, it was not worth the price; for it will easily be put right. But when my mould is more than half full, as I hope, from that middle upwards, the fire rising according to its nature, this head of the Perseus and that of the Medusa will come out very well: so be quite certain of it."

When I had said him these fine reasons of mine, with many other endless ones which, so as not to be too long, I do not write down, the Duke, shaking his head, went off with God.

Chapter 75

Having given myself confidence out of my own good spirit, and having driven away all those thoughts which presented themselves before me from hour to hour (which often made me weep bitterly and repent of my leaving France, for having come to Florence, my sweet native place, only to give alms to my said six nieces, and for such a good deed I saw myself shown the beginning of so much evil), for all this I certainly promised myself that, on finishing my begun work of the Perseus, all my troubles ought to be turned into the highest pleasure and glorious good.

And so, having got my vigour back, with all my strength, both of my body and of my purse, although little money was left me, I began to procure for myself several stacks of pine wood, which I got from the pine wood of the Seristori, near Montelupo; and while I was waiting for it, I clothed my Perseus with those clays which I had prepared several months before, so that they should have their proper seasoning. And when I had made its coat of clay, which is called a coat in the art, and had very well armed it and girded it about with irons with great diligence, I began with a slow fire to draw out the wax, which came out through many vents I had made, since the more of them one makes, the better the moulds fill.

And when I had finished getting the wax out, I made a sleeve round my Perseus, that is round the said mould, of bricks, weaving one over the other, and I left a great many spaces, where the fire could better exhale: then I began to put wood in it, quite gently, and I kept fire on it two days and two nights continually; so that, all the wax having been got out of it, and the said mould having afterwards been very well baked, I at once began to empty out the pit to bury my mould in, with all those fine methods that the fine art commands us. When I had finished emptying out the said pit, then I took my mould, and by strength of windlasses and of good ropes I carefully set it upright; and having hung it a braccio above the level of my furnace, having got it very well upright so that it hung exactly in the middle of its pit, very gently I let it come down right to the bottom of the furnace, and it settled with all the care that can be imagined in the world.

And when I had done this fine labour, I began to pack it round with the same earth I had taken out of it; and as I raised the earth, I put in its vents, which were little pipes of baked clay that are used for sinks and other like things. As soon as I saw that I had it very well firm, and that that way of packing it with putting those pipes properly in their places, and that those workmen of mine had well understood my method, which was very different from that of all the other masters of such a profession; being sure that I could trust them, I turned to my furnace, which I had had filled with many pigs of copper and other pieces of bronze; and having arranged them one over another in the way the art shows us, that is raised up, making a way for the flames of the fire, so that the said metal takes its heat sooner and with it melts and reduces itself to a bath, so with good spirit I said that they should set fire to the said furnace.

And putting in that pine wood, which by reason of that unctuousness of the resin the pine makes, and because my little furnace was so well made, it worked so well that I was forced to run now to one side and now to another with such labour that it was unbearable for me; and still I forced myself on. And in addition it came upon me that fire caught in the workshop, and we were afraid that the roof would fall on us; on the other side, towards the garden, the sky drove so much water and wind at me that it was cooling my furnace.

So, fighting with these perverse accidents several hours, the labour forced me so much more than my strong constitution could stand up to, so that an intermittent fever leapt on me, the greatest that can be imagined in the world, on account of which I was forced to go and throw myself into bed. And so, very discontented, being forced to go, I turned to all those who were helping me, who were about ten or more, among masters of bronze casting and labourers and countrymen and my own particular workmen of the shop; among whom was one Bernardino Mannellini of the Mugello, whom I had brought up several years; and to him I said, after commending myself to them all: "See, my dear Bernardino, observe the order I have shown you, and be as quick as you can, because the metal will soon be ready: you cannot go wrong, and these other honest men will quickly make the channels, and you can confidently with these two iron rods strike into the two plugs, and I am certain that my mould will fill very well. I feel the greatest illness that I ever felt since I came into the world, and I certainly believe that in a few hours this great illness will have killed me." So, very discontented, I left them, and went off to bed.

Book Two, Chapters 76 to 82

Chapter 76

When I had got into bed, I ordered my maidservants to carry food and drink into the workshop for everybody; and I said to them: "I shall never be alive tomorrow morning." They still gave me courage, telling me that my great illness would pass off, and that it had come on me from too much labour. So I stayed two hours in this great struggle with the fever; and continually I felt it growing, and I kept saying: "I feel myself dying." My maidservant, who managed the whole house, whose name was Monna Fiore of Castel del Rio: this woman was the ablest that was ever born and as much the kindest, and continually she scolded me for having lost heart, and on the other hand she showed me the greatest kindnesses of service that can ever be done in the world. Still, seeing me with such an immeasurable illness and so dismayed, for all her brave heart she could not keep some quantity of tears from falling from her eyes; and yet she took care as much as she could that I should not see them.
