Being in these immeasurable tribulations, I see coming into my room a certain man, who in his person showed himself twisted like a capital S; and he began to say with a certain sound of voice, mournful, afflicted, like those who give the commendation of the soul to men who are going to execution, and he said: "Oh Benvenuto! your work is spoiled, and there is no remedy for it in the world." As soon as I heard the words of that wretch, I let out a cry so immeasurable that it would have been heard from the heaven of fire; and getting out of bed I took my clothes and began to dress; and to the maidservants and my boy and everybody who came near me to help me, I gave either kicks or blows, and I lamented, saying: "Ah, traitors, envious men! This is a treachery done by design; but I swear by God that I shall find it out very well, and before I die I will leave such a proof of myself in the world that more than one man will be left wondering."

When I had finished dressing, I set off in a bad state of mind towards the workshop, where I saw all those people, whom I had left with so much confidence, all standing astonished and dismayed. I began, and said: "Now then, listen to me, and since you have either not known how or not chosen to obey me in the way I taught you, obey me now that I am with you in the presence of my work; and let nobody set himself against me, because cases of this kind need help and not advice." At these words of mine a certain Master Alessandro Lastricati answered me and said: "See, Benvenuto, you want to set about an undertaking which the art never promises, nor can it be done in any way." At these words I turned with such fury and so bent on mischief that he and all the others, all with one voice said: "Come, give your orders, for we will all help you as much as you can order us, as long as we can hold out with our lives." And these affectionate words I think they said thinking that I should soon fall down dead.

At once I went to look at the furnace, and saw the metal all curdled, which thing is called having made a cake. I told two labourers to go across the way, to the house of Il Capretta the butcher, for a stack of wood of young oaks, which had been dry more than a year, which wood Madonna Ginevra, the said Capretta's wife, had offered me; and when the first armfuls came, I began to fill the ash pit. And because oak of that kind makes the most vigorous fire of all the other kinds of wood, whereas alder or pine wood is used for melting for artillery, because it is a mild fire; oh, when that cake began to feel that terrible fire, it began to clear itself, and to flash. On the other side I pressed on with the channels, and I had sent others onto the roof to keep off the fire, which by the greater force of that fire had caught the more; and on the garden side I had had certain boards set up and other carpets and cloths, which sheltered me from the water.

Chapter 77

Then, when I had given a remedy for all these great furies, in the loudest voice I said now to this man and now to that: "Bring here, take away there;" so that, seeing the said cake beginning to liquefy, all that company obeyed me with such willingness that every one of them did the work of three. Then I had a half loaf of tin taken, which weighed about 60 pounds, and I threw it on the cake inside the furnace, which, with the other helps both of wood and of stirring now with irons and now with poles, in a short space of time became liquid. Now, seeing that I had brought a dead man back to life, against the belief of all those ignorant people, such vigour came back to me that I did not notice whether I had any fever any more or any fear of death.

All at once a noise is heard with a very great flash of fire, so that it seemed exactly as if a thunderbolt had been created there in our presence; at which unaccustomed and fearful fright everybody was dismayed, and I more than the others. When that great noise and brightness had passed, we began to look one another in the face again; and seeing that the lid of the furnace had burst and had lifted so that the bronze was spilling out, at once I had the mouths of my mould opened and at the same time had the two plugs struck.

And seeing that the metal did not run with that quickness it usually did, knowing that the cause perhaps was that the alloy had been consumed by the strength of that terrible fire, I had all my plates and bowls and dishes of tin taken, which were about two hundred, and one by one I put them in front of my channels, and part of them I had thrown into the furnace; so that, everybody seeing that my bronze had become very well liquid, and that my mould was filling, they all helped me and obeyed me spiritedly and cheerfully; and I, now here and now there, gave orders, helped, and said: "Oh God, You who by Your immense powers rose again from the dead, and gloriously ascended to heaven!" so that in a moment my mould filled; on account of which I knelt down and with all my heart thanked God for it; then I turned to a dish of salad which was there on a poor bench, and with great appetite I ate and drank together with all that company; then I went off to bed sound and cheerful, because it was two hours before day; and as if I had never had any illness in the world, so sweetly I rested.

That good maidservant of mine, without my saying anything to her, had provided me with a fat little capon; so that, when I got out of bed, which was near the dinner hour, she came to meet me cheerfully, saying: "Oh, is this the man who felt himself dying? I believe that those blows and kicks you gave us last night, when you were so furious, that with that devilish fury you seemed to have, that so immeasurable fever of yours, perhaps frightened that you might give it some too, took itself off in flight."

And so all my poor little household, freed from so much terror and from such immeasurable labours, at once sent out to buy, in place of those tin plates and bowls, as many earthenware dishes, and we all dined cheerfully, and I never remember in my life either dining with greater gladness or with a better appetite. After dinner all those who had helped me came to find me, who cheerfully rejoiced together, thanking God for everything that had happened, and saying that they had learned and seen done things which were held impossible by the other masters. I too, somewhat boastful, thinking myself a little bit clever, gloried in it; and putting my hand in my purse, I paid and satisfied them all.

That bad man, my mortal enemy, Messer Pier Francesco Riccio, the Duke's majordomo, with great diligence tried to find out how the thing had gone; so that those two men, whom I had suspected of having made that cake for me, told him that I was not a man, but rather I was an out-and-out great devil, because I had done what the art could not do; with so many other great things, which would have been too much for a devil. Since they said far more than what had happened, perhaps as an excuse for themselves, the said majordomo wrote it at once to the Duke, who was at Pisa, in still more terrible terms and fuller of greater marvels than they had told him.

Chapter 78

Having let my cast work cool for two days, I began to uncover it very gently; and I found, first of all, the head of the Medusa, which had come out very well by virtue of the vents, as I said to the Duke that the nature of fire is to go upwards; then I went on to uncover the rest, and found the other head, that is the Perseus's, which had likewise come out very well; and this gave me much more wonder, because as is seen, it is a good deal lower than that of the Medusa. And because the mouths of the said work had been set on the top of the Perseus's head and by the shoulders, I found that at the end of the said head of the Perseus exactly all the bronze that was in my furnace had come to an end. And it was a marvellous thing that there was nothing at all left over from the pouring mouths, nor was anything wanting either; which gave me such wonder that it really seemed to be a miraculous thing, truly guided and handled by God.

I went on happily to finish uncovering it, and always found everything come out very well, until we got to the foot of the right leg that rests, where I found the heel come out; and going on, I saw it to be all full, so that on one side I greatly rejoiced and on the other I was half sorry, only because I had told the Duke that it could not come out. So, finishing uncovering it, I found that the toes of the said foot had not come out, and not only the toes, but there was a little wanting above the toes, so that nearly half of it was missing; and although it gave me that little extra labour, I was very glad of it, only to show the Duke that I understood what I was doing. And although a great deal more of that foot had come out than I had thought, the cause of it had been that from the said many various accidents the metal had been hotter than the rule of the art allows; and also from my having had to help it with the alloy in the way that has been said, with those tin plates, a thing that has never been used by anybody else.

Now, having seen my work come out so well, I went off at once to Pisa to find my Duke; who gave me as gracious a welcome as can be imagined in the world; and the Duchess did the same; and although that majordomo of theirs had told them the whole of it, it seemed to their Excellencies another and a more stupendous and marvellous thing to hear it told by me in my own voice; and when I came to that foot of the Perseus, which had not come out, as I had told His most illustrious Excellency beforehand, I saw him fill with wonder, and he told the Duchess of it, just as I had told him beforehand. Now, seeing those lords of mine so pleasant towards me, I then begged the Duke to let me go as far as Rome. So he kindly gave me leave, and told me to come back quickly to finish his Perseus, and made me letters of recommendation to his ambassador, who was Averardo Serristori: and it was the first years of Pope Julius del Monte.

Chapter 79

Before I left, I gave orders to my workmen to go on in the way I had shown them. And the reason why I went was that, having made for Bindo d'Antonio Altoviti a portrait of his head, as large as life, in bronze, and having sent it to him as far as Rome, he had put this portrait of his in a study of his, which was very richly adorned with antiques and other fine things; but the said study was not made for sculptures, nor for paintings either, because the windows came below the said fine works, so that, those sculptures and paintings having their light the wrong way, they did not show well, in the way they would have done if they had had their reasonable lights.

One day the said Bindo happened to be at his door, and Michelangelo Buonarroti the sculptor passing by, he begged him to be so good as to come into his house to see a study of his; and so he took him in. As soon as he had gone in, and seen it, he said: "Who has been the master who has portrayed you so well and in so fine a manner? And know that that head pleases me as much as, and somewhat better than, those antiques do; and yet there are good ones among them that are seen here; and if these windows were above them, as they are below them, they would show themselves so much better, that that portrait of yours among these many fine works would do itself great honour."

As soon as the said Michelangelo had left the said Bindo's house, he wrote me a most pleasant letter which ran thus: "My Benvenuto, I have known you these many years for the greatest goldsmith that ever there was any notice of; and now I shall know you for a sculptor of the same kind. Know that Messer Bindo Altoviti took me to see a head, his portrait, in bronze, and told me that it was from your hand; I had much pleasure in it; but it grieved me much that it was put in a bad light, for if it had its reasonable light, it would show itself the fine work that it is."

This letter was full of the most affectionate words and the most favourable towards me: so that before I left to go to Rome, I had shown it to the Duke, who read it with much affection, and said to me: "Benvenuto, if you write to him and make him want to come back to Florence, I would make him one of the Forty-Eight." So I wrote him a most affectionate letter, and in it I told him on the Duke's behalf a hundred times more than I had had orders for; and so as not to make a mistake, I showed it to the Duke before I sealed it, and said to His most illustrious Excellency: "My lord, I have perhaps promised him too much." He answered and said: "He deserves more than you have promised him, and I will perform more than that for him." To that letter of mine Michelangelo never made any answer, on account of which the Duke showed himself very much displeased with him.

Chapter 80

Now, when I had got to Rome, I went to lodge in the house of the said Bindo Altoviti: he at once told me how he had shown his bronze portrait to Michelangelo, and how he had praised it so much; so we talked about this at great length. But because he had in his hands twelve hundred gold scudi in gold of mine, which the said Bindo had kept for me along with five thousand of the same, which he had lent to the Duke, four thousand of which were his and mine were in his name, he gave me that profit of my share which came to me; which was the reason why I set about making him the said portrait. And because, when the said Bindo saw it in wax, he sent me a present of 50 gold scudi by one Ser Giuliano Paccalli a notary of his, who lived with him, which money I would not take and sent back by the same man, and afterwards I said to the said Bindo: "It is enough for me that you keep that money of mine alive; and that it earns me something" — I noticed that he had a bad mind towards me, because instead of making a fuss of me, as he was used to do, he showed himself stiff towards me; and although he kept me in the house, he never showed himself openly to me, but stayed sulky. Still with few words we settled it: I lost my workmanship on that portrait of his and the bronze too, and we came to terms that he should keep that money of mine at 15 per cent for my natural life.

Chapter 81

First of all I had gone to kiss the Pope's feet; and while I was talking with the Pope, there came up Messer Averardo Serristori, who was our Duke's ambassador; and because I had started certain discussions with the Pope, by which I believe I should easily have come to terms with him and would gladly have gone back to Rome because of the great difficulties I had in Florence; but I noticed that the said ambassador had worked against it.

I went to find Michelangelo Buonarroti and repeated to him that letter which from Florence I had written him on the Duke's behalf. He answered me that he was employed on the fabric of Saint Peter's, and that for that reason he could not leave. Then I told him that, since he had settled on the model of the said building, he could leave his Urbino, who would obey very well when he gave him his orders; and I added many other words of promise, saying them on the Duke's behalf. He at once looked at me fixedly, and sneering said: "And how are you content with him?" Although I said that I was most content, and that I was very well treated, he showed that he knew most of my troubles; and so he answered me that it would be difficult to be able to leave. Then I added that he would do better to go back to his own country, which was governed by a most just Lord and the greatest lover of the arts of any lord that was ever born into the world.

As I have said above, he had with him a workman of his who was from Urbino, who had been with him many years and had served him more as a boy and a servant than as anything else: and one could see why, for the said man had learned nothing of the art; and because I had pressed Michelangelo with so many good arguments that he did not know what to say on the spot, he turned to his Urbino in a way of asking him what he thought about it. This Urbino of his at once, in a boorish way of his, in a very loud voice said this: "I never mean to part from my Messer Michelangelo, until either I skin him or he skins me." At these foolish words I was forced to laugh, and without saying goodbye to him, with my shoulders down I turned and went away.

Chapter 82

After I had done my business so badly with Bindo Altoviti, by losing my bronze head and by giving him my money for my life, I was clear about what sort of thing the faith of merchants is, and so, discontented, I went back to Florence. At once I went to the Palace to visit the Duke; and His most illustrious Excellency was at Castello, above the Ponte a Rifredi. I found in the Palace Messer Pier Francesco Riccio, the majordomo, and when I wanted to come up to him to make the usual ceremonies, at once with immeasurable astonishment he said: "Oh, you are back!" and with the same astonishment, clapping his hands, he said: "The Duke is at Castello;" and turning his back on me he went off. I could neither know nor imagine why that beast had made those gestures.

At once I went off to Castello, and having gone into the garden, where the Duke was, I saw him from a distance, and when he saw me, he made a sign of being astonished, and let me know that I should go away. I, who had promised myself that His Excellency would make me the same caresses and greater still than he had made me when I went, now seeing so great an oddity, went back to Florence very discontented; and having taken up my business again, pressing on to bring my work to an end, I could not imagine from what such an accident could come: except that, noticing the way Messer Sforza and certain others of those closest to the Duke looked at me, the wish came on me to ask Messer Sforza what this meant; who, smiling, said: "Benvenuto, attend to being an honest man, and do not trouble about anything else."

A few days later I was given the chance of speaking to the Duke, and he made me certain clouded caresses and asked me what was being done in Rome: so as best I knew how I struck up a conversation, and told him of the head I had made in bronze for Bindo Altoviti, with all that had followed. I noticed that he stood listening to me with great attention: and I told him likewise the whole of the business of Michelangelo Buonarroti. At which he showed some indignation; and at the words of his Urbino, of that skinning he had spoken of, he laughed heartily; then he said: "So much the worse for him;" and I went away.

Certainly that Ser Pier Francesco, the majordomo, must have done me some ill office with the Duke, which did not succeed for him: for God, the lover of truth, defended me, as He has always up to this age of mine saved me from so many immeasurable dangers, and I hope He will save me until the end of this life of mine, troubled though it is; still I go forward, only by His power, with good spirit, and no fury of fortune or of perverse stars frightens me: only let God keep me in His grace.

Book Two, Chapters 83 to 88

Chapter 83

Now hear a terrible thing that happened, most pleasant reader. With all the diligence I knew and could, I attended to bringing my work to an end, and in the evening I went to sit up in the Duke's wardrobe, helping those goldsmiths who worked there for His most illustrious Excellency; for most of those works they made were from my designs: and because I saw that the Duke took great pleasure both in seeing the work go on and in chatting with me, it also suited me to go there sometimes by day.

Being one day among others in the said wardrobe, the Duke came as usual and much more willingly, His most illustrious Excellency knowing that I was there; and as soon as he arrived he began to talk with me about many different and most pleasant things, and I answered him to the purpose, and I had so charmed him that he showed himself more pleasant to me than he had ever shown himself in the past. All at once one of his secretaries appeared, who, speaking in His Excellency's ear, since it was perhaps a matter of much importance, at once the Duke rose and went off into another room with the said secretary.

And because the Duchess had sent to see what His most illustrious Excellency was doing, the page said to the Duchess: "The Duke is talking and laughing with Benvenuto, and is in a thoroughly good humour." Hearing this, the Duchess at once came into the wardrobe and, not finding the Duke there, sat down beside us; and having watched us work a while, with great pleasantness she turned to me and showed me a string of large pearls, and truly very rare ones, and asking me what I thought of them, I told her that it was a very beautiful thing. Then Her most illustrious Excellency said to me: "I want the Duke to buy it for me; so, my Benvenuto, praise it to the Duke as much as you know and can in the world."

At these words I, with as much reverence as I knew, opened myself to the Duchess, and said: "My lady, I thought this string of pearls was Your most illustrious Excellency's; and because reason does not allow anybody ever to say certain things, once I know it is not Your most illustrious Excellency's, it falls to me to say them, and indeed it is necessary for me to say them; let Your most illustrious Excellency know that, this being very much my profession, I see in these pearls a great many defects, on account of which I would never advise Your Excellency to buy it." At these words of mine she said: "The merchant lets me have it for six thousand scudi: for if it had not one or two of those little defects, it would be worth more than twelve thousand." Then I said that even if that string were of altogether infinite goodness, I would never advise anybody to go as high as five thousand scudi; because pearls are not jewels; pearls are a fish bone and in the space of time they fail; but diamonds, and rubies and emeralds do not grow old, and sapphires: these four are jewels, and it is these one should buy.

At these words of mine, the Duchess, somewhat scornful, said to me: "I have a mind now for these pearls, and so I beg you to take them to the Duke, and praise them as much as you can and know in the world; and although you may seem to yourself to be telling some few lies, tell them to do me a service; for it will be good for you." I, who have always been the greatest friend of truth and the enemy of lies, and being under necessity, since I did not want to lose the favour of so great a princess, so, discontented, took those cursed pearls, and went with them into that other room, where the Duke had withdrawn. Who, as soon as he saw me, said: "Oh Benvenuto, what are you doing?" Uncovering those pearls, I said: "My lord, I come to show you a most beautiful string of pearls, most rare and truly worthy of Your most illustrious Excellency; and for eighty pearls, I do not believe that so many were ever put together that would show better in a string; so buy them, my lord, for they are miraculous." At once the Duke said: "I do not want to buy them, because they are not those pearls nor of that goodness you say, and I have seen them, and they do not please me." Then I said: "Forgive me, my lord, for these pearls exceed in infinite beauty all the pearls that were ever set in a string."

The Duchess had got up, and was standing behind a door and heard everything I said; so that, when I had said more than a thousand things beyond what I write, the Duke turned to me with a kindly look, and said to me: "Oh my Benvenuto, I know that you understand these things very well: and if these pearls were of those many rare virtues you attribute to them, it would not seem a hardship to me to buy them, both to please the Duchess and to have them; because things of this kind are necessary to me, not so much for the Duchess as for my other affairs of my sons and daughters." And I at these words of his, since I had begun to tell lies, went on telling them with still greater boldness, giving them the greatest colour of truth, so that the Duke should believe me, trusting the Duchess to help me when the time came. And because more than two hundred scudi would have come to me for making such a bargain, and the Duchess had hinted at it to me, I had made up my mind and disposed myself not to take a soldo of it, only for my own safety, so that the Duke should never think I did it out of greed.

Again the Duke with the most pleasant words went on to say to me: "I know that you understand these things very well: so if you are that honest man that I have always thought you to be, now tell me the truth." Then, my eyes reddening and becoming somewhat wet with tears, I said: "My lord, if I tell the truth to Your most illustrious Excellency, the Duchess becomes my mortal enemy, on account of which I shall be forced to go off with God, and the honour of my Perseus, which I have promised to this most noble School of Your most illustrious Excellency, my enemies will at once bring to shame; so I commend myself to Your most illustrious Excellency."

Chapter 84

The Duke, having realized that everything I had said had been said as it were by force, said: "If you have faith in me, do not fear anything in the world." Again I said: "Alas, my lord, how can it be that the Duchess should know nothing of it?" At these words of mine the Duke raised his hand in pledge and said: "Reckon that you have buried them in a little casket of diamonds." At these honourable words, I at once told the truth of what I understood about those pearls, and that they were not worth much more than two thousand scudi.

The Duchess, having heard us grow quiet, because we were speaking as low as can be, came forward, and said: "My lord, of your grace buy me this string of pearls, because I have the greatest wish for it, and your Benvenuto has said that he never saw a finer one." Then the Duke said: "I do not want to buy it." "Why, my lord, will Your Excellency not content me by buying this string of pearls?" "Because it does not please me to throw money away." Again the Duchess said: "Oh, how throw money away, when your Benvenuto, in whom you deservedly have such faith, has told me it is a bargain by more than three thousand scudi?" Then the Duke said: "My lady, my Benvenuto has told me that if I buy it, I shall be throwing my money away, because these pearls are neither round nor equal, and there are a good many old ones among them; and that this is true, now look at this one and this other, and look here and there: so they are not the thing for me."

At these words the Duchess looked at me with the worst possible spirit, and threatening me with her head she went away, so that I was quite tempted to go off with God and vanish out of Italy; but because my Perseus was almost finished, I would not fail to bring it out: but let every man consider in what heavy trouble I found myself. The Duke had ordered his doorkeepers in my presence always to let me go in through the rooms and wherever His Excellency was; and the Duchess had ordered those same men that every time I arrived in that palace, they should drive me away; so that as soon as they saw me, they at once left those doors and drove me away; but they took care that the Duke should not see them, so that if the Duke saw me before those wretches did, either he called me or he made me a sign to come in.

The Duchess called that broker Bernardone, of whose slackness and vile worthlessness she had complained so much to me, and commended herself to him, as she had done to me; who said: "My lady, leave it to me." This great rascal went before the Duke with this string in his hand. The Duke, as soon as he saw it, told him to get out of his sight. Then the said rascal with that ugly voice of his, which he sounded through his ugly ass's nose, said: "Eh! my lord, buy this string for that poor lady, who is dying of longing for it, and cannot live without it." And adding many other foolish gross words of his, and the Duke having grown tired of him, he said to him: "Either get out of my sight, or blow yourself up on the spot." This great rascal, who knew very well what he was doing, because if either by way of blowing himself up or by singing La bella Franceschina he could get the Duke to make that purchase, he won the Duchess's favour and moreover his brokerage, which came to several hundred scudi: and so he blew himself up. The Duke gave him several slaps on those ugly cheeks of his, and to get rid of him he hit him a little harder than he usually did. At these hard blows on those ugly cheeks of his, not only did they turn too red, but the tears came down. With them he began to say: "Eh! my lord, a faithful servant of yours, who tries to do good and is content to bear every sort of unpleasantness, so long as that poor lady is content."

The Duke having grown too tired of this wretched man, both from the slaps and for love of the Duchess, whom His most illustrious Excellency always wished to content, said at once: "Get out of my sight and bad luck to you, and go, make your bargain, for I am content to do everything the lady Duchess wants." Now here one sees the rage of bad fortune against a poor man and the shameful luck that favours a wretch: I lost all the Duchess's favour, which was a good cause for taking the Duke's from me too; and he won that heavy brokerage and their favour: so it is not enough to be an honest and skilled man.

Chapter 85

At this time the war of Siena broke out; and the Duke, wanting to fortify Florence, distributed the gates among his sculptors and architects; where to me were assigned the Porta al Prato and the little Arno gate, which is on the meadow where one goes to the mills; to the knight Bandinello the gate of San Frediano; to Pasqualino d'Ancona, the gate of San Pier Gattolini; to Giuliano di Baccio d'Agnolo, the carpenter, the gate of San Giorgio; to Il Particino, the carpenter, the gate of San Niccolò; to Francesco da Sangallo, the sculptor, called Il Margolla, was given the gate of the Croce; and to Giovanbattista, called Il Tasso, was given the gate of the Pinti: and so certain other bastions and gates to various engineers, whom I do not remember and who are not to my purpose either.

The Duke, who has truly always been of good wit, went round his city by himself; and when His most illustrious Excellency had well examined it and made up his mind, he called Lattanzio Gorini, who was a paymaster of his: and because this Lattanzio too took some pleasure in this profession, His most illustrious Excellency had him draw all the ways in which he wanted the said gates fortified, and to each of us he sent his own gate drawn out; so that, seeing the one that fell to me, and it seeming to me that the way was not according to right reason, but rather was thoroughly wrong, at once with this drawing in my hand I went off to find my Duke; and wanting to show His Excellency the defects of that drawing given me, no sooner had I begun to speak than the Duke turned on me furiously, and said: "Benvenuto, in making figures very well I shall yield to you, but in this profession I want you to yield to me; so keep to the drawing I have given you."

To these fierce words I answered as kindly as I knew how in the world and said: "Even in the fine way of making figures, my lord, I have learned from Your most illustrious Excellency; still we have always argued it a little together; so about this fortifying of your city, which matters much more than making figures, I beg Your most illustrious Excellency to be so good as to listen to me, and so, talking it over with Your Excellency, you will be the better able to show me the way I am to serve you." So that, with these most pleasant words of mine, he kindly set about disputing it with me; and when I had shown His most illustrious Excellency with living and clear reasons that in the way he had drawn for me it would not have been good, His Excellency said to me: "Oh go, and make a drawing yourself, and I shall see if it pleases me." So I made two drawings according to the reason of the true way of fortifying those two gates, and took them to him, and having recognized the true from the false, His Excellency pleasantly said to me: "Oh go, and do it your own way, for I am content." Then with great diligence I began.

Chapter 86

There was on guard at the Porta al Prato a Lombard captain: this was a man of a terrible robust shape, and with very boorish words; and he was presumptuous and most ignorant. This man at once began to ask me what I meant to do; to whom I pleasantly showed my drawings, and with extreme labour I tried to make him understand the way I meant to take. Now this boorish beast now shook his head, and now he turned this way and now that, often changing the setting of his legs, twisting the moustaches of his beard, which he had very large, and often pulling the fold of his cap down over his eyes, often saying: "By God, a plague on it! I do not understand this business of yours." So that, this beast having become wearisome to me, I said: "Then leave it to me to do, for I do understand it;" and turning my back on him to go about my business, this man began to threaten with his head; and with his left hand, putting it on the pommel of his sword, he made the point of it rise a little, and said: "Ho there, master, you will make me have a quarrel with you to the blood." I turned on him in great anger, because he had made me lose my temper, and said: "It will seem less labour to me to have a quarrel with you than to build this bastion at this gate." In a moment we both put our hands on our swords, and we did not draw them right out before there came up a number of honest men, both of our Florentines and other courtiers; and most of them shouted at him, telling him that he was in the wrong, and that I was a man to give him a good account of himself, and that if the Duke knew it, it would go hard with him. So he went about his business: and I began my bastion.

And when I had given the orders for the said bastion, I went to the other little Arno gate, where I found a captain from Cesena, the most courteous and gallant man I ever knew of that profession: he seemed to be a gentle young lady, and at need he was one of the bravest men and the most deadly that can be imagined. This gentleman watched me so closely that many times he made me shy: he wanted to understand and I pleasantly showed him: it is enough that we vied with each other as to who could make the greater fuss of the other, so that I made this bastion a great deal better than that one.

Having nearly finished my bastions, certain of Piero Strozzi's people having made a raid, the countryside of Prato had been so frightened that everything was being moved out of it, and for this reason all the carts of that district came loaded, everybody carrying his goods into the city. And because the carts touched one another, and there were a very great number of them, seeing such a disorder, I told the guards of the gates to see that at that gate there should not be a disorder such as happened at the gates of Turin; for if there were need to make use of the portcullis, it could not do its office, because it would be left hanging on one of those carts. Hearing these words of mine, that great beast of a captain turned on me with insulting words, and I answered him as much; so that we came nearer to doing far worse than that first time: still we were parted; and I, having finished my bastions, unexpectedly got several scudi, which came in useful to me, and I gladly went back to finish my Perseus.

Chapter 87

In these days certain antiques having been found in the district of Arezzo, among which was the Chimera, which is that bronze lion which is seen in the rooms next to the great hall of the Palace; and together with the said Chimera a quantity of small statuettes had been found, also of bronze, which were covered with earth and rust, and each of them was missing either the head or the hands or the feet; the Duke took pleasure in cleaning them up himself with certain little goldsmith's chisels. It happened that I had occasion to speak to His most illustrious Excellency; and while I was talking with him, he handed me a small hammer with which I struck those little chisels the Duke held in his hand, and in that way the said little figures were uncovered from the earth and the rust. So, going on for several evenings, the Duke set me to work, where I began to make again those limbs which were missing from the said little figures.

And His Excellency taking so much pleasure in that little bit of those small things, he made me work by day as well, and if I was slow in going there, His most illustrious Excellency sent for me. Several times I let His Excellency know that if I was drawn away from the Perseus by day, several difficulties would follow; and the first, which frightened me most, was that the long time I saw my work was taking should not be the cause of His most illustrious Excellency growing tired of it, as afterwards happened to me; the other was that I had several workmen, and when I was not present, they made two notable troubles. And the first was that they spoiled my work for me, and the other that they worked as little as possible; so that the Duke was content that I should go there only from 24 hours onwards.

And because I had so marvellously sweetened His most illustrious Excellency towards me, that in the evening when I came to him, he always increased his caresses to me. In these days they were building those new rooms towards the Lions; so that His Excellency, wanting to withdraw to a more private place, had had a certain little room fitted up for himself in these newly made rooms, and had ordered me to go through his wardrobe, where I passed secretly over the ceiling of the great hall, and through certain little passages I went most secretly to the said little room: whereas within a few days the Duchess deprived me of it, having all those conveniences of mine shut up; so that every evening when I arrived at the Palace, I had to wait a good while because the Duchess was in those antechambers through which I had to pass, at her privy; and as she was ailing, I never arrived once without disturbing her.

Now for this and for other reasons I had become so wearisome to her that in no way could she bear to see me; and with all this great discomfort and endless unhappiness of mine, I patiently went on going there; and the Duke had given such express orders that as soon as I knocked at those doors, they were opened to me, and without saying anything to me they let me go in everywhere; so that it sometimes happened that, coming in quietly and so unexpectedly through those private rooms, I found the Duchess at her privy; who at once fell out with me with such raging fury that I was frightened, and she always said to me: "When will you ever have finished mending these little figures? for by now this coming of yours has grown too wearisome to me." To whom I answered kindly: "My lady, my only patroness, I desire nothing else but to serve you with faith and with extreme obedience; and because these works, which the Duke has ordered me, will last many months, let Your most illustrious Excellency tell me if you do not want me to come here any more; I will not come in any way and let him call whom he likes; and even if the Duke calls me, I shall say that I feel ill and in no way will I ever come here." At these words of mine she said: "I do not say that you should not come here and I do not say that you should not obey the Duke; but it does seem to me that these works of yours never have an end."

Whether the Duke had got some hint of this, or however it may have been, His Excellency began again: as it came near 24 hours, he sent to call me; and the man who came to call me always said to me: "Take care not to fail to come, for the Duke is waiting for you;" and so I went on, with these same difficulties, several evenings. And one evening among others, coming in as usual, the Duke, who must have been talking with the Duchess about matters perhaps secret, turned on me with the greatest fury in the world; and I, somewhat frightened, wanting to withdraw quickly, all at once he said: "Come in, my Benvenuto, and go over there to your business, and I shall be with you shortly." While I was passing, Signor Don Grazia, a little child of very few years, took me by the cloak and made me the most pleasant little jokes such a baby can make; at which the Duke, wondering, said: "Oh, what a pleasant friendship this is that my children have with you!"

Chapter 88

While I was working at these trifles of no moment, the prince and Don Giovanni and Don Ferrando and Don Grazia stood over me all evening, and secretly from the Duke they kept pricking me: so I begged them of their grace to keep still. They answered me, saying: "We cannot." And I said to them: "What cannot be done is not wanted; now go on, then." All at once the Duke and the Duchess broke into laughter.

Another evening, having finished those four little bronze figures which are set in the base, which are Jupiter, Mercury, Minerva, and Danae the mother of Perseus with her little Perseus sitting at her feet, having had them carried into the said room where I worked in the evening, I set them in a row, raised a little above eye level, so that they made a very beautiful sight. Having heard of it, the Duke came somewhat earlier than usual; and because the person who reported it to His most illustrious Excellency must have put it much higher than it was, for he told him: "Better than the ancients," and such like things, my Duke came along together with the Duchess cheerfully talking of nothing but my work; and I at once got up and went to meet them. Who with those ducal and fine welcomes of his raised his right hand, in which he held a bergamot pear, larger than can be seen and most beautiful, and said: "Take this, my Benvenuto, plant this pear in the garden of your house." At those words I pleasantly answered, saying: "Oh my lord, does Your most illustrious Excellency say in earnest that I should plant it in the garden of my house?" Again the Duke said: "In the garden of the house, which is yours; have you understood me?" Then I thanked His Excellency, and the Duchess likewise, with the best ceremonies I knew how to make in the world.

Then they both sat down, opposite the said little figures, and for more than two hours they never talked of anything but the beautiful little figures; so that such an immoderate wish had come on the Duchess that she said to me then: "I do not want these fine little figures to go to waste on that base down in the square, where they would be in danger of being spoiled; rather I want you to arrange them for me in a room of mine, where they will be kept with that reverence their most rare excellence deserves." At these words I set myself against her with endless reasons; and seeing that she had made up her mind that I was not to put them in the base where they are, I waited for the next day and went to the Palace at twenty-two hours; and finding that the Duke and the Duchess had ridden out, having already got my base ready, I had the said little figures carried down, and at once leaded them in, as they were to stay.

Oh! when the Duchess heard of it, such anger grew in her that if it had not been for the Duke, who nobly helped me, I should have fared very badly: and both for that anger about the string of pearls and for this she worked so much that the Duke gave up that little pleasure of his; which was the cause of my not having to go there any more, and at once I went back to those same difficulties as before, as to getting into the Palace.

Book Two, Chapters 89 to 96

Chapter 89

I went back to the Loggia, where I had already brought the Perseus and was finishing it with the difficulties already spoken of, that is without money, and with other accidents, half of which would have dismayed a man armed in diamonds. Still going on in my usual way, one morning among others, having heard mass at San Piero Scheraggio, there came in front of me Bernardone the broker, that wretched goldsmith, and by the Duke's kindness he was overseer of the mint; and as soon as he was barely outside the door of the church, the swine let go four cracks, which must have been heard from San Miniato. To whom I said: "Ah, swine, coward, ass, is that the sound of your filthy talents?" and I ran for a stick. He quickly withdrew into the mint, and I stood at the crack of my door, and outside I kept a little boy of mine to make me a sign when this swine came out of the mint. Now, having waited a good while, and growing tired of it, and that little anger having settled, considering that blows are not struck on terms, from which some trouble might come, I made up my mind to take my revenge in another way.

And because this happened around the feast of our Saint John, a day or two before it, I made four verses about him, and stuck them up in the corner of the church where people pissed and shat, and they ran like this:

	Here lies Bernardone, ass and hog,
	spy, thief, broker, into whom Pandora
	poured the greatest evils, and then that great
	sheep Master Bigoxen Bandinello carried them over from him.

The affair and the verses went round the palace, and the Duke and the Duchess laughed at them; and before he knew of it, a great quantity of people had stopped there, and were making the greatest laughter in the world: and because they looked towards the mint and fixed their eyes on Bernardone, his son Master Baccio, noticing it, at once in great anger tore them down and bit his finger, threatening with that ugly voice of his which comes out through his nose: he made a great show of bluster.

Chapter 90

When the Duke heard that all my work of the Perseus could be shown as finished, one day he came to see it and showed by many plain signs that it satisfied him greatly; and turning to certain lords who were with His most illustrious Excellency he said: "Although this work seems very beautiful to us, it has still to please the people; so, my Benvenuto, before you give it its last finish I would like you for love of me to open this front part a little, for half a day, to my Square, to see what the people say of it; because there is no doubt that from seeing it shut in like this to seeing it in the open field, it will show itself in a different way from what it shows shut in as it is."

At these words I said humbly to His most illustrious Excellency: "Know, my lord, that it will show half as well again. Oh, how does Your most illustrious Excellency not remember having seen it in the garden of my house, in which it showed itself so well in such a great width of space that Bandinello came through the garden of the Innocenti to see it, and with all his bad and worst nature, it forced him and he spoke well of it, who never spoke well of anybody in his life? I see that Your most illustrious Excellency believes him too much." At these words of mine, sneering a little scornfully, but still with many pleasant words, he said: "Do it, my Benvenuto, only for a little satisfaction of mine."

And when he had gone, I began to give orders for uncovering it; and because there was still a little gold wanting, and certain varnishes and other such small things which belong to the finish of a work, I muttered scornfully and complained, cursing that accursed day which was the cause of bringing me to Florence; because by now I saw the very great and certain loss I had made by my departure from France, and I could not see or know yet what good I might hope for from this lord of mine in Florence; because from the beginning to the middle and to the end, everything I had done had always been done to my own very damaging disadvantage; and so, discontented, the next day I uncovered it.

Now, as it pleased God, as soon as it was seen, there went up a shout so immeasurable in praise of the said work that this was the cause of consoling me somewhat. And the people never left off continually sticking up papers on the shoulders of the door, which held a little screen, while I gave it its finish. I say that on that same day, on which it was kept uncovered several hours, more than twenty sonnets were stuck up, all in the most immeasurable praise of my work; after I had covered it again, every day there were stuck up on it quantities of sonnets and of Latin verses and Greek verses; because it was vacation time at the University of Pisa, all those most excellent learned men and the scholars vied with one another.

But what gave me the greatest contentment, with hope of my greater welfare with my Duke, was that those of the art, that is sculptors and painters, they too vied as to who could speak best. And among the others, whom I valued most, was the able painter Jacopo da Pontormo, and more than he his excellent Bronzino, the painter, who was not content with having several stuck up there, but sent me some as far as my house by his Sandrino, which said such good things, in that fine way of his, which is very rare, that this was the cause of consoling me somewhat. And so I covered it up again, and pressed on to finish it.

Chapter 91

My Duke, although His Excellency had heard of this favour that had been done me over that little glimpse by this most excellent School, said: "I have great pleasure that Benvenuto has had this little contentment, which will be the reason why he will give it its wished-for finish sooner and with more diligence; but let him not think that afterwards, when it is seen wholly uncovered and when it can be seen all round, the people will speak in this way; rather all the defects in it will be discovered, and many will be laid to it that are not there; so let him arm himself with patience."

Now these were Bandinello's words said to the Duke, with which he cited the works of Andrea del Verrocchio, who made that fine Christ and Saint Thomas in bronze, which is seen on the front of Orsanmichele; and he cited many other works, right up to the admirable David of the divine Michelangelo Buonarroti, saying that it did not show well except from the front view; and then he spoke of his Hercules and Cacus and of the endless and shameful sonnets that had been stuck up on it, and he spoke ill of these people. My Duke, who believed him well enough, had prompted him to say those words, and thought for certain that it would in great part turn out that way, because that envious Bandinello never left off speaking ill; and once among many other times, that hangman Bernardone the broker being present, to bear out Bandinello's words, he said to the Duke: "Know, my lord, that making large figures is another kind of soup from making little ones: I do not mean to say that he has not made the little figures very well; but you will see that he will not succeed there." And with these gross words he mixed many others, plying his trade as a spy, in which he mixed a heap of lies.

Chapter 92

Now as it pleased my glorious Lord and immortal God, I finished it entirely, and one Thursday morning I uncovered the whole of it. At once, when it was not yet broad day, there gathered such an infinite quantity of people that it would be impossible to tell it, and all with one voice vied with one another as to who could speak best of it. The Duke stood at a low window of the Palace, which is above the door, and so, half hidden inside the window, he heard everything that was said of the said work: and after he had listened several hours, he got up with such heartiness and such contentment that turning to his Messer Sforza he said this: "Sforza, go and find Benvenuto and tell him from me that he has contented me much more than I expected, and tell him that I shall content him in such a way that I will make him wonder; so tell him to be of good cheer."

So the said Messer Sforza did me this glorious errand, which comforted me, both that day for this good news, and because the people pointed me out with their fingers to this man and that, as a marvellous and new thing. Among the others there were two gentlemen, who were sent by the Viceroy of Sicily to our Duke on their business. Now these two pleasant men came at me in the square, since I was pointed out to them as I passed by; so that in a rush they caught me up, and at once, with their caps in their hands, they made me the most ceremonious oration, which would have been too much for a pope: I still humbled myself as much as I could; but they overwhelmed me so much that I began to beg them, of their grace, that we should agree to get out of the square, because the people were stopping to look at me more fixedly than they did at my Perseus.

And in the midst of these ceremonies they were so bold as to ask me to go to Sicily, and that they would make me such terms that I should be content; and they told me how Fra Giovanagnolo of the Servites had made them a fountain full and adorned with many figures, but that they were not of that excellence they saw in the Perseus, and that they had made him rich. I did not let them finish saying all they would have liked to say, for I said to them: "I wonder greatly at you, that you should ask me to leave so great a lord, a lover of the arts more than any other prince that was ever born, and moreover finding myself in my own country, the school of all the greatest arts. Oh! if I had an appetite for great gain, I could have stayed in France in the service of that great King Francis, who gave me a thousand gold scudi for my keep, and moreover paid me for the making of all my works, so that every year I had cleared more than four thousand gold scudi a year; and I left in Paris the labours of four past years." With these and other words I cut short the ceremonies, and thanked them for the great praises they had given me, which are the greatest rewards that can be given to a man who labours with skill; and that they had made my will to do well grow so much that I hoped within a few years to come to show another work, which I hoped would please the admirable Florentine School much more than that one. The two gentlemen would have liked to pick up the thread of the ceremonies again; but with a doffing of my cap and with great reverence I said goodbye to them.

Chapter 93

After I had let two days go by, and seen that the great praises kept always growing, then I disposed myself to go and show myself to my lord Duke; who with great pleasantness said to me: "My Benvenuto, you have satisfied and contented me; but I promise you that I shall content you in such a way that I will make you wonder: and I tell you more, that I do not want tomorrow to go by." At these admirable promises, at once I turned all my greatest powers both of soul and of body in a moment to God, thanking Him in truth: and at the same moment I came up to my Duke, and, half weeping with joy, I kissed his robe; then I added, saying: "Oh my glorious lord, truly a most generous lover of the arts and of those men who labour in them; I beg Your most illustrious Excellency to do me the favour of letting me first go for a week to thank God; because I well know my own immeasurable great labour, and I know that my good faith has moved God to my help: for this and for every other miraculous succour, I want to go on pilgrimage for eight days, always thanking my immortal God, who always helps whoever truly calls on Him."

Then the Duke asked me where I wanted to go. To whom I said: "Tomorrow morning I shall set out and go to Vallombrosa, then to Camaldoli and to the Hermitage, and I shall go as far as the baths of Santa Maria and perhaps as far as Sestile, because I hear that there are fine antiques there: then I shall come back by way of San Francesco della Vernia, and always thanking God, contented I shall come back to serve you." At once the Duke cheerfully said to me: "Go, and come back, for you truly please me, but leave me two lines as a reminder, and leave the rest to me." At once I made four verses, in which I thanked His most illustrious Excellency, and gave them to Messer Sforza, who put them into the Duke's hand on my behalf: who took them; then he put them into the hand of the said Messer Sforza, and told him: "See that you put them before me every day, because if Benvenuto came back and found that I had not dispatched the matter, I believe he would kill me;" and so, laughing, His Excellency said that he should remind him of it. These very words Messer Sforza said to me that evening, laughing and also wondering at the great favour the Duke showed me: and pleasantly he said to me: "Go, Benvenuto, and come back, for I envy you."

Chapter 94

In the name of God I left Florence, always singing psalms and prayers in honour and glory of God for the whole of that journey; in which I had the greatest pleasure, because the season was very beautiful, being summer, and the journey and the country where I had never been before seemed to me so beautiful that I was left wondering and content. And because a young workman of mine had come as my guide, who was from the Baths, and was called Cesare, I was greatly made much of by his father and by all his household; among whom was an old man of more than seventy, a most pleasant man: this was the said Cesare's uncle, and he made profession of being a physician and surgeon, and dabbled somewhat in alchemy. This good man showed me how those Baths had a mine of gold and silver, and had me see many very beautiful things of that country; so that I had some of the greatest pleasures I ever had.

Having grown familiar with me in his own way, one day among others he said to me: "I do not want to fail to tell you a thought of mine, to which, if His Excellency would lend his ear, I believe it would be a very useful thing: and this is that around Camaldoli there is seen a pass so open that Piero Strozzi could not only pass safely, but he could rob Poppi without any resistance;" and with that, not only had he shown it to me in words, but he drew out of his pouch a sheet of paper, on which this good old man had drawn out all that country in such a way that the great danger could very well be seen and was plainly known to be real. I took the drawing and at once left the Baths, and as fast as I could, coming back by the way of Prato Magno and by San Francesco della Vernia, I got back to Florence: and without stopping, having only pulled off my boots, I went to the Palace.

And when I was by the Badia, I met my Duke, who was coming along by the way of the Palace of the Podestà: who, as soon as he saw me, gave me a most gracious welcome together with a little wonder, saying to me: "Oh why have you come back so soon? for I was not expecting you for another week." To whom I said: "For Your most illustrious Excellency's service I have come back, for I would gladly have stayed several days going about that most beautiful country." "And what good business?" said the Duke. To whom I said: "My lord, it is necessary that I tell you and show you things of great importance." So I went off with him to the Palace. When we got to the Palace he took me privately into a room where we were alone. Then I told him the whole of it, and showed him that little drawing; which he showed himself most grateful for. And when I told His Excellency that it was necessary to find a quick remedy for such a thing, the Duke stayed a little within himself, and then said to me: "Know that we are in agreement with the Duke of Urbino, who is to take care of it himself; but keep it to yourself." And with a great show of his good favour, I went back home.

Chapter 95

The next day I showed myself and the Duke, after a little conversation, cheerfully said to me: "Tomorrow without fail I mean to dispatch your business; so be of good cheer." I, who held it for the most certain thing, waited for the next day with great longing. When the longed-for day came, I went off to the Palace; and as by custom it always seems to happen that bad news is given with more diligence than good, Messer Iacopo Guidi, His most illustrious Excellency's secretary, called me with his mouth twisted and in a haughty voice, and having drawn himself all together, with his person all stiffened as though frozen, he began to speak in this manner: "The Duke says that he wants to know from you what you ask for your Perseus."

I was left bewildered and astonished: and I answered at once that I was never one to ask a price for my labours, and that this was not what His Excellency had promised me two days before. At once this man in a louder voice told me that he expressly commanded me on the Duke's behalf to say what I wanted for it, on pain of the entire disgrace of His most illustrious Excellency. I, who had promised myself not merely to have gained something by the great caresses made me by His most illustrious Excellency, but rather had promised myself to have gained the whole of the Duke's favour, because I never asked him for any greater thing than only his good grace: now this manner of doing it, unlooked for by me, made me come into such a fury: and all the more from its being put to me in the way that venomous toad did. I said that even if the Duke gave me ten thousand scudi, he would not be paying me for it, and that if I had ever thought of coming to such deserts, I would never have settled here. At once this spiteful fellow said a quantity of insulting words to me; and I did the like to him.

The next day after, as I was paying my respects to the Duke, His Excellency beckoned me; so I came up; and he in anger said to me: "Cities and great palaces are made with ten thousand ducats." To whom I answered at once that His Excellency would find endless men who would know how to make him cities and palaces; but that of Perseuses he would perhaps not find a man in the world who would know how to make him such a one. And at once I left without saying or doing anything else.

Some few days later, the Duchess sent for me and told me that I should refer the difference I had with the Duke to her, because she boasted that she would do something that would content me. To these kindly words I answered that I had never asked any greater reward for my labours than the good grace of the Duke, and that His most illustrious Excellency had promised it me; and that there was no need for me to refer to their most illustrious Excellencies what, from the first days when I began to serve them, I had freely referred to them altogether; and moreover I added that if His most illustrious Excellency gave me only a crazia, which is worth five farthings, for my labours, I would call myself content and satisfied, provided His Excellency did not deprive me of his good grace. At these words of mine, the Duchess, smiling a little, said: "Benvenuto, you would do best to do what I tell you;" and turning her back on me, she got up and left me. I, who thought I was doing my best in using those humble words, found that my worst came of it, because, although she had had that little anger with me, she had in her afterwards a certain way of acting, which was good.

Chapter 96

At this time I was very familiar with Girolamo degli Albizi, who was commissary of His Excellency's militia; and one day among others he said to me: "Oh Benvenuto, it would be well to put some order into this little unpleasantness you have with the Duke; and I tell you, if you had faith in me, I would have the heart to mend it; because I know what I am saying. When the Duke gets angry in earnest, you will come off very badly: let this be enough for you; I cannot tell you everything."

And because I had been told by somebody, perhaps a mischief-maker, after the Duchess had spoken to me, who said that he had heard it said that the Duke, on some occasion or other given him, had said: "For less than two farthings I will throw away the Perseus and so all the differences will be ended" — now because of this fear I told Girolamo degli Albizi that I referred the whole thing to him, and that whatever he did, I should be quite content with all of it, provided I remained in the Duke's favour. This gallant man, who understood the soldier's art very well, especially that of the militia men, who are all countrymen, but who took no pleasure in the art of sculpture and therefore understood nothing at all about it, so that, speaking with the Duke, he said: "My lord, Benvenuto has referred himself to me, and has begged me to commend him to Your most illustrious Excellency." Then the Duke said: "And I too refer myself to you, and shall be content with everything you judge." So that the said Girolamo made out a paper very ingeniously and much in my favour, and judged that the Duke should give me three thousand five hundred gold scudi in gold, which should be enough not as a reward for such a fine work, but only for a little keep for me; it was enough that I was content; with many other words, which altogether came to the said price. The Duke signed it very willingly, as much as I was discontented with it.

When the Duchess heard of it, she said: "It would have been much better for that poor man to have referred it to me, for I would have had him given five thousand gold scudi;" and one day when I had gone to the Palace, the Duchess said the same words to me in the presence of Messer Alamanno Salviati, and mocked me, telling me that all the trouble I had served me right.

The Duke ordered that I should be paid a hundred gold scudi in gold a month, until the said sum, and so it went on for some months. Then Messer Antonio de' Nobili, who had had the said commission, began to give me fifty of them, and afterwards sometimes he gave me twenty-five and sometimes he did not give them to me at all; so that, seeing myself so drawn out, I said affectionately to the said Messer Antonio, begging him to tell me the reason why he did not finish paying me. He too answered me kindly: in which answer it seemed to me that he opened himself a little too much, because — let whoever understands judge it — first he told me that the reason why he did not go on with my payment was the too great scarcity of money the Palace had, but that he promised me that as soon as money came, he would pay me; and he added, saying: "Alas! if I did not pay you, I should be a great rascal." I wondered to hear him say such a word, and by it I promised myself that when he could, he would pay me. On account of which quite the contrary followed, so that, seeing myself tormented, I grew angry with him and said many bold and choleric words to him, and reminded him of everything he had told me would happen. Still he died, and I am still owed five hundred gold scudi up to now, when we are near the end of the year 1566.

I was also still owed a remainder of my salaries, which it seemed to me they no longer reckoned on paying me, because about three years had passed; but a dangerous illness came on the Duke, so that he was forty-eight hours without being able to pass water; and seeing that the doctors' remedies did him no good, perhaps he turned to God, and for this he wanted everybody to be paid the allowances due to them, and I too was paid; but I was not paid the remainder of my Perseus.

Book Two, Chapters 97 to 100

Chapter 97

I had almost half made up my mind to say nothing more about my unlucky Perseus; but because there is an occasion which forces me, and a notable one, I shall pick up the thread for a little, going back somewhat. I thought I was doing my best when I told the Duchess that I could no longer make a submission of what was no longer in my power, because I had told the Duke that I was content with everything His most illustrious Excellency wished to give me: and this I said thinking to make myself somewhat agreeable; and with that little bit of humility I was trying by every fit remedy to appease the Duke somewhat, because a few days before we came to the settlement by Albizi, the Duke had shown himself much displeased with me.

And the cause was that, when I was complaining to His Excellency of certain most foul robberies that were being done me by Messer Alfonso Quistello and Messer Iacopo Polverino, the fiscal, and more than all of them by Ser Giovanbattista Brandini of Volterra; so, putting these arguments of mine with some show of passion, I saw the Duke come into as great an anger as can ever be imagined. And when His most illustrious Excellency had come into this great fury, he said to me: "This case is like that of your Perseus, for which you have asked ten thousand scudi: you let yourself be too much overcome by your own interest; so I mean to have it valued, and I shall give you everything that is judged for me."

At these words I answered at once somewhat too boldly and half in anger — a thing which it is not fitting to use with great lords — and said: "Oh how is it possible that my work should be valued at its price, when there is not a man in Florence today who would know how to make it?" Then the Duke grew into a greater fury, and said a great many angry words, among which he said: "There is a man in Florence today who would know how to make one like it, and so he will know very well how to judge it." He meant Bandinello, knight of Saint James.

Then I said: "My lord, Your most illustrious Excellency has given me the means, so that I have made in the greatest School in the world a great and most difficult work, which has been praised more than any work that has ever been uncovered in this most divine School; and what makes me boldest has been that those excellent men, who know and who are of the art, such as Bronzino the painter, this man has taken pains and has made me four sonnets, saying the most choice and glorious words it is possible to say; and it is because of this admirable man, perhaps, that the whole city has been moved to such a great noise; and I say indeed that if he attended to sculpture as he does to painting, he might perhaps know very well how to do it. And I say more to Your most illustrious Excellency, that my master Michelangelo Buonarroti would indeed have made one like it, when he was younger, and would not have endured less labour than I have; but now that he is very old, he would certainly not do it; so that I do not believe that today there is notice of a man who would know how to carry it out. So my work has had the greatest reward I could wish for in the world: and all the more since Your most illustrious Excellency, not only has called yourself content with my work, but more than any other man you have praised it to me. Oh, what greater and what more honourable reward can be wished for? I say for a certainty that Your Excellency could not pay me in more glorious coin: nor with any treasure whatever can it be equalled, most certainly: so I am too well paid, and I thank Your most illustrious Excellency for it with all my heart."

At these words the Duke answered and said: "On the contrary, you do not think I have enough to be able to pay you for it; and I tell you that I shall pay you for it far more than it is worth." Then I said: "I did not imagine that I should have any other reward from Your Excellency, but I call myself most fully paid by that first one which the School has given me, and with this I mean to go off with God this very minute, without ever going back to that house Your most illustrious Excellency gave me, nor do I ever want to care to see Florence again." We were just by Santa Felicita and His Excellency was going back to the Palace. At these choleric words of mine the Duke at once turned in great anger and said to me: "Do not go, and take good care that you do not go;" so that I, half frightened, accompanied him to the Palace.

When His Excellency had got to the Palace, he called the bishop de' Bartolini, who was Archbishop of Pisa, and called Messer Pandolfo della Stufa, and told them to tell Baccio Bandinelli on his behalf to consider that work of mine, the Perseus, well, and to value it, because the Duke wanted to pay me its just price. These two honest men at once found the said Bandinello, and having done the errand to him, he told them that he had considered that work very well, and that he knew all too well what it was worth; but because he was at odds with me over other past matters, he did not want to meddle with my affairs in any way. Then these two gentlemen added and said: "The Duke has told us that, on pain of his disgrace, he commands you to set a price on it; and if you want two or three days to consider it well, take them: then tell us what you think that labour deserves." He answered that he had considered it very well, and that he could not fail in the Duke's commands, and that that work had come out very rich and beautiful, so that it seemed to him that it deserved sixteen thousand gold scudi and more. At once the good gentlemen reported it to the Duke, who was badly angered; and likewise they told it again to me. To whom I answered that in no way did I want to accept Bandinello's praises, seeing that this bad man speaks ill of everybody. These words of mine were repeated to the Duke, and it was for this that the Duchess wanted me to refer myself to her. All this is the pure truth: it is enough that I would have done best to let myself be judged by the Duchess, because I should have been paid quickly, and should have had that greater reward.

Chapter 98

The Duke let me know by Messer Lelio Torello, his auditor, that he wanted me to make certain scenes in low relief in bronze round the choir of Santa Maria del Fiore; and because the said choir was Bandinello's undertaking, I did not want to enrich his wretched works with my labours; and although the said choir was not his design, because he understood nothing in the world about architecture (the design was Giuliano di Baccio d'Agnolo's, the carpenter, who spoiled the dome): it is enough that there is no merit in it at all; and for the one and for the other reason I did not in any way want to do such a work, but I always said humanely to the Duke that I would do everything His most illustrious Excellency commanded me, so that His Excellency charged the Wardens of Santa Maria del Fiore to come to terms with me, and that His Excellency would give me only my allowance of the two hundred scudi a year and that for everything else he wanted the said Wardens to provide out of the funds of the said Works.

So that I appeared before the said Wardens, who told me all the orders they had from the Duke; and because with them it seemed to me that I could put my arguments much more safely, I began to show them that so many scenes in bronze would be a very great expense, all of which would be thrown away: and I gave all the reasons, by which they were quite convinced. The first was that that order of the choir was altogether wrong, and was made without any reason, nor was there seen in it either art, or convenience, or grace, or design; the other was that the said scenes had to be set so low that they would come too far below the eye, and that they would be a pissing place for dogs, and would continually be full of every filth; and that for the said reasons I in no way wanted to make them.

Only so as not to throw away the rest of my best years and not to serve His most illustrious Excellency, whom I so much desired to please and to serve; so, if His Excellency wanted to make use of my labours, let him let me make the middle door of Santa Maria del Fiore, which would be a work that would be seen, and would be much more to the glory of His most illustrious Excellency; and I would bind myself by contract that, if I did not make it better than that one which is the more beautiful, of the doors of San Giovanni, I would take nothing for my labours; but if I brought it off according to my promise, I was content that it should be valued, and then they should give me a thousand scudi less than it was valued at by the men of the art.

These Wardens were much pleased with what I had proposed to them, and went to speak of it to the Duke — and among the others there was Piero Salviati — thinking to tell the Duke something that would be most welcome to him; and it was quite the contrary; and he said that I always wanted to do the opposite of everything he wished me to do: and without any other conclusion the said Piero left the Duke.

When I heard this, at once I went off to find the Duke, who showed himself somewhat displeased with me; whom I begged to be so good as to listen to me, and so he promised me: so that I began at the beginning; and with so many fine arguments I gave him to understand the truth of the matter, showing His Excellency that it was a great expense thrown away: so that I had much softened him by telling him that if it did not please His most illustrious Excellency that that door should be made, it was necessary to make two pulpits for that choir, and that these would be two great works and would be to the glory of His most illustrious Excellency, and that I would make in them a great quantity of scenes in bronze, in low relief, with many ornaments: so I softened him and he ordered me to make the models.

I made several models and endured the greatest labours: and among the others I made one with eight sides, with much greater study than I had put into the others, and it seemed to me that it was much more convenient for the service it had to do. And because I had taken them several times to the Palace, His Excellency let me know by Messer Cesare, the keeper of the wardrobe, that I should leave them. After the Duke had seen them, I saw that of these His Excellency had chosen the least beautiful. One day His Excellency had me called, and in talking of these said models I told him and showed him with many reasons that the one with eight sides would have been much more convenient for such a service, and much more beautiful to see. The Duke answered me that he wanted me to make it square, because it pleased him much more that way; and so he talked with me very pleasantly a good while. I did not fail to say everything that occurred to me, in defence of the art. Whether the Duke knew that I was telling the truth, and still wanted it done his own way, a long time went by in which nothing was said to me.

Chapter 99

At this time the great marble for the Neptune had been brought by the river Arno and then taken along the Greve onto the road of Poggio a Caiano, so that it might afterwards be more easily brought to Florence by that level road, where I went to see it. And although I knew for a certainty that the Duchess out of her own favour had had it given to the knight Bandinello; not out of any envy I bore Bandinello, but rather moved by pity for the poor ill-fortuned marble (let a man beware, whatever the thing may be, when it is subject to a bad destiny, that in trying to save it from some plain harm, it happens that it falls into something much worse, as the said marble did into the hands of Bartolomeo Ammannati, of whom the truth shall be told in its place), when I had seen the most beautiful marble, I at once took its height and its thickness in every direction, and having gone back to Florence, I made several little models to the purpose.

Then I went to Poggio a Caiano, where the Duke was and the Duchess and the Prince their son; and finding them all at table, the Duke was eating apart with the Duchess, so that I set about entertaining the Prince.
