And having entertained him a good while, the Duke, who was in a room nearby, heard me, and with much favour had me called; and when I had come into their Excellencies' presences, with many pleasant words the Duchess began to talk with me: in which talk little by little I began to speak of that most beautiful marble I had seen; and I began to say how their ancestors had made their most noble School so full of excellence only by making all the talented men vie together in their professions; and in that excellent way had been made the admirable dome, and the most beautiful doors of San Giovanni, and so many other fine temples and statues, which made a crown of so much excellence for their city, which since the ancients had never had its equal.

At once the Duchess said to me in anger that she knew very well what I meant to say; and she said that in her presence I should never speak of that marble again, because I was displeasing her by it. I said: "Then do I displease you by wanting to be the advocate of your Excellencies, doing everything so that you may be better served? Consider, my lady: if your most illustrious Excellencies are content that everybody should make a model of a Neptune, even though you are resolved that Bandinello shall have it, this will be the cause of Bandinello, for his own honour, setting himself with greater study to make a fine model than he will do knowing that he has no competitors: and in this way you, my lords, will be much better served and you will not take away the spirit of the excellent School, and you will see who is roused to the good: I mean to the fine manner of this admirable art; and you, my lords, will show that you take pleasure in it and understand it."

The Duchess in great anger told me that I had made her sick of it, and that she wanted that marble to be Bandinello's, and she said: "Ask the Duke, for His Excellency too wants it to be Bandinello's." When the Duchess had spoken, the Duke, who had all this time been quiet, said: "It is twenty years since I had that fine marble quarried on purpose for Bandinello, and so I want Bandinello to have it, and let it be his."

At once I turned to the Duke, and said: "My lord, I beg Your most illustrious Excellency to do me the favour of letting me say four words to Your Excellency for your own service." The Duke told me to say everything I wanted, and that he would listen to me. Then I said: "Know, my lord, that that marble from which Bandinello made his Hercules and Cacus was quarried for that admirable Michelangelo Buonarroti, who had made a model of a Samson with four figures, which would have been the most beautiful thing in the world; and your Bandinello got out of it two figures alone, badly made and all patched together: on account of which the excellent School still cries out at the great wrong that was done to that fine marble. I believe that more than a thousand sonnets were stuck up on it, in reproach of that wretched work; and I know that Your most illustrious Excellency remembers it very well.

"And so, my worthy lord, if those men who had such a charge were so unwise that they took that fine marble from Michelangelo, for whom it had been quarried, and gave it to Bandinello, who spoiled it, as is seen; oh! will you ever allow that this still far more beautiful marble, even though it belongs to Bandinello, who would spoil it, should not be given to some other able man who would make something of it for you? See to it, my lord, that everybody who wishes may make a model and that then they may all be uncovered to the School, and Your most illustrious Excellency will hear what the School says; and Your Excellency with that good judgment of yours will know how to choose the best, and in this way you will not throw your money away, nor will you take away the excellent spirit of so admirable a School, which is today matchless in the world: which is all to the glory of Your most illustrious Excellency."

When the Duke had listened to me most kindly, he at once got up from the table and turning to me, said: "Go, my Benvenuto, and make a model, and win that fine marble for yourself, because you are telling me the truth, and I know it." The Duchess, threatening me with her head, said in scorn, muttering, I do not know what; and I made them my reverence, and went back to Florence, and it seemed a thousand years to me until I could set my hand to the said model.

Chapter 100

When the Duke came to Florence, without letting me know anything he came to my house, where I showed him two little models different from one another; and although he praised them both to me, he told me that one of them pleased him more than the other, and that I should finish well the one that pleased him, for it would be good for me: and because His Excellency had seen the one Bandinello had made and other men's too, His Excellency praised mine by far the more, as I was told by many of his courtiers, who had heard it.

Among the other notable things worth taking great account of, was that, the Cardinal of Santa Fiore having come to Florence, and the Duke taking him to Poggio a Caiano, on the way, as they passed, and seeing the said marble, the Cardinal praised it greatly, and then asked to whom His Excellency had assigned the working of it. The Duke said at once: "To my Benvenuto, who has made a very beautiful model of it." And this was repeated to me by men of good faith: and because of this I went off to find the Duchess and took her some pleasant little things of my art, which Her most illustrious Excellency held very dear; then she asked me what I was working at: to whom I said: "My lady, I have taken it as a pleasure to make one of the most laborious works that were ever made in the world: and this is a Crucifix of the whitest marble, on a cross of the blackest marble, and it is as large as a big living man."

At once she asked me what I meant to do with it. I said to her: "Know, my lady, that I would not give it to anybody who gave me two thousand gold ducats in gold for it; because no man ever set himself to such an extreme labour as such a work; nor would I have bound myself to make it for any lord whatever, for fear of being left in shame by it. I have bought the marbles with my own money, and I have kept a young man about two years, who has helped me, and between marbles and irons on which it is fixed, and wages, it costs me more than three hundred scudi; so that I would not give it for two thousand gold scudi; but if Your most illustrious Excellency will do me one entirely lawful favour, I will willingly make you a free present of it: I only beg Your most illustrious Excellency not to disfavour me, nor to favour me either, in the models which His most illustrious Excellency has ordered to be made of the Neptune for the great marble."

She said in great scorn: "Then you make no account at all of my help or my hindrance?" "On the contrary, I make great account of it, my lady; or why do I offer to give you what I value at two thousand ducats? But I trust so much to my laborious and disciplined studies, that I promise myself to win the palm, even if that great Michelangelo Buonarroti were in it, from whom, and never from anybody else, I have learned all I know: and it would be much dearer to me that he should make a model, who knows so much, than these others who know little; because with that so great master of mine I could gain a great deal, whereas with these others there is nothing to be gained." When I had said my words, she got up half in scorn, and I went back to my work, pressing on with my model as much as I could.

And when I had finished it, the Duke came to see it, and there were with him two ambassadors, that of the Duke of Ferrara and that of the Signory of Lucca, and so it pleased him greatly, and the Duke said to those lords: "Benvenuto truly deserves it." Then both the said men favoured me greatly, and more the ambassador of Lucca, who was a lettered person, and a doctor. I, who had drawn a little apart so that they might say everything they thought, hearing myself favoured, at once came up, and turning to the Duke, said: "My lord, Your most illustrious Excellency ought to take one more admirable care: to command that whoever wishes should make another model in clay, of exactly the size that comes out of that marble; and in that way Your most illustrious Excellency will see much better who deserves it; and I tell you: that if Your Excellency gives it to one who does not deserve it, you will not do a wrong to him who deserves it, rather you will do a great wrong to yourself, because you will get harm and shame by it; whereas doing the contrary, by giving it to one who deserves it, first of all you will get the greatest glory by it and will spend your treasure well, and men of talent will then believe that you take pleasure in these things and understand them."

As soon as I had said these words, the Duke shrugged his shoulders, and having started off to go away, the ambassador of Lucca said to the Duke: "My lord, this Benvenuto of yours is a terrible man." The Duke said: "He is much more terrible than you say; and it would have been good for him if he had not been so terrible, because by now he would have had things which he has not had." These very words were repeated to me by that same ambassador, as though reproving me that I ought not to behave so. To whom I said that I wished my lord well, as his affectionate and faithful servant, and did not know how to play the flatterer.

Then, several weeks having passed, Bandinello died; and it was believed that, besides his own excesses, this unhappiness, seeing himself lose the marble, was a good part of the cause.

Book Two, Chapters 101 to 107

Chapter 101

The said Bandinello had heard that I had made that Crucifix I spoke of above: he at once set his hand to a piece of marble, and made that Pietà which is seen in the church of the Nunziata. And because I had dedicated my Crucifix to Santa Maria Novella, and had already fixed the hooks there to put it up, I only asked to make under the feet of my Crucifix, in the ground, a little coffin, to go into after I am dead. The said friars told me that they could not grant me such a thing without asking their Wardens; to whom I said: "Oh friars, why did you not ask the Wardens first about giving a place to my fine Crucifix, since without their leave you have let me put up the hooks and the other things?" And for this reason I would no longer give my so extreme labours to the church of Santa Maria Novella, although afterwards those Wardens came to find me and begged me for it.

At once I turned to the church of the Nunziata, and talking of giving it in the way I had wanted to give it to Santa Maria Novella, those good friars of the said Nunziata all in agreement told me to put it in their church, and to make my tomb there in whatever way seemed and pleased me best. Bandinello, having got wind of this, set himself with great diligence to finishing his Pietà, and asked the Duchess to get him that chapel which had belonged to the Pazzi; which was got with difficulty: and as soon as he had it, with great haste he set up his work there, which was not altogether finished when he died. The Duchess said that she had helped him in life and that she would help him in death too; and that although he was dead, I was never to plan on having that marble. Then Bernardone the broker told me one day, when we met in the country, whom the Duchess had given the marble to; to whom I said: "Oh unlucky marble! certainly in Bandinello's hands it had come off badly, but in Ammannati's hands it has come off a hundred times worse!"

I had had orders from the Duke to make the clay model, of the size that came out of the marble, and he had had me provided with wood and clay, and had a bit of a screen made for me in the loggia, where my Perseus is, and paid me a labourer. I set my hand to it with all the diligence I could, and made the skeleton of wood by my own good rule, and carried it happily to its finish, not caring to make it in marble, because I knew that the Duchess had made up her mind that I should not have it, and for this I did not care about it: I only liked to endure that labour, with which I promised myself that, when I had finished it, the Duchess, who was after all a person of wit, seeing that she would have seen it by then, I promised myself that she would have been sorry to have done so immeasurable a wrong to the marble and to herself.

One was being made by Giovanni the Fleming in the cloisters of Santa Croce, and one was being made by Vincenzio Danti of Perugia, in the house of Messer Ottaviano de' Medici; another was begun by Moschino's son at Pisa, and another was being made by Bartolomeo Ammannati in the Loggia, for they had divided it up among them.

When I had it all well roughed out, and wanted to begin to finish the head, to which I had already given a little first working, the Duke had come down from the Palace, and Giorgetto the painter had taken him into Ammannati's room, to let him see the Neptune, on which the said Giorgino had worked with his own hand for a great many days together with the said Ammannati and with all his workmen. While the Duke was looking at it, I was told that he was very little satisfied with it; and although the said Giorgino wanted to fill him up with those chatterings of his, the Duke shook his head, and turning to his Messer Gianstefano, said: "Go and ask Benvenuto if his giant is so far forward that he would be content to give me a little sight of it."

The said Messer Gianstefano very shrewdly and most kindly did me the errand on the Duke's behalf; and moreover he told me that if it did not seem to me that my work was yet fit to be shown, I should freely say so: because the Duke knew very well that I had had little help for so great an undertaking. I said that he should come and welcome, and although my work was little advanced, His most illustrious Excellency's understanding was such that he would very well judge what it might turn out when finished.

So the said gentleman did the errand to the Duke, who came willingly: and as soon as His Excellency entered the room, casting his eyes on my work, he showed himself much satisfied with it: then he went right round it, stopping at the four views, exactly as a man would have done who was most expert in the art; then he made many great signs and gestures of showing that it pleased him, and only said: "Benvenuto, you have only to give it a final skin;" then he turned to those who were with His Excellency, and said much good of my work, saying: "The small model which I saw at his house pleased me greatly; but this work of his has surpassed the goodness of the model."

Chapter 102

As it pleased God, who does everything for our good — I mean for those who acknowledge Him and who believe in Him, God always defends them — in these days there came before me a certain rascal from Vicchio, called Piermaria d'Anterigoli, and by nickname lo Sbietta: this man's trade is that of a shepherd, and because he is a close relative of Messer Guido Guidi, the physician and today provost of Pescia, I lent him my ears. He offered to sell me a farm of his for my natural life, which farm I did not want to go and see, because I wanted to finish my model of the giant Neptune; and also because there was no need for me to see it, since he was selling it to me by its income: which the said man had given me in a note of so many bushels of grain and of wine, oil and fodder and chestnuts and other advantages, which I reckoned that at the time we were then in, the said goods were worth much more than a hundred gold scudi in gold, and I gave him six hundred and fifty scudi counting the taxes.

So that, having left me a paper in his own hand that he meant always, for as long as I lived, to keep up the said incomes for me, I did not care to go and see the said farm; but I did inform myself, as best I could, whether the said Sbietta and Ser Filippo, his brother of the same blood, were in such good circumstances that I should be safe. So by many different persons who knew them, I was told that I was quite safe.

We called by agreement Ser Pierfrancesco Bertoldi, notary at the Merchants' Court; and the first thing I put into his hands everything that the said Sbietta meant to keep up for me, thinking that the said paper had to be named in the contract: so that the said notary, who drew it, attended to the twenty-two boundaries, which the said Sbietta named to him, and as I see it he did not remember to include in the said contract what the said seller had offered me; and I, while the notary wrote, was working; and because he took several hours to write, I did a great piece of the head of the said Neptune. So, having finished the said contract, Sbietta began to make the greatest fuss of me in the world, and I did the like to him. He made me presents of kids, cheeses, capons, curd cheeses and a great deal of fruit, so that I began to be half ashamed of it: and for these kindnesses I took him, every time he came to Florence, away from the inn; and many times he had one or another of his relatives with him, who came too; and in pleasant ways he began to tell me that it was a shame that I should have bought a farm, and that so many weeks had gone by now, and I could not make up my mind to leave my business a little to my workmen for three days and go and see it. He was able to do so much with his flattering me that in my ill hour I went to see it; and the said Sbietta received me in his house with so many caresses and with so much honour that he could not have done more for a duke; and his wife made more fuss of me than he did; and in this way we went on a good while, until everything came about that they had planned to do, he and his brother Ser Filippo.

Chapter 103

I did not fail to press on with my work of the Neptune, and had already roughed the whole of it out, as I said above, by a very good rule, which nobody before me had ever used or known; so that, although I was sure I should not have the marble for the reasons given above, I believed I should soon have finished, and at once let it be seen in the Square, only for my own satisfaction.

The season was warm and pleasant, so that, being so made much of by these two rascals, I set out one Wednesday, which was a double feast day, from my farm at Trespiano, and I had made a good breakfast, so that it was past twenty hours when I arrived at Vicchio; and at once I found Ser Filippo at the gate of Vicchio, who seemed to know that I was coming; so many caresses did he make me, and he took me to Sbietta's house, where his shameless wife was, and she too made me immeasurable caresses; to whom I gave a hat of the finest straw; because she said she had never seen a finer one. Sbietta was not there then.

As evening drew on, we all had supper together very pleasantly: then I was given an honourable room, where I rested in a very clean bed; and to two servants of mine the like was given, according to their rank. In the morning, when I got up, the same caresses were made me. I went to see my farm, which pleased me: and so much grain and other fodder was made over to me; and then, having come back to Vicchio, the priest Ser Filippo said to me: "Benvenuto, do not fear; for even if you have not found there the whole of what has been promised you, be of good cheer, for it will be made up to you and more, because you have had to do with honest men: and know that we have dismissed that farm labourer, because he is a bad one." This labourer was called Mariano Rosegli, who several times said to me: "Look well to your own affairs, for in the end you will know which of us is the greater rogue." This countryman, when he said these words to me, sneered in a certain bad way, shaking his head, as much as to say: "Just go along, and you will find out." I made a bit of a bad judgment of it, but I did not imagine anything of what happened to me.

Having come back from the farm, which is two miles away from Vicchio, towards the mountains, I found the said priest, who with his usual caresses was waiting for me; so we went to eat all together: this was not a dinner, but was a good luncheon. Then, going about Vicchio for a stroll, the market had already begun; I saw myself looked at by all those people of Vicchio as at something unusual to see, and more than by any of the others by an honest man, who has lived for many years in Vicchio, and his wife makes bread to sell. He has near there, about a mile off, certain good holdings of his own; still he is content to live in that way. This honest man lives in a house of mine, which is in Vicchio, which was made over to me with the said farm, which is called the farm of the Fonte; and he said to me: "I am in your house, and at the proper time I shall give you your rent; or if you want it in advance, I will do it in any way you like: it is enough that you and I shall always be in agreement."

And while we were talking, I saw that this man kept fixing his eyes on me: so that I, forced by such a thing, said to him: "Now tell me, my dear Giovanni, why have you looked at me so fixedly several times?" This honest man said to me: "I will gladly tell you, if you, as the man you are, will promise me not to say that I told you." So I promised him. Then he said to me: "Know that that wretched priest Ser Filippo, not many days ago, was boasting of the cleverness of his brother Sbietta, saying how he had sold his farm to an old man for his life, who would not get through the whole year. You have had to do with a pair of rascals, so try to live as long as you can, and open your eyes, because you need to; I do not want to tell you any more."

Chapter 104

Going about the market for a stroll, I found there Giovanbattista Santini, and he and I were taken to supper by the said priest; and, as I have said before, it was about twenty hours, and on my account we had supper at such an early hour, because I had said that in the evening I meant to go back to Trespiano: so that things were quickly got ready, and Sbietta's wife bestirred herself, and among the others a certain Cecchino Buti, their farm servant.

When the salads had been made, and we were beginning to want to sit down at table, that bad priest, giving a certain nasty little laugh of his, said: "You must forgive me, because I cannot have supper with you, for a matter of great importance has come up for me on my brother Sbietta's account: since he is not here, I must take his place." We all begged him and we could never turn him from it: he went off, and we began to have supper.

When we had eaten the salads out of certain large dishes in common, and they were beginning to give us boiled meat, a bowl came for each of us. Santini, who was at table opposite me, said: "They are giving you all crockery different from the rest: now did you ever see finer?" I told him that I had not noticed such a thing. He also told me that I should call Sbietta's wife to the table, who, she and that Cecchino Buti, were running backwards and forwards, all extraordinarily busy. At last I begged that woman so much that she came; who complained, saying to me: "My dishes have not pleased you. That is why you eat so little." When I had praised the supper several times, telling her that I had never eaten either with more appetite or better, at last I said that I was eating exactly as much as I needed. I would never have imagined why that woman pressed me so hard to eat.

When we had finished supper it was past twenty-one hours, and I wanted to get back to Trespiano that evening, so as to be able to go the next day to my work at the Loggia: so I said goodbye to them all, and having thanked the woman I set off.

I was not three miles off before it seemed to me that my stomach was burning, and I felt myself in such distress that it seemed a thousand years to me until I could reach my farm at Trespiano. As it pleased God I arrived at night, with great labour, and at once gave orders to go and rest. In the night I could never rest, and moreover my bowels were moved, which forced me several times to go to the privy, so that, when broad day had come, feeling myself burning in the fundament, I wanted to see what it was: I found the cloth very bloody.

At once I imagined I had eaten something poisonous, and over and over again I went examining myself as to what it could have been: and there came back into my memory those plates and bowls and little bowls, given me different from the others by the said wife of Sbietta; and how that bad priest, Sbietta's brother, having taken so much trouble to do me so much honour, then would not stay to supper with us; and there also came back into my memory the said priest's having said how his Sbietta had made such a fine stroke by having sold a farm to an old man for his life, who would never get through the year; for those words had been repeated to me by that honest man Giovanni Sardella. So that I made up my mind that they had given me in a little bowl of sauce, which was made very well and very pleasant to eat, a pinch of sublimate, because sublimate does all those harms which I saw I had; but because I am used to eating little sauce or seasoning with meat, other than salt, I happened to eat two mouthfuls of that sauce, because it was so good to the taste. And I went on remembering how the said wife of Sbietta pressed me many times in different ways, telling me to eat that sauce: so that I knew for a certainty that with that said sauce they had given me that little bit of sublimate.

Chapter 105

Finding myself afflicted in that way, I still went all the same to work on my giant in the said Loggia: so that in a few days afterwards the great illness so overcame me that it laid me up in bed. As soon as the Duchess heard that I was ill, she had the work of the unlucky marble given free to Bartolomeo Ammannati, who sent to tell me by Messer —— that I might do what I liked with my begun model, because he had won the marble.

This Messer —— was one of the lovers of the wife of the said Bartolomeo Ammannati; and because he was the most favoured as being courteous and discreet, this said Ammannato gave him every convenience, of which there would be great things to tell. Still I do not want to do as Bandinello, his master, did, who with his talk went outside the art; it is enough that I said I had always guessed it; and that he should tell Bartolomeo to work hard, so that he might show himself grateful to fortune for so great a favour, which she had so undeservedly done him.

So, discontented, I stayed in bed, and had myself treated by that most excellent man Master Francesco da Montevarchi, the physician, and together with him Master Raffaello de' Pilli treated me in surgery; because that sublimate had so burned my bowel in the fundament that I could not hold my excrement at all. And because the said Master Francesco, having seen that the poison had done all the harm it could, since it had not been enough to overcome the strength of the strong nature he found in me, said to me one day: "Benvenuto, thank God, because you have won; and do not fear, for I mean to cure you, to spite the rascals who have wanted to do you harm." Then Master Raffaellino said: "This will be one of the finest and one of the most difficult cures that there has ever been notice of: know, Benvenuto, that you have eaten a mouthful of sublimate." At these words Master Francesco cut in over his voice and said: "Perhaps it was some venomous grub." I said that I knew most certainly that it was poison and who had given it me: and here every one of us was silent. They attended to treating me more than six whole months; and I was more than a year before I could make use of my life.

Chapter 106

At this time the Duke went off to make his entry into Siena, and Ammannati had gone some months before to make the triumphal arches. A bastard son that Ammannati had, had stayed behind in the Loggia, and had taken away certain curtains that were over my model of the Neptune, which, since it was not finished, I kept covered. At once I went to complain to Signor Don Francesco, the Duke's son, who showed himself well disposed to me, and I told him how they had uncovered my figure for me, which was unfinished; that if it had been finished, I would not have cared about it. To this the said Prince answered me, threatening a little with his head, and said: "Benvenuto, do not care about its being uncovered, because they do so much the more against themselves; and if all the same you are content for me to have it covered for you, I will have it covered at once." And with these words His most illustrious Excellency added many others much in my favour, in the presence of many lords. Then I told him that I begged His Excellency to give me the means of being able to finish it, because I wanted to make a present of it together with the small model to His Excellency. He answered me that he willingly accepted the one and the other, and that he would have me given all the conveniences I asked for.

So I fed on this little bit of favour, which was the cause of the saving of my life; because, so many immeasurable troubles and unhappinesses having come on me at once, I saw myself failing: by that little bit of favour I comforted myself with some hope of life.

Chapter 107

The year having already gone by since I had the farm of the Fonte from Sbietta, and besides all the unpleasantnesses done me both of poisons and of their other robberies, seeing that the said farm did not yield me half of what they had offered me, and I had, besides the contracts, a paper in Sbietta's own hand, in which he bound himself before witnesses to keep up the said incomes for me, I went off to the lords Councillors; for at that time Messer Alfonso Quistello was alive and was the fiscal, and he sat with the lords Councillors; and among the Councillors were Averardo Serristori and Federigo de' Ricci: I do not remember all their names: there was also one of the Alessandri: it is enough that they were a set of men of great account.

Now, having told my case to the magistracy, they all with one voice wanted the said Sbietta to give me back my money, except Federigo de' Ricci, who at that time made use of the said Sbietta; so that they all condoled with me that Federigo de' Ricci was holding them back from dispatching the matter for me; and among the others Averardo Serristori with all the rest; although he made an extraordinary noise about it, and so did the man of the Alessandri: so that the said Federigo having held the thing up so long that the magistracy had come to the end of its term of office, the said gentleman found me one morning, after they had come out onto the square of the Nunziata, and without any respect in the world said in a loud voice: "Federigo de' Ricci has been able to do so much more than all the rest of us, that you have been murdered against our will."

I do not want to say anything else about this, because it would too much offend those who hold the supreme power of the government; it is enough that I was murdered at the pleasure of a rich citizen, only because he made use of that shepherd.

Book Two, Chapters 108 to 113

Chapter 108

The Duke being at Livorno, I went to find him, only to ask him for my leave. Feeling my strength coming back to me, and seeing that I was not employed on anything, it grieved me to do so great a wrong to my studies; so that, having made up my mind, I went off to Livorno, and found the Duke there, who gave me a most gracious welcome. And because I stayed there several days, every day I rode out with His Excellency, and had plenty of leisure to be able to say everything I wanted, because the Duke went out of Livorno and rode four miles along the sea, where he was having a small fortress made, and so as not to be troubled by too many people, he was glad that I should talk with him.

So that one day, seeing very notable favours done me, I began deliberately to speak of Sbietta, that is of Piermaria d'Anterigoli, and said: "My lord, I want to tell Your most illustrious Excellency a marvellous case, by which Your Excellency will know the cause which hindered me from being able to finish my Neptune in clay, which I was working at in the Loggia. Let Your most illustrious Excellency know that I had bought a farm for my life from Sbietta." It is enough that I told the whole of it in detail, never staining the truth with anything false.

Now when I came to the poison, I said that, if I had ever been a servant welcome in the sight of His most illustrious Excellency, he ought, instead of punishing Sbietta or those who gave me the poison, to give them something good; because the poison was not enough to kill me; but it was exactly enough to purge me of a deadly viscous humour, which I had inside my stomach and my intestines; "which has worked in such a way that whereas, being as I was, I could have lived three or four years, this kind of medicine has done so much that I believe I have gained life for more than twenty years; and for this, with more willingness than ever, I thank God the more; and so it is true what I have sometimes heard said by certain people, who say: 'God sends us evil, that He may put good in.'" The Duke stood listening to me for more than two miles of the way, always with great attention; he only said: "Oh, bad people!" I concluded that I was obliged to them and went on to other pleasant talk.

I watched for a suitable day, and finding him pleasant as I wanted, I begged His most illustrious Excellency to give me my good leave, so that I might not throw away some years while I was still good for making something, and that as for what I was still owed of my Perseus, His most illustrious Excellency might give it me when it pleased him. And with this talk I spread myself with many long ceremonies in thanking His most illustrious Excellency, who answered me nothing at all in the world, but rather it seemed to me that he showed that he had taken it badly.

The next day after, Messer Bartolomeo Concino, the Duke's secretary, one of the chief ones, found me, and half blustering, said to me: "The Duke says that if you want your leave, he will give it you; but if you want to work, he will set you to work: if only you could do as much as His Excellency will give you to do!" I answered him that I desired nothing but to have work, and above all from His most illustrious Excellency more than from all the rest of the men in the world, be they pope or emperors or kings; I would more gladly serve His most illustrious Excellency for a soldo than anybody else for a ducat. Then he said to me: "If that is your mind, you are already agreed without saying anything more; so go back to Florence and be of good cheer, because the Duke wishes you well." So I went back to Florence.

Chapter 109

As soon as I was in Florence, there came to find me a certain man called Raffaellone Scheggia, a weaver of cloth of gold, who said this to me: "My Benvenuto, I want to bring you to an agreement with Piermaria Sbietta": to whom I said that nobody could bring us to an agreement but the lords Councillors, and that in this batch of Councillors Sbietta would not have a Federigo de' Ricci, who for a present of two fat kids, without caring either for God or for his own honour, would keep up so wicked a fight and do so foul a wrong to holy justice.

Having said these words, together with many others, this Raffaello always affectionately kept telling me that a thrush one could eat in peace was much better than a very fat capon, even if one were certain of having it, and having it in the midst of so much war: and he told me that the ways of lawsuits sometimes drag on so long that in that time I would have done better to spend it on some fine work, by which I would gain much greater honour and much greater profit. I, who knew that he was telling the truth, began to lend an ear to his words; so that in a short time he brought us to an agreement in this way: that Sbietta should take the said farm from me on lease for seventy gold scudi in gold a year, for the whole time during my natural life.

When we came to make the contract, which was drawn by Ser Giovanni di Ser Matteo da Falgano, Sbietta said that in the way we had talked of it, it would carry a heavier tax; and that he would not fail me — "and so it is well that we should make this lease from five years to five years" — and that he would keep his faith with me, without ever renewing any more lawsuits. And so that rascal of a priest, his brother, promised me too; and in the way described, of the five years, the contract was made.

Chapter 110

Wanting to go on to another subject, and to leave off for a while talking of this immeasurable villainy, I am forced first to tell what followed on the five years of the lease, at the end of which those two rascals would not keep up any of the promises they had made me, but rather wanted to give me back my farm and would not hold it on lease any more. On account of which I began to complain, and they flung the contract in my face; so that by way of their bad faith I could not help myself.

Seeing this, I told them that the Duke and the Prince of Florence would not put up with men being murdered so foully in their cities. Now this fright was of such force that they put upon me that same Raffaello Scheggia who had made that first agreement; and they said that they did not want to give me the gold scudi in gold, as they had given me for the five past years: to whom I answered that I wanted nothing less. The said Raffaello came to find me, and said: "My Benvenuto, you know that I am on your side: now they have referred the whole thing to me;" and he showed it me written in their own hand. I, who did not know that he was a close relative of theirs, thought this very good, and so I referred myself to him altogether and entirely.

This gallant man came one evening at half an hour into the night, and it was in the month of August, and with so many words of his he forced me to have the contract drawn, only because he knew that if it had been put off until morning, that trick he meant to play me would not have succeeded for him. So the contract was made, that he should give me sixty-five scudi in money a year of rent, in two payments every year, during the whole of my natural life. And although I shook myself and would not be patient about it at all, he showed the paper in my own hand, with which he moved everybody to put me in the wrong; and he said that he had done it all for my good and that he was on my side; and neither the notary nor the others knowing that he was their relative, they all put me in the wrong: on account of which I gave way in good time, and I shall try to live as long as I possibly can.

After this I made another mistake in the month of December following, 1566. I bought half the farm of the Poggio from them, that is from Sbietta, for two hundred scudi in money, which borders on that first one of mine of the Fonte, with three years' right of redemption, and I let it to them on lease. I did it to do them good. It would take far too long a spreading out in writing if I wanted to tell the great cruelties they have done me; I mean to refer it altogether and entirely to God, who has always defended me from those who have wanted to do me harm.

Chapter 111

Having entirely finished my marble Crucifix, it seemed to me that by setting it upright and putting it raised some braccia above the ground, it ought to show much better than keeping it on the ground; and although it showed well, once I had set it upright it showed a great deal better, so that I was very well satisfied with it: and so I began to show it to whoever wanted to see it.

As God willed, it was spoken of to the Duke and to the Duchess; so that when they came from Pisa, one day unexpectedly both their most illustrious Excellencies with all the nobility of their Court came to my house only to see the said Crucifix: which pleased them so much that the Duke and the Duchess never left off giving me endless praises; and so consequently all those lords and gentlemen who were present. Now when I saw that they were much satisfied, I began pleasantly to thank them, telling them that their having taken from me the labour of the marble of the Neptune had been the very cause of my having carried out such a work, in which nobody else had ever set himself before me; and although I had endured the greatest labour I ever endured in the world, it seemed to me that I had spent it well, and all the more since their most illustrious Excellencies praised it to me so much; and because I could never believe I should find anybody who could be worthier of it than their most illustrious Excellencies, I willingly made them a present of it; only I begged them, before they went away, to be so good as to come into the ground floor of my house.

At these words of mine they got up at once pleasantly, and left the workshop, and having come into the house they saw my little model of the Neptune and of the fountain, which the Duchess had never seen before that moment. It had such force in the Duchess's eyes that at once she raised a cry of wonder beyond reckoning; and turning to the Duke she said: "Upon my life, I did not think it a tenth part as beautiful." At these words the Duke said to her several times: "Oh, did I not tell you so?" And so between them they talked of it a good while, much to my honour; then the Duchess called me to her, and after many praises given me by way of excusing herself, for in the comment on those words she seemed almost to be asking my pardon, she then told me that she wanted me to have a marble quarried to my own liking, and wanted me to set to work on it. At those kindly words I said that if their most illustrious Excellencies gave me the conveniences, I would willingly for love of them set myself to such a laborious undertaking. To this the Duke at once answered and said: "Benvenuto, you shall be given all the conveniences you know how to ask for, and moreover what I shall give you of my own accord, which will be worth far more;" and with these pleasant words they went away, and left me quite content.

Chapter 112

Many weeks having gone by, and nothing being said about me; so that, seeing that no orders were given to do anything, I was half in despair. At this time the Queen of France sent Messer Baccio del Bene to our Duke to ask him for money on loan; and the Duke kindly served her in it, so it was said; and because Messer Baccio del Bene and I were very familiar friends, having recognized one another in Florence, we were very glad to see each other; so that he told me all those great favours His most illustrious Excellency was doing him; and in talking he asked me how I had great works in hand. On account of which I told him, as it had happened, the whole case of the great Neptune and of the fountain, and the great wrong the Duchess had done me.

At these words he told me on the Queen's behalf how Her Majesty had the greatest desire to finish the tomb of King Henry her husband, and that Daniele da Volterra had undertaken to make a great bronze horse, and that the time he had promised it for had gone by, and that a great deal of ornament was to go on the said tomb; so that if I wanted to go back to France to my castle, she would have me given all the conveniences I knew how to ask for, provided I had a mind to serve her. I told the said Messer Baccio to ask for me from my Duke; that if His most illustrious Excellency were content with it, I would willingly go back to France. Messer Baccio cheerfully said: "We shall go back together;" and he set it down as done.

So the next day, when he was speaking with the Duke, the talk came round to me; so that he told the Duke that, if it were with his good favour, the Queen would make use of me. To this the Duke at once answered and said: "Benvenuto is that able man the world knows; but now he no longer wants to work;" and having gone on to other talk, the next day I went to find the said Messer Baccio, who told me the whole of it again. At this I, who could hold back no longer, said: "Oh, if, since His most illustrious Excellency gave me nothing to do, I of my own accord have made one of the most difficult works that were ever made by anybody in the world, and it costs me more than two hundred scudi, which I have spent out of my poverty; oh, what would I have done, if His most illustrious Excellency had set me to work! I tell you truly that a great wrong is done me." The good gentleman repeated to the Duke everything I had answered. The Duke told him that he was joking, and that he wanted me for himself; so that several times I stirred up the matter of going off with God. The Queen would not talk about it any more so as not to displease the Duke, and so I was left quite thoroughly discontented.

Chapter 113

At this time the Duke went off, with all his Court and with all his sons, except the Prince who was in Spain: they went through the maremmas of Siena; and on that journey he came to Pisa. The Cardinal took the poison of that bad air first of all: so that a few days later a pestilential fever attacked him and in a short time killed him. This was the Duke's right eye: he was handsome and good, and it was the greatest loss. I let several days go by, until I thought that the tears would be dried: then I went off to Pisa.
